Acts — Saints Holy Bible cross-references
977 completed source verses; 993 retained pairs; 1003 source units read; 26 deferred units.
First unfinished: Acts 2:9. Next new source review in this book: none. All original source units in this book have been read. Overall next new source: Romans 3:1.
Completed means an initial curated cross-reference pass: the complete supplied English source unit and each retained target passage were read, and each pair has a specific explanation. It does not mean exhaustive coverage or independent specialist approval.
Original Saints numbering is retained.

Acts 1:1
In my first account, Theophilus, I wrote about all that Jesus began both to do and to teach
  Luke 1:1–1:4 — thematic parallel: Both accounts address Theophilus and describe the writing of a narrative. Acts recalls its first account about Jesus' deeds and teaching; Luke explains its orderly account and purpose of assurance. The shared addressee is not given an additional unrecorded biography.
    Luke 1:1 Since many have undertaken to compile an account of the events fulfilled among us,
    Luke 1:2 just as those who from the beginning were eyewitnesses and servants of the word handed them down to us,
    Luke 1:3 it seemed good to me also, having followed everything carefully from the beginning, to write an orderly account for you, most excellent Theophilus,
    Luke 1:4 so that you may know the certainty of the matters in which you have been instructed.

Acts 1:2
until the day when, after giving instructions through the Holy Spirit to the apostles whom He had chosen, He was taken up.
  Luke 24:50–24:51 — narrative parallel: Both describe Jesus being taken into heaven after His ministry with the disciples. Acts adds Spirit-mediated instructions to chosen apostles; Luke describes the Bethany setting and blessing. Luke does not repeat every detail of the Spirit's role stated in Acts.
    Luke 24:50 And He led them out as far as toward Bethany, and lifted His hands and blessed them.
    Luke 24:51 And it happened that while He blessed them, He parted from them and was carried up into heaven.

Acts 1:3
To them He also presented Himself alive after His suffering, with many proofs, appearing to them over forty days and speaking about the kingdom of God.
  Luke 24:36–24:43 — narrative parallel: Luke describes a post-suffering appearance with bodily evidence and eating; Acts summarizes many proofs and appearances over forty days. This supplies a concrete resurrection-appearance comparison without making Luke's single scene the entirety of Acts' forty-day period.
    Luke 24:36 While they were speaking these things, He Himself stood among them and said to them, “Peace be with you.”
    Luke 24:37 But they were startled and became afraid, thinking they were seeing a spirit.
    Luke 24:38 And He said to them, “Why are you troubled, and why do doubts arise in your heart?
    Luke 24:39 See My hands and My feet, that it is I Myself. Feel Me and see, because a spirit does not have flesh and bones as you see that I have.”
    Luke 24:40 And having said this, He showed them His hands and feet.
    Luke 24:41 While they still did not believe for joy and were marveling, He said to them, “Do you have anything to eat here?”
    Luke 24:42 They gave Him a portion of broiled fish.
    Luke 24:43 And He took it and ate before them.

Acts 1:4
While gathered with them, He ordered them not to leave Jerusalem, but to wait for the Father’s promise, “which,” He said, “you heard from Me;
  Luke 24:49 — narrative parallel: Both have Jesus tell His followers to remain in the city for the Father's promise. Luke describes clothing with power from on high; Acts explicitly says not to leave Jerusalem while awaiting that promise. The different power images are retained.
    Luke 24:49 And behold, I am sending out the promise of My Father upon you; but remain in the city until you are clothed with power from on high.”

Acts 1:5
for John baptized with water, but you will be baptized in the Holy Spirit not many days from now.”
  Luke 3:16 — allusion: Jesus recalls the contrast between John's water baptism and the coming baptism in the Holy Spirit. Luke records John's water/Spirit contrast and additionally mentions fire. Acts' timing promise does not itself add fire to this sentence.
    Luke 3:16 John answered them all, “I baptize you with water, but One stronger than I is coming, whose sandal strap I am not worthy to untie. He will baptize you in the Holy Spirit and fire.

Acts 1:6
So those who had come together asked Him, “Lord, is this the time when Thou art restoring the kingdom to Israel?”
  Acts 3:20–3:21 — thematic parallel: Both use restoration and time language concerning Jesus' mission. Here followers ask about restoring Israel's kingdom; Peter later speaks of refreshing and the restoration of all things announced by prophets. The later speech does not supply the date withheld in the following answer.
    Acts 3:20 so that times of refreshing may come from the presence of the Lord, and He may send the Christ appointed for you, Jesus,
    Acts 3:21 whom heaven must receive until the times of restoration of all things of which God spoke through the mouth of His holy prophets from long ago.

Acts 1:7
He said to them, “It is not for you to know times or seasons that the Father has set by His own authority.
  Matthew 24:36 — thematic parallel: Both restrict the hearers' access to timing, with different statements about the Father. Matthew concerns the day and hour and names others who do not know; Acts concerns times and seasons set by the Father's authority. The questions and temporal terms differ rather than providing a restoration calendar.
    Matthew 24:36 Concerning that day and hour, no one knows, not even the angels of the heavens, nor the Son, but the Father alone.

Acts 1:8
But you will receive power when the Holy Spirit comes upon you, and you will be My witnesses in Jerusalem and [in] all Judea and Samaria, and to the end of the earth.”
  Luke 24:47–24:49 — narrative parallel: Both join Jerusalem-starting witness with promised power. Luke speaks of all nations and being clothed with power from on high; Acts specifies the Spirit, Judea, Samaria, and the earth's end. Their geographical and image formulations remain distinct.
    Luke 24:47 and that repentance for the forgiveness of sins should be proclaimed in His name to all the nations—beginning from Jerusalem.
    Luke 24:48 You are witnesses of these things.
    Luke 24:49 And behold, I am sending out the promise of My Father upon you; but remain in the city until you are clothed with power from on high.”

Acts 1:9
After saying these things, He was lifted up as they watched, and a cloud took Him from their sight.
  Luke 24:50–24:51 — narrative parallel: Both narrate Jesus' ascension in the followers' presence. Luke adds His blessing and the Bethany setting; Acts adds their watching and the cloud taking Him from sight. The cloud is not silently inserted into Luke's wording.
    Luke 24:50 And He led them out as far as toward Bethany, and lifted His hands and blessed them.
    Luke 24:51 And it happened that while He blessed them, He parted from them and was carried up into heaven.

Acts 1:10
As they were gazing into heaven while He went, look—two men stood beside them in white garments.
  Luke 24:4 — narrative parallel: Both describe two male figures standing beside witnesses in distinctive garments. Luke places flashing clothing at the empty tomb; Acts places white garments during the ascension. The recurring scene pattern does not identify them as the same two individuals.
    Luke 24:4 And it happened that, while they were perplexed about this, behold, two men stood beside them in flashing clothing.

Acts 1:11
They said, “Men of Galilee, why do you stand looking into heaven? This Jesus, who was taken up from you into heaven, will come in the same way as you saw Him go into heaven.”
  Matthew 24:30 — thematic parallel: The promised return of the ascended Jesus can be compared with the Son of Man's coming on heaven's clouds. Acts' preceding cloud belongs to His departure; Matthew adds power, glory, and mourning. The comparison does not fix the date or collapse departure and return into one event.
    Matthew 24:30 Then the sign of the Son of Man will appear in heaven, and then all the tribes of the earth will mourn; they will see the Son of Man coming upon the clouds of heaven with power and great glory.

Acts 1:12
Then they returned to Jerusalem from the mountain called Olivet, which is near Jerusalem, a Sabbath’s journey away.
  Luke 24:52 — narrative parallel: Both describe the followers' return to Jerusalem after the ascension. Acts gives Olivet and the Sabbath-journey distance; Luke gives worship and great joy. The distance description and the response of joy are different retained details.
    Luke 24:52 And they, having worshiped Him, returned to Jerusalem with great joy,

Acts 1:13
When they entered, they went up to the upper room where they were staying: Peter and John and James and Andrew, Philip and Thomas, Bartholomew and Matthew, James son of Alphaeus, Simon the Zealot, and Judas son of James.
  Luke 6:13–6:16 — narrative parallel: The eleven named here correspond to members of Luke's earlier Twelve list, while Judas Iscariot is absent. Luke narrates their initial selection and includes the betrayer; Acts records the remaining group before replacement. The comparison traces named membership across their initial selection and the later vacancy.
    Luke 6:13 When day came, He called His disciples and chose twelve from them, whom He also named apostles:
    Luke 6:14 Simon, whom He also named Peter, and his brother Andrew, and James and John, and Philip and Bartholomew,
    Luke 6:15 and Matthew and Thomas, and James son of Alphaeus, and Simon called the Zealot,
    Luke 6:16 and Judas son of James, and Judas Iscariot, who became a traitor.

Acts 1:14
All these devoted themselves with one accord to prayer, together with women, Mary the mother of Jesus, and His brothers.
  John 7:5 — thematic parallel: John earlier states that Jesus' brothers did not believe in Him; Acts later places His brothers in common prayer with His mother and the apostles. The reports differ in setting, and neither supplies an exact date or individual account of each brother's change.
    John 7:5 For not even His brothers believed in Him.

Acts 1:15
In those days Peter stood up among the brothers and said—about a hundred and twenty people were gathered together—
  Acts 15:7 — thematic parallel: Both present Peter rising to address fellow believers during a communal decision. Acts 1 concerns the apostolic vacancy in the following speech; Acts 15 concerns the Gentile question. The recurring speaker pattern does not equate the meetings or their decisions.
    Acts 15:7 After much debate, Peter rose and said to them, “Men, brothers, you know that in earlier days God made a choice among you, that through my mouth the Gentiles should hear the word of the good news and believe.

Acts 1:16
“Men, brothers, the Scripture had to be fulfilled, which the Holy Spirit spoke beforehand through David’s mouth concerning Judas, who became a guide to those who arrested Jesus;
  Luke 22:47 — narrative parallel: Peter identifies Judas as a guide to Jesus' arresters; Luke describes Judas, one of the Twelve, going ahead of the crowd toward Jesus. Acts also interprets Scripture's fulfillment, which is not itself stated in Luke's arrest-introduction verse.
    Luke 22:47 While He was still speaking, behold, a crowd came, and the one called Judas, one of the Twelve, was going before them. He drew near to Jesus to kiss Him.

Acts 1:17
for he was counted among us and received his allotted share in this ministry.”
  John 6:70–6:71 — thematic parallel: Both place Judas within the chosen Twelve before his betrayal. Acts emphasizes his counted membership and ministry share; John records Jesus choosing the Twelve and identifies the prospective betrayer. The chosen status is not mistaken for an endorsement of the betrayal.
    John 6:70 Jesus answered them, “Did I not choose you, the Twelve? Yet one of you is a devil.”
    John 6:71 He was speaking of Judas, son of Simon Iscariot, for this man was about to hand Him over, one of the Twelve.

Acts 1:18
Now this man acquired a field from the wages of wrongdoing, and falling headlong, he burst open in the middle, and all his entrails poured out.
  Matthew 27:3–27:8 — narrative parallel: Both associate Judas, betrayal payment, death, and a field. Acts describes his acquiring a field and falling headlong with his body bursting; Matthew describes returned money, hanging, and a priestly purchase. No unrecorded rope-breaking or combined sequence is supplied.
    Matthew 27:3 Then Judas, who had handed Him over, saw that He had been condemned. Filled with regret, he returned the thirty silver coins to the chief priests and elders,
    Matthew 27:4 saying, “I sinned by handing over innocent blood.” They said, “What is that to us? See to it yourself.”
    Matthew 27:5 He threw the silver coins into the sanctuary and withdrew; then he went away and hanged himself.
    Matthew 27:6 The chief priests took the silver coins and said, “It is not lawful to put them into the treasury, because they are the price of blood.”
    Matthew 27:7 After taking counsel, they bought the potter’s field with them as a burial place for strangers.
    Matthew 27:8 Therefore that field has been called Field of Blood until today.

Acts 1:19
This became known to all who lived in Jerusalem, so that the field was called in their own language Hakeldamach—that is, Field of Blood.
  Matthew 27:6–27:8 — narrative parallel: Both explain a field called Blood in connection with Judas' payment. Acts supplies the local name Hakeldamach and public knowledge; Matthew gives the priests' blood-money discussion and purchase. Their stated details and purchase agencies are not made identical.
    Matthew 27:6 The chief priests took the silver coins and said, “It is not lawful to put them into the treasury, because they are the price of blood.”
    Matthew 27:7 After taking counsel, they bought the potter’s field with them as a burial place for strangers.
    Matthew 27:8 Therefore that field has been called Field of Blood until today.

Acts 1:20
“For it is written in the book of Psalms: ‘Let his dwelling become deserted, and let there be no one living in it,’ and, ‘Let another take his office of oversight.’
  Psalms 68:26 — quotation: Peter applies the deserted-dwelling Psalm language to Judas. The Psalm speaks of their homestead and dwellings; Acts gives the application in the singular. The original Psalm does not name Judas, and its plural wording is not silently rewritten.
    Psalms 68:26 Let their homestead become deserted; let no one dwell in their dwellings.
  Psalms 108:8 — quotation: Peter quotes the request that another receive the office of oversight. The Psalm joins that request with shortened days; Acts applies it to the apostolic vacancy. The quoted office language does not itself describe Matthias' later selection procedure.
    Psalms 108:8 Let his days become few, and may another take his oversight.

Acts 1:21
Therefore, of the men who accompanied us throughout the time when the Lord Jesus went in and out among us,
  Mark 3:14–3:15 — thematic parallel: Acts seeks a replacement from those who accompanied Jesus; Mark describes the initial Twelve being appointed to be with Him and be sent. The shared companionship-and-ministry pattern does not say the two candidates were in Mark's original Twelve.
    Mark 3:14 He appointed twelve, whom He also named apostles, to be with Him and to be sent out by Him to proclaim
    Mark 3:15 and to have authority to cast out the demons.

Acts 1:22
beginning with John’s baptism until the day when He was taken up from us, one of these must become with us a witness of His resurrection.”
  Luke 24:46–24:48 — thematic parallel: Both assign witness concerning Jesus' resurrection and mission to His followers. Acts defines the replacement candidate's accompanying period from John's baptism through ascension; Luke sets witness after the suffering-and-resurrection declaration. The candidate criteria are not made a universal rule for every later ministry.
    Luke 24:46 and said to them, “Thus it is written that the Christ should suffer and rise from the dead on the third day,
    Luke 24:47 and that repentance for the forgiveness of sins should be proclaimed in His name to all the nations—beginning from Jerusalem.
    Luke 24:48 You are witnesses of these things.

Acts 1:23
They put forward two: Joseph called Barsabbas, who was also named Justus, and Matthias.
  Acts 1:26 — internal candidate and selection result: Matthias appears among the two proposed candidates and is later the one upon whom the lot falls, counted with the eleven apostles. The named candidate-to-result connection does not identify Joseph Barsabbas with another Justus or supply a later biography for either candidate.
    Acts 1:26 They cast lots for them, and the lot fell on Matthias, and he was counted with the eleven apostles.

Acts 1:24
They prayed and said, “Thou, Lord, who knowest everyone’s heart, show which one of these two Thou hast chosen
  Acts 15:8–15:9 — thematic parallel: Both appeal to divine knowledge of hearts in a community decision. Acts 1 asks the Lord to show the chosen candidate; Acts 15 describes God knowing hearts and giving the Spirit to Gentiles without distinction. The different decisions and divine titles remain as written.
    Acts 15:8 God, who knows the heart, bore witness to them by giving them the Holy Spirit just as He did to us.
    Acts 15:9 He made no distinction between us and them, cleansing their hearts by faith.

Acts 1:25
to take the place in this ministry and apostleship from which Judas turned aside to go to his own place.”
  John 17:12 — thematic parallel: Acts describes Judas leaving his apostolic ministry; Jesus' prayer describes the loss exception as the son of destruction and relates it to Scripture. This is a contextual comparison concerning the betrayer, not a supplied geographical definition of 'his own place.'
    John 17:12 When I was with them, I kept them in Thy name, which Thou hast given Me, and guarded them; and not one of them perished except the son of destruction, so that the Scripture might be fulfilled.

Acts 1:26
They cast lots for them, and the lot fell on Matthias, and he was counted with the eleven apostles.
  Joshua 18:10 — thematic parallel: Both use lots in a religiously framed decision. Acts reports Matthias selected after prayer for the Lord's choice; Joshua casts lots before the Lord for land division. The procedure's different purpose is retained rather than making land allocation an apostolic succession rule.
    Joshua 18:10 Joshua cast a lot for them at Shiloh before the Lord.

Acts 2:1
When the day of Pentecost arrived, they were all together in the same place.
  Leviticus 23:15–23:16 — thematic parallel: Leviticus counts seven weeks and fifty days before the new grain offering. This supplies festival-calendar background for Pentecost in Acts; it does not narrate the Spirit's arrival or determine the number of people gathered in the house.
    Leviticus 23:15 And you shall count from the day after the Sabbath, from the day you bring the sheaf of the presentation, seven complete weeks.
    Leviticus 23:16 Until the day after the last week you shall count fifty days, and then present a new sacrifice unto the Lord.

Acts 2:2
Suddenly a sound came from heaven like a violent wind rushing along, and it filled the whole house where they were sitting.
  John 3:8 — thematic parallel: Both join wind or its sound with Spirit language. Acts describes a sound like a rushing violent wind; Jesus' saying uses the wind's sound and movement to describe those born of the Spirit. The images are compared without turning Acts' likeness into a stated physical storm.
    John 3:8 The wind blows where it wills, and you hear its sound, but you do not know where it comes from or where it goes; so it is with everyone born of the Spirit.”

Acts 2:3
Tongues like fire appeared to them, separating and settling on each one of them.
  Luke 3:16 — thematic parallel: Luke joins Holy Spirit and fire in John's baptism announcement; Acts describes distributed tongues like fire at the Spirit's arrival. This is a qualified image comparison, not an assertion that Acts exhausts every meaning of fire in John's announcement.
    Luke 3:16 John answered them all, “I baptize you with water, but One stronger than I is coming, whose sandal strap I am not worthy to untie. He will baptize you in the Holy Spirit and fire.

Acts 2:4
They were all filled with the Holy Spirit and began to speak in other tongues, as the Spirit gave them utterance.
  Acts 1:5 — promise and fulfillment: The Spirit's arrival realizes the core of Jesus' near-term promise. Acts 2 describes filling and Spirit-given speech in other tongues; Acts 1 uses baptism language and does not specify the languages. The different descriptions are retained.
    Acts 1:5 for John baptized with water, but you will be baptized in the Holy Spirit not many days from now.”

Acts 2:5
Now there were Jews living in Jerusalem, devout men from every nation under heaven.
  Deuteronomy 16:16 — thematic parallel: Deuteronomy requires festival appearance before the Lord, including the Feast of Weeks. Acts describes devout Jews from many nations living in Jerusalem. The festival law is background, not proof that every person described was only a temporary pilgrim.
    Deuteronomy 16:16 Three times in the year shall every male among you appear before the Lord your God, in the place which the Lord shall choose: in the Feast of Unleavened Bread, and in the Feast of Weeks, and in the Feast of the Pitching of Tents. You shall not appear before the Lord your God empty,

Acts 2:6
When this sound occurred, the crowd came together and was bewildered, because each one heard them speaking in his own language.
  Genesis 11:7–11:9 — thematic parallel: Genesis describes language confusion that prevents mutual understanding and scatters people; Acts describes bewilderment because each hears his own language. This is a contrasting language-and-gathering comparison, not an assertion that Pentecost made every language one language again.
    Genesis 11:7 “Come, let Us go down and confound their language there, so that they may no longer understand one another’s speech.”
    Genesis 11:8 And the Lord scattered them from there over the face of the whole earth, and they ceased building the city and the tower.
    Genesis 11:9 Therefore its name was called Confusion, because there the Lord confounded the languages of the whole earth, and from there the Lord God scattered them over the face of the whole earth.

Acts 2:7
They were astonished and amazed, saying, “Look, are not all these people who are speaking Galileans?
  Luke 22:59 — thematic parallel: Acts' hearers remark that the speakers are Galileans; an accuser in Luke identifies Peter as a Galilean during the denial. These are different reported reactions. Neither supplies a complete account of the languages normally spoken by all Galileans.
    Luke 22:59 After about one hour had passed, someone else insisted, saying, “Truly, this man also was with Him, for he too is a Galilean.”

Acts 2:8
How is it, then, that each of us hears in our own native language?
  Acts 2:4–2:6 — thematic parallel: The native-language question corresponds to the narrator's earlier description of Spirit-given speech and each hearer's own-language hearing. This links the crowd's perplexity with the narrated phenomenon; no separately named interpreter or second language miracle is added.
    Acts 2:4 They were all filled with the Holy Spirit and began to speak in other tongues, as the Spirit gave them utterance.
    Acts 2:5 Now there were Jews living in Jerusalem, devout men from every nation under heaven.
    Acts 2:6 When this sound occurred, the crowd came together and was bewildered, because each one heard them speaking in his own language.

Acts 2:11
both Jews and converts; Cretans and Arabs—we hear them speaking in our own tongues about the mighty deeds of God.”
  Psalms 95:3 — thematic parallel: The hearers say they hear God's mighty deeds in their languages; the Psalm calls for His wonders to be declared among all peoples. The content and international scope correspond, without claiming the Psalm names these particular languages or predicts Pentecost explicitly.
    Psalms 95:3 Announce His glory among the nations, His wonders among all the peoples.

Acts 2:12
They were all astonished and perplexed, saying to one another, “What does this mean?”
  Acts 2:16–2:18 — thematic parallel: The crowd asks what the phenomenon means; Peter subsequently identifies it through Joel's Spirit-outpouring prophecy. This is the question-and-explanation development within one account, not an independent second witness or a claim that the whole crowd accepted the explanation.
    Acts 2:16 Rather, this is what was spoken through the prophet Joel:
    Acts 2:17 ‘In the last days, God says, I will pour out of My Spirit upon all flesh. Your sons and your daughters will prophesy; your young men will see visions, and your elders will dream dreams.
    Acts 2:18 Even upon My male slaves and My female slaves in those days I will pour out of My Spirit, and they will prophesy.

Acts 2:13
But others mocked them and said, “They are full of sweet wine.”
  1 Corinthians 14:23 — thematic parallel: Both describe an adverse outsider reaction to tongues. Acts reports a charge of sweet-wine drunkenness; Paul describes outsiders potentially calling an assembled tongue-speaking church mad. The reactions and settings differ, and neither accusation is endorsed.
    1 Corinthians 14:23 Therefore, if the whole Church gathers in the same place and everyone speaks in tongues, and uninitiated people or unbelievers enter, will they not say that you are mad?

Acts 2:14
Peter stood with the eleven, raised his voice, and addressed them: “Men of Judea and all who live in Jerusalem, let this be known to you, and listen closely to my words.
  Acts 1:26 — thematic parallel: Peter stands with the eleven after Matthias has been counted with the eleven apostles. The preceding replacement supplies the membership context for the public apostolic group. This is an internal narrative connection, not another list of individually named participants.
    Acts 1:26 They cast lots for them, and the lot fell on Matthias, and he was counted with the eleven apostles.

Acts 2:15
These people are not drunk, as you suppose, for it is the third hour of the day.
  Ephesians 5:18 — thematic parallel: Peter denies drunkenness before explaining the Spirit's work; Ephesians contrasts drunkenness with being filled with the Spirit. Acts also gives the third-hour observation. The negative statement is preserved rather than treating drunkenness as the prescribed spiritual condition.
    Ephesians 5:18 And do not become drunk with wine, in which there is reckless excess, but be filled in the Spirit,

Acts 2:16
Rather, this is what was spoken through the prophet Joel:
  Joel 3:1–3:5 — quotation: Peter explicitly identifies Joel as the prophetic source of the following citation. The saved Joel division places this passage in chapter3. Acts applies its outpouring language here without separately narrating every cosmic sign in the quoted passage as already completed.
    Joel 3:1 “And it shall be after these things, that I will pour out of My Spirit upon all flesh; and your sons and your daughters shall prophesy, your elders shall dream dreams, and your young men shall see visions.
    Joel 3:2 Even upon male slaves and female slaves in those days I will pour out of My Spirit.
    Joel 3:3 I will give wonders in heaven and upon the earth: blood, fire and a vapor of smoke.
    Joel 3:4 The sun will turn to darkness and the moon to blood before the coming of the Lord’s great and manifest day.
    Joel 3:5 Everyone who calls upon the Lord’s name will be saved, for on Mount Zion and in Jerusalem there will be one who escapes, as the Lord said, and bearers of good news whom the Lord has called.”

Acts 2:17
‘In the last days, God says, I will pour out of My Spirit upon all flesh. Your sons and your daughters will prophesy; your young men will see visions, and your elders will dream dreams.
  Joel 3:1 — quotation: Both describe God's Spirit poured upon all flesh and sons, daughters, elders, and young men receiving prophetic revelations. Acts introduces last-days wording and places young men before elders; Joel's temporal introduction and order are retained as written.
    Joel 3:1 “And it shall be after these things, that I will pour out of My Spirit upon all flesh; and your sons and your daughters shall prophesy, your elders shall dream dreams, and your young men shall see visions.

Acts 2:18
Even upon My male slaves and My female slaves in those days I will pour out of My Spirit, and they will prophesy.
  Joel 3:2 — quotation: Both extend the Spirit's outpouring to male and female slaves. Acts explicitly adds that they will prophesy. The servant status and both sexes are retained without replacing the original terms with a different social category.
    Joel 3:2 Even upon male slaves and female slaves in those days I will pour out of My Spirit.

Acts 2:19
I will give wonders in heaven above and signs on the earth below: blood and fire and a cloud of smoke.
  Joel 3:3 — quotation: Both announce heavenly and earthly wonders with blood, fire, and smoke imagery. Acts adds above/below distinctions and the word signs in its presentation. The quotation comparison does not claim these physical signs are all narrated as occurring in the house.
    Joel 3:3 I will give wonders in heaven and upon the earth: blood, fire and a vapor of smoke.

Acts 2:20
The sun will be changed into darkness and the moon into blood before the great and manifest day of the Lord comes.
  Joel 3:4 — quotation: Both describe the sun's darkening and the moon becoming blood before the Lord's great day. The comparison follows the saved Greek-based division rather than substituting another edition's chapter numbering or supplying a modern date.
    Joel 3:4 The sun will turn to darkness and the moon to blood before the coming of the Lord’s great and manifest day.

Acts 2:21
And everyone who calls upon the name of the Lord will be saved.’
  Joel 3:5 — quotation: Acts quotes the promise that everyone invoking the Lord's name will be saved. Joel continues with Zion, Jerusalem, and the Lord's calling. Acts' quoted clause is not treated as if it reproduced the entirety of Joel's verse.
    Joel 3:5 Everyone who calls upon the Lord’s name will be saved, for on Mount Zion and in Jerusalem there will be one who escapes, as the Lord said, and bearers of good news whom the Lord has called.”

Acts 2:22
Men of Israel, hear these words: Jesus the Nazarene was a man attested to you by God through mighty works, wonders, and signs that God did through Him in your midst, as you yourselves know.
  John 5:36 — thematic parallel: Peter describes God's works through Jesus as attestation; Jesus in John describes the Father's given works as testimony that He was sent. The speakers and settings differ, while the works-and-divine-witness relation is shared.
    John 5:36 But I have testimony greater than John’s: for the works the Father has given Me to complete, the very works I do, bear witness concerning Me that the Father has sent Me.

Acts 2:23
This man, handed over according to God’s determined purpose and foreknowledge, you nailed up and killed through the hands of lawless men.
  Luke 22:22 — thematic parallel: Both hold divine purpose and human responsibility together. Acts names God's determined purpose and foreknowledge alongside its addressed hearers' killing through lawless hands; Luke says the Son of Man goes as determined and pronounces woe on the betrayer. The particular agents differ.
    Luke 22:22 For the Son of Man indeed goes according to what has been determined, but woe to that man through whom He is handed over!”

Acts 2:24
God raised Him, releasing Him from the pangs of death, because it was impossible for Him to be held by it.
  Romans 6:9 — thematic parallel: Both connect Jesus' resurrection with death's inability to retain dominion. Acts speaks of God releasing Jesus from death's pangs, with death unable to hold Him; Romans says the risen Christ no longer dies and death no longer rules Him. Their explanatory images differ.
    Romans 6:9 knowing that Christ, having been raised from the dead, no longer dies; death no longer rules over Him.

Acts 2:25
For David says concerning Him, ‘I kept the Lord before me always, for He is at my right hand so that I will not be shaken.
  Psalms 15:8 — quotation: Peter introduces David's words about keeping the Lord before him and being unshaken with the Lord at his right hand. The Psalm supplies that first-person statement; Peter's Christ-directed application is the speech's interpretation.
    Psalms 15:8 I kept seeing the Lord before me continually, because He is at my right hand, so that I may not be shaken.

Acts 2:26
Therefore my heart rejoiced and my tongue exulted; moreover, my flesh will dwell in hope,
  Psalms 15:9 — quotation: Both describe the rejoicing heart and tongue and flesh dwelling in hope. The Psalm's bodily hope language is retained; Peter's surrounding argument interprets it in relation to Jesus' resurrection rather than making David's body undying in its tomb.
    Psalms 15:9 Therefore my heart rejoiced, and my tongue exulted; indeed, even my flesh will dwell in hope,

Acts 2:27
because Thou wilt not abandon my soul to Hades, nor allow Thy Holy One to see decay.
  Psalms 15:10 — quotation: The citation joins the soul not being abandoned to Hades with the Holy One not seeing decay. The Psalm supplies the wording, while Peter's following argument identifies its resurrection application. Hades is not silently replaced with a different afterlife term.
    Psalms 15:10 for Thou wilt not abandon my soul to Hades, nor let Thy holy one see corruption.

Acts 2:28
Thou hast made known to me the paths of life; Thou wilt fill me with gladness in Thy presence.’
  Psalms 15:11 — quotation: Both speak of made-known paths of life and joy in God's presence. The Psalm continues beyond the portion quoted in Acts. The comparison preserves the quotation's actual extent rather than adding every remaining Psalm phrase to Peter's words.
    Psalms 15:11 Thou madest known to me ways of life; Thou wilt fill me with gladness in Thy presence; pleasures are at Thy right hand for ever.

Acts 2:29
Men, brothers, I may speak openly to you about the patriarch David: he both died and was buried, and his tomb is among us to this day.
  1 Kings 2:10 — thematic parallel: Kings narrates David's death and burial in the city of David, the ancestral fact Peter invokes. Acts additionally asserts the tomb's continuing presence among its hearers. The older narrative does not identify a modern tomb location.
    1 Kings 2:10 David lay down with his fathers and was buried in the City of David.

Acts 2:30
Since he was a prophet and knew that God had sworn an oath to him to seat one from the fruit of his loins upon his throne,
  Psalms 131:11 — allusion: Peter recalls God's oath to David concerning a descendant upon his throne. The Psalm supplies the sworn throne-and-offspring promise. The offspring expressions in the two passages are compared as written rather than silently making their complete sentences identical.
    Psalms 131:11 The Lord swore truth to David, and He will certainly not annul it: “From the fruit of your belly I will set one on your throne.

Acts 2:31
he foresaw and spoke about the resurrection of the Christ: He was neither abandoned to Hades, nor did His flesh see decay.
  Psalms 15:10 — promise and fulfillment: Peter explicitly interprets the Hades-and-decay saying as foreseen speech about the Christ's resurrection. The Psalm itself does not name Jesus, and Acts' explanatory flesh-language is distinguished from the Psalm's designation of the Holy One.
    Psalms 15:10 for Thou wilt not abandon my soul to Hades, nor let Thy holy one see corruption.

Acts 2:32
God raised this Jesus, and all of us are witnesses of it.
  Luke 24:46–24:48 — promise and fulfillment: Jesus' resurrection-and-witness instruction in Luke is followed by Peter's declaration that God raised Jesus and the group are witnesses. This links commission with subsequent testimony within the literary sequence rather than claiming the accounts are independent historical attestations.
    Luke 24:46 and said to them, “Thus it is written that the Christ should suffer and rise from the dead on the third day,
    Luke 24:47 and that repentance for the forgiveness of sins should be proclaimed in His name to all the nations—beginning from Jerusalem.
    Luke 24:48 You are witnesses of these things.

Acts 2:33
Therefore, exalted to God’s right hand and having received from the Father the promised Holy Spirit, He has poured out what you both see and hear.
  John 7:39 — promise and fulfillment: John anticipates believers receiving the Spirit in relation to Jesus' glorification; Acts describes the exalted Jesus receiving the Father's promised Spirit and pouring out what is seen and heard. The giver/receiver roles and John's exact 'Spirit was not yet' wording remain distinct.
    John 7:39 He said this concerning the Spirit, whom those who believed in Him were about to receive; for the Spirit was not yet, because Jesus had not yet been glorified.

Acts 2:34
For David did not ascend into the heavens, but he himself says, ‘The Lord said to my Lord, “Sit at My right hand
  Psalms 109:1 — quotation: Peter introduces the Lord's address to 'my Lord' and the right-hand command. The Psalm contains the whole instruction, which Acts divides across two source units. Peter's statement about David not ascending is part of his interpretation, not a verbatim line in this Psalm.
    Psalms 109:1 Of David. A psalm. The Lord said to my lord: Sit at My right hand until I make your enemies a footstool for your feet.

Acts 2:35
until I make Thy enemies a footstool for Thy feet.”’
  Psalms 109:1 — quotation: This continues the cited command with enemies made a footstool. The same Psalm verse also contains the preceding right-hand clause. Preserving the whole target verse keeps the divided Acts quotation in its original scriptural context.
    Psalms 109:1 Of David. A psalm. The Lord said to my lord: Sit at My right hand until I make your enemies a footstool for your feet.

Acts 2:36
Therefore let the whole house of Israel know with certainty that God has made Him both Lord and Christ—this Jesus whom you crucified.”
  Philippians 2:9–2:11 — thematic parallel: Both associate divine exaltation with Jesus' lordship. Acts calls the crucified Jesus Lord and Christ; Philippians describes God's exaltation and confession of Jesus Christ as Lord to the Father's glory. The made-Lord and exaltation formulations remain distinct.
    Philippians 2:9 Therefore God also highly exalted Him and graciously gave Him the name above every name,
    Philippians 2:10 so that at the name of Jesus every knee should bend, of those in heaven and on earth and under the earth,
    Philippians 2:11 and every tongue confess that Jesus Christ is Lord, to the glory of God the Father.

Acts 2:37
When they heard this, they were pierced to the heart and said to Peter and the other apostles, “Men, brothers, what should we do?”
  Luke 3:10–3:11 — thematic parallel: Both crowds ask what they should do in response to proclamation. Acts describes pierced hearts and Peter's subsequent repentance-and-baptism answer; Luke has John's instruction about sharing clothing and food. The same question is not given an identical answer in both scenes.
    Luke 3:10 The crowds asked him, “What then should we do?”
    Luke 3:11 He answered them, “Let the one who has two tunics share with the one who has none, and let the one who has food do likewise.”

Acts 2:38
Peter said to them, “Repent, and let each one of you be baptized upon the name of Jesus Christ for the forgiveness of your sins, and you will receive the gift of the Holy Spirit.
  Luke 24:47–24:49 — promise and fulfillment: Peter's call to repentance and forgiveness in Jesus Christ's name develops Jesus' Jerusalem-starting proclamation commission and promised power. Acts additionally names baptism and the Spirit's gift. Luke's commission is not rewritten as the exact baptism formula spoken here.
    Luke 24:47 and that repentance for the forgiveness of sins should be proclaimed in His name to all the nations—beginning from Jerusalem.
    Luke 24:48 You are witnesses of these things.
    Luke 24:49 And behold, I am sending out the promise of My Father upon you; but remain in the city until you are clothed with power from on high.”

Acts 2:39
For the promise is for you, for your children, and for all who are far away—as many as the Lord our God calls to Himself.”
  Joel 3:5 — allusion: Peter extends the promise to hearers, their children, and those far away whom God calls; Joel's ending names bearers of good news whom the Lord called. The calling language corresponds, while the descriptions of recipients and messengers are not declared identical.
    Joel 3:5 Everyone who calls upon the Lord’s name will be saved, for on Mount Zion and in Jerusalem there will be one who escapes, as the Lord said, and bearers of good news whom the Lord has called.”

Acts 2:40
With many other words he bore solemn witness and urged them, saying, “Be saved from this crooked generation.”
  Deuteronomy 32:5 — allusion: Peter's crooked-generation warning recalls the crooked-generation language of Moses' song. Acts calls the present hearers to be saved; Deuteronomy censures unfaithful conduct. This is a moral and verbal comparison, not an inherited judgment upon every later descendant.
    Deuteronomy 32:5 Blemished children, not His, have sinned—a crooked and perverse generation.

Acts 2:41
So those who welcomed his word were baptized, and about three thousand persons were added that day.
  Acts 4:4 — thematic parallel: Both describe growth following reception of the word. Acts 2 reports about three thousand persons baptized and added that day; Acts 4 gives a total of about five thousand men. The figures have different descriptions and are not treated as two identical new additions.
    Acts 4:4 But many of those who heard the word believed, and the number of the men came to about five thousand.

Acts 2:42
They devoted themselves to the apostles’ teaching and to fellowship, to the breaking of bread and to the prayers.
  Acts 20:7 — thematic parallel: Both community gatherings combine instruction with breaking bread. Acts 2 also lists fellowship and prayers; Acts 20 describes a first-day gathering and Paul's extended speech. The comparison does not decide every meal's ritual status beyond the supplied wording.
    Acts 20:7 On the first day of the week, when we had gathered to break bread, Paul spoke with them. He intended to leave the next day and prolonged his message until midnight.

Acts 2:43
Awe came upon everyone, and many wonders and signs took place through the apostles.
  Acts 5:12 — thematic parallel: Both summarize many signs and wonders through the apostles. Acts 2 adds general awe; Acts 5 specifies the apostles' hands and the united gathering at Solomon's Portico. These are internal recurring summaries, not independent records of each individual sign.
    Acts 5:12 Many signs and wonders were taking place among the people through the apostles’ hands. They were all together with one accord in Solomon’s Portico.

Acts 2:44
All who believed were together and held everything in common.
  Acts 4:32 — thematic parallel: Both describe believers sharing possessions in common. Acts 4 expands the description with one heart and soul and no private ownership claim within the sharing practice. This is the narrative's expanded community description, not independent attestation or an added legal ownership statute.
    Acts 4:32 The multitude of those who believed was of one heart and soul. Not one of them claimed that anything he possessed was his own, but they held everything in common.

Acts 2:45
They would sell their properties and possessions and distribute the proceeds to all, as anyone had need.
  Luke 12:33 — thematic parallel: Jesus tells His followers to sell possessions and give alms; Acts describes repeated sales and distribution according to need. Luke adds heavenly-treasure imagery, while Acts describes the community's practice. The command and the later descriptive action retain their different forms.
    Luke 12:33 Sell your possessions and give alms. Make for yourselves purses that do not wear out, an unfailing treasure in the heavens, where no thief draws near and no moth destroys.

Acts 2:46
Day by day they devoted themselves with one accord to gathering in the temple, and breaking bread in their homes, they shared food with gladness and simplicity of heart,
  Acts 5:42 — thematic parallel: Both describe daily activity in the Temple and in homes. Acts 2 emphasizes shared food, gladness, and simplicity of heart; Acts 5 emphasizes teaching and proclaiming Jesus as Christ. The shared locations do not make their complete activity lists identical.
    Acts 5:42 Every day, in the temple and in their homes, they never ceased teaching and proclaiming the good news of Jesus the Christ.

Acts 2:47
praising God and enjoying favor with all the people. And day by day the Lord added those who were being saved to their gathering.
  Acts 5:13–5:14 — thematic parallel: Both join public regard for the believers with growth. Acts 2 includes praise and daily additions by the Lord; Acts 5 includes high esteem, reluctance to join, and added men and women. Its stated reluctance is not erased by treating public approval as universal membership.
    Acts 5:13 None of the rest dared to join them, but the people held them in high esteem.
    Acts 5:14 More and more believers were added to the Lord, multitudes of both men and women,

Acts 3:1
Peter and John were going up to the temple at the ninth hour, the hour of prayer.
  Acts 10:30 — thematic parallel: Peter and John go to Temple prayer at the ninth hour; Cornelius later describes praying at home at the ninth hour. The shared prayer hour does not identify the places, participants, or subsequent events as the same.
    Acts 10:30 Cornelius said, “Four days ago, at this hour, I was praying in my house at the ninth hour, and look—a man stood before me in shining clothing.

Acts 3:2
A man unable to walk from his mother’s womb was being carried there. They used to place him each day at the temple gate called Beautiful, to ask for alms from those entering the temple.
  Acts 14:8 — narrative parallel: Both describe a man unable to walk from his mother's womb. Acts 14 explicitly adds never having walked and locates the man at Lystra; Acts 3 places its man at the Beautiful Gate for alms. These are distinct congenital-disability healings.
    Acts 14:8 In Lystra a man was sitting who had no strength in his feet, unable to walk from his mother’s womb; he had never walked.

Acts 3:3
When he saw Peter and John about to enter the temple, he asked to receive alms.
  Acts 3:6–3:8 — internal alms request and different response: The request is for alms; Peter's response states his lack of silver and gold and gives a command in Jesus' name, followed by bodily strengthening and walking. The narrative answers this particular request with healing, without transferring the episode to another beggar or claiming a monetary payment.
    Acts 3:6 But Peter said, “Silver and gold I do not have, but what I have I give you: in the name of Jesus Christ the Nazarene, get up and walk.”
    Acts 3:7 Taking him by the right hand, he raised him up. At once his feet and ankles were strengthened.
    Acts 3:8 Leaping up, he stood and began walking. He entered the temple with them, walking and leaping and praising God.

Acts 3:4
Peter, together with John, looked intently at him and said, “Look at us.”
  Acts 14:9 — narrative parallel: Both apostolic healing scenes describe an intent look at the disabled man. Acts 3 also records the command to look at Peter and John; Acts 14 says Paul perceived faith for healing. Paul's explicit perception is not silently assigned to the earlier scene.
    Acts 14:9 He listened to Paul speaking. Paul looked intently at him and saw that he had faith to be made well,

Acts 3:5
He paid attention to them, expecting to receive something from them.
  Acts 3:6–3:8 — internal expectation and narrated response: The man expects to receive something; Peter's reply distinguishes silver and gold from what he gives in Jesus' name, and the following actions describe healing. Something remains the source's unspecified expectation, not an explicit statement of prior healing faith or of disappointment.
    Acts 3:6 But Peter said, “Silver and gold I do not have, but what I have I give you: in the name of Jesus Christ the Nazarene, get up and walk.”
    Acts 3:7 Taking him by the right hand, he raised him up. At once his feet and ankles were strengthened.
    Acts 3:8 Leaping up, he stood and began walking. He entered the temple with them, walking and leaping and praising God.

Acts 3:6
But Peter said, “Silver and gold I do not have, but what I have I give you: in the name of Jesus Christ the Nazarene, get up and walk.”
  Luke 5:24–5:25 — narrative parallel: Peter commands 'get up and walk' in Jesus Christ's name; Luke has Jesus tell the paralyzed man to rise, take his bed, and go home. The commands and recovery correspond, while the actors, conditions, and bed detail differ.
    Luke 5:24 But so that you may know that the Son of Man has authority on earth to forgive sins”—He said to the paralyzed man—“I tell you, rise, take up your little bed, and go home.”
    Luke 5:25 Immediately he rose before them, took up what he had been lying on, and went home glorifying God.

Acts 3:7
Taking him by the right hand, he raised him up. At once his feet and ankles were strengthened.
  Mark 1:31 — narrative parallel: Both healing scenes include taking the person's hand and raising the person. Peter takes a right hand and the man's feet and ankles become strong; Jesus raises the fevered woman and her fever leaves. The people and illnesses are different.
    Mark 1:31 He came to her, took her by the hand, and raised her up. The fever left her, and she served them.

Acts 3:8
Leaping up, he stood and began walking. He entered the temple with them, walking and leaping and praising God.
  Isaiah 35:6 — allusion: The healed man's leaping can be compared with Isaiah's promise of a lame person leaping like a deer. Acts adds walking into the Temple and praising God. This is a possible restoration-image echo, not a direct quotation or an assertion that every other Isaiah image occurs here.
    Isaiah 35:6 Then the lame person will leap like a deer, and the tongue of those with difficulty speaking will be clear, for water burst out in the wilderness and a ravine in a thirsty land,

Acts 3:9
All the people saw him walking and praising God.
  Luke 18:43 — narrative parallel: Both describe public observation of a healed person praising God. Luke's formerly blind man follows Jesus and the onlookers also praise God; Acts' man walks and praises while the people see him. Their disabilities and the precise observers' responses differ.
    Luke 18:43 And at once he received his sight and followed Him, glorifying God. And all the people, seeing this, gave praise to God.

Acts 3:10
They recognized him as the one who used to sit for alms at the temple’s Beautiful Gate, and they were filled with wonder and astonishment at what had happened to him.
  John 9:8–9:9 — narrative parallel: Both connect public response with recognizing a formerly disabled beggar. Acts' people recognize the Temple beggar and marvel; John's neighbors debate the formerly blind man's identity. These are different men and different degrees of agreement.
    John 9:8 Then the neighbors and those who had seen him before as a beggar said, “Is this not the one who sat and begged?”
    John 9:9 Some said, “This is he.” Others said, “No, but he is like him.” He said, “I am he.”

Acts 3:11
As he held on to Peter and John, all the people ran together to them at the portico called Solomon’s, utterly astonished.
  John 10:23 — narrative parallel: Both name Solomon's portico in the Temple setting. Acts gathers Peter, John, the healed man, and the astonished people there; John describes Jesus walking there at Dedication. The location comparison does not merge the occasions.
    John 10:23 and Jesus was walking in the temple, in Solomon’s Portico.

Acts 3:12
When Peter saw this, he addressed the people: “Men of Israel, why do you marvel at this, or why do you stare at us as though we had made him walk by our own power or godliness?
  Acts 14:14–14:15 — narrative parallel: Both apostolic healing accounts redirect a crowd away from crediting the human messengers. Peter denies that personal power or godliness made the man walk; Paul and Barnabas reject the Lystra crowd's response and point to the living Creator. Their particular misconceptions differ.
    Acts 14:14 When the apostles Barnabas and Paul heard this, they tore their garments and rushed out into the crowd, crying out
    Acts 14:15 and saying, “Men, why are you doing these things? We too are human beings, of the same nature as you. We proclaim good news to you: turn from these worthless things to the living God, who made heaven and earth and the sea and everything in them.

Acts 3:13
The God of Abraham and the God of Isaac and the God of Jacob, the God of our fathers, has glorified His Servant Jesus, whom you handed over and denied before Pilate, when he had decided to release Him.
  Exodus 3:6 — quotation: Peter uses the ancestral God formula naming Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob. Exodus records God's self-identification to Moses with those patriarchs. Acts then speaks of Jesus' glorification; that further application is not a verbatim part of Exodus' bush statement.
    Exodus 3:6 And He said to him, “I am the God of your father, the God of Abraham, and the God of Isaac, and the God of Jacob.” And Moses turned his face away, for he feared to look before God.
  Luke 23:20–23:25 — narrative parallel: Peter recalls handing over and denying Jesus before a Pilate who had decided on release. Luke narrates Pilate wanting release, the crowd's insistence, and his yielding to their demand. The speech and narrative correspond without adding another release attempt or shifting every agent into one undifferentiated group.
    Luke 23:20 Pilate addressed them again, wanting to release Jesus.
    Luke 23:21 But they kept shouting, saying, “Crucify, crucify Him!”
    Luke 23:22 And for the third time he said to them, “Why, what evil has this man done? I found no ground for death in Him. Therefore I will punish Him and release Him.”
    Luke 23:23 But they pressed him with loud voices, demanding that He be crucified, and their voices prevailed.
    Luke 23:24 And Pilate decided that their demand should be granted.
    Luke 23:25 He released the man who had been thrown into prison for uprising and murder, whom they were demanding; but he handed Jesus over to their will.

Acts 3:14
But you denied the Holy and Righteous One and asked for a murderer to be granted to you.
  Luke 23:18–23:19 — narrative parallel: Acts recalls asking for a murderer instead of the Holy and Righteous One. Luke records the demand for Barabbas and explains his imprisonment for insurrection and murder. Acts supplies its own titles for Jesus rather than quoting those titles from these Luke verses.
    Luke 23:18 But they cried out all together, saying, “Take this man away, and release Barabbas to us!”
    Luke 23:19 He had been thrown into prison for a certain uprising that took place in the city and for murder.

Acts 3:15
You killed the Author of life, whom God raised from the dead; we are witnesses of this.
  John 11:25–11:26 — thematic parallel: Peter names Jesus the Author of life and testifies to God raising Him. John records Jesus' self-description as Resurrection and Life and His promise to believers. The related life-and-resurrection claims have different speakers and immediate settings.
    John 11:25 Jesus said to her, “I am the resurrection and the life; the one who believes in Me will live, even if he dies,
    John 11:26 and everyone who lives and believes in Me will certainly never die. Do you believe this?”

Acts 3:16
Through faith in His name, His name has strengthened this man whom you see and know; and the faith that is through Him has given him this complete health before all of you.
  Mark 5:34 — thematic parallel: Both connect restored health with faith related to Jesus. Mark explicitly names the woman's faith; Acts describes faith in Jesus' name and faith through Him without naming a particular human possessor of that faith. The patient, condition, and stated form of faith differ.
    Mark 5:34 And He said to her, “Daughter, your faith has saved you; go in peace, and be well, free from your affliction.”

Acts 3:17
And now, brothers, I know that you acted in ignorance, as did your rulers.
  Luke 23:34 — thematic parallel: Both speak of ignorance surrounding Jesus' death. Peter names his hearers and their rulers; Luke records Jesus praying forgiveness for 'them' because they do not know what they do. The pronoun's referents are not automatically made coextensive with every person in Peter's address.
    Luke 23:34 But Jesus said, “Father, forgive them, for they do not know what they are doing.” And dividing His garments, they cast lots.

Acts 3:18
But what God announced beforehand through the mouth of all the prophets—that His Christ would suffer—He has fulfilled in this way.
  Luke 24:25–24:27 — thematic parallel: Both relate the Christ's suffering to the prophets' scriptural message. Luke has Jesus explain suffering and glory through Moses and the prophets; Peter says God fulfilled what He announced about suffering. These are related interpretations in different settings, not independent historical attestations.
    Luke 24:25 And He said to them, “O foolish ones, and slow in heart to believe all that the prophets spoke!
    Luke 24:26 Was it not necessary for the Christ to suffer these things and enter into His glory?”
    Luke 24:27 And beginning from Moses and from all the prophets, He interpreted to them in all the Scriptures the things concerning Himself.

Acts 3:19
Repent, therefore, and turn back, so that your sins may be wiped away,
  Isaiah 43:25 — thematic parallel: Both use the wiping-away image for sins or lawlessness. Isaiah has God announce blotting out and not remembering; Peter calls for repentance and turning so sins may be wiped away. The command to the hearers is distinguished from God's described act.
    Isaiah 43:25 I am, I am the One who wipes out your lawless acts, and I will certainly not remember.

Acts 3:20
so that times of refreshing may come from the presence of the Lord, and He may send the Christ appointed for you, Jesus,
  Acts 1:11 — thematic parallel: Both anticipate the ascended Jesus' coming. Acts 1 records the messengers' return promise; Peter speaks of refreshing from the Lord and sending the appointed Christ. This develops a future expectation rather than dating its fulfillment.
    Acts 1:11 They said, “Men of Galilee, why do you stand looking into heaven? This Jesus, who was taken up from you into heaven, will come in the same way as you saw Him go into heaven.”

Acts 3:21
whom heaven must receive until the times of restoration of all things of which God spoke through the mouth of His holy prophets from long ago.
  Luke 1:69–1:70 — thematic parallel: Both describe God's speaking through His holy prophets from long ago. Luke places that formula with salvation in David's house; Acts places it with Christ's heavenly reception and restoration. The shared prophetic-history language does not equate the two complete announcements or settle every meaning of restoration.
    Luke 1:69 and has raised up a horn of salvation for us in the house of David His servant,
    Luke 1:70 as He spoke through the mouth of His holy prophets from long ago:

Acts 3:22
Moses said, ‘The Lord your God will raise up for you a prophet like me from among your brothers. You shall listen to him in everything he says to you.
  Deuteronomy 18:15 — quotation: Moses' promised prophet from the brothers, like Moses and to be heard, supplies the core citation. Acts also says to hear everything the prophet speaks. The prophet comparison is preserved without making Deuteronomy explicitly name Jesus.
    Deuteronomy 18:15 The Lord your God shall raise up unto you a prophet from among your brethren, like unto me; you shall hearken unto him,

Acts 3:23
Every person who does not listen to that prophet will be destroyed from among the people.’
  Deuteronomy 18:19 — quotation: Both warn against failing to hear the promised prophet's words. Deuteronomy says God will exact retribution from the unheeding person; Acts states destruction from among the people. The consequence is related but the entire English sentence is not presented as identical.
    Deuteronomy 18:19 And the man who will not hearken unto whatsoever the prophet shall speak in My name, I will exact retribution from him.

Acts 3:24
And all the prophets, from Samuel and those who followed—as many as spoke—also announced these days.
  1 Samuel 3:19–3:21 — thematic parallel: Samuel is established as the Lord's prophet and recipient of His word in the older narrative. Peter names Samuel at the beginning of his prophetic sequence. This supplies the named prophet's role, not a claim that these verses contain a particular oracle naming Pentecost or Jesus' resurrection.
    1 Samuel 3:19 Samuel grew, and the Lord was with him, and none of his words fell to the ground.
    1 Samuel 3:20 All Israel, from Dan to Beersheba, knew that Samuel was faithful as a prophet to the Lord.
    1 Samuel 3:21 The Lord continued to make Himself known at Shiloh, for the Lord revealed Himself to Samuel. Samuel was recognized as a prophet of the Lord for all Israel, from the ends of the land to the ends. Eli was very old, and his sons continued on their way, and their way was evil before the Lord.

Acts 3:25
You are sons of the prophets and of the covenant God made with your fathers, saying to Abraham, ‘In your seed all the families of the earth will be blessed.’
  Genesis 22:18 — quotation: Peter invokes the Abrahamic seed-and-blessing promise. Genesis speaks of all the earth's nations being blessed in Abraham's seed; Acts says all the families of the earth. The scope terms and Peter's covenant-heir application remain distinct.
    Genesis 22:18 And in your seed all the nations of the earth shall be blessed, because you have listened to My voice.”

Acts 3:26
God raised up His Servant and sent Him first to you, to bless you by turning each of you away from your wicked deeds.”
  Isaiah 59:20 — allusion: Both describe a saving mission through turning away wickedness among God's people. Isaiah names the Deliverer, Zion, and Jacob; Acts names God's Servant sent first to its hearers to bless them through turning. This is a possible prophetic-background comparison with different titles and wording.
    Isaiah 59:20 The deliverer will come for Zion’s sake and turn ungodliness away from Jacob.

Acts 4:1
While they were speaking to the people, the priests, the captain of the temple, and the Sadducees came upon them,
  Acts 5:17–5:18 — narrative parallel: Both describe the priestly leadership associated with Sadducees acting against the apostles. Acts 4 includes the Temple captain and the teaching confrontation; Acts 5 explicitly names jealousy and another arrest. The motives and individual incidents are not made identical.
    Acts 5:17 The high priest and all who were with him—the party of the Sadducees—rose up, filled with jealousy.
    Acts 5:18 They laid hands on the apostles and placed them in the public prison.

Acts 4:2
greatly troubled because they were teaching the people and proclaiming in Jesus the resurrection from the dead.
  Acts 23:6–23:8 — thematic parallel: Both connect a council conflict with resurrection teaching. Acts 4 concerns proclamation in Jesus; Acts 23 concerns Paul's resurrection hope and explicitly explains Sadducean denial. They are different hearings, not one undifferentiated confrontation.
    Acts 23:6 When Paul perceived that one part consisted of Sadducees and the other of Pharisees, he cried out in the council, “Men, brothers, I am a Pharisee, a son of Pharisees. I am being judged concerning the hope and resurrection of the dead.”
    Acts 23:7 As he said this, dissension arose between the Pharisees and Sadducees, and the multitude was divided.
    Acts 23:8 For the Sadducees say there is no resurrection, nor angel nor spirit, but the Pharisees acknowledge both.

Acts 4:3
They laid hands on them and placed them in custody until the next day, for it was already evening.
  Luke 21:12 — promise and fulfillment: Jesus forecasts followers being seized and imprisoned for His name; Acts narrates seizure and overnight custody of the apostles after their proclamation. This instance corresponds to the forecast without claiming its entire list of later hearings occurs here.
    Luke 21:12 But before all these things they will lay their hands on you and persecute you, handing you over to synagogues and prisons, and you will be led before kings and governors because of My name.

Acts 4:4
But many of those who heard the word believed, and the number of the men came to about five thousand.
  Acts 2:41 — thematic parallel: Both summarize growth after reception of the word. Acts 4 gives about five thousand men as the number reached; Acts 2 gives about three thousand persons added that day after baptism. Their different counting descriptions are preserved rather than adding both as equivalent new arrivals.
    Acts 2:41 So those who welcomed his word were baptized, and about three thousand persons were added that day.

Acts 4:5
The next day their rulers, elders, and scribes gathered in Jerusalem,
  Luke 22:66 — narrative parallel: Both portray elders and scribes gathering for a hearing after detention. Luke concerns Jesus before the council at daybreak; Acts concerns the apostles the following day in Jerusalem. The legal settings correspond without merging the people, charges, or outcomes.
    Luke 22:66 When day came, the council of elders of the people gathered, both chief priests and scribes, and they led Him into their council,

Acts 4:6
together with Annas the high priest, Caiaphas, John, Alexander, and all who belonged to the high-priestly family.
  Luke 3:2 — thematic parallel: Both name Annas and Caiaphas in a high-priestly setting. Luke places them in the Baptist's opening context; Acts places Annas with Caiaphas and a priestly family at the apostolic hearing. John and Alexander are not identified with unrelated same-name people.
    Luke 3:2 during the high priesthood of Annas and Caiaphas, God’s word came to John, Zechariah’s son, in the wilderness.

Acts 4:7
They made them stand in their midst and asked, “By what power or by what name did you do this?”
  Luke 20:1–20:2 — narrative parallel: Both involve religious authorities questioning the basis of a public religious work. Luke asks Jesus about authority and its giver during Temple teaching; Acts asks the apostles about power and name after healing. The question forms and respondents differ.
    Luke 20:1 And it happened on one of the days, as He was teaching the people in the temple and proclaiming good news, that the chief priests and the scribes came up with the elders
    Luke 20:2 and said to Him, “Tell us by what authority Thou doest these things, or who it is that gave Thee this authority.”

Acts 4:8
Then Peter, filled with the Holy Spirit, said to them, “Rulers of the people and elders,
  Luke 12:11–12:12 — promise and fulfillment: Jesus promises the Holy Spirit's instruction when followers answer authorities; Acts describes Peter filled with the Spirit before addressing rulers and elders. The narrated response corresponds to that promise without turning it into a claim about every later courtroom utterance.
    Luke 12:11 When they bring you before the synagogues and the rulers and authorities, do not be anxious about how or what you should say in your defense, or what you should say,
    Luke 12:12 for the Holy Spirit will teach you in that very hour what must be said.”

Acts 4:9
if we are being examined today concerning a good deed done to a disabled man, and by what means he has been made well,
  John 9:15–9:16 — narrative parallel: Both place a healing under religious investigation. Peter calls it a good deed to a disabled man; John's investigators question a formerly blind man and divide over Sabbath observance and signs. The patients, procedures, and immediate disputed issues differ.
    John 9:15 So the Pharisees also asked him again how he received his sight. He said to them, “He put mud on my eyes, and I washed, and I see.”
    John 9:16 Then some of the Pharisees said, “This man is not from God, because He does not keep the Sabbath.” But others said, “How can a sinful man do such signs?” And there was division among them.

Acts 4:10
let it be known to all of you and to all the people of Israel that in the name of Jesus Christ the Nazarene, whom you crucified, whom God raised from the dead—in Him this man stands before you in good health.
  Acts 3:6–3:8 — thematic parallel: Peter's answer names the Jesus Christ through whose name the earlier walking cure was commanded and performed. The prior narrative supplies that event; the answer additionally proclaims crucifixion and resurrection. It is a recital within one account, not an independent second cure.
    Acts 3:6 But Peter said, “Silver and gold I do not have, but what I have I give you: in the name of Jesus Christ the Nazarene, get up and walk.”
    Acts 3:7 Taking him by the right hand, he raised him up. At once his feet and ankles were strengthened.
    Acts 3:8 Leaping up, he stood and began walking. He entered the temple with them, walking and leaping and praising God.

Acts 4:11
He is the stone despised by you, the builders, which has become the cornerstone.
  Psalms 117:22 — quotation: Peter identifies Jesus with the stone rejected by builders and now the cornerstone. His speech directly addresses its hearers as builders and uses despising language; the Psalm supplies the rejected-stone image without naming this council. The application and wording are distinguished.
    Psalms 117:22 The stone the builders rejected— this has become the head of the corner.

Acts 4:12
Salvation is in no one else, for there is no other name under heaven given among human beings by which we must be saved.”
  John 14:6 — thematic parallel: Both make Jesus uniquely decisive for salvation or access to the Father. Acts speaks of no other saving name under heaven; John speaks of no one coming to the Father except through Jesus. These are related exclusive formulations, not identical sentences.
    John 14:6 Jesus says to him, “I am the way and the truth and the life. No one comes to the Father except through Me.

Acts 4:13
When they observed Peter’s and John’s boldness and realized that they were unlettered, ordinary men, they were amazed. They also recognized that they had been with Jesus.
  John 7:15 — thematic parallel: Both connect astonishment with a religious speaker's learning. Acts describes Peter and John as unlettered, ordinary men yet bold and associated with Jesus; John records surprise at Jesus' knowledge of the writings without study. The descriptions concern different people and do not themselves settle their reading or writing abilities.
    John 7:15 So the Jews marveled, saying, “How does this man know the writings without having studied?”

Acts 4:14
Seeing the man who had been healed standing with them, they had nothing to say against it.
  John 9:18–9:20 — narrative parallel: Both authorities confront evidence about a formerly disabled man. Acts says the healed man standing there leaves nothing to say against it; John's investigators initially doubt and seek parental confirmation of congenital blindness. The evidence and stages of response differ.
    John 9:18 So the Jews did not believe concerning him that he had been blind and received his sight, until they called the parents of the man who had received his sight
    John 9:19 and asked them, saying, “Is this your son, whom you say was born blind? How, then, does he now see?”
    John 9:20 His parents answered, “We know that this is our son and that he was born blind.

Acts 4:15
They ordered them to leave the council and conferred with one another,
  Acts 5:34 — narrative parallel: Both council scenes order the apostles outside before further deliberation. Acts 4 leads to threats after an admission about the sign; Acts 5 has Gamaliel call for a brief removal before his counsel. The procedure recurs in separate hearings with different advice.
    Acts 5:34 But a Pharisee named Gamaliel, a teacher of the law honored by all the people, stood in the council and ordered the men to be taken outside briefly.

Acts 4:16
saying, “What should we do with these men? That a notable sign has taken place through them is evident to everyone living in Jerusalem, and we cannot deny it.
  John 11:47–11:48 — narrative parallel: Both councils ask what to do while acknowledging extraordinary works. Acts concerns an undeniable public healing through the apostles; John concerns Jesus' many signs and fear of wider belief and Roman action. The speakers' admissions do not make their complete reasoning identical.
    John 11:47 Then the chief priests and the Pharisees gathered a council and said, “What are we doing? For this man does many signs.
    John 11:48 If we leave Him like this, everyone will believe in Him, and the Romans will come and take away both our place and our nation.”

Acts 4:17
But so that it spreads no further among the people, let us warn them with threats not to speak to anyone in this name again.”
  Acts 5:28 — thematic parallel: The council proposes a threat to stop speaking in the name; the later hearing recalls the strict prohibition on teaching in that name. This tracks the developing ban and later charge, without treating the reported prohibition as divine authorization.
    Acts 5:28 saying, “Did we not strictly command you not to teach in this name? Yet look—you have filled Jerusalem with your teaching and intend to bring this man’s blood upon us.”

Acts 4:18
They called them in and ordered them neither to speak nor to teach at all in the name of Jesus.
  Acts 5:40 — thematic parallel: Both record an explicit order against speaking in Jesus' name. Acts 4 prohibits both speaking and teaching; Acts 5 adds beating and release. The later ban recurs in another hearing rather than supplying a beating to the earlier scene.
    Acts 5:40 They summoned the apostles, beat them, ordered them not to speak in the name of Jesus, and released them.

Acts 4:19
Peter and John answered them, “Judge whether it is right before God to listen to you rather than to God;
  Acts 5:29 — thematic parallel: Peter and John ask whether listening to authorities instead of God is right; the later apostles explicitly say God must be obeyed rather than human beings. The question and subsequent rule correspond within the recurring teaching ban.
    Acts 5:29 Peter and the apostles answered, “We must obey God rather than human beings.

Acts 4:20
for we cannot keep from speaking about what we have seen and heard.”
  1 John 1:1–1:3 — thematic parallel: Both ground proclamation in what has been seen and heard. Acts names Peter and John refusing silence; the Epistle speaks through its witnessing 'we' about the manifested Life and fellowship. The comparison does not identify every member of the Epistle's first-person plural.
    1 John 1:1 What was from the beginning, what we have heard, what we have seen with our eyes, what we looked upon and our hands touched, concerning the word of life—
    1 John 1:2 and the life was manifested, and we have seen and bear witness and announce to you the eternal life that was with the Father and was manifested to us—
    1 John 1:3 what we have seen and heard we announce also to you, so that you too may have fellowship with us. And our fellowship is with the Father and with His Son Jesus Christ.

Acts 4:21
After further threats, they released them, finding no way to punish them because of the people, since everyone was glorifying God for what had happened.
  Luke 20:19 — narrative parallel: Both show concern about the people's reaction limiting hostile leaders' immediate action. Luke concerns seizing Jesus after the parable; Acts concerns punishing the apostles while people glorify God for healing. The particular motives, actions, and public responses differ.
    Luke 20:19 And the scribes and the chief priests sought to lay hands on Him at that very hour, but they feared the people, for they knew that He had spoken this parable against them.

Acts 4:22
For the man in whom this sign of healing had occurred was more than forty years old.
  Acts 3:2 — thematic parallel: The earlier narrative identifies the man as unable to walk from birth; this explanation gives his age as more than forty. Together they establish the condition's long-standing character. The age is not reduced to exactly forty or assigned to another healed man.
    Acts 3:2 A man unable to walk from his mother’s womb was being carried there. They used to place him each day at the temple gate called Beautiful, to ask for alms from those entering the temple.

Acts 4:23
After their release, they went to their own people and reported all that the chief priests and elders had said to them.
  Acts 12:17 — narrative parallel: Both portray Peter reporting to fellow believers after release from confinement. Acts 4 includes John and recounts the chief priests' and elders' words; Acts 12 describes an angelic escape and a report of the Lord's deliverance. The release methods and report contents differ.
    Acts 12:17 He motioned with his hand for them to be silent and related how the Lord had brought him out of prison. He said, “Report these things to James and the brothers.” Then he left and went to another place.

Acts 4:24
On hearing it, they lifted their voice together to God and said, “Master, Thou art the One who made heaven and earth and the sea and everything in them,
