  Psalms 145:6 — allusion: The prayer invokes the Creator of heaven, earth, sea, and everything in them, matching the Psalm's creation formula. The Psalm also speaks of keeping truth for ever; the Acts prayer proceeds to the community's present threats. The complete passages are not identical.
    Psalms 145:6 who made heaven and earth, the sea and all that is in them, who keeps truth for ever,

Acts 4:25
who through the Holy Spirit, by the mouth of our father David, Thy servant, said, ‘Why did nations rage and peoples devise empty things?
  Psalms 2:1 — quotation: The prayer attributes the raging-nations and vain-peoples question to God through the Spirit and David. The Psalm supplies the question; the attribution is not silently inserted as a new heading into the Psalm itself.
    Psalms 2:1 Why did nations rage, and peoples meditate on empty things?

Acts 4:26
The kings of the earth took their stand, and the rulers gathered together against the Lord and against His Christ.’
  Psalms 2:2 — quotation: Both describe kings and rulers assembling against the Lord. Acts uses His Christ where the Psalm says His anointed, then applies the pattern to named actors in Jesus' Passion. Those proper names belong to the community's interpretation rather than this Psalm verse.
    Psalms 2:2 The kings of the earth stood ready, and the rulers gathered together, against the Lord and against His anointed. Diapsalma.

Acts 4:27
For truly, in this city, both Herod and Pontius Pilate, with the Gentiles and the peoples of Israel, gathered against Thy holy Servant Jesus, whom Thou didst anoint,
  Luke 23:7–23:12 — narrative parallel: The prayer names Herod and Pontius Pilate among those gathered against Jesus; Luke narrates their respective involvement and becoming friends that day. Acts additionally interprets the event through anointing and the Psalm. The quoted Psalm and Luke do not themselves contain the whole prayer.
    Luke 23:7 And learning that He belonged to Herod’s jurisdiction, he sent Him to Herod, who was himself also in Jerusalem in those days.
    Luke 23:8 When Herod saw Jesus, he rejoiced greatly, for he had long wanted to see Him because he had heard about Him, and he hoped to see some sign performed by Him.
    Luke 23:9 He questioned Him at length, but He gave him no answer.
    Luke 23:10 And the chief priests and scribes stood there, accusing Him vehemently.
    Luke 23:11 Herod also, with his troops, treated Him with contempt and mocked Him. He clothed Him in splendid clothing and sent Him back to Pilate.
    Luke 23:12 Herod and Pilate became friends with one another on that very day, for previously they had been at enmity with each other.

Acts 4:28
to do whatever Thy hand and Thy purpose had determined beforehand should happen.
  Acts 2:23 — thematic parallel: Both describe Jesus' Passion through divine purpose alongside human action. The prayer speaks of God's hand and predetermined purpose; Peter's sermon speaks of purpose and foreknowledge while charging its hearers and lawless agents. Predetermination does not remove the responsibility explicitly retained in the sermon.
    Acts 2:23 This man, handed over according to God’s determined purpose and foreknowledge, you nailed up and killed through the hands of lawless men.

Acts 4:29
And now, Lord, look upon their threats, and grant Thy servants to speak Thy word with all boldness,
  Ephesians 6:19–6:20 — thematic parallel: Both seek bold proclamation under opposition. The community asks God for bold speech despite threats; Ephesians asks prayer for bold gospel speech while its speaker is an ambassador in chains. The groups, confinement status, and requests' wording differ.
    Ephesians 6:19 and for me, so that a word may be given to me when I open my mouth, to make known with boldness the mystery of the gospel,
    Ephesians 6:20 for which I serve as an ambassador bound by a chain, so that in it I may speak boldly, as I ought to speak.

Acts 4:30
while Thou stretchest out Thy hand for healing, and signs and wonders take place through the name of Thy holy Servant Jesus.”
  Acts 5:12 — thematic parallel: The prayer asks God to extend His hand in healing and signs through Jesus' name; the later summary describes signs through the apostles' hands. Divine agency and human instrumentality are related but remain different grammatical descriptions.
    Acts 5:12 Many signs and wonders were taking place among the people through the apostles’ hands. They were all together with one accord in Solomon’s Portico.

Acts 4:31
When they had prayed, the place where they were gathered was shaken. They were all filled with the Holy Spirit and spoke God’s word with boldness.
  Acts 2:4 — thematic parallel: Both describe all present being filled with the Holy Spirit and speaking. Acts 2 specifies other tongues; Acts 4 specifies God's word with boldness and adds the shaking place. The accounts do not establish identical attendance or identical manifestations in every filling.
    Acts 2:4 They were all filled with the Holy Spirit and began to speak in other tongues, as the Spirit gave them utterance.

Acts 4:32
The multitude of those who believed was of one heart and soul. Not one of them claimed that anything he possessed was his own, but they held everything in common.
  Acts 2:44 — thematic parallel: Both describe believers holding possessions in common. Acts 4 adds one heart and soul and the absence of private claims within that sharing; Acts 2 is the earlier concise summary. This expanded description is not independent evidence of a separate property system.
    Acts 2:44 All who believed were together and held everything in common.

Acts 4:33
With great power the apostles gave their testimony to the resurrection of the Lord Jesus, and great grace was upon them all.
  Acts 1:8 — promise and fulfillment: Jesus promises power for witness; the summary describes the apostles' powerful testimony to His resurrection. Acts 4 also speaks of great grace upon all. The commission and subsequent testimony correspond while their full formulations differ.
    Acts 1:8 But you will receive power when the Holy Spirit comes upon you, and you will be My witnesses in Jerusalem and [in] all Judea and Samaria, and to the end of the earth.”

Acts 4:34
For there was not a needy person among them: all who owned lands or houses would sell them and bring the proceeds of what was sold,
  Deuteronomy 15:4 — allusion: Both use the absence of a needy person as a community ideal. Deuteronomy frames it with God's land blessing; Acts describes owners selling property to meet needs. The correspondence does not make every condition and mechanism of the land-covenant passage identical to the Acts practice.
    Deuteronomy 15:4 For there shall be none in need among you; for the Lord your God shall surely bless you in the land which the Lord your God gives you for a possession, to inherit it,

Acts 4:35
laying them at the apostles’ feet; distribution was made to each as anyone had need.
  Acts 2:45 — thematic parallel: Both specify distribution according to each person's need. Acts 4 describes proceeds placed at the apostles' feet before distribution; Acts 2 gives the earlier general selling-and-sharing summary. The repeated practice is internal narrative continuity.
    Acts 2:45 They would sell their properties and possessions and distribute the proceeds to all, as anyone had need.

Acts 4:36
Joseph, whom the apostles called Barnabas—which means Son of Encouragement—a Levite and a native of Cyprus,
  Acts 11:22–11:24 — thematic parallel: The introduced Barnabas later serves the Antioch believers and exhorts them to remain with the Lord. Acts 4 gives his Joseph name, encouragement gloss, and Cypriot Levite background; Acts 11 gives his conduct and character. His role is not assigned to a different Joseph merely sharing a name.
    Acts 11:22 The report about them reached the ears of the Church in Jerusalem, and they sent Barnabas to travel as far as Antioch.
    Acts 11:23 When he arrived and saw the grace of God, he rejoiced and encouraged them all to remain with the Lord with steadfast purpose of heart,
    Acts 11:24 for he was a good man, full of the Holy Spirit and faith. A considerable crowd was added to the Lord.

Acts 4:37
sold a field he owned, brought the money, and laid it at the apostles’ feet.
  Acts 5:1–5:4 — narrative parallel: Barnabas' narrated sale and gift at the apostles' feet precede another sale and gift with secret withholding and deception. Peter explicitly acknowledges the couple's authority over the property and proceeds. The contrast concerns the narrated deception rather than making every partial gift inherently sinful.
    Acts 5:1 A man named Ananias, together with his wife Sapphira, sold a property.
    Acts 5:2 He secretly kept back part of the price, with his wife also knowing about it, and brought a portion and laid it at the apostles’ feet.
    Acts 5:3 Peter said, “Ananias, why has Satan filled your heart, so that you lied to the Holy Spirit and secretly kept back part of the price of the land?
    Acts 5:4 While it remained unsold, did it not remain yours? And after it was sold, was it not under your authority? Why have you put this deed in your heart? You have not lied to human beings but to God.”

Acts 5:1
A man named Ananias, together with his wife Sapphira, sold a property.
  Acts 4:36–4:37 — narrative parallel: The couple's property sale follows Barnabas' field sale and gift. Both are presented in the community's giving context; the following verses distinguish the couple's deception. Barnabas is not identified with Ananias, and sale alone is not labeled the offense.
    Acts 4:36 Joseph, whom the apostles called Barnabas—which means Son of Encouragement—a Levite and a native of Cyprus,
    Acts 4:37 sold a field he owned, brought the money, and laid it at the apostles’ feet.

Acts 5:2
He secretly kept back part of the price, with his wife also knowing about it, and brought a portion and laid it at the apostles’ feet.
  Joshua 7:1 — allusion: Both narratives describe a concealed appropriation connected with community wrongdoing. Joshua concerns Achar taking devoted goods; Acts concerns part of the couple's own sale proceeds and the deception later exposed. Their property status and offenses are not made identical.
    Joshua 7:1 The sons of Israel committed a great offence and misappropriated what was under the ban. Achar son of Carmi, son of Zambri, son of Zerah, of the tribe of Judah, took some of it, and the Lord became angry with the sons of Israel.

Acts 5:3
Peter said, “Ananias, why has Satan filled your heart, so that you lied to the Holy Spirit and secretly kept back part of the price of the land?
  John 13:2 — thematic parallel: Acts describes Satan filling Ananias' heart so that he lied to the Spirit; John describes the devil putting into the heart that Judas should hand Jesus over. The latter sentence does not explicitly name the heart's possessor. The deeds and influence verbs remain distinct.
    John 13:2 And while supper was taking place, the devil having already put it into the heart that Judas, son of Simon Iscariot, should hand Him over,

Acts 5:4
While it remained unsold, did it not remain yours? And after it was sold, was it not under your authority? Why have you put this deed in your heart? You have not lied to human beings but to God.”
  Acts 5:3 — thematic parallel: Peter describes the same falsehood first as lying to the Holy Spirit and then as lying to God. The comparison preserves that divine attribution alongside his explicit acknowledgment of control over the property and proceeds. The offense is not merely having retained private property.
    Acts 5:3 Peter said, “Ananias, why has Satan filled your heart, so that you lied to the Holy Spirit and secretly kept back part of the price of the land?

Acts 5:5
When Ananias heard these words, he fell down and breathed his last. Great fear came upon all who heard.
  Acts 12:23 — thematic parallel: Both narrated deaths follow an offense relating to God. Ananias hears Peter and falls dead with resulting fear; Acts 12 explicitly describes an angel striking Herod and his dying after the glory offense. The angel and bodily details are not imported into Ananias' death.
    Acts 12:23 Immediately an angel of the Lord struck him because he did not give the glory to God. He was eaten by worms and breathed his last.

Acts 5:6
The younger men rose, wrapped him up, carried him out, and buried him.
  Acts 5:9–5:10 — thematic parallel: Peter later refers to those who buried the husband, and the narrative then has young men carry out and bury the wife. This tracks the burial party through one connected incident rather than creating a separate burial custom or an independent account.
    Acts 5:9 Peter said to her, “Why did you both agree to test the Spirit of the Lord? Look, the feet of those who buried your husband are at the door, and they will carry you out.”
    Acts 5:10 At once she fell at his feet and breathed her last. When the young men came in, they found her dead; they carried her out and buried her beside her husband.

Acts 5:7
About three hours later, his wife came in, not knowing what had happened.
  Acts 5:2 — thematic parallel: The wife's ignorance of what had happened follows the narrator's earlier statement that she knew about the withheld price. The objects of knowledge differ: complicity in the transaction is not knowledge of her husband's intervening death. The two reports are not treated as contradictory.
    Acts 5:2 He secretly kept back part of the price, with his wife also knowing about it, and brought a portion and laid it at the apostles’ feet.

Acts 5:8
Peter said to her, “Tell me, did you sell the land for this amount?” She said, “Yes, for that amount.”
  Joshua 7:19–7:21 — thematic parallel: Both questioning scenes concern concealed property wrongdoing, but the answers differ. Achar confesses his taking and hiding; Sapphira confirms the false price claim. The older devoted goods are not equated with a rule requiring the couple to donate all their own property.
    Joshua 7:19 Joshua said to Achar, “Give glory today to the Lord, the God of Israel, and make confession. Tell me what you have done, and do not hide it from me.”
    Joshua 7:20 Achar answered Joshua, “Truly I have sinned before the Lord, the God of Israel. This is what I did:
    Joshua 7:21 I saw among the spoil a beautiful patterned carpet, two hundred didrachms of silver, and one tongue-shaped bar of gold weighing fifty didrachms. I coveted them and took them. They are hidden in the ground in my tent, and the silver is hidden underneath them.”

Acts 5:9
Peter said to her, “Why did you both agree to test the Spirit of the Lord? Look, the feet of those who buried your husband are at the door, and they will carry you out.”
  Deuteronomy 6:16 — thematic parallel: Moses prohibits testing the Lord God; Peter accuses the couple of agreeing to test the Spirit of the Lord. The related testing language is compared without making the different descriptions or the incidents at their respective settings identical.
    Deuteronomy 6:16 You shall not put the Lord your God to the test, as you tested Him at Testing.

Acts 5:10
At once she fell at his feet and breathed her last. When the young men came in, they found her dead; they carried her out and buried her beside her husband.
  Acts 5:5–5:6 — narrative parallel: The wife's sudden death and removal parallel the husband's earlier death and burial. Acts adds her falling at Peter's feet and burial beside him; the earlier account explicitly mentions wrapping the body. The narrated sequence is retained without inventing a medical cause.
    Acts 5:5 When Ananias heard these words, he fell down and breathed his last. Great fear came upon all who heard.
    Acts 5:6 The younger men rose, wrapped him up, carried him out, and buried him.

Acts 5:11
Great fear came upon the whole Church and upon all who heard these things.
  Acts 19:17 — thematic parallel: Both connect knowledge of an extraordinary event with widespread fear. Acts 5 concerns the Church and hearers after the deaths; Acts 19 concerns Ephesus after the failed exorcism and also magnifies Jesus' name. The events, audiences, and described consequences differ.
    Acts 19:17 This became known to all the Jews and Greeks living in Ephesus. Fear fell upon them all, and the name of the Lord Jesus was magnified.

Acts 5:12
Many signs and wonders were taking place among the people through the apostles’ hands. They were all together with one accord in Solomon’s Portico.
  Acts 4:30 — thematic parallel: The preceding prayer asks God for healing and signs through Jesus' name; this summary reports signs through the apostles' hands. The divine hand in the request and human hands in the summary remain distinct descriptions of agency.
    Acts 4:30 while Thou stretchest out Thy hand for healing, and signs and wonders take place through the name of Thy holy Servant Jesus.”

Acts 5:13
None of the rest dared to join them, but the people held them in high esteem.
  Acts 2:47 — thematic parallel: Both report favorable public regard. Acts 2 also describes the Lord adding people; Acts 5 holds esteem alongside reluctance to join. The comparison preserves that reluctance rather than treating public approval and personal joining as identical.
    Acts 2:47 praising God and enjoying favor with all the people. And day by day the Lord added those who were being saved to their gathering.

Acts 5:14
More and more believers were added to the Lord, multitudes of both men and women,
  Acts 11:24 — thematic parallel: Both summaries describe people being added to the Lord. Acts 5 specifies multitudes of men and women in its Jerusalem setting; Acts 11 places a considerable crowd in Barnabas' Antioch ministry. The communities and descriptions are not merged into one numerical total.
    Acts 11:24 for he was a good man, full of the Holy Spirit and faith. A considerable crowd was added to the Lord.

Acts 5:15
so that they even carried the sick into the streets and laid them on small beds and mats, so that as Peter came by, at least his shadow might fall upon one of them.
  Mark 6:55–6:56 — narrative parallel: Both crowds carry sick people on beds or mats to places of expected healing. Mark records the request to touch Jesus' garment and says those who touched Him were saved; Acts reports the wish for Peter's shadow to fall. This verse's expectation is not rewritten as an explicit statement that the shadow itself cured.
    Mark 6:55 and ran through that whole country. They began carrying the sick around on their mats to wherever they heard He was.
    Mark 6:56 Wherever He entered villages, cities, or fields, they laid the sick in the marketplaces and begged Him to let them touch even the fringe of His garment. As many as touched Him were saved.

Acts 5:16
The crowd also came together from the towns around Jerusalem, bringing the sick and those troubled by unclean spirits, and they were all healed.
  Matthew 4:24 — narrative parallel: Both describe groups bringing sick and demon-troubled people for healing. Matthew concerns Jesus and reports spreading news into Syria; Acts concerns the apostolic setting and towns around Jerusalem. The agents and locations differ rather than describing the same gathering.
    Matthew 4:24 The report about Him spread throughout Syria, and they brought Him all who were ill, afflicted with various diseases and torments, those oppressed by demons, those suffering seizures, and paralytics; and He healed them.

Acts 5:17
The high priest and all who were with him—the party of the Sadducees—rose up, filled with jealousy.
  Acts 4:1–4:2 — thematic parallel: The priestly and Sadducean opposition recurs. Acts 4 names trouble over resurrection proclamation; Acts 5 explicitly names jealousy. The repeated group context does not make both passages state exactly the same motive.
    Acts 4:1 While they were speaking to the people, the priests, the captain of the temple, and the Sadducees came upon them,
    Acts 4:2 greatly troubled because they were teaching the people and proclaiming in Jesus the resurrection from the dead.

Acts 5:18
They laid hands on the apostles and placed them in the public prison.
  Acts 12:3–12:4 — narrative parallel: Both imprison apostolic witnesses before a later divine deliverance. Acts 5 concerns the apostles in the public prison; Acts 12 names Peter under Herod and describes guards. Those particular officials and guard arrangements are not inserted into Acts 5.
    Acts 12:3 When he saw that this pleased the Jews, he proceeded to arrest Peter as well. These were the days of Unleavened Bread.
    Acts 12:4 He seized him and put him in prison, handing him over to four squads of four soldiers to guard him, intending to bring him out before the people after the Passover.

Acts 5:19
But during the night an angel of the Lord opened the prison doors, led them out, and said,
  Acts 12:7–12:10 — narrative parallel: Both prison escapes involve an angel and opened access from confinement. Acts 5 concerns the apostles and a directive to teach; Acts 12 concerns Peter, falling chains, passing guards, and an opening iron gate. The extra Acts 12 details are not treated as details explicitly recorded in both.
    Acts 12:7 And look—an angel of the Lord stood beside him, and light shone in the cell. Striking Peter’s side, he woke him and said, “Rise quickly.” The chains fell from his hands.
    Acts 12:8 The angel said to him, “Fasten your belt and put on your sandals.” He did so. Then he said to him, “Wrap your cloak around you and follow me.”
    Acts 12:9 Peter went out and followed. He did not know that what was happening through the angel was real; he thought he was seeing a vision.
    Acts 12:10 They passed the first guard post and the second and came to the iron gate leading into the city. It opened for them of its own accord. They went out and continued along one street, and immediately the angel left him.

Acts 5:20
“Go, stand in the temple, and speak to the people all the words of this life.”
  John 6:68 — thematic parallel: The angel commands speaking all the words of this life; Peter in John says Jesus has words of eternal life. This compares life-related proclamation language. Acts' 'this life' is not silently changed into John's complete 'eternal life' formulation.
    John 6:68 Simon Peter answered Him, “Lord, to whom shall we go? Thou hast words of eternal life.

Acts 5:21
When they heard this, they entered the temple at daybreak and began teaching. The high priest and those with him arrived, called together the council and the whole assembly of elders of the sons of Israel, and sent to the jail to have them brought in.
  Luke 21:37–21:38 — narrative parallel: Both describe early public Temple teaching. Luke describes Jesus' recurring day-teaching and people coming early; Acts describes the apostles obeying at daybreak after release. Acts additionally narrates the authorities' gathering, which is not part of Luke's brief teaching summary.
    Luke 21:37 By day He was teaching in the temple, but at night He went out and lodged on the mount called Olivet.
    Luke 21:38 And all the people would come early in the morning to Him in the temple to hear Him.

Acts 5:22
But when the officers arrived, they did not find them in the prison. They returned and reported,
  Acts 12:18–12:19 — narrative parallel: Both prison-deliverance accounts lead to a search that fails to find the prisoners. Acts 5 reports officers returning to the council; Acts 12 reports soldiers' disturbance and Herod's response. Different prisoners and responses remain distinct.
    Acts 12:18 When day came, there was no small disturbance among the soldiers over what had become of Peter.
    Acts 12:19 Herod searched for him and did not find him. After examining the guards, he ordered them to be led away for execution. Then he went down from Judea to Caesarea and stayed there.

Acts 5:23
“We found the jail securely locked and the guards standing at the doors, but when we opened it, we found no one inside.”
  Acts 5:19 — thematic parallel: The officers report locked doors, guards, and no prisoners; the earlier narrative says an angel opened the doors and led the apostles out. These describe different moments and perspectives. No unrecorded relocking procedure or method of evading the guards is supplied.
    Acts 5:19 But during the night an angel of the Lord opened the prison doors, led them out, and said,

Acts 5:24
When the captain of the temple and the chief priests heard these words, they were perplexed about them, wondering what this would come to.
  Acts 5:22–5:23 — internal report and stated reaction: The these words heard by the temple captain and chief priests are the officers' preceding report that the jail was locked and guarded yet empty. This directly identifies the report behind their perplexity without supplying their private conclusions or treating another ruler's surprise as the same reaction.
    Acts 5:22 But when the officers arrived, they did not find them in the prison. They returned and reported,
    Acts 5:23 “We found the jail securely locked and the guards standing at the doors, but when we opened it, we found no one inside.”

Acts 5:25
Then someone came and reported to them, “Look, the men you put in prison are standing in the temple and teaching the people.”
  Acts 5:20–5:21 — thematic parallel: The report that the imprisoned men are teaching in the Temple corresponds to the angel's earlier command and the narrated obedience. It is an internal report of that public activity, not a second release or an independently preserved chronicle.
    Acts 5:20 “Go, stand in the temple, and speak to the people all the words of this life.”
    Acts 5:21 When they heard this, they entered the temple at daybreak and began teaching. The high priest and those with him arrived, called together the council and the whole assembly of elders of the sons of Israel, and sent to the jail to have them brought in.

Acts 5:26
The captain then went with the officers and brought them, without force, for they feared the people might stone them.
  Luke 20:5–20:6 — narrative parallel: Both authority groups fear being stoned by the people. Luke concerns an answer about John's baptism and the people's conviction that John was a prophet; Acts concerns the captain and officers avoiding force in an arrest. The decisions and their immediate grounds differ.
    Luke 20:5 And they reasoned among themselves, saying, “If we say, ‘From heaven,’ He will say, ‘Why did you not believe him?’
    Luke 20:6 But if we say, ‘From people,’ all the people will stone us, for they are convinced that John was a prophet.”

Acts 5:27
They brought them and made them stand before the council. The high priest questioned them,
  Acts 4:5–4:7 — narrative parallel: Both bring the apostles before the council for questioning. Acts 4 asks about power and name after healing; Acts 5 has the high priest confront them with the earlier ban and ensuing accusations. These are separate hearings rather than one repeated transcript.
    Acts 4:5 The next day their rulers, elders, and scribes gathered in Jerusalem,
    Acts 4:6 together with Annas the high priest, Caiaphas, John, Alexander, and all who belonged to the high-priestly family.
    Acts 4:7 They made them stand in their midst and asked, “By what power or by what name did you do this?”

Acts 5:28
saying, “Did we not strictly command you not to teach in this name? Yet look—you have filled Jerusalem with your teaching and intend to bring this man’s blood upon us.”
  Acts 4:17–4:18 — thematic parallel: The high priest's reminder of a strict teaching prohibition points back to the earlier council's threat and order. Acts 5 additionally accuses the apostles of filling Jerusalem and bringing blood responsibility upon the leaders. Those added accusations are not made words of the earlier order.
    Acts 4:17 But so that it spreads no further among the people, let us warn them with threats not to speak to anyone in this name again.”
    Acts 4:18 They called them in and ordered them neither to speak nor to teach at all in the name of Jesus.

Acts 5:29
Peter and the apostles answered, “We must obey God rather than human beings.
  Acts 4:19–4:20 — thematic parallel: The apostles' explicit rule of obeying God rather than humans develops Peter and John's earlier question about whose voice to heed and refusal to stop testifying. It is the recurring response to the teaching ban, not a general endorsement of every act of defiance.
    Acts 4:19 Peter and John answered them, “Judge whether it is right before God to listen to you rather than to God;
    Acts 4:20 for we cannot keep from speaking about what we have seen and heard.”

Acts 5:30
The God of our fathers raised Jesus, whom you killed by hanging Him on a tree.
  Deuteronomy 21:22–21:23 — allusion: Both use hanging-on-a-tree language. Deuteronomy regulates the display and burial of an executed body; Acts speaks of Jesus being killed by hanging and God raising Him. The legal background is compared without making the procedures identical or adding the whole curse statement to Peter's verse.
    Deuteronomy 21:22 And if there is in a man a sin deserving judgment of death, and he dies, and you hang him upon a tree,
    Deuteronomy 21:23 his body shall not remain upon the tree overnight, but you shall surely bury him that day; for everyone who hangs upon a tree is cursed by God. And you shall not defile the land which the Lord your God gives you for a possession.

Acts 5:31
God exalted Him to His right hand as Leader and Savior, to grant repentance to Israel and forgiveness of sins.
  Acts 11:18 — thematic parallel: Acts 5 describes Jesus' exaltation as Leader and Savior to grant Israel repentance and forgiveness; Acts 11 recognizes God granting Gentiles repentance unto life. The gift-and-repentance language corresponds while the named recipients, life/forgiveness expressions, and agents remain as stated.
    Acts 11:18 When they heard these things, they fell silent and glorified God, saying, “Then God has granted even the Gentiles repentance leading to life.”

Acts 5:32
We are witnesses of these things, and so is the Holy Spirit, whom God has given to those who obey Him.”
  John 15:26–15:27 — promise and fulfillment: Jesus promises the Spirit's testimony together with His followers' witness; Acts names the apostles and Holy Spirit as witnesses. Acts additionally says God gives the Spirit to those obeying Him. The different sending, witnessing, and gift statements are preserved.
    John 15:26 “When the Advocate comes, whom I will send you from the Father, the Spirit of truth who proceeds from the Father, He will bear witness about Me.
    John 15:27 And you also bear witness, because you have been with Me from the beginning.

Acts 5:33
When they heard this, they were enraged and wanted to kill them.
  Acts 7:54 — thematic parallel: Both describe an enraged response to hearing testimony. Acts 5 explicitly states the wish to kill the apostles; Acts 7 describes anger in the hearers' hearts and gnashing teeth against Stephen. The reactions recur but the subsequent events are different.
    Acts 7:54 When they heard these things, they were cut to the heart with rage and ground their teeth at him.

Acts 5:34
But a Pharisee named Gamaliel, a teacher of the law honored by all the people, stood in the council and ordered the men to be taken outside briefly.
  Acts 22:3 — thematic parallel: Acts 5 introduces Gamaliel as an honored Pharisaic law teacher; Paul later names instruction at Gamaliel's feet in the ancestral law. The name-and-teaching-role link does not supply an unrecorded conversion of Gamaliel or equate all his views with Paul's later proclamation.
    Acts 22:3 “I am a Jewish man, born in Tarsus of Cilicia, but brought up in this city at the feet of Gamaliel, educated in the strict observance of the ancestral law, zealous for God as all of you are today.

Acts 5:35
He said to them, “Men of Israel, take care what you are about to do to these men.
  Jeremiah 33:16–33:19 — thematic parallel: Both assembly accounts contain counsel against rash treatment of religious messengers, with historical examples in the surrounding speeches. Jeremiah's elders discuss Micah and Hezekiah; Gamaliel's speech uses different cases and a conditional God-origin argument. The older speakers are not made authors of Gamaliel's exact advice.
    Jeremiah 33:16 And the rulers and all the people said to the priests and false prophets: There is no sentence of death for this man, because he spoke to us in the name of the Lord our God.
    Jeremiah 33:17 And men from the land’s elders rose and said to the whole congregation of the people:
    Jeremiah 33:18 Micah the Morathite lived in the days of Hezekiah king of Judah and said to all Judah’s people: Thus said the Lord: Zion will be plowed as a field, Jerusalem will become an untrodden place, and the house’s mountain a grove of woodland.
    Jeremiah 33:19 Did Hezekiah and all Judah kill him? Was it not that they feared the Lord and entreated the Lord’s face, and the Lord ceased from the evils He spoke against them? And we have done great evils against our own lives!

Acts 5:36
For before these days Theudas arose, claiming to be somebody, and about four hundred men joined him. He was killed, and all who followed him were dispersed and came to nothing.
  Acts 5:37–5:39 — internal paired examples and argument: Gamaliel follows Theudas' death and dispersed followers with Judas' death and scattered followers, then contrasts an overthrown human undertaking with work from God. These are his paired examples and stated argument, not an external identification of either leader or a reconstruction of dates absent from this passage.
    Acts 5:37 After him Judas the Galilean arose in the days of the registration and drew people away after him. He too perished, and all who followed him were scattered.
    Acts 5:38 And now I tell you, keep away from these men and leave them alone. For if this plan or this work is of human origin, it will be overthrown;
    Acts 5:39 but if it is from God, you will not be able to overthrow them. You might even be found fighting against God.” They were persuaded by him.

Acts 5:37
After him Judas the Galilean arose in the days of the registration and drew people away after him. He too perished, and all who followed him were scattered.
  Acts 5:36 — internal paired leader-and-followers examples: After him connects Judas with Gamaliel's preceding Theudas example: both leaders die and their followers disperse. The registration belongs to Judas' notice and about four hundred to Theudas'. The examples remain distinct, without identifying the registration with another census or harmonizing an external chronology.
    Acts 5:36 For before these days Theudas arose, claiming to be somebody, and about four hundred men joined him. He was killed, and all who followed him were dispersed and came to nothing.

Acts 5:38
And now I tell you, keep away from these men and leave them alone. For if this plan or this work is of human origin, it will be overthrown;
  Proverbs 19:21 — thematic parallel: The human-work condition can be compared with the proverb's distinction between human reasonings and the Lord's enduring counsel. Gamaliel speaks conditionally about this plan or work; the proverb does not identify these apostles or supply a universal test based solely on a movement's immediate success.
    Proverbs 19:21 Many are the reasonings in a man’s heart, but the counsel of the Lord remains for ever.

Acts 5:39
but if it is from God, you will not be able to overthrow them. You might even be found fighting against God.” They were persuaded by him.
  Isaiah 14:27 — thematic parallel: Gamaliel warns that God-origin work cannot be overthrown and opposition could be against God. Isaiah asks who can scatter the holy God's counsel or turn back His raised hand. The related divine-purpose theme does not turn Isaiah's historical judgment into the same event as this council.
    Isaiah 14:27 For what the holy God has counseled, who will scatter? And who will turn back the high hand?

Acts 5:40
They summoned the apostles, beat them, ordered them not to speak in the name of Jesus, and released them.
  Matthew 10:17 — promise and fulfillment: Jesus forecasts council proceedings and scourging of His followers; Acts describes the council's beating, renewed ban, and release. Matthew specifically mentions synagogues, whereas Acts does not locate the beating in that particular setting or give a count of blows.
    Matthew 10:17 Beware of people, for they will hand you over to councils and flog you in their synagogues.

Acts 5:41
So they left the council rejoicing that they had been counted worthy to suffer dishonor for the Name.
  Matthew 5:11–5:12 — thematic parallel: Jesus calls persecuted followers to rejoice on His account; the apostles leave rejoicing at being counted worthy of dishonor for the Name. The Acts response exemplifies the teaching without reproducing its whole reward-and-prophet explanation.
    Matthew 5:11 Blessed are you when they revile you and persecute you and falsely speak every evil thing against you for My sake.
    Matthew 5:12 Rejoice and be glad, for great is your reward in the heavens; for thus they persecuted the prophets before you.

Acts 5:42
Every day, in the temple and in their homes, they never ceased teaching and proclaiming the good news of Jesus the Christ.
  Luke 19:47 — narrative parallel: Both describe daily Temple teaching amid opposition. Luke concerns Jesus and the leaders seeking His destruction; Acts concerns apostles continuing to proclaim Jesus as Christ in the Temple and homes. The shared practice does not make the people or complete activity descriptions identical.
    Luke 19:47 And He was teaching daily in the temple. But the chief priests and the scribes, and the leading men of the people, were seeking to destroy Him.

Acts 6:1
In those days, as the disciples increased in number, a complaint arose from the Hellenists against the Hebrews, because their widows were being overlooked in the daily service.
  1 Timothy 5:16 — widow-care comparison: Acts reports Hellenist widows overlooked in daily service; the letter assigns widow assistance to believing relatives and the Church. This concerns practical community support, without supplying the Acts widows' ages, family circumstances or an identical dispute.
    1 Timothy 5:16 If any believing woman has widows, let her assist them, and let the Church not be burdened, so that it may assist those who are truly widows.

Acts 6:2
The twelve called the multitude of the disciples together and said, “It is not fitting for us to leave the word of God to serve tables.
  Exodus 18:18–18:22 — delegation comparison: The apostles seek to preserve word service while others handle tables; Jethro advises Moses to share lesser judgments so the burden is bearable. The tasks and leaders differ; the comparison does not make the seven judges or identify their office.
    Exodus 18:18 You shall be worn out with unbearable exhaustion, both you and all this people who are with you; this matter is heavy for you, and you cannot do it alone.
    Exodus 18:19 Now therefore listen to me, and I will counsel you, and God shall be with you. Represent the people in matters pertaining to God, and bring their words to God.
    Exodus 18:20 And you shall solemnly instruct them in the ordinances of God and His law, and show them the ways in which they shall walk, and the works which they shall do.
    Exodus 18:21 And choose for yourself from all the people able men who fear God, righteous men who hate arrogance; and you shall set them over the people as rulers of thousands, and rulers of hundreds, and rulers of fifties, and rulers of tens.
    Exodus 18:22 And they shall judge the people at all times; the weighty matter they shall bring to you, but the lesser judgments they shall judge themselves; and they shall lighten your burden, and bear it with you.

Acts 6:3
So, brothers, select from among you seven men of good reputation, full of the Spirit and wisdom, whom we will put in charge of this task.
  Exodus 18:21 — qualified-delegate comparison: Both select qualified men for delegated responsibilities. Acts specifies seven, reputation, Spirit and wisdom; Exodus specifies able, God-fearing righteous men who hate arrogance and different numerical ranks. These qualifications and duties are preserved rather than made identical.
    Exodus 18:21 And choose for yourself from all the people able men who fear God, righteous men who hate arrogance; and you shall set them over the people as rulers of thousands, and rulers of hundreds, and rulers of fifties, and rulers of tens.

Acts 6:4
But we will devote ourselves to prayer and to the service of the word.”
  Colossians 4:2–4:4 — prayer-and-word ministry comparison: The apostles devote themselves to prayer and word service. Colossians urges persevering prayer, including prayer that God open a door for the word and its clear proclamation. The apostles' commitment and the community's requested prayers have different speakers and roles.
    Colossians 4:2 Persevere in prayer, keeping watch in it with thanksgiving,
    Colossians 4:3 praying at the same time for us also, so that God may open for us a door for the word, to speak the mystery of Christ, for which I am also bound,
    Colossians 4:4 so that I may make it clear as I ought to speak.

Acts 6:5
The proposal pleased the whole multitude. They chose Stephen, a man full of faith and the Holy Spirit, Philip, Prochorus, Nicanor, Timon, Parmenas, and Nicolaus, a convert from Antioch.
  Acts 21:8 — internal person reference: The selection list includes Philip; the later Caesarea account explicitly calls Philip the evangelist one of the seven. This identifies a concrete later reference to the seven-member appointment, without independently verifying every other listed name or making Philip one of the Twelve.
    Acts 21:8 The next day we departed and came to Caesarea. We entered the house of Philip the evangelist, who was one of the seven, and stayed with him.

Acts 6:6
They presented them before the apostles, who prayed and laid their hands upon them.
  Acts 13:2–13:3 — internal commissioning pattern: Both appointments include prayer and laying on hands before assigned work. Acts 13 additionally names fasting and the Spirit's call of Barnabas and Saul; Acts 6 presents the seven before the apostles. The people, tasks and stated initiating actions differ.
    Acts 13:2 While they were ministering to the Lord and fasting, the Holy Spirit said, “Set apart for Me Barnabas and Saul for the work to which I have called them.”
    Acts 13:3 Then, after fasting and praying and laying their hands upon them, they sent them away.

Acts 6:7
The word of God grew, and the number of disciples in Jerusalem increased greatly. A large number of the priests also became obedient to the faith.
  Acts 12:24 — internal growth-summary recurrence: Both summary notices say the word of God grew. Acts 6 additionally specifies Jerusalem disciples and many priests obeying the faith; Acts 12 says grew and multiplied after a different episode. The groups, periods and numerical extent are not equated.
    Acts 12:24 But the word of God grew and multiplied.

Acts 6:8
Stephen, full of grace and power, was doing great wonders and signs among the people.
  Acts 5:12 — internal signs-and-wonders recurrence: Both describe signs and wonders among the people. Acts 6 names Stephen, full of grace and power; Acts 5 attributes such works to the apostles' hands. This repeated ministry description does not turn Stephen into one of the Twelve.
    Acts 5:12 Many signs and wonders were taking place among the people through the apostles’ hands. They were all together with one accord in Solomon’s Portico.

Acts 6:10
They could not withstand the wisdom and the Spirit with which he spoke.
  Luke 21:15 — promise and narrative correspondence: Jesus promises mouth and wisdom that opponents cannot resist or contradict; Stephen's opponents cannot withstand his wisdom and the Spirit with which he speaks. The specific opposition-and-wisdom correspondence is retained while Jesus' promise and the Spirit-associated narration keep their distinct forms.
    Luke 21:15 for I will give you a mouth and wisdom that all who oppose you will be unable to resist or contradict.

Acts 6:11
Then they secretly persuaded men to say, “We have heard him speaking blasphemous words against Moses and God.”
  Matthew 26:59–26:61 — false-testimony trial comparison: Stephen's opponents secretly procure a blasphemy allegation; Matthew explicitly describes the council seeking false testimony against Jesus. The accused people and alleged words differ. The comparison concerns manufactured testimony in these confrontations, without endorsing either reported accusation.
    Matthew 26:59 The chief priests and the whole council sought false testimony against Jesus to put Him to death,
    Matthew 26:60 but found none, although many false witnesses came forward. Finally two came forward
    Matthew 26:61 and said, “This Man said, ‘I can destroy God’s sanctuary and build it in three days.’”

Acts 6:12
They stirred up the people, the elders, and the scribes, and came upon him, seized him, and brought him to the council.
  Acts 5:26–5:27 — internal council-arrest comparison: Both describe Jesus' witnesses brought before the council. Stephen is seized amid stirred-up opposition; the earlier apostles are brought without force because officers fear the people. These contrasting seizure details and different hearings are kept distinct.
    Acts 5:26 The captain then went with the officers and brought them, without force, for they feared the people might stone them.
    Acts 5:27 They brought them and made them stand before the council. The high priest questioned them,

Acts 6:13
They set up false witnesses who said, “This man never stops speaking words against this holy place and the law.
  Mark 14:57–14:58 — false-witness temple-charge comparison: Both accounts explicitly label witnesses false and include charges concerning the sacred place. Mark reports a sanctuary-demolition/rebuilding allegation against Jesus; Acts alleges Stephen speaks against this holy place and the law. Neither accusation becomes the narrator's endorsement.
    Mark 14:57 Some stood up and bore false witness against Him, saying,
    Mark 14:58 “We heard Him say, ‘I will tear down this sanctuary made by hands, and in three days I will build another not made by hands.’”

Acts 6:14
For we have heard him say that this Jesus the Nazarene will destroy this place and change the customs Moses handed down to us.”
  John 2:19–2:21 — contrast of temple-related speech: The witnesses allege Stephen said Jesus would destroy this place and change Mosaic customs. John reports Jesus telling His hearers to destroy the sanctuary and promising to raise it, with the narrator explaining His body. Speakers, agency, object and stated claims are not interchanged.
    John 2:19 Jesus answered them, “Destroy this sanctuary, and in three days I will raise it.”
    John 2:20 So the Jews said, “This sanctuary was built in forty-six years, and wilt Thou raise it in three days?”
    John 2:21 But He was speaking about the sanctuary of His body.

Acts 6:15
All who were seated in the council looked intently at him and saw his face like the face of an angel.
  Judges 13:6 — appearance comparison: The council sees Stephen's face like an angel's face; Manoah's wife describes a visitor's appearance like God's messenger, very glorious. These are specific messenger-like appearance descriptions, not a claim that Stephen is an angel or that Acts explicitly describes rays or a veil.
    Judges 13:6 The woman came and told her husband, saying, “A man of God came to me. His appearance was like the appearance of God’s messenger, very glorious. I asked where he was from, and he did not tell me his name.

Acts 7:1
The high priest said, “Are these things true?”
  Matthew 26:62–26:63 — high-priest questioning comparison: Both council narratives have the high priest question the accused after testimony. Matthew asks Jesus about the witnesses and then His identity, with silence reported; Acts asks Stephen whether the charges are true before his speech. No identical wording, priest identity or hearing is asserted.
    Matthew 26:62 The high priest rose and said to Him, “Dost Thou answer nothing? What is this that they testify against Thee?”
    Matthew 26:63 But Jesus remained silent. The high priest said to Him, “I adjure Thee by the living God to tell us whether Thou art the Christ, the Son of God.”

Acts 7:2
He answered, “Men, brothers and fathers, listen. The God of glory appeared to our father Abraham while he was in Mesopotamia, before he lived in Haran,
  Genesis 12:6–12:7 — Abraham-appearance comparison: Stephen locates God's appearance before Haran, in Mesopotamia. Genesis narrates an appearance near Shechem after Abram has entered Canaan. Both concern divine appearance to Abraham, but their stated settings and stages are not collapsed into the same appearance.
    Genesis 12:6 And Abram passed through the length of the land, to the place of Shechem, to the high oak. Now the Canaanites lived in the land at that time.
    Genesis 12:7 And the Lord appeared to Abram and said to him, “To your seed I will give this land.” And there Abram built an altar to the Lord, who had appeared to him.

Acts 7:3
and said to him, ‘Leave your land and your relatives, and come to the land I will show you.’
  Genesis 12:1 — quotation: Stephen's leave-land-and-relatives and shown-land command corresponds to the Lord's command to Abram. Genesis additionally names departure from the father's house. Its narrative placement and Stephen's preceding Mesopotamia notice are not silently rewritten.
    Genesis 12:1 And the Lord said to Abram, “Go out from your land, and from your kindred, and from your father’s house, to the land that I will show you.

Acts 7:4
Then he left the land of the Chaldeans and lived in Haran. From there, after his father died, God moved him to this land in which you now live.
  Genesis 11:31–12:5 — ancestral journey comparison: Genesis narrates Terah taking the family from the Chaldeans to Haran and records his death there; its following account describes Abram's departure to Canaan. Stephen explicitly says God moved Abraham after his father's death. The distinct agencies and chronological notices remain, without additional birth-order or age arithmetic.
    Genesis 11:31 And Terah took Abram his son, Lot the son of Haran, his grandson, and Sara his daughter-in-law, the wife of Abram his son. And he led them out of the country of the Chaldeans to go into the land of Canaan. But he came as far as Harran and settled there.
    Genesis 11:32 And the days of Terah in Harran were two hundred and five years, and Terah died in Harran.
    Genesis 12:1 And the Lord said to Abram, “Go out from your land, and from your kindred, and from your father’s house, to the land that I will show you.
    Genesis 12:2 And I will make you a great nation, and I will bless you and make your name great; and you shall be blessed.
    Genesis 12:3 And I will bless those who bless you, and those who curse you I will curse; and in you all the tribes of the earth shall be blessed.”
    Genesis 12:4 And Abram went as the Lord had spoken to him, and Lot went with him. Now Abram was seventy-five years old when he went out from Harran.
    Genesis 12:5 And Abram took Sara his wife, and Lot his brother’s son, and all the possessions they had acquired, and every soul they had acquired in Harran; and they set out for the land of Canaan, and came into the land of Canaan.

Acts 7:5
He gave him no inheritance in it, not even a footstep’s space, but promised to give it to him as a possession and to his seed after him, although he had no child.
  Genesis 15:2–15:7 — promise background: Genesis combines Abram's childlessness, a promised heir and seed, and God's giving this land to inherit. Stephen joins childlessness with promised possession and says no footstep's inheritance was given then. The target does not independently state that last measurement.
    Genesis 15:2 And Abram said, “Master, what wilt Thou give me? I depart childless; and the son of Masek, the slave woman born in my house, is this Damascus Eliezer.”
    Genesis 15:3 And Abram said, “Since Thou hast given me no seed, a slave born in my house shall inherit from me.”
    Genesis 15:4 And immediately the voice of the Lord came to him, saying, “This man shall not inherit from you; but the one who will come from you, he shall inherit from you.”
    Genesis 15:5 And He brought him outside and said to him, “Look now toward heaven, and count the stars, if you can count them.” And He said, “So shall your seed be.”
    Genesis 15:6 And Abram believed God, and it was reckoned to him as righteousness.
    Genesis 15:7 And He said to him, “I am God, who brought you out from the country of the Chaldeans, to give you this land to inherit.”

Acts 7:6
God spoke in this way: his seed would live as strangers in a foreign land, and they would enslave and mistreat them for four hundred years.
  Genesis 15:13 — quotation: Both predict Abraham's seed living as strangers and suffering enslavement and affliction for four hundred years. Stephen's compressed formulation and the saved Genesis wording are preserved, without substituting a different chronological total.
    Genesis 15:13 And it was said to Abram, “Know for certain that your seed shall live as strangers in a land not their own; and they shall enslave them, and afflict them, and humble them for four hundred years.

Acts 7:7
‘But I will judge the nation they serve as slaves,’ God said, ‘and after these things they will come out and worship Me in this place.’
  Genesis 15:14 — quotation correspondence: The judgment of the enslaving nation and the descendants' departure correspond to Genesis. Genesis adds much baggage; Stephen adds worship in this place. That worship clause is not attributed to this Genesis unit.
    Genesis 15:14 But I will judge the nation they serve; and afterward they shall come out here with much baggage.
  Exodus 3:12 — related worship promise: God's promise to Moses joins bringing the people from Egypt with serving God on this mountain; Stephen joins departure with worship in this place. This supplies a specific related promise while retaining mountain versus place and their different recipients and contexts.
    Exodus 3:12 And God said to Moses, “I will be with you; and this shall be the sign to you that I send you: when you have brought My people out of Egypt, you shall serve God upon this mountain.”

Acts 7:8
He gave him the covenant of circumcision. Thus Abraham fathered Isaac and circumcised him on the eighth day; Isaac fathered Jacob, and Jacob the twelve patriarchs.
  Genesis 21:3–21:4 — ancestral narrative reference: Both name Abraham fathering Isaac and circumcising him on the eighth day. Genesis names Sarah and obedience to God's command; Stephen situates this in the covenant and continues through Jacob and the patriarchs. The later genealogy is not independently supplied by these two target units.
    Genesis 21:3 And Abraham called the name of his son who was born to him, whom Sarah bore to him, Isaac.
    Genesis 21:4 And Abraham circumcised Isaac on the eighth day, as God had commanded him.

Acts 7:9
The patriarchs, jealous of Joseph, sold him into Egypt. But God was with him,
  Genesis 39:1–39:2 — Joseph narrative reference: Both place Joseph in Egypt after sale and affirm God's presence with him. Genesis names the Ishmaelites and Potiphar; Stephen stresses the patriarchs' jealousy and sale. Their different actor descriptions and emphases are kept rather than reduced to an identical sentence.
    Genesis 39:1 And Joseph was brought down into Egypt; and Potiphar, Pharaoh’s eunuch, the chief cook, an Egyptian man, bought him from the hands of the Ishmaelites who had brought him down there.
    Genesis 39:2 And the Lord was with Joseph, and he was a prosperous man; and he was in the house of his Egyptian master.

Acts 7:10
rescued him from all his afflictions, and gave him favor and wisdom before Pharaoh, king of Egypt, who appointed him ruler over Egypt and his whole household.
  Genesis 41:38–41:41 — Joseph promotion reference: Pharaoh recognizes Joseph's God-given discernment and places him over his house and Egypt. Stephen summarizes divine rescue, favor, wisdom and that promotion. Genesis supplies the specific appointment, not a separate complete account of every affliction mentioned in Stephen's summary.
    Genesis 41:38 And Pharaoh said to all his servants, “Shall we find such a man as this, who has the Spirit of God in him?”
    Genesis 41:39 And Pharaoh said to Joseph, “Since God has shown you all these things, there is no man more prudent and discerning than you.
    Genesis 41:40 “You shall be over my house, and all my people shall obey your mouth; only in the throne shall I be above you.”
    Genesis 41:41 And Pharaoh said to Joseph, “Behold, I set you today over all the land of Egypt.”

Acts 7:11
Famine came upon all Egypt and Canaan, with great affliction, and our fathers could find no provisions.
  Genesis 47:13 — famine narrative reference: Both explicitly describe famine affecting Egypt and Canaan with severe distress. Stephen adds the fathers' lack of provisions; Genesis describes the lands languishing after Jacob's arrival. This does not identify the exact stage of the famine or equate the family's provisions at each stage.
    Genesis 47:13 And there was no grain in all the land, for the famine had become exceedingly severe; and the land of Egypt and the land of Canaan languished because of the famine.

Acts 7:12
When Jacob heard that there was grain in Egypt, he sent our fathers there the first time.
  Genesis 42:1–42:3 — first grain-journey reference: Both describe Jacob hearing of Egyptian grain and sending sons to obtain it. Genesis specifies ten brothers, whereas Stephen says our fathers and marks the first journey. The earlier twelve-patriarch list is not substituted for Genesis's ten travelers.
    Genesis 42:1 And Jacob saw that there was grain for sale in Egypt, and said to his sons, “Why are you idle?
    Genesis 42:2 “Behold, I have heard that there is grain in Egypt. Go down there and buy us a little food, that we may live and not die.”
    Genesis 42:3 And Joseph’s ten brothers went down to buy grain from Egypt.

Acts 7:13
On the second visit Joseph made himself known to his brothers, and Joseph’s family became known to Pharaoh.
  Genesis 45:1–45:4 — Joseph recognition reference: Genesis narrates Joseph making himself known to his brothers and their troubled response; Stephen places recognition on the second visit and also mentions the family becoming known to Pharaoh. The target's hearing of weeping in Pharaoh's house is not treated as a verbatim account of that latter family notice.
    Genesis 45:1 And Joseph could no longer bear all those who stood beside him, but said, “Send everyone away from me.” And no one else stood with Joseph when he made himself known to his brothers.
    Genesis 45:2 And he lifted up his voice with weeping; and all the Egyptians heard, and it was heard in Pharaoh’s house.
    Genesis 45:3 And Joseph said to his brothers, “I am Joseph. Is my father still living?” And his brothers could not answer him, for they were troubled.
    Genesis 45:4 And Joseph said to his brothers, “Draw near to me.” And they drew near. And he said, “I am Joseph your brother, whom you sold into Egypt.

Acts 7:14
Joseph sent and summoned his father Jacob and all his relatives, seventy-five persons in all.
  Genesis 46:26–46:27 — ancestral count comparison: Stephen's summons mentions seventy-five persons; the supplied Greek-based Genesis totals Jacob's house entering Egypt at seventy-five, alongside sixty-six and nine in its breakdown. The stated scenes and groupings remain distinct; neither total is replaced with seventy or forced into an unstated individual roster.
    Genesis 46:26 And all the souls who entered into Egypt with Jacob, who came from his loins, apart from the wives of Jacob’s sons, were sixty-six souls in all.
    Genesis 46:27 And Joseph’s sons who were born to him in the land of Egypt were nine souls. All the souls of Jacob’s house who entered into Egypt were seventy-five.

Acts 7:15
Jacob went down to Egypt, and he and our fathers died.
  Genesis 49:33 — Jacob death reference: Stephen summarizes Jacob's descent and the deaths of Jacob and the fathers; Genesis narrates Jacob dying after his burial instructions. This specifically supports the Jacob-death component, not every ancestor's death or the entire migration chronology.
    Genesis 49:33 And Jacob ceased charging his sons; and he drew up his feet upon the bed, and died, and was added to his people.

Acts 7:16
They were brought over to Shechem and placed in the tomb Abraham had bought for a price in silver from the sons of Hamor in Shechem.
  Genesis 33:18–33:19 — Shechem purchase comparison with unresolved differences: Both mention a Shechem/Hamor purchase, but Stephen says Abraham bought a tomb from Hamor's sons for silver; Genesis names Jacob buying a field from Hamor, father of Shechem, for a hundred lambs. Buyer, object, seller description and price differ; no reconciliation or replacement is supplied.
    Genesis 33:18 And Jacob came to Salem, a city of Shechem, which is in the land of Canaan, when he came from Mesopotamia of Syria; and he camped before the city.
    Genesis 33:19 And he bought the portion of the field where he had pitched his tent from Hamor the father of Shechem for a hundred lambs.

Acts 7:17
As the time of the promise God had declared to Abraham drew near, the people grew and multiplied in Egypt,
  Exodus 1:7 — Israelite growth reference: Both describe Israel's growth and multiplication in Egypt. Stephen relates this to the approaching promise's time; Exodus emphasizes the people's great numerical strength. The target does not independently supply Stephen's particular promise-timing explanation.
    Exodus 1:7 And the sons of Israel increased and multiplied, and became numerous and exceedingly, exceedingly strong; and the land multiplied them.

Acts 7:18
until another king arose over Egypt, who did not know Joseph.
  Exodus 1:8 — quotation correspondence: Both introduce another Egyptian king who did not know Joseph. This is a direct narrative correspondence; no extra royal name, dynasty or date is inferred.
    Exodus 1:8 And another king arose over Egypt, who did not know Joseph.

Acts 7:19
He dealt craftily with our people and mistreated our fathers, forcing them to expose their infants so that they would not be kept alive.
  Exodus 1:22 — infant-killing policy reference: Stephen describes oppression forcing exposure of infants so they would not live. Exodus explicitly commands throwing Hebrew male children into the river while keeping females alive. The sex-specific command and method are retained rather than silently expanded into Stephen's more compressed formulation.
    Exodus 1:22 And Pharaoh commanded all his people, saying, “Cast every male child born to the Hebrews into the river, but keep every female child alive.”

Acts 7:20
At that time Moses was born, and he was beautiful before God. He was brought up for three months in his father’s house.
  Exodus 2:2 — Moses infancy reference: Both describe Moses' beauty and three months of concealment or upbringing. Stephen says beautiful before God and in his father's house; Exodus describes the child's beauty and states 'they hid him.' The evaluators, wording, and parental actions remain distinct.
    Exodus 2:2 She conceived and bore a male child. Seeing that he was beautiful, they hid him for three months.

Acts 7:21
When he was exposed, Pharaoh’s daughter took him up and brought him up as her own son.
  Exodus 2:5–2:10 — Moses rescue and adoption reference: Both describe Pharaoh's daughter receiving Moses and his becoming her son. Exodus gives the basket discovery, compassion, paid nursing by his mother and later presentation when he grew; Stephen compresses these stages into rescue and upbringing. The additional stages are not erased.
    Exodus 2:5 Pharaoh’s daughter came down to bathe at the river while her attendants walked beside it. She saw the basket in the marsh, sent her attendant, and had it brought to her.
    Exodus 2:6 And she opened it, and saw a child weeping in the basket; and Pharaoh’s daughter had compassion on him, and said, “This is one of the Hebrew children.”
    Exodus 2:7 And his sister said to Pharaoh’s daughter, “Do you want me to call a nursing woman from among the Hebrews, that she may nurse the child for you?”
    Exodus 2:8 Pharaoh’s daughter said to her, “Go.” The young woman went and called the child’s mother.
    Exodus 2:9 And Pharaoh’s daughter said to her, “Keep this child for me, and nurse him for me, and I will give you your wages.” And the woman took the child and nursed him.
    Exodus 2:10 When the child grew, she brought him to Pharaoh’s daughter, and he became her son. She named him Moses, saying, “I took him up out of the water.”

Acts 7:22
Moses was educated in all the wisdom of the Egyptians and was powerful in his words and deeds.
  Luke 24:19 — mighty-word-and-deed description comparison: Stephen describes Moses as powerful in words and deeds; the travelers describe Jesus as a prophet mighty in deed and word before God and the people. This is a bounded description comparison between different figures, not evidence that Jesus received Moses' Egyptian education.
    Luke 24:19 And He said to them, “What things?” They said to Him, “The things concerning Jesus the Nazarene, who was a prophet mighty in deed and word before God and all the people,

Acts 7:23
When he reached the age of forty, it came into his heart to visit his brothers, the sons of Israel.
  Exodus 2:11 — Moses visit reference: Both describe Moses going out to his Israelite brothers when grown. Stephen specifies forty years and his inward resolve; Exodus describes seeing their suffering and an Egyptian striking a Hebrew. Neither the age nor the inward explanation is inserted into Exodus.
    Exodus 2:11 During those many days, when Moses had grown, he went out to his brothers, the sons of Israel. Seeing their suffering, he saw an Egyptian man striking a Hebrew, one of his own brothers, the sons of Israel.

Acts 7:24
Seeing one of them being wronged, he defended him and avenged the oppressed man by striking down the Egyptian.
  Exodus 2:11–2:12 — Moses striking the Egyptian reference: Both narrate Moses seeing an Egyptian wronging a Hebrew and striking the Egyptian down. Exodus adds looking around and hiding him in sand; Stephen calls the act defense and vengeance for the oppressed man. Those evaluative and action details are not made identical.
    Exodus 2:11 During those many days, when Moses had grown, he went out to his brothers, the sons of Israel. Seeing their suffering, he saw an Egyptian man striking a Hebrew, one of his own brothers, the sons of Israel.
    Exodus 2:12 He looked this way and that, and, seeing no one, struck down the Egyptian and hid him in the sand.

Acts 7:25
He supposed his brothers understood that God was giving them deliverance through his hand, but they did not understand.
  Acts 7:35 — internal rejected-deliverer development: Stephen first reports Moses' supposition that his brothers understood deliverance through him, then later affirms God's sending of the very rejected Moses as ruler and redeemer. Presumption at the earlier encounter and later divine commission are distinct; the link does not supply divine approval of the Egyptian's killing.
    Acts 7:35 This Moses, whom they denied, saying, ‘Who appointed you ruler and judge?’—God has sent this very man as both ruler and redeemer, through the hand of the angel who appeared to him in the bush.

Acts 7:26
The next day he came upon them as they were fighting and tried to reconcile them in peace, saying, ‘Men, you are brothers. Why are you wronging one another?’
  Exodus 2:13 — Moses mediation reference: Both place Moses before fighting Hebrews on the next day. Exodus addresses the wrongdoer about striking his neighbor; Stephen presents a brotherhood appeal and attempted reconciliation. The formulations are related without assigning equal guilt to both men.
    Exodus 2:13 And he went out on the second day, and saw two Hebrew men fighting; and he said to the one who did wrong, “Why do you strike your neighbour?”

Acts 7:27
But the one wronging his neighbor pushed him away and said, ‘Who appointed you ruler and judge over us?
  Exodus 2:14 — quotation correspondence: The who-made-you-ruler-and-judge challenge corresponds to Exodus. Stephen additionally narrates the wrongdoer pushing Moses away; that physical action is not inserted into the target's spoken objection.
    Exodus 2:14 But he said, “Who made you a ruler and judge over us? Do you want to kill me, as you killed the Egyptian yesterday?” And Moses was afraid, and said, “Has this matter become so plainly known?”

Acts 7:28
Do you want to kill me as you killed the Egyptian yesterday?’
  Exodus 2:14 — quotation: Both report the objection asking whether Moses would kill the speaker as he killed the Egyptian yesterday. This is the reported question, not a prediction that Moses intended another killing.
    Exodus 2:14 But he said, “Who made you a ruler and judge over us? Do you want to kill me, as you killed the Egyptian yesterday?” And Moses was afraid, and said, “Has this matter become so plainly known?”

Acts 7:29
At this saying Moses fled and became a stranger in the land of Midian, where he fathered two sons.
  Exodus 2:15–2:22 — Moses flight and settlement reference: Both describe flight to Midian, settlement and fatherhood. Exodus adds Pharaoh's death threat, the well encounter, marriage and Gershom's birth; Stephen attributes flight to the saying and mentions two sons. This target names one son's birth rather than independently narrating both.
    Exodus 2:15 Pharaoh heard about this and sought to kill Moses. Moses withdrew from Pharaoh’s presence and settled in the land of Midian. Having come to the land of Midian, he sat down by the well.
    Exodus 2:16 The priest of Midian had seven daughters who tended the sheep of their father Jethro. They came and drew water until they filled the troughs to water the sheep of their father Jethro.
    Exodus 2:17 The shepherds came and drove them away, but Moses rose and rescued them, drew water for them, and watered their sheep.
    Exodus 2:18 And they came to Reuel their father, and he said to them, “Why have you returned so quickly today?”
    Exodus 2:19 They said, “An Egyptian man rescued us from the shepherds. He drew water for us and watered our sheep.”
    Exodus 2:20 And he said to his daughters, “And where is he? Why have you left the man behind? Call him, therefore, that he may eat bread.”
    Exodus 2:21 Moses settled with the man, who gave him his daughter Zipporah as his wife.
    Exodus 2:22 And the woman conceived and bore a son; and Moses called his name Gershom, saying, “I am a sojourner in a foreign land.”
  Exodus 18:2–18:4 — Moses two-sons reference: The later Exodus notice explicitly names Moses' wife and two sons, Gershom and Eliezer, whose names recall sojourning and deliverance from Pharaoh. This supplies the two-son counterpart without independently dating both births to Stephen's Midian interval.
    Exodus 18:2 Jethro, Moses’ father-in-law, took Zipporah, Moses’ wife, after she had been sent away,
    Exodus 18:3 and his two sons. One of them was named Gershom, for Moses had said, “I was a sojourner in a foreign land.”
    Exodus 18:4 The second was named Eliezer, for he had said, “My father’s God was my helper and delivered me from Pharaoh’s hand.”

Acts 7:30
When forty years had passed, an angel appeared to him in the wilderness of Mount Sinai, in the flame of a burning bush.
  Exodus 3:1–3:2 — burning-bush reference: Both describe an angel appearing in a fiery bush to Moses in the wilderness setting. Exodus names Horeb and his shepherding; Stephen names Sinai and a forty-year interval. The names, chronology and angel description are preserved without adding an elapsed period to Exodus.
    Exodus 3:1 Moses was tending the sheep of Jethro his father-in-law, the priest of Midian. He led the sheep toward the wilderness and came to Mount Horeb.
    Exodus 3:2 An angel of the Lord appeared to him in a flame of fire from the bush. He saw that the bush was burning with fire, yet it was not consumed.

Acts 7:31
When Moses saw it, he marveled at the sight. As he approached to examine it, the Lord’s voice came:
  Exodus 3:3–3:4 — burning-bush approach reference: Both describe Moses approaching to examine the remarkable bush and the Lord's voice addressing him. Exodus quotes the repeated Moses call; Stephen describes marveling and then introduces the following divine words. The formulations are not treated as a single identical quotation.
    Exodus 3:3 And Moses said, “I will turn aside and see this great sight, why the bush is not consumed.”
    Exodus 3:4 When the Lord saw that he was approaching to look, the Lord called to him from the bush, “Moses, Moses!” He replied, “What is it?”

Acts 7:32
‘I am the God of your fathers, the God of Abraham and Isaac and Jacob.’ Moses trembled and did not dare to look closely.
  Exodus 3:6 — quotation correspondence: Both identify the speaker as the God of Abraham, Isaac and Jacob and describe Moses' fear of looking. Stephen says he trembled and did not dare look closely; Exodus says he turned his face away. These responses are kept as written.
    Exodus 3:6 And He said to him, “I am the God of your father, the God of Abraham, and the God of Isaac, and the God of Jacob.” And Moses turned his face away, for he feared to look before God.

Acts 7:33
The Lord said to him, ‘Remove the sandals from your feet, for the place on which you stand is holy ground.
  Exodus 3:5 — quotation: Both command removal of footwear because the standing place is holy ground. Exodus additionally says not to approach nearer; Stephen's shorter citation does not reproduce that prohibition.
    Exodus 3:5 And He said, “Do not come near here. Untie the footwear from your feet; for the place where you stand is holy ground.”

Acts 7:34
I have indeed seen the mistreatment of My people in Egypt and heard their groaning, and I have come down to rescue them. Now come, let Me send you to Egypt.’
  Exodus 3:7–3:10 — compressed quotation and commission: Both join God's seeing Egypt's affliction, hearing suffering, coming down to rescue and sending Moses. Exodus says cry and explicitly sends him to Pharaoh; Stephen says groaning and Egypt. Its compressed citation does not replace those distinct expressions.
    Exodus 3:7 And the Lord said to Moses, “I have surely seen the affliction of My people in Egypt, and have heard their cry because of their taskmasters; for I know their pain.
    Exodus 3:8 I have come down to rescue them from the hand of the Egyptians and bring them out of that land into a good and spacious land, a land flowing with milk and honey, to the place of the Canaanites, Hittites, Amorites, Perizzites, Girgashites, Hivites, and Jebusites.
    Exodus 3:9 And now, behold, the cry of the sons of Israel has come to Me; and I have seen the oppression with which the Egyptians oppress them.
    Exodus 3:10 Come now, and I will send you to Pharaoh king of Egypt; and you shall bring My people, the sons of Israel, out of the land of Egypt.”

Acts 7:35
This Moses, whom they denied, saying, ‘Who appointed you ruler and judge?’—God has sent this very man as both ruler and redeemer, through the hand of the angel who appeared to him in the bush.
  Acts 7:27 — internal quoted objection callback: Stephen explicitly repeats the earlier who-appointed-you-ruler-and-judge rejection while asserting God's subsequent sending of that same Moses. The earlier human objection and the later divine commissioning are opposed within one speech, not independent records.
    Acts 7:27 But the one wronging his neighbor pushed him away and said, ‘Who appointed you ruler and judge over us?

Acts 7:36
He led them out, doing wonders and signs in the land of Egypt, in the Red Sea, and in the wilderness for forty years.
  Exodus 14:29–14:31 — sea-deliverance reference: Stephen's summary includes the sea deliverance; Exodus describes Israel crossing on dry ground, rescue from the Egyptians and trust in God and Moses. This concretely illustrates the sea component, without making the target itself narrate all Egyptian signs or the forty wilderness years.
    Exodus 14:29 But the sons of Israel walked on dry ground in the midst of the sea, and the water was a wall for them on the right and a wall on the left.
    Exodus 14:30 The Lord rescued Israel that day from the Egyptians’ hand, and Israel saw the Egyptians dead on the seashore.
    Exodus 14:31 Israel saw the great hand in what the Lord had done to the Egyptians. The people feared the Lord and trusted God and His attendant Moses.

Acts 7:37
This is the Moses who said to the sons of Israel, ‘God will raise up for you a prophet like me from among your brothers.’
  Deuteronomy 18:15 — quotation: Stephen's promised prophet like Moses from the brothers corresponds to Moses' promise in Deuteronomy. The target also commands hearing him; it does not itself name Jesus in this unit.
    Deuteronomy 18:15 The Lord your God shall raise up unto you a prophet from among your brethren, like unto me; you shall hearken unto him,

Acts 7:38
He is the one who was in the assembly in the wilderness with the angel who spoke to him on Mount Sinai and with our fathers. He received living oracles to give to us,
  Deuteronomy 4:12–4:14 — lawgiving comparison: Both concern the wilderness reception and transmission of God's instruction through Moses. Deuteronomy describes the Lord's fire-voice, covenant and teaching commands; Stephen names an angel, Sinai's assembly and living oracles. The angel and Lord formulations are not silently made identical.
    Deuteronomy 4:12 And the Lord spoke unto you out of the midst of the fire; you heard the sound of words, but saw no likeness, only a voice.
    Deuteronomy 4:13 And He declared unto you His covenant, which He commanded you to do, even the ten words; and He wrote them upon two tablets of stone.
    Deuteronomy 4:14 And the Lord commanded me at that time to teach you ordinances and judgments, that you should do them in the land whither you go to inherit it.

Acts 7:39
but our fathers were unwilling to obey him. They pushed him away and turned in their hearts toward Egypt,
