  Numbers 14:2–14:4 — return-to-Egypt rebellion comparison: Stephen describes refusal of Moses and hearts turning toward Egypt in his calf account; Numbers records a later complaint and proposal to appoint a leader and return to Egypt. This compares rejection and Egyptian nostalgia, without identifying the occasions or spoken proposals.
    Numbers 14:2 And all the sons of Israel grumbled against Moses and Aaron. The whole congregation said to them: If only we had died in the land of Egypt! Or if only we had died in this wilderness!
    Numbers 14:3 And why is the Lord bringing us into this land to fall in war? Our wives and children will become plunder. So now it is better for us to return to Egypt.
    Numbers 14:4 And they said to one another, Let us appoint a leader and return to Egypt.

Acts 7:40
saying to Aaron, ‘Make us gods who will go before us. As for this Moses who led us out of the land of Egypt, we do not know what has happened to him.’
  Exodus 32:1 — quotation: Both ask Aaron for gods to go before them and profess ignorance of what happened to Moses who led them out of Egypt. Exodus explicitly introduces Moses' delay; the quoted objection remains the people's speech.
    Exodus 32:1 When the people saw that Moses delayed coming down from the mountain, they gathered against Aaron and said, “Arise, and make us gods to go before us. As for this Moses, the man who led us out of Egypt, we do not know what has happened to him.”

Acts 7:41
They made a calf in those days, offered sacrifice to the idol, and rejoiced in the works of their hands.
  Exodus 32:4–32:6 — calf-worship reference: Both describe the made calf, sacrifice and celebration. Exodus narrates Aaron's making, altar, offerings, eating and play; Stephen stresses an idol and rejoicing in their handwork. The descriptions are compared without endorsing the calf's divine claim.
    Exodus 32:4 He received them from their hands, fashioned them with an engraving tool, and made them into a cast calf. He said, “These are your gods, Israel, who brought you up from the land of Egypt.”
    Exodus 32:5 When Aaron saw it, he built an altar opposite it and proclaimed, “Tomorrow is a feast of the Lord.”
    Exodus 32:6 He rose early the next day, offered whole burnt offerings, and brought a sacrifice of deliverance. The people sat down to eat and drink and rose up to play.

Acts 7:42
But God turned away and handed them over to worship the host of heaven, as it is written in the book of the prophets: ‘Did you offer Me slaughtered victims and sacrifices for forty years in the wilderness, house of Israel?
  Amos 5:25 — quotation: Stephen's forty-year wilderness sacrifice question corresponds to Amos' question addressed to Israel. Stephen's preceding interpretation about God handing people over to heaven's host is not presented as wording contained in this single Amos unit.
    Amos 5:25 Did you offer Me slaughtered offerings and sacrifices in the wilderness for forty years, house of Israel?

Acts 7:43
You took up the tent of Moloch and the star of your god Raiphan, the images you made to worship them. And I will deport you beyond Babylon.’
  Amos 5:26–5:27 — quotation with named-location difference: Both mention Moloch's tent, Raiphan's star, made images and deportation. The supplied Amos says beyond Damascus; Acts says beyond Babylon. Those different destination names remain explicit rather than being silently harmonized.
    Amos 5:26 You took up the tent of Moloch and the star of your god Raiphan, their figures that you made for yourselves.
    Amos 5:27 I will deport you beyond Damascus,” says the Lord; God Almighty is His name.

Acts 7:44
Our fathers had the Tent of Testimony in the wilderness, as the One who spoke to Moses directed him to make it according to the pattern he had seen.
  Exodus 25:8–25:9 — tabernacle-pattern reference: Both connect the sanctuary or tabernacle's making with a revealed pattern shown to Moses on the mountain. Exodus also names vessel patterns and God's appearing among the people; Stephen emphasizes the wilderness Tent of Testimony. Their additional details remain distinct.
    Exodus 25:8 And you shall make Me a sanctuary, and I will appear among you.
    Exodus 25:9 And you shall make it for Me according to all that I show you upon the mountain, the pattern of the tabernacle, and the pattern of all its vessels; this is how you shall make it.

Acts 7:45
Our fathers in their turn received it and brought it in with Joshua when they took possession of the nations’ land, whose people God drove out before our fathers. It remained until the days of David,
  Joshua 18:1 — tabernacle-in-conquered-land reference: Stephen describes the inherited Tent entering the occupied land with Joshua; Joshua describes the congregation pitching the Tabernacle of Testimony at Shiloh while the land was under their control. The target does not independently supply the entry sequence or Stephen's until-David limit.
    Joshua 18:1 The whole congregation of the sons of Israel assembled at Shiloh and pitched the Tabernacle of Testimony there. The land was held under their control.

Acts 7:46
who found favor before God and asked to find a dwelling for the house of Jacob.
  Psalms 131:1–131:5 — David dwelling-seeking comparison: The psalm recalls David's vow to find the Lord a place and a dwelling for the God of Jacob. Stephen speaks of David's favor and seeking a dwelling for the house of Jacob. The house-of-Jacob and God-of-Jacob expressions, and request versus vow, remain distinct.
    Psalms 131:1 A song of the ascents. Remember David, Lord, and all his gentleness,
    Psalms 131:2 how he swore to the Lord, vowed to the God of Jacob:
    Psalms 131:3 “I will not enter the dwelling of my house, nor climb onto my bed’s couch;
    Psalms 131:4 I will not give sleep to my eyes, slumber to my eyelids, or rest to my temples,
    Psalms 131:5 until I find a place for the Lord, a dwelling for the God of Jacob.”

Acts 7:47
But Solomon built Him a house.
  1 Kings 6:2 — Solomon temple-building reference: Both explicitly attribute building the Lord's house to Solomon. Kings supplies its dimensions; Stephen gives a short ancestral summary. No familiar alternative measurements are substituted for the saved target.
    1 Kings 6:2 The house that King Solomon built for the Lord was forty cubits long, twenty cubits wide, and twenty-five cubits high.

Acts 7:48
Yet the Most High does not dwell in things made by hands, as the prophet says:
  Acts 17:24 — internal divine-dwelling recurrence: Stephen says the Most High does not dwell in things made by hands; Paul's later speech says the world-making God does not dwell in sanctuaries made by hands. The parallel is a specific divine-dwelling claim in different addresses, not identification of their audiences or every worship practice.
    Acts 17:24 The God who made the world and everything in it, being Lord of heaven and earth, does not dwell in sanctuaries made by hands.

Acts 7:49
‘Heaven is My throne, and the earth the footstool of My feet. What kind of house will you build for Me, says the Lord, or what place is there for My rest?
  Isaiah 66:1 — quotation: Both declare heaven God's throne and earth His footstool, then question what house or resting place people can build. The speaker and creation-and-house imagery correspond directly.
    Isaiah 66:1 Thus says the Lord: “Heaven is My throne, and earth the footstool of My feet. What sort of house will you build for Me, or what place is My rest?

Acts 7:50
Did not My hand make all these things?’
  Isaiah 66:2 — quotation correspondence: Stephen's question about God's hand making all these things corresponds to Isaiah's declaration that His hand made them. Isaiah continues with their belonging to Him and regard for humble hearers; those additions are not inserted into Stephen's shorter question.
    Isaiah 66:2 For My hand made all these things, and all these things are Mine,” says the Lord. “And to whom will I look except the humble and quiet one who trembles at My words?

Acts 7:51
You stiff-necked people, uncircumcised in hearts and ears! You always resist the Holy Spirit; as your fathers did, so do you.
  Deuteronomy 10:16 — paired moral-image correspondence: Stephen calls his hearers stiff-necked and uncircumcised in hearts and ears; Deuteronomy commands circumcising heart-hardness and ceasing to stiffen the neck. This is a specific paired moral image, not a claim about physical circumcision or every Jewish person.
    Deuteronomy 10:16 And you shall circumcise your hardness of heart, and shall stiffen your neck no more.
  Isaiah 63:10 — Spirit-resistance comparison: Stephen charges his hearers with resisting the Holy Spirit as their fathers did; Isaiah recalls ancestors disobeying and provoking God's holy spirit. The ancestral-disobedience correspondence does not make every episode or consequence identical.
    Isaiah 63:10 But they disobeyed and provoked His holy spirit. He turned toward hostility against them and Himself fought them.

Acts 7:52
Which of the prophets did your fathers not persecute? They killed those who announced beforehand the coming of the Righteous One, whose betrayers and murderers you have now become—
  Luke 11:47–11:49 — prophet-persecution comparison: Jesus' address connects the fathers' killing prophets with later persecution of God's messengers; Stephen charges his hearers with ancestral persecution and betrayal and murder of the Righteous One. The addressed groups, stages and titles remain distinct, without extending the accusation to an ethnicity.
    Luke 11:47 Woe to you, because you build the tombs of the prophets, but your fathers killed them!
    Luke 11:48 So you are witnesses and approve the deeds of your fathers: they killed them, and you build.
    Luke 11:49 For this reason the wisdom of God also said, ‘I will send them prophets and apostles, and some of these they will kill and persecute,’

Acts 7:53
you who received the law through the ordinances of angels and did not keep it.”
  Galatians 3:19 — angel-mediated law comparison: Both explicitly associate the law with angelic mediation. Stephen charges his hearers with not keeping it; Galatians discusses transgressions, the promised seed and a mediator. The different arguments and singular/plural angel descriptions in Stephen's wider speech are not collapsed.
    Galatians 3:19 Why, then, the law? It was added because of transgressions, until the seed should come to whom the promise had been made, and it was ordained through angels by the hand of a mediator.

Acts 7:54
When they heard these things, they were cut to the heart with rage and ground their teeth at him.
  Acts 5:33 — internal council-response recurrence: Both council hearings produce an enraged reaction to testimony and deadly hostility. Acts 7 adds teeth-grinding toward Stephen; Acts 5 reports the desire to kill the apostles. These are separate hearings and different reported expressions of rage.
    Acts 5:33 When they heard this, they were enraged and wanted to kill them.

Acts 7:55
But he, full of the Holy Spirit, gazed into heaven and saw the glory of God and Jesus standing at God’s right hand.
  Luke 22:69 — exaltation promise and vision comparison: Stephen sees God's glory and Jesus standing at God's right hand while full of the Holy Spirit; Jesus' earlier saying places the Son of Man seated at the right hand of God's power. The identity-and-position correspondence preserves standing versus seated and vision versus promise.
    Luke 22:69 But from now on the Son of Man will be seated at the right hand of the power of God.”

Acts 7:56
He said, “Look, I see the heavens opened and the Son of Man standing at God’s right hand.”
  Luke 22:69 — Son-of-Man right-hand comparison: Both explicitly place the Son of Man at God's right hand. Stephen reports opened heavens and standing; Jesus predicts being seated from now on. The posture, wording and speakers are not treated as identical.
    Luke 22:69 But from now on the Son of Man will be seated at the right hand of the power of God.”

Acts 7:57
They cried out with a loud voice, stopped their ears, and rushed upon him together.
  Acts 7:51 — internal ear-image and reaction comparison: Stephen's hearers physically stop their ears after his charge of uncircumcised ears and resisting the Spirit. This links a specific earlier moral image with the narrated reaction, while keeping metaphor, physical action and the crowd's unreported private motives distinct.
    Acts 7:51 You stiff-necked people, uncircumcised in hearts and ears! You always resist the Holy Spirit; as your fathers did, so do you.

Acts 7:58
They drove him outside the city and began stoning him. The witnesses laid their garments at the feet of a young man called Saul.
  Acts 22:20 — internal explicit retrospective: Paul later recalls Stephen's bloodshed, his own approval and guarding the killers' garments, corresponding to the Saul-and-garments notice. The later statement does not separately repeat every city-exit or stoning detail, and is a retrospect within Acts.
    Acts 22:20 And when the blood of Stephen, Thy witness, was being poured out, I myself stood by, approving and guarding the garments of those who were killing him.’

Acts 7:59
They stoned Stephen as he called out, saying, “Lord Jesus, receive my spirit.”
  Luke 23:46 — dying-prayer comparison: Both dying speakers entrust their spirit in prayer. Stephen addresses Lord Jesus and asks Him to receive it; Jesus addresses the Father and entrusts His spirit into His hands. The addressees and exact petitions are kept distinct.
    Luke 23:46 And Jesus cried out with a loud voice and said, “Father, into Thy hands I entrust My spirit.” Having said this, He breathed His last.

Acts 7:60
Kneeling down, he cried with a loud voice, “Lord, do not hold this sin against them.” After saying this, he fell asleep.
  Luke 23:34 — prayer-for-persecutors comparison: Stephen asks the Lord not to hold this sin against them; Jesus asks the Father to forgive them because they do not know what they do. The mercy petitions are related, but Stephen's kneeling and falling asleep are not inserted into Jesus' crucifixion prayer.
    Luke 23:34 But Jesus said, “Father, forgive them, for they do not know what they are doing.” And dividing His garments, they cast lots.

Acts 8:1
Saul approved of his killing. That day a great persecution arose against the Church in Jerusalem, and all except the apostles were scattered through the regions of Judea and Samaria.
  Acts 22:20 — internal explicit retrospective: Paul later recalls standing by and approving Stephen's bloodshed while guarding the killers' garments. This corresponds specifically to Saul's approval here; the later recollection does not independently repeat the whole Church-dispersion notice or the apostles' exception.
    Acts 22:20 And when the blood of Stephen, Thy witness, was being poured out, I myself stood by, approving and guarding the garments of those who were killing him.’

Acts 8:2
Devout men buried Stephen and made great lamentation over him.
  Genesis 50:10–50:13 — burial-and-lament comparison: Devout men bury Stephen with great lamentation; Jacob's burial narrative likewise combines burial with great mourning. The mourners and narrative settings differ; Genesis gives seven days of mourning, while Acts states no duration here. This is a funerary-action comparison, not an identification of either burial company or rite.
    Genesis 50:10 And they came to the threshing floor of Atad, which is beyond the Jordan; and they lamented him with a great and exceedingly grievous lamentation, and he mourned his father seven days.
    Genesis 50:11 And the inhabitants of the land of Canaan saw the mourning at the threshing floor of Atad, and said, “This is a great mourning for the Egyptians.” Therefore its name was called Mourning of Egypt, which is beyond the Jordan.
    Genesis 50:12 And his sons did thus to him, and buried him there.
    Genesis 50:13 And his sons carried him up into the land of Canaan, and buried him in the double cave, the cave which Abraham bought for a burial possession from Ephron the Hittite, opposite Mamre.

Acts 8:3
But Saul was ravaging the Church, entering house after house, dragging away both men and women, and handing them over to prison.
  Galatians 1:13 — persecution retrospective comparison: The letter recalls ravaging the Church of God and persecution beyond measure, corresponding to Saul's church-ravaging activity. Acts additionally narrates entering houses and imprisoning men and women. The retrospective does not independently describe every house raid.
    Galatians 1:13 For you have heard of my former conduct in Judaism, how I persecuted the Church of God beyond measure and ravaged it,

Acts 8:4
Those who were scattered went about proclaiming the word as good news.
  Acts 11:19 — internal dispersion callback: The later notice explicitly traces dispersed word-proclaimers to the affliction over Stephen, extending travel to Phoenicia, Cyprus and Antioch. It recalls the same dispersion-and-proclamation development without making its later destinations identical to the earlier regional summary.
    Acts 11:19 Those who had been scattered by the affliction that arose over Stephen traveled as far as Phoenicia, Cyprus, and Antioch, speaking the word to no one except Jews alone.

Acts 8:5
Philip went down to the city of Samaria and proclaimed the Christ to them.
  Acts 1:8 — mission-geography correspondence: Samaria is explicitly included in Jesus' announced witness geography; Philip's proclamation of the Christ in Samaria fits that geographical extension. This does not place Philip among the particular original hearers of the promise or make every detail of his mission explicit there.
    Acts 1:8 But you will receive power when the Holy Spirit comes upon you, and you will be My witnesses in Jerusalem and [in] all Judea and Samaria, and to the end of the earth.”

Acts 8:6
The crowds paid attention together to what Philip said, as they heard and saw the signs he was doing.
  Acts 14:3–14:4 — word-and-signs response comparison: Philip's speech and visible signs attract concerted attention; the later mission likewise links the Lord's gracious word with granted signs, but its city is divided. The common word/sign association does not imply identical audiences or a universally uniform response.
    Acts 14:3 So they stayed a considerable time, speaking boldly in reliance on the Lord, who bore witness to the word of His grace by granting signs and wonders to take place through their hands.
    Acts 14:4 The people of the city were divided: some sided with the Jews and others with the apostles.

Acts 8:7
For unclean spirits came out of many who had them, crying with a loud voice, and many who were paralyzed or unable to walk were healed.
  Mark 1:25–1:26 — unclean-spirit departure comparison: Both narrate unclean spirits coming out with loud cries. Mark concerns one man at Jesus' command; Acts concerns many during Philip's ministry and also other healings. The agents, numbers, bodily conditions and additional actions are not merged.
    Mark 1:25 Jesus rebuked him, saying, “Be muzzled, and come out of him!”
    Mark 1:26 The unclean spirit convulsed him, cried out with a loud voice, and came out of him.

Acts 8:8
There was great joy in that city.
  Acts 13:48 — mission-joy comparison: Both describe joy in connection with the gospel's reception. Samaria's city rejoices after the narrated signs and healings; the later Gentile hearers rejoice and glorify the word. Acts 8 does not thereby declare that every city resident shares every belief statement of the later group.
    Acts 13:48 When the Gentiles heard this, they rejoiced and glorified the word of the Lord, and as many as had been appointed to eternal life believed.

Acts 8:9
A man named Simon had previously practiced magic in the city and astonished the people of Samaria, claiming that he himself was someone great.
  Acts 13:6–13:8 — magician portraits comparison: Both narratives introduce a named magician in a setting reached by Christian proclamation. Simon's self-importance and Samarian influence differ from Bar-Jesus/Elymas' proconsular association and active opposition to faith. These are different figures, not independent attestations of Simon's particular claims.
    Acts 13:6 They traveled through the whole island as far as Paphos and found a certain magician, a Jewish false prophet named Bar-Jesus,
    Acts 13:7 who was with the proconsul Sergius Paulus, an intelligent man. The proconsul summoned Barnabas and Saul and sought to hear the word of God.
    Acts 13:8 But Elymas the magician—for that is how his name is translated—opposed them, seeking to turn the proconsul away from the faith.

Acts 8:10
Everyone, from small to great, paid attention to him, saying, “This man is the Power of God that is called Great.”
  Acts 14:11–14:13 — human-directed divine-status claims comparison: Both report audiences assigning extraordinary God-associated status to people. Simon receives the Power-of-God title; the Lycaonians identify Barnabas and Paul with gods and prepare sacrifice. These are reported claims with different wording and actions, not identical theological beliefs endorsed by the narrator.
    Acts 14:11 When the crowds saw what Paul had done, they raised their voice, saying in Lycaonian, “The gods have come down to us in human likeness.”
    Acts 14:12 They called Barnabas Zeus and Paul Hermes, because he was the leading speaker.
    Acts 14:13 The priest of Zeus, whose shrine was before the city, brought bulls and garlands to the gates and wanted to offer sacrifice with the crowds.

Acts 8:11
They paid attention to him because he had astonished them with his magical practices for a long time.
  Acts 8:13 — internal astonishment-role reversal: Simon formerly astonished the people through magic; later Simon himself is astonished while seeing signs and great works after staying close to Philip. This is an internal reversal of who is astonished. The long duration belongs only to the earlier magic, and the later belief-and-baptism sequence precedes the stated astonishment; the sources of astonishment and any final assessment of his faith are not equated.
    Acts 8:13 Simon himself also believed, and after being baptized he stayed close to Philip. Seeing signs and great works of power taking place, he was astonished.

Acts 8:12
But when they believed Philip as he proclaimed the good news about the kingdom of God and the name of Jesus Christ, both men and women were baptized.
  Acts 18:8 — hearing-belief-baptism pattern: Both describe hearing the Christian message, believing and baptism. Acts 8 names Philip's kingdom-and-Jesus proclamation and men and women; Acts 18 names Crispus, his household and many Corinthians. The stated groups, ministers and demographic details are not transferred.
    Acts 18:8 Crispus, the synagogue ruler, believed in the Lord with his whole household. Many of the Corinthians who heard believed and were baptized.

Acts 8:13
Simon himself also believed, and after being baptized he stayed close to Philip. Seeing signs and great works of power taking place, he was astonished.
  John 2:23–2:25 — sign-associated belief comparison: John explicitly describes people believing while seeing signs and then says Jesus did not entrust Himself to them. Acts states Simon's belief and baptism, then describes his astonishment at signs and great works. The sequence and stated reactions differ; neither account supplies an additional verdict here about the other group's faith.
    John 2:23 While He was in Jerusalem at the Passover, during the feast, many believed in His name, seeing the signs He was doing.
    John 2:24 But Jesus Himself did not entrust Himself to them, because He knew everyone
    John 2:25 and because He had no need for anyone to testify concerning humanity, for He Himself knew what was within a person.

Acts 8:14
When the apostles in Jerusalem heard that Samaria had received the word of God, they sent Peter and John to them.
  Acts 11:22 — Jerusalem delegation pattern: Both accounts describe Jerusalem hearing of reception in another mission region and sending representatives. The apostles send Peter and John toward Samaria; the Church sends Barnabas as far as Antioch. The sending bodies, destinations and delegates remain distinct.
    Acts 11:22 The report about them reached the ears of the Church in Jerusalem, and they sent Barnabas to travel as far as Antioch.

Acts 8:15
They went down and prayed for them to receive the Holy Spirit,
  Luke 11:13 — prayer-for-the-Spirit correspondence: Peter and John pray that others receive the Holy Spirit; Jesus teaches that the Father gives the Holy Spirit to those who ask Him. The promise and this intercession are related without making their speakers, recipients or narrated timing identical.
    Luke 11:13 So if you, being evil, know how to give good gifts to your children, how much more will the Father from heaven give the Holy Spirit to those who ask Him!”

Acts 8:16
for He had not yet fallen upon any of them; they had only been baptized into the name of the Lord Jesus.
  Acts 10:44–10:48 — baptism-and-Spirit sequence contrast: Samaria is already baptized into the Lord Jesus' name before the Spirit has fallen upon them. Cornelius' hearers receive the Spirit before Peter orders baptism in Jesus Christ's name. These explicitly different sequences are preserved rather than turned into one universal order.
    Acts 10:44 While Peter was still speaking these words, the Holy Spirit fell upon all who were hearing the message.
    Acts 10:45 The believers from the circumcision who had come with Peter were astonished that the gift of the Holy Spirit had been poured out even upon the Gentiles,
    Acts 10:46 for they heard them speaking in tongues and magnifying God. Then Peter said,
    Acts 10:47 “Can anyone withhold the water so that these people, who have received the Holy Spirit just as we have, should not be baptized?”
    Acts 10:48 He ordered them to be baptized in the name of Jesus Christ. Then they asked him to stay for some days.

Acts 8:17
Then they laid their hands upon them, and they received the Holy Spirit.
  Acts 19:5–19:6 — hands-and-Spirit reception comparison: Both associate laying on hands with the Holy Spirit's reception or coming. Acts 8 names the visiting apostles; Acts 19 names Paul after baptism into the Lord Jesus' name and adds tongues and prophecy. Those extra manifestations are not inserted into Acts 8.
    Acts 19:5 When they heard this, they were baptized into the name of the Lord Jesus.
    Acts 19:6 When Paul laid his hands upon them, the Holy Spirit came upon them, and they spoke in tongues and prophesied.

Acts 8:18
When Simon saw that the Spirit was given through the laying on of the apostles’ hands, he offered them money,
  Acts 8:20 — internal offer-and-rebuke connection: Simon's money offer receives Peter's rebuke for thinking God's gift could be acquired with money. This identifies the stated meaning and rejection of the offer in the same exchange, not an independently attested transaction or a successful purchase.
    Acts 8:20 But Peter said to him, “May your silver perish with you, because you thought you could acquire the gift of God with money!

Acts 8:19
saying, “Give me this authority too, so that anyone upon whom I lay my hands may receive the Holy Spirit.”
  Acts 8:17 — internal request antecedent: Simon asks for authority to impart the Spirit through his hands, the action he has just observed when the apostles lay hands and people receive the Spirit. The preceding event supplies his request's concrete referent; it does not grant him that authority.
    Acts 8:17 Then they laid their hands upon them, and they received the Holy Spirit.

Acts 8:20
But Peter said to him, “May your silver perish with you, because you thought you could acquire the gift of God with money!
  Matthew 10:8 — gift-versus-commercialization comparison: Peter rebukes treating God's gift as purchasable; Jesus directs freely received healing and liberating ministry to be freely given. The gift types and immediate instructions differ, and the comparison does not supply a completed sale or every rule about material ministry support.
    Matthew 10:8 Heal the sick, raise the dead, cleanse lepers, cast out demons. Freely you received; freely give.

Acts 8:21
You have neither part nor allotted share in this matter, for your heart is not upright before God.
  Psalms 77:37 — heart-uprightness correspondence: Peter says Simon's heart is not upright before God; the psalm describes a heart not upright with Him and covenant unfaithfulness. The shared moral description does not identify Simon with every person or event in the psalm's ancestral account.
    Psalms 77:37 but their heart was not upright with Him, nor were they faithful in His covenant.

Acts 8:22
Repent, therefore, of this wickedness of yours, and plead with the Lord that perhaps the intention of your heart may be forgiven you.
  Isaiah 55:6–55:7 — repentance-and-forgiveness comparison: Both call for turning from wicked ways or counsels toward God and forgiveness. Peter specifically urges repentance and prayer concerning the heart's intention, with perhaps; Isaiah calls people to God and promises mercy upon return. The different formulations do not narrate Simon's subsequent forgiveness.
    Isaiah 55:6 Seek God, and when you find Him, call upon Him; and when He draws near to you,
    Isaiah 55:7 let the ungodly forsake his ways, and the lawless man his counsels; and let him return to the Lord, and he will receive mercy; for He will abundantly forgive your sins.

Acts 8:23
For I see that you are in bitter gall and a bond of unrighteousness.”
  Deuteronomy 29:17 — gall-and-bitterness imagery correspondence: Peter describes bitter gall and a bond of unrighteousness; Deuteronomy warns about a root springing in gall and bitterness amid turning to foreign gods. The paired bitter/gall image corresponds, while root, bond and each person's situation remain distinct.
    Deuteronomy 29:17 Is there among you a man, or woman, or family, or tribe, whose mind has turned aside from the Lord your God, to go and serve the gods of those nations? Is there among you a root springing up in gall and bitterness?

Acts 8:24
Simon answered, “You yourselves plead with the Lord for me, so that none of the things you have spoken may come upon me.”
  1 Samuel 12:19–12:20 — intercession after reproof comparison: Both speakers ask others to pray to the Lord so threatening consequences will not occur. Samuel's people explicitly confess the added evil of requesting a king; Simon asks that the things spoken not come upon him. Their offenses, speakers and subsequent instructions differ, and Simon's unreported final repentance is not decided.
    1 Samuel 12:19 All the people said to Samuel, “Pray to the Lord your God for your servants, and we will certainly not die. We have added to all our sins the evil of asking a king for ourselves.”
    1 Samuel 12:20 Samuel said to the people, “Do not fear. You have done all this evil, but do not turn aside from following the Lord. Serve the Lord with all your heart.

Acts 8:25
After bearing solemn witness and speaking the word of the Lord, they returned toward Jerusalem, proclaiming the good news in many Samaritan villages.
  Acts 8:14 — internal delegation-and-return connection: The returning witnesses are the Peter-and-John delegation previously sent from Jerusalem to Samaria. Their return and village proclamation develop that commission's narrative course, without making the earlier sending notice an independent record of all their later teaching stops.
    Acts 8:14 When the apostles in Jerusalem heard that Samaria had received the word of God, they sent Peter and John to them.

Acts 8:26
An angel of the Lord spoke to Philip, saying, “Rise and go south to the road that descends from Jerusalem to Gaza.” This is a desert road.
  Acts 10:3–10:6 — angel-directed mission preparation comparison: Both angelic communications direct movements that lead to gospel instruction. Philip is sent to the desert Gaza road; Cornelius is directed to summon Peter from Joppa. The commanded travelers, locations and angelic wording remain distinct.
    Acts 10:3 About the ninth hour of the day, he clearly saw in a vision an angel of God come in to him and say, “Cornelius.”
    Acts 10:4 He stared at him in fear and said, “What is it, sir?” The angel said to him, “Your prayers and your alms have ascended as a memorial before God.
    Acts 10:5 Now send men to Joppa and summon a certain Simon who is called Peter.
    Acts 10:6 He is staying with a certain Simon, a tanner, whose house is beside the sea.”

Acts 8:27
He rose and went. And look—an Ethiopian man, a eunuch and an official of Candace, queen of the Ethiopians, who was over all her treasury, had come to Jerusalem to worship.
  Isaiah 56:3–56:7 — eunuch-and-foreigner worship background: Isaiah addresses eunuchs and foreigners with promises concerning God's house and prayer, including covenant conditions. Acts introduces an Ethiopian eunuch returning from Jerusalem worship. This supplies relevant inclusion-and-worship background without independently verifying his observance of every named condition or assigning an unstated legal status.
    Isaiah 56:3 Let no foreigner attached to the Lord say, “Will the Lord then separate me from His people?” And let no eunuch say, “I am a dry tree.”
    Isaiah 56:4 Thus says the Lord: “To the eunuchs who keep My Sabbaths, choose what I wish, and hold fast to My covenant,
    Isaiah 56:5 I will give them in My house and wall a renowned place better than sons and daughters; I will give them an everlasting name, and it will not fail.
    Isaiah 56:6 And the foreigners attached to the Lord, to serve Him and love the Lord’s name, to become His male and female slaves, and all those keeping My Sabbaths from profanation and holding fast to My covenant—
    Isaiah 56:7 I will bring them into My holy mountain and make them rejoice in My house of prayer. Their whole burnt offerings and sacrifices will be acceptable on My altar, for My house will be called a house of prayer for all nations,”

Acts 8:28
He was returning, seated in his chariot, and reading the prophet Isaiah.
  Acts 8:32–8:33 — internal Scripture identification: The narrator later quotes the Isaiah passage this traveler is reading. That identifies the text behind the earlier Isaiah-reading notice, without independently verifying the chariot journey or reconstructing every other passage he may have read.
    Acts 8:32 The passage of Scripture he was reading was this: “Like a sheep He was led to slaughter, and as a lamb is silent before its shearer, so He does not open His mouth.
    Acts 8:33 In His humiliation His judgment was taken away. Who will recount His generation? For His life is taken from the earth.”

Acts 8:29
The Spirit said to Philip, “Go near and join this chariot.”
  Acts 10:19–10:20 — Spirit-directed approach comparison: The Spirit directs Philip to approach the chariot and Peter to accompany the waiting men. These are concrete mission-encounter directions with different recipients and actions; the earlier angelic communications are not collapsed into the Spirit's spoken instructions.
    Acts 10:19 While Peter was pondering the vision, the Spirit said to him, “Look, three men are seeking you.
    Acts 10:20 Rise, go down, and go with them without hesitation, because I have sent them.”

Acts 8:30
Philip ran up, heard him reading Isaiah the prophet, and said, “Do you understand what you are reading?”
  Nehemiah 8:8 — Scripture-reading and understanding comparison: Philip asks whether the hearer understands the Isaiah reading; Nehemiah narrates reading God's law, Esdras teaching and explaining, and the people understanding. The individual question and the public law-reading scene differ, while reading with understanding is a specific common concern.
    Nehemiah 8:8 They read from a book of God’s law, and Esdras taught and explained with knowledge of the Lord; the people understood the reading.

Acts 8:31
He said, “How could I, unless someone guides me?” And he invited Philip to come up and sit with him.
  Nehemiah 8:8 — guided Scripture understanding comparison: The traveler asks for guidance and invites Philip beside him; Nehemiah describes Esdras' explanation accompanying the law reading so the people understand. The books, audiences and forms of instruction differ; the comparison does not reconstruct Philip's whole explanation.
    Nehemiah 8:8 They read from a book of God’s law, and Esdras taught and explained with knowledge of the Lord; the people understood the reading.

Acts 8:32
The passage of Scripture he was reading was this: “Like a sheep He was led to slaughter, and as a lamb is silent before its shearer, so He does not open His mouth.
  Isaiah 53:7 — quotation: Both describe the silent sheep led to slaughter and lamb before its shearer. Isaiah also begins with affliction. This is the quoted textual correspondence, distinct from any later explanation of the passage's referent.
    Isaiah 53:7 And because he has been afflicted, he does not open his mouth. Like a sheep he was led to slaughter, and as a lamb is silent before its shearer, so he does not open his mouth.

Acts 8:33
In His humiliation His judgment was taken away. Who will recount His generation? For His life is taken from the earth.”
  Isaiah 53:8 — quotation: The humiliation, removed judgment, generation question and life taken from earth correspond to Isaiah's supplied wording. Isaiah additionally states being led to death because of the people's iniquities; that continuation is not inserted into Acts' shorter citation.
    Isaiah 53:8 In his humiliation his judgment was taken away. Who will declare his generation? For his life is taken from the earth; because of the iniquities of My people he was led to death.

Acts 8:34
The eunuch said to Philip, “I ask you, about whom does the prophet say this—about himself or about someone else?”
  Acts 8:35 — internal question-and-exposition response: The eunuch asks whom the prophet means; the next narration says Philip begins from this Scripture and announces Jesus. This connects the question with the narrated exposition, without inventing a verbatim yes/no answer or making the entire sermon available.
    Acts 8:35 Philip opened his mouth and, beginning from this Scripture, proclaimed to him the good news of Jesus.

Acts 8:35
Philip opened his mouth and, beginning from this Scripture, proclaimed to him the good news of Jesus.
  Luke 24:27 — Christ-centered Scripture exposition comparison: Philip begins with this Isaiah text to announce Jesus; Jesus interprets the things concerning Himself from Moses and all the prophets. The common Scripture-to-Jesus exposition does not identify the complete passage lists or equate the teachers and occasions.
    Luke 24:27 And beginning from Moses and from all the prophets, He interpreted to them in all the Scriptures the things concerning Himself.

Acts 8:36
As they went along the road, they came to some water, and the eunuch said, “Look, water! What prevents me from being baptized?”
  Acts 10:47 — water-baptism obstacle question comparison: Both ask what could prevent water baptism. The eunuch asks about himself after Philip's proclamation; Peter asks about Cornelius' company after their Spirit reception. The speakers, recipients and stated preceding events differ; the absent Acts 8:37 is not supplied.
    Acts 10:47 “Can anyone withhold the water so that these people, who have received the Holy Spirit just as we have, should not be baptized?”

Acts 8:38
He ordered the chariot to stop. Both Philip and the eunuch went down into the water, and Philip baptized him.
  Mark 1:9–1:10 — water-baptism narrative comparison: Both narrate baptism by another person and movement into or out of water. Acts names Philip and the eunuch going down, followed by baptism; Mark names John baptizing Jesus and Jesus coming up. The participants, sequence descriptions and physical details are not made identical.
    Mark 1:9 In those days Jesus came from Nazareth in Galilee and was baptized in the Jordan by John.
    Mark 1:10 Immediately as He came up out of the water, He saw the heavens being torn open and the Spirit descending like a dove into Him.

Acts 8:39
When they came up out of the water, the Spirit of the Lord snatched Philip away, and the eunuch saw him no more, for he went on his way rejoicing.
  1 Kings 18:12 — Spirit-transport comparison: Acts narrates the Lord's Spirit snatching Philip away; Obadiah fears the Lord's Spirit will carry Elijah to an unknown land. A narrated removal and an anticipated possibility are distinct, and the target does not independently attest Philip's departure or the eunuch's joy.
    1 Kings 18:12 If I leave you, the Lord’s Spirit will carry you to a land I do not know. I will come to report to Ahab, and he will kill me. Yet your servant has feared the Lord from his youth.

Acts 8:40
Philip was found at Azotus, and as he passed through, he proclaimed the good news in all the towns until he came to Caesarea.
  Acts 21:8 — internal Philip-and-Caesarea reference: Philip's proclamation route ends at Caesarea; the later travel account finds Philip the evangelist, one of the seven, with a house there. This provides a person-and-location continuation without independently attesting Azotus, every intervening town or a stated duration.
    Acts 21:8 The next day we departed and came to Caesarea. We entered the house of Philip the evangelist, who was one of the seven, and stayed with him.

Acts 9:1
Saul, still breathing threat and murder against the Lord’s disciples, went to the high priest
  Galatians 1:13 — persecution retrospective comparison: Saul's continuing murderous threats against disciples correspond to the letter's recollection of extreme persecution and ravaging the Church. Acts supplies the high-priest approach and immediate threats; the letter does not separately narrate each threatened act.
    Galatians 1:13 For you have heard of my former conduct in Judaism, how I persecuted the Church of God beyond measure and ravaged it,

Acts 9:2
and asked him for letters to the synagogues in Damascus, so that if he found any belonging to the Way, both men and women, he might bring them bound to Jerusalem.
  Acts 22:4–22:5 — internal explicit retrospective: Paul later recalls persecuting the Way, binding men and women and receiving letters before the Damascus journey to bring people to Jerusalem. The retrospective states received letters and punishment; Acts 9 narrates the request and names the synagogues. These are related reports with distinct wording.
    Acts 22:4 I persecuted this Way to the point of death, binding and handing over to prisons both men and women,
    Acts 22:5 as the high priest and the whole council of elders can testify for me. From them I also received letters to the brothers, and I went toward Damascus to bring those there bound to Jerusalem for punishment.

Acts 9:3
As he traveled and drew near Damascus, suddenly a light from heaven flashed around him.
  Acts 22:6 — internal explicit retrospective: Both describe approaching Damascus and a sudden heavenly light flashing around Saul or the first-person speaker. The later report adds about noon and brilliance; those details are not silently inserted into the earlier narration.
    Acts 22:6 As I was traveling and drawing near Damascus, about noon, a brilliant light from heaven suddenly flashed around me.

Acts 9:4
He fell to the ground and heard a voice saying to him, “Saul, Saul, why are you persecuting Me?”
  Acts 26:14 — internal explicit retrospective: Both report falling and the repeated Saul-name persecution question. Acts 9 here specifies Saul falling; Acts 26 says all fell and adds Hebrew speech and the goads clause. The reports' scope and wording remain, without inventing an unreported sequence or adding goads to Acts 9.
    Acts 26:14 When we had all fallen to the ground, I heard a voice speaking to me in the Hebrew language: ‘Saul, Saul, why are you persecuting Me? It is hard for you to kick against goads.’

Acts 9:5
He said, “Who art Thou, Lord?” He answered, “I am Jesus, whom you are persecuting.
  Acts 22:8 — internal explicit retrospective: The identity question and Jesus' reply identifying Himself as the persecuted One recur in Paul's later account. Acts 22 adds the Nazarene designation; it is not inserted into Acts 9's shorter self-identification.
    Acts 22:8 I answered, ‘Who art Thou, Lord?’ He said to me, ‘I am Jesus the Nazarene, whom you are persecuting.’

Acts 9:6
But rise and enter the city, and you will be told what you must do.”
  Acts 22:10 — internal explicit retrospective: Both direct Saul to rise and enter the city to be told his task. Acts 22 names Damascus, adds his what-should-I-do question and says everything appointed; Acts 9 does not repeat those additional words.
    Acts 22:10 I said, ‘What should I do, Lord?’ The Lord said to me, ‘Rise and go into Damascus, and there you will be told about everything appointed for you to do.’

Acts 9:7
The men traveling with him stood speechless, hearing the voice but seeing no one.
  Acts 22:9 — internal retelling with preserved hearing difference: Acts 9 says the companions stood speechless, hearing the voice but seeing no one; Acts 22 says they saw the light but did not hear the voice of the One speaking to Paul. The actual hearing descriptions are preserved, without replacing not-hearing with an unstated failure to understand.
    Acts 22:9 Those with me saw the light, but they did not hear the voice of the One speaking to me.

Acts 9:8
Saul rose from the ground, but although his eyes were open, he could see nothing. They led him by the hand and brought him into Damascus.
  Acts 22:11 — internal explicit retrospective: Both describe Saul unable to see and being led by the hand into Damascus. Acts 9 says his eyes were open; Acts 22 attributes inability to the light's brightness. Neither clause is transferred into the other report as its own wording.
    Acts 22:11 Since I could not see because of the brightness of that light, I was led by the hand by those with me and came into Damascus.

Acts 9:9
For three days he could not see, and he neither ate nor drank.
  Acts 9:18–9:19 — internal deprivation-and-recovery connection: The later sight restoration and taking food correspond to the earlier blindness and abstention from food. This tracks the narrative change of condition; the later units neither explicitly report drinking nor independently specify the earlier three-day duration.
    Acts 9:18 At once something like scales fell from his eyes, and he regained his sight. He rose and was baptized,
    Acts 9:19 and after taking food he regained strength. He stayed for some days with the disciples in Damascus.

Acts 9:10
There was a disciple in Damascus named Ananias. The Lord said to him in a vision, “Ananias.” He said, “Here I am, Lord.”
  Acts 22:12–22:13 — internal Ananias reference: Both identify Ananias as the Damascus participant who comes to Saul. Acts 9 calls him a disciple and reports the Lord's vision-address; Acts 22 describes his law-devotion and good reputation. The later report does not independently narrate his initial vision or identify him with another Ananias.
    Acts 22:12 A certain Ananias, a man devout according to the law and well spoken of by all the Jews living there,
    Acts 22:13 came to me, stood beside me, and said, ‘Brother Saul, regain your sight.’ At that very hour I regained my sight and looked at him.

Acts 9:11
The Lord said to him, “Rise, go to the street called Straight, and seek in Judas’s house a man named Saul from Tarsus. Look, he is praying,
  Acts 21:39 — internal Tarsus identity correspondence: Saul is identified as from Tarsus; the later Paul self-description names Tarsus in Cilicia. Acts 13:9 explicitly supplies the Saul/Paul name connection. This correspondence does not independently attest Straight Street, Judas's house or the prayer, and no other Judas is identified.
    Acts 21:39 Paul said, “I am a Jewish man from Tarsus in Cilicia, a citizen of no insignificant city. I ask you, allow me to speak to the people.”

Acts 9:12
and in a vision he has seen a man named Ananias come in and lay hands upon him so that he may regain his sight.”
  Acts 9:17–9:18 — internal vision and narrated outcome: The vision anticipates Ananias entering, laying hands and restored sight; the later narration records the visit, hands and sight recovery. It adds the Jesus message, Spirit-filling purpose and baptism. The vision and outcome belong to the same account, not independent witnesses.
    Acts 9:17 Ananias went and entered the house. Laying his hands upon him, he said, “Brother Saul, the Lord has sent me—Jesus, who appeared to you on the road as you came—so that you may regain your sight and be filled with the Holy Spirit.”
    Acts 9:18 At once something like scales fell from his eyes, and he regained his sight. He rose and was baptized,

Acts 9:13
Ananias answered, “Lord, I have heard from many about this man, how much evil he has done to Thy saints in Jerusalem.
  Acts 8:3 — internal persecution-report antecedent: Ananias reports hearing of Saul's harm to the Jerusalem saints; the earlier Jerusalem persecution narrative describes his church raids and imprisonment of men and women. A character's reported information and the narrator's account are kept distinct.
    Acts 8:3 But Saul was ravaging the Church, entering house after house, dragging away both men and women, and handing them over to prison.

Acts 9:14
Here too he has authority from the chief priests to bind all who call upon Thy name.”
  Acts 9:1–9:2 — internal commission reference: Ananias mentions chief-priest authority to bind those calling Jesus' name; the earlier narrative describes requesting the high priest's letters for Damascus synagogues and people of the Way. The grantor descriptions and group-designations are related without being made verbatim identical.
    Acts 9:1 Saul, still breathing threat and murder against the Lord’s disciples, went to the high priest
    Acts 9:2 and asked him for letters to the synagogues in Damascus, so that if he found any belonging to the Way, both men and women, he might bring them bound to Jerusalem.

Acts 9:15
But the Lord said to him, “Go, for this man is a chosen vessel for Me, to carry My name before Gentiles and kings and the sons of Israel.
  Acts 26:16–26:18 — internal mission-commission comparison: Jesus tells Ananias that Saul is a chosen vessel bearing His name before Gentiles, kings and Israel; Paul's later account reports Jesus appointing him a servant and witness sent to the people and Gentiles. Recipients, setting and specified audiences differ rather than becoming one speech.
    Acts 26:16 But rise and stand on your feet, for I have appeared to you for this purpose: to appoint you a servant and witness of the things in which you have seen Me and those in which I will appear to you,
    Acts 26:17 rescuing you from the people and from the Gentiles, to whom I am sending you,
    Acts 26:18 to open their eyes, so that they may turn from darkness to light and from Satan’s authority to God, so that they may receive forgiveness of sins and an allotted share among those sanctified by faith in Me.’

Acts 9:16
For I will show him how much he must suffer for My name.”
  Acts 20:22–20:24 — internal foretold suffering comparison: The Lord tells Ananias of Saul's necessary suffering for His name; Paul later describes Spirit testimony that bonds and afflictions await him while completing Jesus' ministry. The voices, times and particular forecasts are distinct, not a claim that every suffering event is listed here.
    Acts 20:22 And now, look, bound in spirit, I am going to Jerusalem, not knowing what will meet me there,
    Acts 20:23 except that in every city the Holy Spirit bears witness to me, saying that bonds and afflictions await me.
    Acts 20:24 But I consider my life of no value to myself, so that I may complete my course and the ministry I received from the Lord Jesus: to bear solemn witness to the good news of God’s grace.

Acts 9:17
Ananias went and entered the house. Laying his hands upon him, he said, “Brother Saul, the Lord has sent me—Jesus, who appeared to you on the road as you came—so that you may regain your sight and be filled with the Holy Spirit.”
  Acts 22:12–22:13 — internal Ananias encounter retrospective: Both have Ananias come and address Brother Saul before restored sight. Acts 9 adds laying hands, Jesus' sending and the purpose of sight and Spirit filling; Acts 22 quotes regain-your-sight and immediate seeing. Neither report is expanded with the other's unquoted dialogue.
    Acts 22:12 A certain Ananias, a man devout according to the law and well spoken of by all the Jews living there,
    Acts 22:13 came to me, stood beside me, and said, ‘Brother Saul, regain your sight.’ At that very hour I regained my sight and looked at him.

Acts 9:18
At once something like scales fell from his eyes, and he regained his sight. He rose and was baptized,
  Acts 22:13–22:16 — internal restoration-and-baptism retrospective: Both narrate or direct sight restoration and baptism in the Ananias encounter. Acts 9 says something like scales fell; Acts 22 supplies calling, witness and washing-sins/name language. The simile and additional words remain in their respective accounts.
    Acts 22:13 came to me, stood beside me, and said, ‘Brother Saul, regain your sight.’ At that very hour I regained my sight and looked at him.
    Acts 22:14 He said, ‘The God of our fathers appointed you to know His will, to see the Righteous One, and to hear a voice from His mouth,
    Acts 22:15 for you will be His witness to all people of what you have seen and heard.
    Acts 22:16 Now why do you delay? Rise, be baptized, and wash away your sins, calling upon His name.’

Acts 9:19
and after taking food he regained strength. He stayed for some days with the disciples in Damascus.
  1 Kings 19:7–19:8 — food-and-renewed-strength comparison: Saul takes food and regains strength after deprivation; Elijah eats and drinks and travels in that food's strength after the angel's instruction. The needs, agents and subsequent activities differ, without transferring Elijah's forty-day journey to Saul.
    1 Kings 19:7 The Lord’s angel returned a second time, touched him, and said to him, “Rise, eat, for the journey is too much for you.”
    1 Kings 19:8 He rose, ate, and drank, and went in the strength of that food forty days and forty nights to Mount Horeb.

Acts 9:20
Immediately he proclaimed Jesus in the synagogues, saying, “He is the Son of God.”
  Acts 13:32–13:33 — internal Jesus-Son proclamation correspondence: Saul's synagogue proclamation calls Jesus God's Son; the later sermon announces God's promise fulfilled in Jesus and cites the psalm's My-Son declaration. The shorter proclamation and later scriptural argument are distinct occasions and forms.
    Acts 13:32 We proclaim to you the good news of the promise made to the fathers:
    Acts 13:33 God has fulfilled this for us, their children, by raising up Jesus, as it is also written in the second psalm: ‘Thou art My Son; today I have begotten Thee.’

Acts 9:21
All who heard him were astonished and said, “Is this not the one who devastated those in Jerusalem who call upon this name? And had he not come here for this purpose, to bring them bound before the chief priests?”
  Galatians 1:23–1:24 — former-persecutor response comparison: Both report hearers recognizing the paradox of the former persecutor now proclaiming the faith. Acts quotes Damascus astonishment and the intended prisoner mission; Galatians reports Judean churches hearing and glorifying God. Their communities and reactions are not identified.
    Galatians 1:23 They only kept hearing, “The one who once persecuted us now proclaims the faith he once ravaged.”
    Galatians 1:24 And they glorified God because of me.

Acts 9:22
But Saul grew stronger and confounded the Jews living in Damascus by demonstrating that Jesus is the Christ.
  Acts 17:1–17:3 — internal synagogue argument pattern: Both describe Saul/Paul arguing that the proclaimed Jesus is the Christ among Jewish hearers. Acts 17 specifies Thessalonica, Scripture reasoning and suffering/resurrection arguments; Acts 9 names Damascus and growing strength. Those detailed arguments are not supplied as the earlier speech's transcript.
    Acts 17:1 After traveling through Amphipolis and Apollonia, they came to Thessalonica, where there was a Jewish synagogue.
    Acts 17:2 As was his custom, Paul went in to them, and for three Sabbaths he reasoned with them from the Scriptures,
    Acts 17:3 explaining and setting before them that the Christ had to suffer and rise from the dead, and saying, “This Jesus whom I proclaim to you is the Christ.”

Acts 9:23
After many days had passed, the Jews conspired to kill him,
  2 Corinthians 11:32–11:33 — Damascus danger-and-escape comparison: Acts describes a Jewish conspiracy to kill Saul in the Damascus context; Paul recalls an ethnarch under Aretas guarding the city to seize him and his escape. The common danger-and-escape setting does not identify the actors, equate capture with killing or supply an unrecorded joint conspiracy.
    2 Corinthians 11:32 In Damascus the ethnarch under King Aretas was guarding the city of the Damascenes to seize me,
    2 Corinthians 11:33 and through a window I was lowered in a basket through the wall, and escaped his hands.

Acts 9:24
but their plot became known to Saul. They were also watching the gates day and night to kill him.
  2 Corinthians 11:32 — Damascus surveillance comparison: Acts says Saul learns of a plot and the gates are watched day and night to kill him; the letter names the ethnarch's guarding the city to seize him. The different actors, stated purposes, gate detail and hours remain, without adding them to the letter.
    2 Corinthians 11:32 In Damascus the ethnarch under King Aretas was guarding the city of the Damascenes to seize me,

Acts 9:25
His disciples took him by night and let him down through the wall, lowering him in a hamper.
  2 Corinthians 11:33 — Damascus basket-escape comparison: Both describe being lowered through the wall in a basket or hamper to escape. Acts names disciples and night; the letter specifies a window and escape from the official's hands. Helpers, timing and window detail are retained in their own reports.
    2 Corinthians 11:33 and through a window I was lowered in a basket through the wall, and escaped his hands.

Acts 9:26
When he arrived in Jerusalem, he tried to join the disciples, but they all feared him, not believing that he was a disciple.
  Galatians 1:22–1:24 — reception of the former persecutor comparison: Acts describes Jerusalem disciples initially fearing Saul and doubting his discipleship; Galatians describes Paul as unknown by sight to the Judean Churches, which kept hearing of the former persecutor's proclamation and glorifying God. This compares reported receptions without identifying the groups, encounters or reactions as identical.
    Galatians 1:22 But I was unknown by sight to the Churches of Judea that are in Christ.
    Galatians 1:23 They only kept hearing, “The one who once persecuted us now proclaims the faith he once ravaged.”
    Galatians 1:24 And they glorified God because of me.

Acts 9:27
Barnabas took him and brought him to the apostles. He related to them how Saul had seen the Lord on the road, how He had spoken to him, and how Saul had spoken boldly in Damascus in the name of Jesus.
  Acts 9:3–9:6 — internal road-encounter report reference: Barnabas reports Saul seeing the Lord and the Lord speaking to him; the earlier narrative describes heavenly light and Jesus' identifying voice. This is a retrospective to the encounter, not permission to supply an unreported visible bodily form or change what the companions saw.
    Acts 9:3 As he traveled and drew near Damascus, suddenly a light from heaven flashed around him.
    Acts 9:4 He fell to the ground and heard a voice saying to him, “Saul, Saul, why are you persecuting Me?”
    Acts 9:5 He said, “Who art Thou, Lord?” He answered, “I am Jesus, whom you are persecuting.
    Acts 9:6 But rise and enter the city, and you will be told what you must do.”
  Acts 9:20–9:22 — internal proclamation report reference: Barnabas' report of bold Damascus proclamation refers to the narrated synagogue proclamation and argument that Jesus is the Christ. Boldness is his reported characterization; the earlier units describe proclamation, astonishment and increasing strength rather than quoting that adjective.
    Acts 9:20 Immediately he proclaimed Jesus in the synagogues, saying, “He is the Son of God.”
    Acts 9:21 All who heard him were astonished and said, “Is this not the one who devastated those in Jerusalem who call upon this name? And had he not come here for this purpose, to bring them bound before the chief priests?”
    Acts 9:22 But Saul grew stronger and confounded the Jews living in Damascus by demonstrating that Jesus is the Christ.

Acts 9:28
He was with them, going in and out in Jerusalem and speaking boldly in the name of the Lord.
  Galatians 1:18–1:19 — Jerusalem apostolic-contact comparison: Acts describes Saul being with the apostles and moving and speaking in Jerusalem; Galatians names a visit to Cephas and seeing James, with three years and fifteen days. The named contacts and time notices are not inserted into Acts or used to reconstruct every movement.
    Galatians 1:18 Then, after three years, I went up to Jerusalem to become acquainted with Cephas, and I stayed with him fifteen days.
    Galatians 1:19 But I saw none of the other apostles except James, the Lord’s brother.

Acts 9:29
He spoke and disputed with the Hellenists, but they attempted to kill him.
  Acts 22:17–22:21 — Jerusalem rejection comparison: Saul's Hellenist dispute and their attempted killing are compared with Paul's later Jerusalem vision report warning that people will not accept his testimony and directing departure. The group, murder attempt, temple vision and divine direction remain distinct narrated details rather than one reconstructed dialogue.
    Acts 22:17 After I returned to Jerusalem, while I was praying in the temple, I fell into a trance
    Acts 22:18 and saw Him saying to me, ‘Hurry, and go out quickly from Jerusalem, because they will not accept your testimony about Me.’
    Acts 22:19 I said, ‘Lord, they themselves know that I used to imprison and beat in the synagogues those who believe in Thee.
    Acts 22:20 And when the blood of Stephen, Thy witness, was being poured out, I myself stood by, approving and guarding the garments of those who were killing him.’
    Acts 22:21 He said to me, ‘Go, for I will send you far away to the Gentiles.’”

Acts 9:30
When the brothers learned this, they brought him down to Caesarea and sent him away to Tarsus.
  Acts 11:25 — internal Tarsus continuation: The brothers send Saul to Tarsus after bringing him to Caesarea; later Barnabas goes to Tarsus to seek him. This supplies a concrete subsequent person-and-location connection, not independent testimony to the escort route or a specified elapsed interval.
    Acts 11:25 He went away to Tarsus to search for Saul,

Acts 9:31
So the Church throughout all Judea, Galilee, and Samaria had peace and was being built up. Walking in the fear of the Lord and in the encouragement of the Holy Spirit, it increased.
  Acts 16:5 — internal Church-strength-and-growth summary: Both summary notices describe the Church or Churches being built up or strengthened and increasing. Acts 9 names peace, fear of the Lord, Spirit encouragement and three regions; Acts 16 specifies strengthened faith and daily numerical increase. Their settings and stated means are not made identical.
    Acts 16:5 So the Churches were strengthened in the faith and increased in number each day.

Acts 9:32
As Peter traveled among them all, he also went down to the saints who lived in Lydda.
  Acts 9:38 — internal Lydda setting callback: The later summons explicitly says Peter was in Lydda and explains its nearness to Joppa, connecting back to this visit among the Lydda saints. It clarifies the narrated location rather than independently attesting all his travels.
    Acts 9:38 Since Lydda was near Joppa, the disciples, hearing that Peter was there, sent two men to him, urging, “Do not delay in coming to us.”

Acts 9:33
There he found a man named Aeneas who had lain on a mat for eight years, for he was paralyzed.
  Luke 5:18 — paralysis-and-bed comparison: Both introduce a paralyzed man associated with a bed or mat. Aeneas has been confined eight years; Luke's men carry another paralyzed man to Jesus. The patients, duration and transport details are distinct, with no congenital condition or eight-year history added to Luke.
    Luke 5:18 And behold, men were carrying on a bed a man who was paralyzed. They sought to bring him in and place him before Him.

Acts 9:34
Peter said to him, “Aeneas, Jesus Christ heals you. Rise and make your bed.” At once he rose.
  Acts 3:6–3:7 — Peter's Jesus-centered healing comparison: Peter attributes Aeneas' healing to Jesus Christ and commands rising and bed-making; earlier he invokes Jesus Christ's name, commands rising and walking and takes the other man's hand. Jesus' agency, different commands, patients and reported manual action are preserved.
    Acts 3:6 But Peter said, “Silver and gold I do not have, but what I have I give you: in the name of Jesus Christ the Nazarene, get up and walk.”
    Acts 3:7 Taking him by the right hand, he raised him up. At once his feet and ankles were strengthened.

Acts 9:35
All who lived in Lydda and Sharon saw him and turned to the Lord.
  Acts 9:42 — internal local-response ending comparison: Both Peter episodes end with local responses to a restoration. Aeneas is seen by all Lydda and Sharon residents who turn to the Lord; Dorcas' restoration becomes known in Joppa and many believe in the Lord. All versus many and seeing versus report remain distinct.
    Acts 9:42 This became known throughout Joppa, and many believed in the Lord.

Acts 9:36
In Joppa there was a woman disciple named Tabitha, which translated is Dorcas. She was full of good works and acts of charity.
  1 Timothy 2:9–2:10 — woman-disciple good-works comparison: Dorcas is introduced as a woman disciple full of good works and charity; the letter commends good works as fitting women professing reverence for God, in contrast to costly adornment. This compares stated moral conduct without supplying Dorcas' marriage, age or dress.
    1 Timothy 2:9 Likewise also women are to adorn themselves in orderly dress, with modesty and self-restraint, not with braids and gold or pearls or costly clothing,
    1 Timothy 2:10 but through good works, as is fitting for women who profess reverence for God.

Acts 9:37
In those days she became ill and died. They washed her and laid her in an upper room.
  1 Kings 17:17–17:19 — death-and-upper-room setting comparison: Both narratives place a body in an upper room following fatal illness. Acts names Dorcas and washing her; Kings describes the widow's son and Elijah carrying him to his bed. The people and bearer differ; washing is stated only in Acts.
    1 Kings 17:17 After these things, the son of the woman, the mistress of the house, became ill. His illness was very severe, until no breath remained in him.
    1 Kings 17:18 She said to Elijah, “What have I to do with you, man of God? You have come to me to bring my wrongdoings to remembrance and to put my son to death!”
    1 Kings 17:19 Elijah said to the woman, “Give me your son.” He took him from her bosom, carried him up to the upper room where he was staying, and laid him on his bed.

Acts 9:38
Since Lydda was near Joppa, the disciples, hearing that Peter was there, sent two men to him, urging, “Do not delay in coming to us.”
  Acts 10:5–10:8 — Peter-summons delegation comparison: Joppa's disciples send two men for Peter in Lydda; Cornelius later sends two slaves and a soldier for Peter in Joppa after angelic instruction. Both are explicit summons delegations, but callers, numbers, locations and purposes differ.
    Acts 10:5 Now send men to Joppa and summon a certain Simon who is called Peter.
    Acts 10:6 He is staying with a certain Simon, a tanner, whose house is beside the sea.”
    Acts 10:7 When the angel who spoke to him had gone, Cornelius called two of his household slaves and a devout soldier from among those who attended him.
    Acts 10:8 He explained everything to them and sent them to Joppa.

Acts 9:39
Peter rose and went with them. When he arrived, they brought him into the upper room. All the widows stood beside him weeping and showing the tunics and garments Dorcas had made while she was with them.
  Acts 9:36 — internal good-works illustration: The weeping widows show clothing Dorcas made while with them, giving concrete context for her earlier good-works and charity description. This is one narrative's characterization and illustration, not proof of every garment's ownership or an independent account of her work.
    Acts 9:36 In Joppa there was a woman disciple named Tabitha, which translated is Dorcas. She was full of good works and acts of charity.

Acts 9:40
Peter put everyone outside, knelt down, and prayed. Turning toward the body, he said, “Tabitha, rise.” She opened her eyes, saw Peter, and sat up.
  Mark 5:40–5:42 — raising-scene comparison: Both narratives put others outside and command a girl or woman to rise. Peter kneels and prays before addressing Tabitha; Jesus takes the girl's hand and uses the translated Aramaic command, and she walks. Acts names Tabitha, while Mark leaves the girl unnamed; the sequence and bodily responses differ, and Mark's retained passage does not report a prayer.
    Mark 5:40 They laughed at Him. But He put them all outside, took the child’s father and mother and those with Him, and went in where the child was.
    Mark 5:41 Taking the child’s hand, He said to her, “Talitha koum,” which translated means, “Little girl, I say to you, arise!”
    Mark 5:42 Immediately the little girl rose and walked around, for she was twelve years old. Immediately they were overcome with great amazement.

Acts 9:41
He gave her his hand and raised her up. Then he called the saints and the widows and presented her alive.
  1 Kings 17:23 — restored-person presentation comparison: Peter calls the saints and widows and presents Tabitha alive; Elijah brings the boy down and gives him to his mother, declaring that he lives. The living-person presentation corresponds, without transferring Peter's hand assistance or identifying the recipients and restored people.
    1 Kings 17:23 He brought him down from the upper room into the house and gave him to his mother. Elijah said, “Behold, your son lives!”

Acts 9:42
This became known throughout Joppa, and many believed in the Lord.
  Acts 9:35 — internal local-response ending comparison: Joppa's widespread report is followed by the note that many believed in the Lord; the earlier Aeneas episode describes Lydda and Sharon residents seeing him and turning to the Lord. The report/seeing distinction and many/all formulations are retained rather than counted as identical populations.
    Acts 9:35 All who lived in Lydda and Sharon saw him and turned to the Lord.

Acts 9:43
He stayed in Joppa for many days with a certain Simon, a tanner.
  Acts 10:5–10:6 — internal host-and-location reference: The later angelic address identifies Peter lodging with Simon the tanner, whose house is by the sea, corresponding to this Joppa stay. Host Simon remains distinct from the guest Simon Peter; the later instructions do not independently measure many days.
    Acts 10:5 Now send men to Joppa and summon a certain Simon who is called Peter.
    Acts 10:6 He is staying with a certain Simon, a tanner, whose house is beside the sea.”

Acts 10:1
In Caesarea there was a man named Cornelius, a centurion of the cohort called the Italian,
  Acts 10:22 — internal Cornelius identity reference: The messengers later name Cornelius as a centurion and describe his character, matching the introduced man. Their report does not independently name the Italian cohort or repeat every location detail; this is an internal person reference.
    Acts 10:22 They said, “Cornelius, a centurion, a righteous and God-fearing man well spoken of by the whole Jewish nation, was directed by a holy angel to summon you to his house and hear words from you.”

Acts 10:2
devout and fearing God with his whole household, giving many alms to the people and praying to God continually.
  Acts 10:4 — internal devotion-and-angelic-response connection: The narrator describes Cornelius' prayers and alms; the angel then says those prayers and alms have ascended as a memorial before God. The stated practices and response are connected within one story, not independent testimony to every household member's conduct.
    Acts 10:4 He stared at him in fear and said, “What is it, sir?” The angel said to him, “Your prayers and your alms have ascended as a memorial before God.

Acts 10:3
About the ninth hour of the day, he clearly saw in a vision an angel of God come in to him and say, “Cornelius.”
  Acts 10:30 — internal explicit vision retrospective: Cornelius later recalls praying at the ninth hour and a man in shining clothing standing before him. The initial account calls the visitor an angel seen in a vision; the report adds four-days-ago and clothing. These descriptions and the named hour are retained distinctly.
    Acts 10:30 Cornelius said, “Four days ago, at this hour, I was praying in my house at the ninth hour, and look—a man stood before me in shining clothing.

Acts 10:4
He stared at him in fear and said, “What is it, sir?” The angel said to him, “Your prayers and your alms have ascended as a memorial before God.
  Acts 10:31 — internal quoted-message retrospective: Both messages join Cornelius' prayer and alms with divine regard. The angel says they ascended as a memorial; Cornelius reports prayer heard and alms remembered. The corresponding message is not reduced to identical wording or a separate independent revelation.
    Acts 10:31 He said, ‘Cornelius, your prayer has been heard, and your alms have been remembered before God.

Acts 10:5
Now send men to Joppa and summon a certain Simon who is called Peter.
  Acts 10:32 — internal command retrospective: Cornelius' later report repeats sending to Joppa and summoning Simon called Peter. The report also repeats lodging details from the next initial unit. This is the same commission retold, not a second independently attested summons.
    Acts 10:32 Send, therefore, to Joppa and summon Simon who is called Peter. He is staying in the house of Simon the tanner beside the sea.’

Acts 10:6
He is staying with a certain Simon, a tanner, whose house is beside the sea.”
  Acts 10:32 — internal lodging-address retrospective: The initial angelic address and Cornelius' report both identify the host Simon the tanner and a house beside the sea. The host is kept distinct from the guest Simon Peter; no absent additional promise about what Peter will say is inserted into this address.
    Acts 10:32 Send, therefore, to Joppa and summon Simon who is called Peter. He is staying in the house of Simon the tanner beside the sea.’

Acts 10:7
When the angel who spoke to him had gone, Cornelius called two of his household slaves and a devout soldier from among those who attended him.
  Acts 10:19 — internal messenger-count connection: Cornelius dispatches two household slaves and a devout soldier; the Spirit later tells Peter three men are seeking him. The count connects the delegation across the narrative, without giving the slaves the soldier's explicitly stated description or supplying their names.
    Acts 10:19 While Peter was pondering the vision, the Spirit said to him, “Look, three men are seeking you.

Acts 10:8
He explained everything to them and sent them to Joppa.
  Acts 10:17 — internal dispatch-and-arrival connection: The men sent to Joppa by Cornelius later appear at Simon's gate after asking directions. This connects the narrated dispatch with its arrival, without reconstructing the explanation Cornelius gave or the unquoted travel conversations.
    Acts 10:17 While Peter was perplexed within himself about what the vision he had seen might mean, look—the men sent by Cornelius, having asked directions to Simon’s house, stood at the gate.

Acts 10:9
The next day, while they were traveling and approaching the city, Peter went up onto the roof to pray, about the sixth hour.
  Acts 11:5 — internal prayer-scene retrospective: Peter later recalls praying in Joppa and seeing the sheet vision in a trance. Acts 10 additionally specifies the rooftop, sixth hour and travelers approaching. Those details are not imported into the shorter retrospective or confused with Cornelius' ninth-hour prayer.
    Acts 11:5 “I was in the city of Joppa praying, and in a trance I saw a vision: something like a large sheet descending, lowered by its four corners from heaven, and it came right to me.

Acts 10:10
He became hungry and wanted to eat. While they were preparing the food, a trance came upon him.
  Acts 11:5 — internal trance retrospective: Both accounts associate Peter's vision with a trance. Acts 10 adds hunger and meal preparation; Peter's later narration foregrounds prayer in Joppa. The different reported circumstances remain rather than supplying a physical explanation for the vision.
    Acts 11:5 “I was in the city of Joppa praying, and in a trance I saw a vision: something like a large sheet descending, lowered by its four corners from heaven, and it came right to me.

Acts 10:11
He saw heaven opened and something like a large sheet descending, being lowered by its four corners to the earth.
  Acts 11:5 — internal vision retrospective: Both describe something like a large sheet descending from heaven by four corners. Peter's report says it came right to him; the narrator says lowered to earth and notes opened heaven. The likeness and additional destination details are preserved.
    Acts 11:5 “I was in the city of Joppa praying, and in a trance I saw a vision: something like a large sheet descending, lowered by its four corners from heaven, and it came right to me.

Acts 10:12
In it were all the four-footed animals and creeping things of the earth and birds of the sky.
  Acts 11:6 — internal animal-list retrospective: Both vision accounts list four-footed animals, creeping things and birds.
