Peter's later report explicitly adds wild animals and his examining the contents. These list and action details are retained without pretending both sentences have identical inventories.
    Acts 11:6 I looked intently into it and examined it, and I saw the four-footed animals of the earth, wild animals, creeping things, and birds of the sky.

Acts 10:13
A voice came to him: “Rise, Peter; slaughter and eat.”
  Acts 11:7 — internal quoted-command retrospective: Peter's report repeats the voice's Rise-Peter-slaughter-and-eat command. This is an explicit quotation counterpart within Acts, not evidence of another independent voice-event or an additional unreported speaker identification.
    Acts 11:7 I also heard a voice saying to me, ‘Rise, Peter; slaughter and eat.’

Acts 10:14
Peter said, “By no means, Lord, for I have never eaten anything common and unclean.”
  Acts 11:8 — internal quoted-refusal retrospective: Both refusals begin By no means, Lord, but Acts10 says never eaten anything common and unclean; Acts11 says nothing common or unclean has ever entered my mouth. The and/or wording and eaten/entered-my-mouth descriptions remain distinct.
    Acts 11:8 But I said, ‘By no means, Lord, for nothing common or unclean has ever entered my mouth.’

Acts 10:15
A voice came to him again, a second time: “What God has cleansed, you must not call common.”
  Acts 11:9 — internal quoted-answer retrospective: Both second-voice statements forbid calling common what God has cleansed. The report explicitly calls the voice from heaven; the initial narration locates this within the animal vision. The shared wording is not independently applied here to every later question.
    Acts 11:9 A voice answered a second time from heaven, ‘What God has cleansed, you must not call common.’

Acts 10:16
This happened three times, and immediately the object was taken up into heaven.
  Acts 11:10 — internal threefold-vision retrospective: Both say the episode happened three times and its object or everything was taken back into heaven. The report's everything and drawn-up-again wording remains distinct from the initial immediately and object notice; no exact repeated subaction is invented.
    Acts 11:10 This happened three times, and everything was drawn up again into heaven.

Acts 10:17
While Peter was perplexed within himself about what the vision he had seen might mean, look—the men sent by Cornelius, having asked directions to Simon’s house, stood at the gate.
  Acts 11:11 — internal arrival retrospective: Both connect the vision's aftermath with Cornelius' delegation arriving at the lodging. Acts 10 gives Peter's perplexity, directions and gate; Acts 11 gives three men and Caesarea as origin. Neither is expanded into a duplicate transcript of the other.
    Acts 11:11 And look, at once three men stood at the house where we were, sent to me from Caesarea.

Acts 10:18
They called out and asked whether Simon, who was called Peter, was staying there.
  Acts 10:5–10:6 — internal summons-instruction connection: The men ask whether Simon called Peter is lodging there, carrying out the specific search directed by the angel's earlier name and address. The question is not itself proof that every address detail was repeated aloud at the gate.
    Acts 10:5 Now send men to Joppa and summon a certain Simon who is called Peter.
    Acts 10:6 He is staying with a certain Simon, a tanner, whose house is beside the sea.”

Acts 10:19
While Peter was pondering the vision, the Spirit said to him, “Look, three men are seeking you.
  Acts 11:11–11:12 — internal Spirit-and-delegation retrospective: The later report names three waiting men and the Spirit's direction to accompany them, corresponding to Peter's current Spirit-address during reflection. The count, divine direction and distinct surrounding details remain; this is one encounter retold.
    Acts 11:11 And look, at once three men stood at the house where we were, sent to me from Caesarea.
    Acts 11:12 The Spirit told me to go with them, making no distinction. These six brothers also went with me, and we entered the man’s house.

Acts 10:20
Rise, go down, and go with them without hesitation, because I have sent them.”
  Acts 11:12 — internal Spirit-command retrospective: Both give the Spirit's direction to go with the men. Acts 10 says without hesitation and I have sent them; Acts 11 says making no distinction and adds six accompanying brothers. The expressions and stated agency are not silently made verbatim identical.
    Acts 11:12 The Spirit told me to go with them, making no distinction. These six brothers also went with me, and we entered the man’s house.

Acts 10:21
Peter went down to the men and said, “I am the one you are seeking. What is the reason you have come?”
  Acts 10:29 — internal purpose-question recurrence: Peter first asks the arriving men why they came and later asks the assembled hearers why they summoned him. This is a concrete repeated invitation to explain the commission at two stages, with different interlocutors rather than a second unrelated inquiry.
    Acts 10:29 Therefore I came without objection when summoned. So I ask, for what reason did you send for me?”

Acts 10:22
They said, “Cornelius, a centurion, a righteous and God-fearing man well spoken of by the whole Jewish nation, was directed by a holy angel to summon you to his house and hear words from you.”
  Acts 10:3–10:6 — internal mission-report reference: The messengers report an angel directing Cornelius to summon Peter and hear him; the earlier narration supplies the angelic appearance and summons. Their public-reputation description is additional and is not treated as an independently verified census of every Jewish person's judgment.
    Acts 10:3 About the ninth hour of the day, he clearly saw in a vision an angel of God come in to him and say, “Cornelius.”
    Acts 10:4 He stared at him in fear and said, “What is it, sir?” The angel said to him, “Your prayers and your alms have ascended as a memorial before God.
    Acts 10:5 Now send men to Joppa and summon a certain Simon who is called Peter.
    Acts 10:6 He is staying with a certain Simon, a tanner, whose house is beside the sea.”

Acts 10:23
So he invited them in and gave them lodging. The next day he rose and went away with them, and some of the brothers from Joppa accompanied him.
  Acts 11:12 — internal journey retrospective: Peter later says six brothers accompanied him and entered the man's house. Acts 10 says some Joppa brothers went with him after lodging the messengers. The later number and house entry clarify the journey, without being inserted into the initial some notice.
    Acts 11:12 The Spirit told me to go with them, making no distinction. These six brothers also went with me, and we entered the man’s house.

Acts 10:24
The following day he entered Caesarea. Cornelius was expecting them and had called together his relatives and close friends.
  Acts 10:27 — internal gathering preparation-and-result: Cornelius gathers relatives and close friends while awaiting Peter; Peter subsequently enters and finds many assembled. This links preparation with the resulting audience, without independently identifying every attendee or assigning them names.
    Acts 10:27 While speaking with him, Peter entered and found many people gathered.

Acts 10:25
As Peter was about to enter, Cornelius met him, fell at his feet, and bowed before him.
  Acts 14:11–14:15 — human-directed honor comparison: Cornelius falls at Peter's feet and bows; the Lycaonian crowd gives Barnabas and Paul divine names and prepares sacrifice before their human-status response. The actions and professed beliefs differ: Acts 10 does not explicitly say Cornelius identified Peter as a god.
    Acts 14:11 When the crowds saw what Paul had done, they raised their voice, saying in Lycaonian, “The gods have come down to us in human likeness.”
    Acts 14:12 They called Barnabas Zeus and Paul Hermes, because he was the leading speaker.
    Acts 14:13 The priest of Zeus, whose shrine was before the city, brought bulls and garlands to the gates and wanted to offer sacrifice with the crowds.
    Acts 14:14 When the apostles Barnabas and Paul heard this, they tore their garments and rushed out into the crowd, crying out
    Acts 14:15 and saying, “Men, why are you doing these things? We too are human beings, of the same nature as you. We proclaim good news to you: turn from these worthless things to the living God, who made heaven and earth and the sea and everything in them.

Acts 10:26
But Peter raised him up, saying, “Rise; I too am a human being.”
  Acts 14:15 — apostolic human-status response comparison: Peter raises Cornelius and says he too is a human being; Barnabas and Paul likewise insist they are humans of the same nature as their hearers. The shared human-status response does not transfer the sacrifice scene or Peter's physical raising action to the other encounter.
    Acts 14:15 and saying, “Men, why are you doing these things? We too are human beings, of the same nature as you. We proclaim good news to you: turn from these worthless things to the living God, who made heaven and earth and the sea and everything in them.

Acts 10:27
While speaking with him, Peter entered and found many people gathered.
  Acts 10:24 — internal gathered-audience reference: The many people Peter finds gathered correspond to Cornelius' preceding assembly of relatives and close friends. This provides a concrete narrative antecedent for the audience, not a new independent attendance count or proof of each person's identity.
    Acts 10:24 The following day he entered Caesarea. Cornelius was expecting them and had called together his relatives and close friends.

Acts 10:28
He said to them, “You know how forbidden it is for a Jewish man to associate with or visit someone of another people. Yet God has shown me to call no human being common or unclean.
  Acts 10:15 — internal application of vision vocabulary: Peter expressly applies the common/unclean vocabulary to human beings after God's cleansing command in the vision. The vision's animal setting and Peter's declared human-association application remain distinct, rather than being replaced by an unstated comprehensive legal ruling.
    Acts 10:15 A voice came to him again, a second time: “What God has cleansed, you must not call common.”

Acts 10:29
Therefore I came without objection when summoned. So I ask, for what reason did you send for me?”
  Acts 10:20 — internal direction-and-response connection: Peter's coming without objection follows the Spirit's instruction to accompany the men without hesitation. The source describes his response to the summons; the earlier command also gives the Spirit's sending explanation, which is not a new speech quoted in his later question.
    Acts 10:20 Rise, go down, and go with them without hesitation, because I have sent them.”

Acts 10:30
Cornelius said, “Four days ago, at this hour, I was praying in my house at the ninth hour, and look—a man stood before me in shining clothing.
  Acts 10:3 — internal explicit vision retrospective: Cornelius recalls a ninth-hour prayer and a man in shining clothing, corresponding to the narrator's ninth-hour vision of an angel. Four-days-ago, prayer and clothing are specified in the report; no fasting clause or identical visitor-description wording is added to either unit.
    Acts 10:3 About the ninth hour of the day, he clearly saw in a vision an angel of God come in to him and say, “Cornelius.”

Acts 10:31
He said, ‘Cornelius, your prayer has been heard, and your alms have been remembered before God.
  Acts 10:4 — internal divine-regard message retrospective: Cornelius' reported prayer-heard/alms-remembered message corresponds to the angel's earlier prayers-and-alms memorial statement. The paired themes match while the reporting voice and heard/remembered versus ascended wording remain distinct.
    Acts 10:4 He stared at him in fear and said, “What is it, sir?” The angel said to him, “Your prayers and your alms have ascended as a memorial before God.

Acts 10:32
Send, therefore, to Joppa and summon Simon who is called Peter. He is staying in the house of Simon the tanner beside the sea.’
  Acts 10:5–10:6 — internal quoted-summons retrospective: The report combines the earlier command to summon Simon Peter from Joppa with the Simon-the-tanner seaside address. It keeps guest and host distinct and supplies no extra speech promise absent from these saved initial instructions.
    Acts 10:5 Now send men to Joppa and summon a certain Simon who is called Peter.
    Acts 10:6 He is staying with a certain Simon, a tanner, whose house is beside the sea.”

Acts 10:33
So I sent to you at once, and you have done well to come. Now we are all here before God to hear everything the Lord has commanded you.”
  Acts 11:13–11:14 — receptive-audience and message-promise comparison: Cornelius welcomes hearing all the Lord has commanded Peter; Peter later reports the angel's promise of words for Cornelius and his household's salvation. The latter promise is additional to Acts 10's quoted summons, and the audiences and statement forms are not made identical.
    Acts 11:13 He reported to us how he had seen the angel standing in his house and saying, ‘Send to Joppa and summon Simon who is called Peter,
    Acts 11:14 who will speak words to you by which you and your whole household will be saved.’

Acts 10:34
Peter opened his mouth and said, “Truly I understand that God shows no partiality,
  Deuteronomy 10:17 — divine-impartiality correspondence: Peter declares God shows no partiality; Deuteronomy describes the Lord as showing no partiality and taking no bribe. The shared divine-character claim supplies scriptural background, while Peter's present recognition and Gentile encounter remain their own narrative context.
    Deuteronomy 10:17 For the Lord your God, He is God of gods and Lord of lords, the great and mighty and fearful God, who shows no partiality, neither takes a bribe;

Acts 10:35
but in every nation, whoever fears Him and practices righteousness is acceptable to Him.
  Romans 2:10–2:11 — impartial moral-regard comparison: Peter says God accepts those fearing Him and practicing righteousness in every nation; Romans describes glory, honor and peace for good-doers, Jew and Greek, and no divine partiality. The contexts and precise criteria remain distinct, not a substituted complete formula.
    Romans 2:10 but glory and honor and peace to everyone who does good, to the Jew first and also to the Greek.
    Romans 2:11 For there is no partiality with God.

Acts 10:36
He sent the word to the sons of Israel, proclaiming good news of peace through Jesus Christ—He is Lord of all.
  Ephesians 2:17 — Christ-associated peace proclamation comparison: Peter describes God sending Israel the peace message through Jesus Christ, Lord of all; Ephesians describes Christ proclaiming peace to far and near hearers. Sender, proclaimed message and the differing audience formulations are preserved rather than turned into one identical statement.
    Ephesians 2:17 And He came and proclaimed peace to you who were far away, and peace to those who were near,

Acts 10:37
You know what took place throughout all Judea, beginning from Galilee after the baptism John proclaimed:
  Mark 1:14–1:15 — Galilean ministry summary comparison: Both place Jesus' ministry in Galilee in relation to John's activity. Acts refers to the baptism John proclaimed and what took place throughout Judea, beginning from Galilee; Mark explicitly says after John was handed over and names Jesus' kingdom announcement. The distinct time markers are not equated.
    Mark 1:14 And after John was handed over, Jesus came into Galilee, proclaiming the gospel of God,
    Mark 1:15 and saying, “The appointed time is fulfilled, and the kingdom of God has drawn near; repent, and believe in the gospel.”

Acts 10:38
Jesus of Nazareth—how God anointed Him with the Holy Spirit and power. He went about doing good and healing all who were oppressed by the devil, because God was with Him.
  Luke 4:18–4:21 — Spirit-and-anointing correspondence: Peter explicitly attributes Jesus' anointing with the Holy Spirit and power to God. Luke has Jesus read the Spirit-and-anointing passage with release and sight imagery and declare its present fulfillment. The reading and ministry summary have different forms and do not list identical beneficiaries.
    Luke 4:18 “The Spirit of the Lord is upon Me, because He anointed Me to proclaim good news to the poor. He has sent Me to proclaim release to captives and recovery of sight to the blind, to send the oppressed away in release,
    Luke 4:19 to proclaim the Lord’s acceptable year.”
    Luke 4:20 He rolled up the scroll, gave it back to the attendant, and sat down. The eyes of everyone in the synagogue were fixed on Him.
    Luke 4:21 He began to say to them, “Today this Scripture has been fulfilled in your ears.”

Acts 10:39
We are witnesses of everything He did both in the country of the Jews and in Jerusalem. They also killed Him by hanging Him on a tree.
  Acts 5:30–5:32 — internal death-and-witness proclamation recurrence: Both speeches involving Peter name Jesus' killing by hanging on a tree and witness testimony. The council address uses you; the Caesarea speech uses they. The changed audiences and pronouns are kept, without assigning Cornelius or every Jewish person the same narrated action.
    Acts 5:30 The God of our fathers raised Jesus, whom you killed by hanging Him on a tree.
    Acts 5:31 God exalted Him to His right hand as Leader and Savior, to grant repentance to Israel and forgiveness of sins.
    Acts 5:32 We are witnesses of these things, and so is the Holy Spirit, whom God has given to those who obey Him.”

Acts 10:40
God raised Him on the third day and granted that He should appear,
  Luke 24:6–24:7 — resurrection third-day correspondence: Peter proclaims God's raising Jesus on the third day and granting appearances; Luke's resurrection announcement recalls Jesus' third-day prediction. The target supports the resurrection/day correspondence without independently describing all the appearances mentioned by Peter.
    Luke 24:6 He is not here, but has been raised. Remember how He spoke to you while He was still in Galilee,
    Luke 24:7 saying that the Son of Man must be handed over into the hands of sinful people and be crucified and rise on the third day.”

Acts 10:41
not to all the people, but to witnesses chosen beforehand by God—to us, who ate and drank with Him after He rose from the dead.
  Luke 24:41–24:43 — risen-Jesus meal comparison: Peter says selected witnesses ate and drank with the risen Jesus; Luke narrates disciples giving Jesus fish and Him eating before them. The shared meal setting does not make Luke explicitly narrate their eating, drinking or every appearance and participant in Peter's summary.
    Luke 24:41 While they still did not believe for joy and were marveling, He said to them, “Do you have anything to eat here?”
    Luke 24:42 They gave Him a portion of broiled fish.
    Luke 24:43 And He took it and ate before them.

Acts 10:42
He commanded us to proclaim to the people and bear solemn witness that He is the One appointed by God as judge of the living and the dead.
  2 Timothy 4:1–4:2 — living-and-dead judgment and proclamation comparison: Peter says Jesus commanded proclamation and God appointed Him judge of living and dead. The letter's charge likewise names Christ Jesus judging living and dead and urges word proclamation. Commissioning voices, recipients and temporal expressions remain distinct.
    2 Timothy 4:1 I charge you before God and Christ Jesus, who is to judge the living and the dead, and by His appearing and His kingdom:
    2 Timothy 4:2 proclaim the word; be ready in season and out of season; reprove, rebuke, exhort, with all patience and teaching.

Acts 10:43
All the prophets bear witness to Him: everyone who believes in Him receives forgiveness of sins through His name.”
  Luke 24:46–24:47 — scriptural mission-and-forgiveness correspondence: Peter joins prophetic witness, believing and forgiveness through Jesus' name; Jesus' concluding instruction joins scriptural fulfillment with repentance and forgiveness proclaimed in His name to all nations. Believing and repentance, speakers and stated scriptural scope are retained without inventing a list of every prophecy.
    Luke 24:46 and said to them, “Thus it is written that the Christ should suffer and rise from the dead on the third day,
    Luke 24:47 and that repentance for the forgiveness of sins should be proclaimed in His name to all the nations—beginning from Jerusalem.

Acts 10:44
While Peter was still speaking these words, the Holy Spirit fell upon all who were hearing the message.
  Acts 11:15 — internal Spirit-event retrospective: Peter later recounts the Spirit falling on the hearers and compares the beginning. Acts 10 says while he was still speaking these words; Acts 11 says as I began to speak. Those distinct timing expressions remain rather than being converted into an identical transcript.
    Acts 11:15 As I began to speak, the Holy Spirit fell upon them just as upon us at the beginning.

Acts 10:45
The believers from the circumcision who had come with Peter were astonished that the gift of the Holy Spirit had been poured out even upon the Gentiles,
  Acts 11:17–11:18 — internal Gentile-gift interpretation comparison: The circumcision believers accompanying Peter marvel at the Spirit's outpouring on Gentiles; his later report calls it the same gift, and Jerusalem hearers glorify God for Gentile repentance leading to life. The companion and Jerusalem audiences and their reactions are not collapsed.
    Acts 11:17 If, then, God gave them the same gift as He gave us when we believed in the Lord Jesus Christ, who was I that I could hinder God?”
    Acts 11:18 When they heard these things, they fell silent and glorified God, saying, “Then God has granted even the Gentiles repentance leading to life.”

Acts 10:46
for they heard them speaking in tongues and magnifying God. Then Peter said,
  Acts 2:4 — Spirit-linked tongues comparison: Both narratives associate Spirit reception or filling with tongues speech. Pentecost specifies other tongues and Spirit-given utterance; Acts 10 says the listeners heard tongues and magnifying God. The target unit does not separately repeat that praise clause or identify the languages of this later scene.
    Acts 2:4 They were all filled with the Holy Spirit and began to speak in other tongues, as the Spirit gave them utterance.

Acts 10:47
“Can anyone withhold the water so that these people, who have received the Holy Spirit just as we have, should not be baptized?”
  Acts 8:36 — water-baptism obstacle question comparison: Peter asks whether anyone can withhold water baptism from these Spirit recipients; the eunuch earlier asks what prevents his baptism when water appears. The shared obstacle-question form preserves different speakers, recipients and Spirit-sequence details, without supplying the absent Acts 8:37.
    Acts 8:36 As they went along the road, they came to some water, and the eunuch said, “Look, water! What prevents me from being baptized?”

Acts 10:48
He ordered them to be baptized in the name of Jesus Christ. Then they asked him to stay for some days.
  Acts 2:38 — Peter's name-associated baptism instruction comparison: Both instructions link baptism with Jesus Christ's name. Acts 2 says upon the name, with repentance and a promised Spirit gift; Acts 10 says in the name after Spirit reception. The prepositions, hearers, sequence and additional request to stay are preserved rather than normalized.
    Acts 2:38 Peter said to them, “Repent, and let each one of you be baptized upon the name of Jesus Christ for the forgiveness of your sins, and you will receive the gift of the Holy Spirit.

Acts 11:1
The apostles and the brothers throughout Judea heard that the Gentiles too had received the word of God.
  Acts 8:14 — narrative parallel: News that Gentiles received God's word reaches the Judean apostles and brothers; earlier, the Jerusalem apostles heard that Samaria received God's word. The repeated reception-report pattern concerns different communities and episodes.
    Acts 8:14 When the apostles in Jerusalem heard that Samaria had received the word of God, they sent Peter and John to them.

Acts 11:2
When Peter went up to Jerusalem, those of the circumcision disputed with him,
  Galatians 2:11–2:12 — thematic parallel: Peter faces criticism from those of the circumcision in Jerusalem. In Galatians, Cephas withdraws from Gentile meals under circumcision pressure and Paul opposes him in Antioch. These are contrasting disputes about his conduct, not the same visit or critic group.
    Galatians 2:11 But when Cephas came to Antioch, I opposed him to his face, because he stood condemned.
    Galatians 2:12 For before certain people came from James, he used to eat with the Gentiles. But when they came, he began drawing back and separating himself, fearing those of the circumcision.

Acts 11:3
saying, “You went in to uncircumcised men and ate with them.”
  Galatians 2:12–2:14 — thematic parallel: The Jerusalem objection is that Peter entered uncircumcised men's company and ate with them. Galatians describes Cephas previously eating with Gentiles and then separating himself. The common table-fellowship issue has different actions and challengers.
    Galatians 2:12 For before certain people came from James, he used to eat with the Gentiles. But when they came, he began drawing back and separating himself, fearing those of the circumcision.
    Galatians 2:13 And the other Jews also joined him in hypocrisy, so that even Barnabas was carried away by their hypocrisy.
    Galatians 2:14 But when I saw that they were not walking straight in line with the truth of the gospel, I said to Cephas before them all, “If you, though a Jew, live like the Gentiles and not like the Jews, how can you compel the Gentiles to live as Jews?

Acts 11:4
Peter began explaining to them in order, saying,
  Luke 1:3–1:4 — thematic parallel: Peter explicitly explains events in order; Luke explicitly offers an orderly account for Theophilus's certainty. This limited parallel concerns ordered presentation for hearers, without identifying Peter's defense with Luke's written prologue or imposing an exact chronological scheme.
    Luke 1:3 it seemed good to me also, having followed everything carefully from the beginning, to write an orderly account for you, most excellent Theophilus,
    Luke 1:4 so that you may know the certainty of the matters in which you have been instructed.

Acts 11:5
“I was in the city of Joppa praying, and in a trance I saw a vision: something like a large sheet descending, lowered by its four corners from heaven, and it came right to me.
  Acts 10:9–10:11 — narrative parallel: Peter recounts the Joppa prayer, trance and four-cornered descending sheet narrated earlier. The recap says it came right to him; the earlier narrative describes its lowering to the earth.
    Acts 10:9 The next day, while they were traveling and approaching the city, Peter went up onto the roof to pray, about the sixth hour.
    Acts 10:10 He became hungry and wanted to eat. While they were preparing the food, a trance came upon him.
    Acts 10:11 He saw heaven opened and something like a large sheet descending, being lowered by its four corners to the earth.

Acts 11:6
I looked intently into it and examined it, and I saw the four-footed animals of the earth, wild animals, creeping things, and birds of the sky.
  Acts 10:12 — narrative parallel: Peter's inspection of animals in the sheet retells its earlier contents. Both list four-footed animals, creeping things and birds; the recap additionally names wild animals rather than repeating an identical list.
    Acts 10:12 In it were all the four-footed animals and creeping things of the earth and birds of the sky.

Acts 11:7
I also heard a voice saying to me, ‘Rise, Peter; slaughter and eat.’
  Acts 10:13 — narrative parallel: The recalled voice repeats the earlier command to Peter to rise, slaughter and eat. This is Peter's report of the same vision, not a second independently narrated command.
    Acts 10:13 A voice came to him: “Rise, Peter; slaughter and eat.”

Acts 11:8
But I said, ‘By no means, Lord, for nothing common or unclean has ever entered my mouth.’
  Acts 10:14 — narrative parallel: Both reports preserve By no means, Lord, but the earlier account says never eaten anything common and unclean, while the defense says nothing common or unclean has ever entered my mouth.
    Acts 10:14 Peter said, “By no means, Lord, for I have never eaten anything common and unclean.”

Acts 11:9
A voice answered a second time from heaven, ‘What God has cleansed, you must not call common.’
  Acts 10:15 — narrative parallel: The second heavenly answer repeats the earlier instruction not to call common what God has cleansed. The link preserves the vision's wording rather than adding a broader food-law ruling.
    Acts 10:15 A voice came to him again, a second time: “What God has cleansed, you must not call common.”

Acts 11:10
This happened three times, and everything was drawn up again into heaven.
  Acts 10:16 — narrative parallel: Both accounts say the event happened three times and the object or contents were taken back into heaven. The earlier narrative adds immediately; the recap says everything was drawn up again.
    Acts 10:16 This happened three times, and immediately the object was taken up into heaven.

Acts 11:11
And look, at once three men stood at the house where we were, sent to me from Caesarea.
  Acts 10:17–10:19 — narrative parallel: The three Caesarean messengers reaching the house correspond to Cornelius's men standing at Simon's gate and the Spirit's announcement that three men seek Peter. The recap supplies its own first-person house description.
    Acts 10:17 While Peter was perplexed within himself about what the vision he had seen might mean, look—the men sent by Cornelius, having asked directions to Simon’s house, stood at the gate.
    Acts 10:18 They called out and asked whether Simon, who was called Peter, was staying there.
    Acts 10:19 While Peter was pondering the vision, the Spirit said to him, “Look, three men are seeking you.

Acts 11:12
The Spirit told me to go with them, making no distinction. These six brothers also went with me, and we entered the man’s house.
  Acts 10:19–10:23 — narrative parallel: Both accounts join the Spirit's instruction to accompany the men with brothers accompanying Peter. Acts 10 says without hesitation and some brothers; the recap says making no distinction and specifies six. Their wording and specificity are retained.
    Acts 10:19 While Peter was pondering the vision, the Spirit said to him, “Look, three men are seeking you.
    Acts 10:20 Rise, go down, and go with them without hesitation, because I have sent them.”
    Acts 10:21 Peter went down to the men and said, “I am the one you are seeking. What is the reason you have come?”
    Acts 10:22 They said, “Cornelius, a centurion, a righteous and God-fearing man well spoken of by the whole Jewish nation, was directed by a holy angel to summon you to his house and hear words from you.”
    Acts 10:23 So he invited them in and gave them lodging. The next day he rose and went away with them, and some of the brothers from Joppa accompanied him.

Acts 11:13
He reported to us how he had seen the angel standing in his house and saying, ‘Send to Joppa and summon Simon who is called Peter,
  Acts 10:30–10:32 — narrative parallel: Peter repeats the householder's report and the instruction to summon Simon called Peter from Joppa. Cornelius's earlier quoted account describes a man in shining clothing; Peter calls the figure the angel, as the initial narrative also does.
    Acts 10:30 Cornelius said, “Four days ago, at this hour, I was praying in my house at the ninth hour, and look—a man stood before me in shining clothing.
    Acts 10:31 He said, ‘Cornelius, your prayer has been heard, and your alms have been remembered before God.
    Acts 10:32 Send, therefore, to Joppa and summon Simon who is called Peter. He is staying in the house of Simon the tanner beside the sea.’

Acts 11:14
who will speak words to you by which you and your whole household will be saved.’
  Acts 16:31–16:32 — teaching parallel: The angel's promised saving message includes the householder and whole household. The jailer is likewise promised salvation in relation to believing in the Lord Jesus, and the word is spoken to everyone in his house. No household ages or unreported individual responses are supplied.
    Acts 16:31 They said, “Believe in the Lord Jesus, and you will be saved, you and your household.”
    Acts 16:32 They spoke the word of the Lord to him, together with all who were in his house.

Acts 11:15
As I began to speak, the Holy Spirit fell upon them just as upon us at the beginning.
  Acts 10:44–10:47 — narrative parallel: Peter identifies the Gentile Spirit-gift event previously narrated while he was still speaking, including the comparison with the believers' own reception. The recap's as I began and the narrative's while still speaking remain distinct formulations.
    Acts 10:44 While Peter was still speaking these words, the Holy Spirit fell upon all who were hearing the message.
    Acts 10:45 The believers from the circumcision who had come with Peter were astonished that the gift of the Holy Spirit had been poured out even upon the Gentiles,
    Acts 10:46 for they heard them speaking in tongues and magnifying God. Then Peter said,
    Acts 10:47 “Can anyone withhold the water so that these people, who have received the Holy Spirit just as we have, should not be baptized?”
  Acts 2:1–2:4 — narrative parallel: Peter's just as upon us at the beginning points back to the community filled with the Holy Spirit at Pentecost. The common Spirit reception does not require the earlier wind-like sound or fire-like tongues to recur in Cornelius's house.
    Acts 2:1 When the day of Pentecost arrived, they were all together in the same place.
    Acts 2:2 Suddenly a sound came from heaven like a violent wind rushing along, and it filled the whole house where they were sitting.
    Acts 2:3 Tongues like fire appeared to them, separating and settling on each one of them.
    Acts 2:4 They were all filled with the Holy Spirit and began to speak in other tongues, as the Spirit gave them utterance.

Acts 11:16
I remembered the Lord’s word, how He said, ‘John baptized with water, but you will be baptized in the Holy Spirit.’
  Acts 1:5 — quotation: Peter explicitly remembers the Lord's water-versus-Spirit baptism saying, which the opening account records from Jesus. The earlier wording also specifies not many days from now; the remembered excerpt does not.
    Acts 1:5 for John baptized with water, but you will be baptized in the Holy Spirit not many days from now.”

Acts 11:17
If, then, God gave them the same gift as He gave us when we believed in the Lord Jesus Christ, who was I that I could hinder God?”
  Acts 10:45–10:47 — narrative parallel: The same Spirit gift to Gentiles underlies Peter's earlier question about withholding baptismal water and his later question about hindering God. The questions have different immediate objects and occur at different stages of the same affair.
    Acts 10:45 The believers from the circumcision who had come with Peter were astonished that the gift of the Holy Spirit had been poured out even upon the Gentiles,
    Acts 10:46 for they heard them speaking in tongues and magnifying God. Then Peter said,
    Acts 10:47 “Can anyone withhold the water so that these people, who have received the Holy Spirit just as we have, should not be baptized?”

Acts 11:18
When they heard these things, they fell silent and glorified God, saying, “Then God has granted even the Gentiles repentance leading to life.”
  Acts 15:7–15:11 — teaching parallel: The Jerusalem listeners acknowledge God-granted Gentile repentance leading to life. Peter later argues that God gave Gentiles the Spirit without distinction and that salvation is through Jesus's grace in the same way. The later council is a separate deliberation.
    Acts 15:7 After much debate, Peter rose and said to them, “Men, brothers, you know that in earlier days God made a choice among you, that through my mouth the Gentiles should hear the word of the good news and believe.
    Acts 15:8 God, who knows the heart, bore witness to them by giving them the Holy Spirit just as He did to us.
    Acts 15:9 He made no distinction between us and them, cleansing their hearts by faith.
    Acts 15:10 Now, therefore, why are you testing God by placing upon the disciples’ neck a yoke that neither our fathers nor we were able to bear?
    Acts 15:11 Rather, we believe that we are saved through the grace of the Lord Jesus, in the same way as they are.”

Acts 11:19
Those who had been scattered by the affliction that arose over Stephen traveled as far as Phoenicia, Cyprus, and Antioch, speaking the word to no one except Jews alone.
  Acts 8:1–8:4 — narrative parallel: This account explicitly resumes the scattering associated with Stephen's affliction. The earlier narrative recounts his killing, the Jerusalem persecution and scattered believers preaching; this continuation extends the route to Phoenicia, Cyprus and Antioch.
    Acts 8:1 Saul approved of his killing. That day a great persecution arose against the Church in Jerusalem, and all except the apostles were scattered through the regions of Judea and Samaria.
    Acts 8:2 Devout men buried Stephen and made great lamentation over him.
    Acts 8:3 But Saul was ravaging the Church, entering house after house, dragging away both men and women, and handing them over to prison.
    Acts 8:4 Those who were scattered went about proclaiming the word as good news.

Acts 11:20
But some of them were men from Cyprus and Cyrene who came to Antioch and also spoke to the Hellenists, proclaiming the good news of the Lord Jesus.
  Acts 9:28–9:29 — narrative parallel: The supplied text says the Antioch preachers addressed Hellenists with the Lord Jesus's good news; earlier Saul spoke boldly in the Lord's name and disputed with Hellenists in Jerusalem. The shared label is preserved without changing it to Greeks or identifying the two groups.
    Acts 9:28 He was with them, going in and out in Jerusalem and speaking boldly in the name of the Lord.
    Acts 9:29 He spoke and disputed with the Hellenists, but they attempted to kill him.

Acts 11:21
The Lord’s hand was with them, and a great number believed and turned to the Lord.
  Acts 14:1–14:3 — narrative parallel: The Lord's hand accompanies proclamation and many believe here; at Iconium many Jews and Greeks believe while the Lord attests the missionaries' word through signs. These are distinct missions with divine support and widespread response, not identical audiences or a transferred miracle list.
    Acts 14:1 In Iconium they entered the Jewish synagogue together and spoke in such a way that a great multitude of both Jews and Greeks believed.
    Acts 14:2 But the Jews who refused to believe stirred up the Gentiles and embittered their minds against the brothers.
    Acts 14:3 So they stayed a considerable time, speaking boldly in reliance on the Lord, who bore witness to the word of His grace by granting signs and wonders to take place through their hands.

Acts 11:22
The report about them reached the ears of the Church in Jerusalem, and they sent Barnabas to travel as far as Antioch.
  Acts 8:14 — narrative parallel: Jerusalem hears of a new community receiving the message and sends a representative to it. The earlier Samaritan report led the apostles to send Peter and John; the Antioch report leads the Church to send Barnabas.
    Acts 8:14 When the apostles in Jerusalem heard that Samaria had received the word of God, they sent Peter and John to them.

Acts 11:23
When he arrived and saw the grace of God, he rejoiced and encouraged them all to remain with the Lord with steadfast purpose of heart,
  Acts 14:22 — teaching parallel: Barnabas urges the Antioch believers to remain with the Lord with steadfast purpose; the later missionary return encourages disciples to remain in the faith. The shared pastoral perseverance instruction has different settings and additional affliction teaching only in the latter.
    Acts 14:22 strengthening the souls of the disciples, encouraging them to remain in the faith, and saying, “Through many afflictions we must enter the kingdom of God.”

Acts 11:24
for he was a good man, full of the Holy Spirit and faith. A considerable crowd was added to the Lord.
  Acts 6:5 — thematic parallel: Barnabas is commended as full of the Holy Spirit and faith; Stephen is similarly called full of faith and the Holy Spirit. The comparison is between the commendations of distinct men, not an identification of Barnabas as one of the Seven.
    Acts 6:5 The proposal pleased the whole multitude. They chose Stephen, a man full of faith and the Holy Spirit, Philip, Prochorus, Nicanor, Timon, Parmenas, and Nicolaus, a convert from Antioch.

Acts 11:25
He went away to Tarsus to search for Saul,
  Acts 9:30 — narrative parallel: The brothers had sent Saul to Tarsus when his life was threatened in Jerusalem. Barnabas now goes to Tarsus to seek him, providing a direct narrative location link rather than a new account of Saul's birth or citizenship.
    Acts 9:30 When the brothers learned this, they brought him down to Caesarea and sent him away to Tarsus.

Acts 11:26
and when he found him, he brought him to Antioch. For a whole year they gathered with the Church and taught a considerable crowd. It was in Antioch that the disciples were first called Christians.
  1 Peter 4:16 — thematic parallel: Acts identifies Antioch as the place where disciples were first called Christians; the epistle instructs anyone suffering as a Christian not to be ashamed. The shared designation is concrete, while the epistle does not recount its first use or this year's teaching.
    1 Peter 4:16 But if anyone suffers as a Christian, let him not be ashamed, but let him glorify God in this matter.

Acts 11:27
In those days prophets came down from Jerusalem to Antioch.
  Acts 15:32 — narrative parallel: The arrival of prophets at Antioch has a later parallel in Judas and Silas, themselves prophets, encouraging the Antioch brothers with many words. This compares prophetic visits and ministry without assigning those later men's names to the unnamed earlier visitors.
    Acts 15:32 Judas and Silas, themselves also prophets, encouraged the brothers with many words and strengthened them.

Acts 11:28
One of them, named Agabus, stood up and indicated through the Spirit that a great famine would come over the whole inhabited world. This happened in the time of Claudius.
  Acts 21:10–21:11 — narrative parallel: Agabus again appears as a prophet from Judea explicitly speaking through or in the name of the Holy Spirit. His later belt-sign concerns Paul's custody, not the earlier famine or independent confirmation of its extent under Claudius.
    Acts 21:10 While we stayed for several days, a prophet named Agabus came down from Judea.
    Acts 21:11 He came to us, took Paul’s belt, bound his own feet and hands, and said, “Thus says the Holy Spirit: ‘The Jews in Jerusalem will bind the man whose belt this is in this way and hand him over into Gentile hands.’”

Acts 11:29
The disciples determined, each according to his means, to send relief to the brothers living in Judea.
  2 Corinthians 8:12–8:14 — teaching parallel: The disciples resolve to relieve Judean brothers according to each one's means. The epistle likewise measures acceptable readiness by what one has and directs abundance toward others' lack. The amounts, recipients and occasions are not equated.
    2 Corinthians 8:12 For if the readiness is present, it is acceptable according to what one has, not according to what one does not have.
    2 Corinthians 8:13 For it is not that there should be relief for others and affliction for you, but on the basis of equality:
    2 Corinthians 8:14 at the present time your abundance is to meet their lack, so that their abundance may also meet your lack, so that there may be equality.

Acts 11:30
This they did, sending it to the elders by the hand of Barnabas and Saul.
  Acts 12:25 — narrative parallel: Barnabas and Saul carry the relief to the elders; the later unit refers to the same named pair completing their service and taking John called Mark. Its printed returned to Jerusalem is preserved, without replacing it with from Jerusalem or Antioch.
    Acts 12:25 Barnabas and Saul returned to Jerusalem after completing their service, and took along John who was called Mark.

Acts 12:1
About that time King Herod laid hands on some of the Church to mistreat them.
  Matthew 10:17–10:18 — thematic parallel: Jesus warns His disciples of being handed over and brought before governors and kings because of Him. Herod's violent action against the Church exemplifies royal persecution; Acts does not narrate a separate formal hearing for every victim.
    Matthew 10:17 Beware of people, for they will hand you over to councils and flog you in their synagogues.
    Matthew 10:18 You will also be brought before governors and kings because of Me, as a testimony to them and to the nations.

Acts 12:2
He killed James, the brother of John, with the sword.
  Mark 10:35–10:39 — thematic parallel: Mark names James and John, sons of Zebedee, and Jesus speaks of their sharing His cup and baptism. James's killing is a concrete suffering parallel; the saying does not specify his sword-death or narrate John's death.
    Mark 10:35 James and John, the sons of Zebedee, approached Him and said to Him, “Teacher, we want Thee to do for us whatever we ask Thee.”
    Mark 10:36 He said to them, “What do you want Me to do for you?”
    Mark 10:37 They said to Him, “Grant us to sit, one at Thy right and one at Thy left, in Thy glory.”
    Mark 10:38 Jesus said to them, “You do not know what you are asking. Can you drink the cup that I drink, or be baptized with the baptism with which I am baptized?”
    Mark 10:39 They said to Him, “We can.” Jesus said to them, “You will drink the cup that I drink, and you will be baptized with the baptism with which I am baptized.

Acts 12:3
When he saw that this pleased the Jews, he proceeded to arrest Peter as well. These were the days of Unleavened Bread.
  Acts 5:17–5:18 — narrative parallel: Herod arrests Peter after seeing that the preceding killing pleased the Jews; earlier the high priest and Sadducees imprisoned the apostles out of jealousy. The repeated apostolic imprisonment has different rulers, motives and prisoner groups.
    Acts 5:17 The high priest and all who were with him—the party of the Sadducees—rose up, filled with jealousy.
    Acts 5:18 They laid hands on the apostles and placed them in the public prison.

Acts 12:4
He seized him and put him in prison, handing him over to four squads of four soldiers to guard him, intending to bring him out before the people after the Passover.
  Acts 16:23–16:24 — narrative parallel: Herod places Peter under four squads of soldiers; later magistrates order Paul and Silas securely held, and the jailer uses an inner cell and stocks. Both narrate deliberate custody before divine intervention, with distinct security arrangements and no shared Passover date asserted.
    Acts 16:23 After inflicting many blows on them, they threw them into prison and ordered the jailer to keep them securely.
    Acts 16:24 Having received such an order, he put them in the inner prison and secured their feet in the stocks.

Acts 12:5
So Peter was kept in prison, but earnest prayer for him was being made to God by the Church.
  Acts 12:12 — narrative parallel: The later scene locates many believers praying in Mary the mother of John Mark's house during the same Peter episode. It gives a concrete setting for the Church's prayer without asserting that every Church member was in that single house.
    Acts 12:12 After considering this, he went to the house of Mary, the mother of John who was called Mark, where many had gathered and were praying.

Acts 12:6
On the night before Herod was to bring him out, Peter was sleeping between two soldiers, bound with two chains, while guards before the door kept watch over the prison.
  Acts 16:24–16:26 — narrative parallel: Peter is chained between soldiers before his release; Paul and Silas are in stocks before an earthquake loosens bonds. Peter is sleeping while the later prisoners pray and sing, and their restraints and subsequent actions differ.
    Acts 16:24 Having received such an order, he put them in the inner prison and secured their feet in the stocks.
    Acts 16:25 About midnight Paul and Silas were praying and singing hymns to God, while the prisoners listened to them.
    Acts 16:26 Suddenly there was a great earthquake, so that the jail’s foundations were shaken. At once all the doors opened, and everyone’s bonds were loosened.

Acts 12:7
And look—an angel of the Lord stood beside him, and light shone in the cell. Striking Peter’s side, he woke him and said, “Rise quickly.” The chains fell from his hands.
  Acts 5:19 — narrative parallel: An angel of the Lord intervenes in apostolic imprisonment in both episodes. Here light, waking Peter and falling chains are described; the earlier angel opens prison doors and leads the apostles out.
    Acts 5:19 But during the night an angel of the Lord opened the prison doors, led them out, and said,

Acts 12:8
The angel said to him, “Fasten your belt and put on your sandals.” He did so. Then he said to him, “Wrap your cloak around you and follow me.”
  Exodus 12:11 — thematic parallel: The angel's belt-and-sandals readiness instruction during the Unleavened Bread and Passover setting recalls girded loins, sandals and haste in the Exodus Passover departure. Peter's cloak and the Israelites' staffs remain different items; this is a bounded readiness parallel.
    Exodus 12:11 And this is how you shall eat it: your loins girded, your sandals upon your feet, and your staffs in your hands; and you shall eat it in haste. It is a Passover to the Lord.

Acts 12:9
Peter went out and followed. He did not know that what was happening through the angel was real; he thought he was seeing a vision.
  Acts 12:11 — narrative parallel: Peter initially thinks the angelic events are a vision and does not know they are real; afterward he explicitly says he now knows the Lord rescued him. The later recognition corrects his initial interpretation within the same narrative.
    Acts 12:11 When Peter came to himself, he said, “Now I know truly that the Lord has sent His angel and rescued me from Herod’s hand and from everything the Jewish people were expecting.”

Acts 12:10
They passed the first guard post and the second and came to the iron gate leading into the city. It opened for them of its own accord. They went out and continued along one street, and immediately the angel left him.
  Acts 16:26 — narrative parallel: Peter's iron gate opens of its own accord during the angel-led escape; an earthquake later opens prison doors and loosens bonds. The shared supernatural opening has different means and does not imply that Paul and Silas escaped as Peter did.
    Acts 16:26 Suddenly there was a great earthquake, so that the jail’s foundations were shaken. At once all the doors opened, and everyone’s bonds were loosened.

Acts 12:11
When Peter came to himself, he said, “Now I know truly that the Lord has sent His angel and rescued me from Herod’s hand and from everything the Jewish people were expecting.”
  Daniel 3:95 — thematic parallel: Peter acknowledges that the Lord sent His angel and rescued him from royal power. Nebuchadnezzar likewise praises God for sending His angel and saving His servants from fire. The rescue acknowledgment is shared, with different speakers, servants and dangers.
    Daniel 3:95 King Nebuchadnezzar spoke and said, “Blessed be the Lord, the God of Sedrach, Misach, and Abdenago, who sent His angel and saved His servants who hoped in Him. They rejected the king’s command and surrendered their bodies to burning so that they would neither serve nor bow to another god except their own God.

Acts 12:12
After considering this, he went to the house of Mary, the mother of John who was called Mark, where many had gathered and were praying.
  Acts 12:5 — narrative parallel: The many praying in John Mark's mother's house give a specific instance of the Church's earnest prayer for imprisoned Peter earlier in the episode. This Mary is identified by her son, without merging her with another Mary or another meeting house.
    Acts 12:5 So Peter was kept in prison, but earnest prayer for him was being made to God by the Church.

Acts 12:13
When he knocked at the outer gate’s door, a slave girl named Rhoda came to answer.
  John 18:16–18:17 — narrative parallel: Both scenes place Peter outside a doorway with a female slave attending it. Rhoda answers his knock at a believing household; John's unnamed doorkeeper participates in his entry into the high priest's courtyard and questions his discipleship. The women are not identified with one another.
    John 18:16 but Peter was standing outside at the door. So the other disciple, who was known to the high priest, went out and spoke to the woman keeping the door, and brought Peter in.
    John 18:17 Then the slave girl keeping the door says to Peter, “You are not also one of this Man’s disciples, are you?” He says, “I am not.”

Acts 12:14
Recognizing Peter’s voice, in her joy she did not open the gate, but ran inside and announced that Peter was standing before it.
  Acts 12:16 — narrative parallel: Rhoda recognizes Peter's voice and announces that he is outside before opening the gate. The later opening and the group's seeing Peter confirm her specific report, with hearing and seeing assigned to different people.
    Acts 12:16 Peter kept knocking. When they opened the door, they saw him and were astonished.

Acts 12:15
They said to her, “You are out of your mind.” But she insisted that it was so. They said, “It is his angel.”
  Luke 24:10–24:11 — narrative parallel: A woman's unexpected report is initially rejected by a believing group; Luke similarly records the apostles disbelieving the women's tomb report. Peter's prison deliverance is not Jesus's resurrection, and their suggestion about his angel is reported speech rather than a narrated identification.
    Luke 24:10 It was Mary Magdalene and Joanna and Mary of James and the other women with them who told these things to the apostles.
    Luke 24:11 But these words seemed to them like nonsense, and they did not believe the women.

Acts 12:16
Peter kept knocking. When they opened the door, they saw him and were astonished.
  Acts 12:14 — narrative parallel: Opening the door and seeing Peter provide the narrative confirmation of Rhoda's earlier voice-recognition report. The group's astonishment follows its delayed admission rather than disproving that Rhoda had heard the actual Peter.
    Acts 12:14 Recognizing Peter’s voice, in her joy she did not open the gate, but ran inside and announced that Peter was standing before it.

Acts 12:17
He motioned with his hand for them to be silent and related how the Lord had brought him out of prison. He said, “Report these things to James and the brothers.” Then he left and went to another place.
  Acts 21:40 — narrative parallel: Peter motions with his hand for silence before relating his deliverance; Paul later motions with his hand and a great silence precedes his address. This is a shared apostolic speech-preface gesture in different gatherings, not the same speech, audience or language claim.
    Acts 21:40 When he gave permission, Paul stood on the steps and motioned with his hand to the people. When a great silence fell, he addressed them in the Hebrew language, saying:

Acts 12:18
When day came, there was no small disturbance among the soldiers over what had become of Peter.
  Acts 5:22–5:24 — narrative parallel: Soldiers are disturbed over Peter's disappearance after guarded imprisonment. Earlier officers find the apostles absent despite locked doors and guards, leaving the temple authorities perplexed. The parallel concerns the unexpected absence and official confusion, not identical prison conditions.
    Acts 5:22 But when the officers arrived, they did not find them in the prison. They returned and reported,
    Acts 5:23 “We found the jail securely locked and the guards standing at the doors, but when we opened it, we found no one inside.”
    Acts 5:24 When the captain of the temple and the chief priests heard these words, they were perplexed about them, wondering what this would come to.

Acts 12:19
Herod searched for him and did not find him. After examining the guards, he ordered them to be led away for execution. Then he went down from Judea to Caesarea and stayed there.
  Acts 16:27–16:28 — narrative parallel: Peter's disappearance leads Herod to order the guards led away for execution. A later jailer, supposing prisoners escaped, prepares to kill himself until Paul stops him. These are contrasting lethal consequences or fears for custodians; neither all guards' completed executions nor an actual later escape is added.
    Acts 16:27 The jailer awoke and saw the prison doors open. He drew his sword and was about to kill himself, supposing the prisoners had escaped.
    Acts 16:28 But Paul cried with a loud voice, “Do yourself no harm, for we are all here.”

Acts 12:20
Herod was fiercely hostile toward the people of Tyre and Sidon. They came to him with one accord and, after winning over Blastus, the king’s chamberlain, asked for peace, because their country received its food from the king’s territory.
  1 Kings 5:23–5:26 — thematic parallel: Tyre and Sidon seek peace because their country receives food from Herod's territory. Earlier Hiram of Tyre obtains household loaves, wheat and oil from Solomon amid a timber exchange and peace covenant. This is a bounded Tyrian food-and-royal-peace background, not the same embassy or food arrangement.
    1 Kings 5:23 My servants will bring them down from Lebanon to the sea. I will make them into rafts as far as the place you send me word about, and shake them apart there; you shall take them away. You shall fulfill my wish by providing loaves for my household.”
    1 Kings 5:24 Hiram supplied Solomon with cedars and all that he desired.
    1 Kings 5:25 Solomon gave Hiram twenty thousand kors of wheat and machir for his household, and twenty thousand beth of beaten oil. Solomon gave this to Hiram each year.
    1 Kings 5:26 The Lord gave Solomon wisdom, as He had promised him. There was peace between Hiram and Solomon, and they made a covenant between themselves.

Acts 12:22
The populace shouted, “A god’s voice, and not a human being’s!”
  Acts 14:11–14:15 — thematic parallel: The populace calls Herod's voice divine rather than human; the Lystra crowd calls Barnabas and Paul gods in human likeness, which the apostles explicitly reject as fellow humans. The different reactions remain: Acts records Herod's failure to give God glory, not an explicit spoken claim that he is a god.
    Acts 14:11 When the crowds saw what Paul had done, they raised their voice, saying in Lycaonian, “The gods have come down to us in human likeness.”
    Acts 14:12 They called Barnabas Zeus and Paul Hermes, because he was the leading speaker.
    Acts 14:13 The priest of Zeus, whose shrine was before the city, brought bulls and garlands to the gates and wanted to offer sacrifice with the crowds.
    Acts 14:14 When the apostles Barnabas and Paul heard this, they tore their garments and rushed out into the crowd, crying out
    Acts 14:15 and saying, “Men, why are you doing these things? We too are human beings, of the same nature as you. We proclaim good news to you: turn from these worthless things to the living God, who made heaven and earth and the sea and everything in them.

Acts 12:23
Immediately an angel of the Lord struck him because he did not give the glory to God. He was eaten by worms and breathed his last.
  2 Maccabees 9:5–9:12 — narrative parallel: Herod is struck by an angel, eaten by worms and dies for withholding God's glory. Antiochus is struck by the Lord amid arrogance, suffers worms and decay, and confesses that a mortal must not think himself equal to God. The distinct rulers and bodily details are retained.
    2 Maccabees 9:5 But the all-seeing Lord, the God of Israel, struck him with an incurable and unseen plague. He had only just finished speaking when an unrelenting pain in his bowels and bitter internal torments seized him—
    2 Maccabees 9:6 most justly, since he had tormented the bowels of others with many strange afflictions.
    2 Maccabees 9:7 Yet he did not cease his haughtiness at all, but was still filled with arrogance, breathing fire in his anger against the Jews and ordering the journey hastened. He happened to fall from the chariot as it rushed along, and in the severe fall all the members of his body were wrenched.
    2 Maccabees 9:8 He who just now, in arrogance beyond human measure, thought he could command the waves of the sea and imagined he could weigh the mountain heights in a balance, was brought down to the ground and carried on a litter, plainly showing God’s power to everyone.
    2 Maccabees 9:9 Worms swarmed up from the impious man’s body, and while he was still alive in pain and anguish, his flesh fell away. The whole army was burdened by the stench of his decay.
    2 Maccabees 9:10 No one could carry the man who a little earlier thought he could touch the stars of heaven, because of the unbearable weight of his smell.
    2 Maccabees 9:11 Then, broken, he began to cease much of his arrogance and come to understanding, his pains increasing every moment under the divine scourge.
    2 Maccabees 9:12 Unable even to endure his own smell, he said, “It is right to submit to God and, being mortal, not to think oneself equal to God.”

Acts 12:24
But the word of God grew and multiplied.
  Acts 6:7 — thematic parallel: Both narrative summaries describe God's word growing. Acts 6 also says disciples increased and many priests obeyed the faith; Acts 12 places the growth summary after Herod's death, without transferring those particular converts to this episode.
    Acts 6:7 The word of God grew, and the number of disciples in Jerusalem increased greatly. A large number of the priests also became obedient to the faith.

Acts 12:25
Barnabas and Saul returned to Jerusalem after completing their service, and took along John who was called Mark.
  Acts 15:37–15:39 — narrative parallel: John called Mark accompanies Barnabas and Saul here; Barnabas later wants to take the same named companion again, and the dispute ends with Barnabas taking Mark to Cyprus. The comparison does not replace this unit's printed returned to Jerusalem or invent John's reason for leaving the intervening mission.
    Acts 15:37 Barnabas wanted to take along John, who was called Mark, as well.
    Acts 15:38 But Paul considered it right not to take along the man who had withdrawn from them in Pamphylia and had not gone with them to the work.
    Acts 15:39 A sharp disagreement arose, so that they separated from one another. Barnabas took Mark and sailed to Cyprus,

Acts 13:1
In the Church at Antioch there were prophets and teachers: Barnabas, Simeon who was called Niger, Lucius of Cyrene, Manaen who had been brought up with Herod the tetrarch, and Saul.
  Acts 11:25–11:27 — narrative parallel: The earlier Antioch setting places Barnabas and Saul teaching with the Church and records prophets arriving there. The present list supplies additional named prophets and teachers; it does not identify every earlier visitor or equate Lucius with Luke.
    Acts 11:25 He went away to Tarsus to search for Saul,
    Acts 11:26 and when he found him, he brought him to Antioch. For a whole year they gathered with the Church and taught a considerable crowd. It was in Antioch that the disciples were first called Christians.
    Acts 11:27 In those days prophets came down from Jerusalem to Antioch.

Acts 13:2
While they were ministering to the Lord and fasting, the Holy Spirit said, “Set apart for Me Barnabas and Saul for the work to which I have called them.”
  Acts 9:15–9:16 — narrative parallel: The Spirit's call sets Barnabas and Saul apart for work; the earlier Lord-to-Ananias message already chooses Saul to carry His name before Gentiles, kings and Israel. Barnabas is not named in that earlier commission, and the two revelations remain distinct.
    Acts 9:15 But the Lord said to him, “Go, for this man is a chosen vessel for Me, to carry My name before Gentiles and kings and the sons of Israel.
    Acts 9:16 For I will show him how much he must suffer for My name.”

Acts 13:3
Then, after fasting and praying and laying their hands upon them, they sent them away.
  Acts 6:6 — narrative parallel: Both ministry appointments involve prayer and laying on hands. The Seven are presented to the apostles in Acts 6; the Antioch community fasts and sends Barnabas and Saul away here. The shared actions do not make the roles or appointers identical.
    Acts 6:6 They presented them before the apostles, who prayed and laid their hands upon them.

Acts 13:4
So, sent out by the Holy Spirit, they went down to Seleucia and from there sailed to Cyprus.
  Acts 13:2–13:3 — narrative parallel: The narrator's sent out by the Holy Spirit directly resumes the Spirit's call and the community's sending after prayer and fasting. Divine commissioning and human sending belong to this same mission, while Seleucia and Cyprus are the ensuing route.
    Acts 13:2 While they were ministering to the Lord and fasting, the Holy Spirit said, “Set apart for Me Barnabas and Saul for the work to which I have called them.”
    Acts 13:3 Then, after fasting and praying and laying their hands upon them, they sent them away.

Acts 13:5
When they came to Salamis, they proclaimed the word of God in the Jewish synagogues. They also had John as an assistant.
  Acts 12:25 — narrative parallel: The preceding narrative says Barnabas and Saul took John called Mark with them; the Cyprus mission now has John as an assistant. The continuous party context supplies the companion link without identifying this John with the apostle or making a Gospel-authorship claim.
    Acts 12:25 Barnabas and Saul returned to Jerusalem after completing their service, and took along John who was called Mark.

Acts 13:6
They traveled through the whole island as far as Paphos and found a certain magician, a Jewish false prophet named Bar-Jesus,
  Acts 13:8 — narrative parallel: The magician introduced as the Jewish false prophet Bar-Jesus continues as the proconsul's opposing magician Elymas. The link follows the immediate person-and-role continuity; it does not invent an etymology translating Bar-Jesus into Elymas or identify him with Jesus Christ.
    Acts 13:8 But Elymas the magician—for that is how his name is translated—opposed them, seeking to turn the proconsul away from the faith.

Acts 13:7
who was with the proconsul Sergius Paulus, an intelligent man. The proconsul summoned Barnabas and Saul and sought to hear the word of God.
  Acts 13:12 — narrative parallel: Sergius Paulus first summons the missionaries because he wants God's word; the later proconsul believes and is astonished at the Lord's teaching. This is the same hearer's narrated response, not evidence that Sergius is Saul or that the miracle alone replaces the teaching.
    Acts 13:12 When the proconsul saw what had happened, he believed, astonished at the teaching of the Lord.

Acts 13:8
But Elymas the magician—for that is how his name is translated—opposed them, seeking to turn the proconsul away from the faith.
  Acts 13:12 — narrative parallel: Elymas tries to turn the proconsul away from faith, but the closing account says the proconsul believes. This direct purpose-and-outcome contrast concerns the official's response; it does not narrate Elymas's conversion.
    Acts 13:12 When the proconsul saw what had happened, he believed, astonished at the teaching of the Lord.

Acts 13:9
Saul, who was also called Paul, filled with the Holy Spirit, looked intently at him
  Acts 9:17 — narrative parallel: Ananias's earlier message to Saul includes being filled with the Holy Spirit; the narrative now explicitly describes Saul, also called Paul, as Spirit-filled. The connection does not turn the present alias notice into a renaming ceremony at conversion.
    Acts 9:17 Ananias went and entered the house. Laying his hands upon him, he said, “Brother Saul, the Lord has sent me—Jesus, who appeared to you on the road as you came—so that you may regain your sight and be filled with the Holy Spirit.”

Acts 13:10
and said, “You who are full of all deceit and all villainy, son of the devil, enemy of all righteousness—will you not stop making crooked the Lord’s straight ways?
  John 8:44 — thematic parallel: Paul combines son-of-the-devil language with deceit in addressing Elymas; Jesus likewise connects devil-parentage with falsehood in addressing His opponents. The comparison concerns these moral characterizations of particular addressees, not biological descent or a blanket ethnic judgment.
    John 8:44 You are from your father, the devil, and you want to carry out your father’s desires. He was a murderer from the beginning and has not stood in the truth, because there is no truth in him. When he speaks the lie, he speaks from what is his own, because he is a liar and its father.

Acts 13:11
Now look, the Lord’s hand is upon you, and you will be blind, unable to see the sun for a time.” Immediately mist and darkness fell upon him, and he went about seeking people to lead him by the hand.
  Acts 9:8–9:9 — narrative parallel: Elymas becomes unable to see and seeks hand-leading; Saul earlier could not see and was led by the hand into Damascus. Saul's three days are specified, whereas Elymas's prediction says for a time and no later restoration is narrated here.
    Acts 9:8 Saul rose from the ground, but although his eyes were open, he could see nothing. They led him by the hand and brought him into Damascus.
    Acts 9:9 For three days he could not see, and he neither ate nor drank.

Acts 13:12
When the proconsul saw what had happened, he believed, astonished at the teaching of the Lord.
  Acts 13:7–13:8 — narrative parallel: The proconsul's belief resolves the earlier contrast between his desire to hear God's word and Elymas's attempt to turn him from faith. The closing emphasis on the Lord's teaching remains alongside what he saw, rather than being reduced to admiration of magic.
    Acts 13:7 who was with the proconsul Sergius Paulus, an intelligent man. The proconsul summoned Barnabas and Saul and sought to hear the word of God.
    Acts 13:8 But Elymas the magician—for that is how his name is translated—opposed them, seeking to turn the proconsul away from the faith.

Acts 13:13
Paul and his companions sailed from Paphos and came to Perga in Pamphylia. John left them and returned to Jerusalem.
  Acts 15:37–15:38 — narrative parallel: The later dispute explicitly recalls John Mark's withdrawal in Pamphylia, corresponding to John's departure from Perga and return to Jerusalem here. Paul's later assessment is reported as his assessment; neither passage supplies John's private reason for leaving.
    Acts 15:37 Barnabas wanted to take along John, who was called Mark, as well.
    Acts 15:38 But Paul considered it right not to take along the man who had withdrawn from them in Pamphylia and had not gone with them to the work.

Acts 13:14
They continued from Perga and arrived at Pisidian Antioch. On the Sabbath day they entered the synagogue and sat down.
  Acts 17:1–17:2 — narrative parallel: Paul's entry into the Pisidian Antioch synagogue on the Sabbath parallels his later customary synagogue reasoning at Thessalonica. The later three Sabbaths, city and missionary company are not transferred to this first visit.
    Acts 17:1 After traveling through Amphipolis and Apollonia, they came to Thessalonica, where there was a Jewish synagogue.
    Acts 17:2 As was his custom, Paul went in to them, and for three Sabbaths he reasoned with them from the Scriptures,

Acts 13:15
After the reading of the law and the prophets, the synagogue rulers sent word to them, saying, “Men, brothers, if you have a word of encouragement for the people, speak.”
  Luke 4:16–4:21 — narrative parallel: Scripture reading in a Sabbath synagogue becomes the occasion for an address in both accounts. Acts names law and prophets and an invitation to encourage; Luke narrates Jesus reading Isaiah and announcing fulfillment, without recording the same invitation or a law reading.
    Luke 4:16 He came to Nazareth, where He had been brought up, and according to His custom entered the synagogue on the Sabbath day. He stood up to read.
    Luke 4:17 The scroll of the prophet Isaiah was given to Him. Unrolling it, He found the place where it was written:
    Luke 4:18 “The Spirit of the Lord is upon Me, because He anointed Me to proclaim good news to the poor. He has sent Me to proclaim release to captives and recovery of sight to the blind, to send the oppressed away in release,
    Luke 4:19 to proclaim the Lord’s acceptable year.”
    Luke 4:20 He rolled up the scroll, gave it back to the attendant, and sat down. The eyes of everyone in the synagogue were fixed on Him.
    Luke 4:21 He began to say to them, “Today this Scripture has been fulfilled in your ears.”

Acts 13:16
Paul rose, motioned with his hand, and said, “Men of Israel and you who fear God, listen.
  Acts 21:40 — narrative parallel: Paul uses a hand gesture before addressing the synagogue audience; later he stands on steps, gestures and addresses a silenced crowd. The formal speech-preface pattern is shared, while Acts 13 does not specify the later Hebrew language or crowd-silence response.
    Acts 21:40 When he gave permission, Paul stood on the steps and motioned with his hand to the people. When a great silence fell, he addressed them in the Hebrew language, saying:

Acts 13:17
The God of this people Israel chose our fathers and exalted the people during their stay as strangers in the land of Egypt. With an uplifted arm He led them out of it,
  Deuteronomy 7:6–7:8 — allusion: Paul's history joins God's choice, the fathers and Egypt's deliverance by an uplifted arm. Deuteronomy joins Israel's election, the oath to its fathers and redemption from Pharaoh with a mighty hand and uplifted arm. This is related historical-covenant language rather than an identical quotation.
    Deuteronomy 7:6 For you are a holy people unto the Lord your God; and the Lord your God has chosen you to be unto Him a people for His own possession, above all the nations upon the face of the earth.
    Deuteronomy 7:7 The Lord preferred you and chose you, not because you were more numerous than all the nations, for you are the fewest of all the nations,
    Deuteronomy 7:8 but because the Lord loved you, and kept the oath which He sware unto your fathers, the Lord brought you forth with a mighty hand and with an uplifted arm, and redeemed you out of the house of bondage, out of the hand of Pharaoh king of Egypt.

Acts 13:18
and for about forty years He put up with their conduct in the wilderness.
  Psalms 94:10 — thematic parallel: The wilderness summary says God put up with the people's conduct for about forty years; the psalm describes His vexation with a straying generation for forty years. Their conduct-and-duration correspondence is retained without replacing Paul's about with an exact chronology.
    Psalms 94:10 For forty years I was vexed with that generation and said, They always stray in heart, and they did not know My ways,

Acts 13:19
He overthrew seven nations in the land of Canaan and gave their land as an inheritance,
  Deuteronomy 7:1 — allusion: Paul looks back on seven Canaanite nations being overthrown and their land inherited. Deuteronomy prospectively names seven nations to be removed as Israel enters its inheritance. The prediction and later summary have different narrative stages.
    Deuteronomy 7:1 And when the Lord your God brings you into the land whither you go to inherit it, and removes great nations from before you, the Hittites, and the Girgashites, and the Amorites, and the Canaanites, and the Perizzites, and the Hivites, and the Jebusites, seven nations numerous and mightier than you,

Acts 13:20
over about four hundred and fifty years. After these things He gave judges until Samuel the prophet.
  Judges 2:16–2:18 — thematic parallel: God's giving judges corresponds to the account of the Lord repeatedly raising judges and rescuing Israel through them. This supports the judges clause, not a calculation assigning the preceding four-hundred-and-fifty-year continuation to the judges' entire duration.
    Judges 2:16 The Lord raised up judges for them and saved them from the hand of their plunderers.
    Judges 2:17 Yet they did not listen even to their judges, for they prostituted themselves after other gods, bowed down to them, and provoked the Lord. They quickly turned aside from the way their fathers had walked in obeying the Lord’s commandments. They did not do as their fathers had done.
    Judges 2:18 Whenever the Lord raised up judges for them, the Lord was with the judge and saved them from the hand of their enemies all the days of the judge. For the Lord was moved by their groaning before those who besieged and harmed them.
  1 Samuel 7:15 — thematic parallel: The historical sequence reaches Samuel the prophet; Samuel is explicitly said to judge Israel throughout his life. This supplies his place in the judging history without establishing the four-hundred-and-fifty-year figure or every chronological boundary.
    1 Samuel 7:15 Samuel judged Israel throughout his life.

Acts 13:21
Then they asked for a king, and God gave them Saul son of Kish, a man of the tribe of Benjamin, for forty years.
  1 Samuel 10:20–10:24 — narrative parallel: Samuel's selection account identifies Benjamin, Saul son of Kish and the Lord's choice of a king. These match the named king, father and tribe in Paul's summary. The selected Samuel passage does not independently state a forty-year reign.
    1 Samuel 10:20 Samuel brought all Israel’s tribes near, and Benjamin’s tribe was selected by lot.
    1 Samuel 10:21 He brought Benjamin’s tribe near by clans. Mattari’s clan was selected by lot. They brought Mattari’s clan near man by man, and Saul son of Kish was selected by lot. He looked for him, but he could not be found.
