    1 Samuel 10:22 Samuel inquired of the Lord again, “Is the man coming here?” The Lord said, “Behold, he has hidden among the baggage.”
    1 Samuel 10:23 He ran and brought him from there and stationed him among the people. He rose above all the people from his shoulders upward.
    1 Samuel 10:24 Samuel said to all the people, “Have you seen the one the Lord has chosen for Himself? There is no one like him among all of you.” All the people acknowledged it and said, “May the king live!”

Acts 13:22
After removing him, He raised up David as king for them, to whom He also bore witness, saying, ‘I have found David son of Jesse, a man after My heart, who will do all My will.’
  Psalms 88:21 — quotation: The beginning of the composite testimony, I have found David, corresponds to the psalm's God-speaking declaration that He found David His servant and anointed him. The psalm does not contain the whole Jesse-and-heart-and-will sentence quoted in Acts.
    Psalms 88:21 I found David My servant; with My holy oil I anointed him,
  1 Samuel 13:14 — allusion: Samuel announces that the Lord will seek a man according to His heart after Saul's disobedience; Acts applies that description explicitly to David. Samuel's statement does not yet name David, and its third-person wording is not the entire first-person testimony in Acts.
    1 Samuel 13:14 But now your kingdom will not stand. The Lord will seek for Himself a man according to His heart, and the Lord will appoint him as ruler over His people, because you have not kept everything the Lord commanded you.”

Acts 13:23
From this man’s seed, according to promise, God brought Israel a Savior, Jesus,
  Psalms 131:11 — thematic parallel: The claim that Jesus comes from David's seed according to promise has concrete Davidic-promise background in God's oath to seat David's bodily offspring on his throne. The psalm itself does not name Jesus or narrate His resurrection.
    Psalms 131:11 The Lord swore truth to David, and He will certainly not annul it: “From the fruit of your belly I will set one on your throne.
  Luke 1:31–1:33 — teaching parallel: The announcement to Mary names Jesus and promises Him David's throne and an everlasting reign. Paul's proclamation identifies the promised Davidic Savior as Jesus. The shared named promise does not derive Mary's genealogy from these verses.
    Luke 1:31 And behold, you will conceive in your womb and bear a Son, and you shall name Him Jesus.
    Luke 1:32 He will be great and will be called Son of the Most High. The Lord God will give Him the throne of His father David,
    Luke 1:33 and He will reign over Jacob’s house forever, and His kingdom will have no end.”

Acts 13:24
after John had proclaimed, before His coming, a baptism of repentance to all the people of Israel.
  Luke 3:3 — narrative parallel: Paul summarizes John proclaiming a baptism of repentance before Jesus's coming; Luke narrates John's Jordan-region proclamation of a baptism of repentance for forgiveness. The ministry is John's preparatory work, not John Mark's activity or a claim that it preceded Jesus's birth.
    Luke 3:3 He went into all the region around the Jordan, proclaiming a baptism of repentance for the forgiveness of sins,

Acts 13:25
As John was completing his course, he said, ‘Who do you think I am? I am not that one. But look, One is coming after me, whose sandals I am not worthy to untie.’
  John 1:20–1:27 — narrative parallel: John's denial and unworthiness to untie the coming One's sandals correspond to the Gospel's not-the-Christ declaration and sandal-strap saying. Acts has John asking what others think he is; the Gospel records the envoys' questions. Their dialogue form and questioners are not identical.
    John 1:20 And he confessed and did not deny, and confessed, “I am not the Christ.”
    John 1:21 They asked him, “What, then? Are you Elijah?” And he said, “I am not.” “Are you the Prophet?” And he answered, “No.”
    John 1:22 So they said to him, “Who are you, so that we may give an answer to those who sent us? What do you say about yourself?”
    John 1:23 He said, “I am a voice of one crying in the wilderness, ‘Make straight the way of the Lord,’ just as Isaiah the prophet said.”
    John 1:24 Now those sent were from the Pharisees.
    John 1:25 And they questioned him and said to him, “Why, then, do you baptize if you are not the Christ, nor Elijah, nor the Prophet?”
    John 1:26 John answered them, saying, “I baptize in water. Among you stands One whom you do not know,
    John 1:27 the One coming after me, whose sandal strap I am not worthy to untie.”

Acts 13:26
Men, brothers, sons of Abraham’s family, and those among you who fear God: to us the message of this salvation has been sent.
  Acts 13:16 — thematic parallel: Paul renews the speech's twofold address: Israel and God-fearers at the opening, Abraham's family and God-fearers here. This is a repeated audience designation within the same speech, not a claim that every God-fearer is a biological descendant of Abraham.
    Acts 13:16 Paul rose, motioned with his hand, and said, “Men of Israel and you who fear God, listen.

Acts 13:27
For those living in Jerusalem and their rulers, failing to recognize Him and the voices of the prophets read every Sabbath, fulfilled those voices by condemning Him.
  Acts 3:17–3:18 — teaching parallel: Both apostolic sermons connect the Jerusalem actors' and rulers' ignorance with fulfillment of the prophets through Christ's suffering. Peter's earlier address and Paul's synagogue sermon are distinct presentations, not a judgment that every Jewish person acted identically.
    Acts 3:17 And now, brothers, I know that you acted in ignorance, as did your rulers.
    Acts 3:18 But what God announced beforehand through the mouth of all the prophets—that His Christ would suffer—He has fulfilled in this way.

Acts 13:28
Although they found no cause for death, they asked Pilate to have Him killed.
  Luke 23:21–23:24 — narrative parallel: Luke records Pilate finding no ground for death while the accusers press for crucifixion and their demand is granted. This corresponds to Paul's no-death-cause yet execution-request summary, without claiming that the accusers themselves verbally admitted Pilate's finding.
    Luke 23:21 But they kept shouting, saying, “Crucify, crucify Him!”
    Luke 23:22 And for the third time he said to them, “Why, what evil has this man done? I found no ground for death in Him. Therefore I will punish Him and release Him.”
    Luke 23:23 But they pressed him with loud voices, demanding that He be crucified, and their voices prevailed.
    Luke 23:24 And Pilate decided that their demand should be granted.

Acts 13:29
When they had completed everything written about Him, they took Him down from the tree and laid Him in a tomb.
  Luke 23:50–23:53 — narrative parallel: The removal of Jesus's body and burial correspond to Luke's account of Joseph taking it down and laying it in a tomb. Luke identifies Joseph as a council member who had not consented to the decision; the link does not assign the burial personally to every preceding hostile accuser.
    Luke 23:50 And behold, there was a man named Joseph, a council member, a good and righteous man.
    Luke 23:51 He had not consented to their decision and action. He was from Arimathea, a city of the Jews, and was awaiting the kingdom of God.
    Luke 23:52 This man approached Pilate and asked for the body of Jesus.
    Luke 23:53 Taking it down, he wrapped it in linen and laid Him in a rock-cut tomb where no one had yet been laid.

Acts 13:30
But God raised Him from the dead.
  Acts 2:24 — teaching parallel: Paul's proclamation that God raised Jesus from the dead repeats Peter's earlier assertion of God raising Him and releasing death's hold. These are shared apostolic resurrection affirmations within Acts, not independently verified modern observations.
    Acts 2:24 God raised Him, releasing Him from the pangs of death, because it was impossible for Him to be held by it.

Acts 13:31
For many days He appeared to those who had gone up with Him from Galilee to Jerusalem, who are now His witnesses to the people.
  Acts 1:3 — narrative parallel: The earlier account says Jesus presented Himself alive after suffering and appeared over forty days; Paul likewise speaks of appearances over many days to His companions. Paul's wording does not specify forty here or restrict every resurrection witness to one identical named list.
    Acts 1:3 To them He also presented Himself alive after His suffering, with many proofs, appearing to them over forty days and speaking about the kingdom of God.

Acts 13:32
We proclaim to you the good news of the promise made to the fathers:
  Acts 26:6–26:8 — teaching parallel: Paul later again centers his explanation on God's promise to the fathers and hope that includes God raising the dead. The same promise-and-resurrection theme occurs in a trial rather than this synagogue proclamation, with different hearers and circumstances.
    Acts 26:6 Now I stand on trial because of hope in the promise made by God to our fathers,
    Acts 26:7 which our twelve tribes, earnestly worshiping night and day, hope to attain. It is concerning this hope that I am accused by Jews, O king.
    Acts 26:8 Why do you consider it unbelievable that God raises the dead?

Acts 13:33
God has fulfilled this for us, their children, by raising up Jesus, as it is also written in the second psalm: ‘Thou art My Son; today I have begotten Thee.’
  Psalms 2:7 — quotation: Acts explicitly identifies the second psalm and quotes the Lord's sonship-and-today-begetting declaration. The supplied psalm has You are My son where Acts has Thou art My Son; the correspondence is preserved without treating the English forms as identical or replacing raising up with extra words.
    Psalms 2:7 announcing the Lord’s decree: The Lord said to me, ‘You are My son; today I have begotten you.

Acts 13:34
God has spoken this way about raising Him from the dead, never again to return to decay: ‘I will give you the holy and sure blessings of David.’
  Isaiah 55:3 — quotation: The holy and sure blessings of David come from Isaiah's everlasting-covenant promise. Acts uses this promise within its argument for Jesus's resurrection and no return to decay; Isaiah's invitation and covenant wording do not themselves explicitly narrate that resurrection.
    Isaiah 55:3 Listen with your ears, and follow My ways; listen to Me, and your soul will live amid good things. And I will make with you an everlasting covenant: the holy and sure blessings of David.

Acts 13:35
Therefore He also says in another place, ‘Thou wilt not allow Thy Holy One to see decay.’
  Psalms 15:10 — quotation: The Holy One's not seeing decay corresponds to the psalm's promise not to let the holy one see corruption, alongside not abandoning the soul to Hades. Acts applies the cited promise to the raised Jesus; the supplied decay and corruption renderings are retained.
    Psalms 15:10 for Thou wilt not abandon my soul to Hades, nor let Thy holy one see corruption.

Acts 13:36
For David, after serving his own generation according to God’s purpose, fell asleep, was gathered to his fathers, and saw decay.
  Acts 2:29–2:31 — teaching parallel: Both sermons distinguish David's death and burial from the resurrection promise concerning Christ. Paul adds serving his generation and seeing decay; Peter mentions the tomb and prophetic foresight. Their related argument is not an identical account of all details.
    Acts 2:29 Men, brothers, I may speak openly to you about the patriarch David: he both died and was buried, and his tomb is among us to this day.
    Acts 2:30 Since he was a prophet and knew that God had sworn an oath to him to seat one from the fruit of his loins upon his throne,
    Acts 2:31 he foresaw and spoke about the resurrection of the Christ: He was neither abandoned to Hades, nor did His flesh see decay.

Acts 13:37
But the One whom God raised did not see decay.
  Acts 2:31–2:32 — teaching parallel: Peter likewise joins Christ's flesh not seeing decay with God raising Jesus. Paul contrasts the raised One with David's decay in the preceding unit. Both affirm the resurrection reading of the promise without providing a new independent inspection of either body.
    Acts 2:31 he foresaw and spoke about the resurrection of the Christ: He was neither abandoned to Hades, nor did His flesh see decay.
    Acts 2:32 God raised this Jesus, and all of us are witnesses of it.

Acts 13:38
Therefore let it be known to you, men, brothers, that through Him forgiveness of sins is proclaimed to you; and from everything from which you could not be justified in the law of Moses,
  Acts 10:43 — teaching parallel: Forgiveness of sins proclaimed through Jesus corresponds to Peter's promise of forgiveness through His name for everyone believing in Him. This target supports the forgiveness clause; Paul's law-and-justification contrast continues grammatically into the next unit.
    Acts 10:43 All the prophets bear witness to Him: everyone who believes in Him receives forgiveness of sins through His name.”

Acts 13:39
in Him everyone who believes is justified.
  Galatians 2:16 — teaching parallel: Both passages connect justification with believing in Christ rather than law as its basis. Acts speaks of everything from which one could not be justified in Moses's law; Galatians states its works-of-law contrast explicitly. The related formulations are not rewritten into one identical sentence.
    Galatians 2:16 yet knowing that a human being is not justified by works of law but through faith in Jesus Christ, we too believed in Christ Jesus, so that we might be justified by faith in Christ and not by works of law, because by works of law no flesh will be justified.

Acts 13:40
Take care, then, lest what is spoken in the prophets come upon you:
  Habakkuk 1:5 — allusion: This introduction to the following prophetic warning is concretely anchored by the scoffers/despisers, marveling and unbelievable-work wording quoted next. Habakkuk's own setting announces the Chaldeans, rather than explicitly narrating the present resurrection proclamation.
    Habakkuk 1:5 Look, you despisers, observe, marvel at wonders and vanish! For I am doing a work in your days that you will certainly not believe if someone recounts it.

Acts 13:41
‘Look, you scoffers, marvel and vanish, for I am doing a work in your days, a work you will never believe even if someone recounts it to you.’”
  Habakkuk 1:5 — quotation: The warning shares the despisers or scoffers address, marvel-and-vanish command and work unbelieved even when reported. Acts has a shorter adapted formulation; the Greek-edition Habakkuk says despisers rather than replacing it with a different edition's among-the-nations wording.
    Habakkuk 1:5 Look, you despisers, observe, marvel at wonders and vanish! For I am doing a work in your days that you will certainly not believe if someone recounts it.

Acts 13:42
As they went out, the people urged that these words be spoken to them on the next Sabbath.
  Acts 13:44 — narrative parallel: The request to hear these words on the next Sabbath is followed by the coming-Sabbath gathering to hear the Lord's word. The latter's almost-whole-city attendance is its own expanded audience description, not a statement that every requester or attendee believed.
    Acts 13:44 On the coming Sabbath, almost the whole city gathered to hear the word of the Lord.

Acts 13:43
After the synagogue gathering was dismissed, many Jews and devout converts followed Paul and Barnabas, who spoke with them and urged them to remain in the grace of God.
  Acts 11:23 — teaching parallel: Paul and Barnabas urge those following them to remain in God's grace; Barnabas earlier encouraged Antioch believers to remain with the Lord after seeing God's grace. Pisidian Antioch and the earlier commissioning community's Antioch are distinct settings.
    Acts 11:23 When he arrived and saw the grace of God, he rejoiced and encouraged them all to remain with the Lord with steadfast purpose of heart,

Acts 13:44
On the coming Sabbath, almost the whole city gathered to hear the word of the Lord.
  Acts 13:42 — narrative parallel: The coming-Sabbath gathering follows the earlier explicit request for these words on the next Sabbath. This links the requested hearing with its narrated occasion, preserving almost the whole city rather than asserting the entire city converted.
    Acts 13:42 As they went out, the people urged that these words be spoken to them on the next Sabbath.

Acts 13:45
When the Jews saw the crowds, they were filled with jealousy and contradicted what Paul was saying, speaking abusively.
  Acts 5:17–5:18 — narrative parallel: Jealousy prompts opposition to the apostolic mission in both episodes. The earlier high priest's Sadducee party imprisons apostles; the present opponents contradict Paul abusively. These groups, actions and places differ, and Jews who followed the missionaries earlier are not included automatically among these opponents.
    Acts 5:17 The high priest and all who were with him—the party of the Sadducees—rose up, filled with jealousy.
    Acts 5:18 They laid hands on the apostles and placed them in the public prison.

Acts 13:46
Paul and Barnabas spoke boldly and said, “It was necessary for God’s word to be spoken to you first. Since you reject it and judge yourselves unworthy of eternal life, look—we are turning to the Gentiles.
  Acts 18:5–18:6 — narrative parallel: Paul later responds to abusive opposition while witnessing to Jews by announcing a turn to Gentiles. Both are local missionary responses; neither comparison turns them into a permanent abandonment of every Jewish hearer, since the narrative continues synagogue ministry.
    Acts 18:5 When Silas and Timothy came down from Macedonia, Paul devoted himself to the word, bearing solemn witness to the Jews that Jesus is the Christ.
    Acts 18:6 When they opposed him and spoke abusively, he shook out his garments and said to them, “Your blood be upon your own heads. I am clean. From now on I will go to the Gentiles.”

Acts 13:47
For the Lord has commanded us in this way: ‘I have appointed you as a light for Gentiles, so that you may bring salvation to the end of the earth.’”
  Isaiah 49:6 — quotation: The light-for-nations and salvation-to-earth's-end commission corresponds to Isaiah's servant saying. Acts explicitly applies the command to us in the missionary speech; Isaiah's fuller wording also concerns Jacob, Israel's dispersion and a covenant, not Paul and Barnabas named there.
    Isaiah 49:6 and He said to me, “It is a great thing for you to be called My servant, to establish Jacob’s tribes and bring back Israel’s dispersion. Behold, I have appointed you as a covenant for a race, a light for nations, so that you may bring salvation to the earth’s extremity.”

Acts 13:48
When the Gentiles heard this, they rejoiced and glorified the word of the Lord, and as many as had been appointed to eternal life believed.
  Acts 11:18 — thematic parallel: Gentile inclusion is associated with life and praise in both accounts. Here Gentiles rejoice, glorify the word and believe with appointed-to-life wording; the earlier Jerusalem listeners glorify God for Gentile repentance leading to life. The different subjects and formulations do not yield an added predestination scheme.
    Acts 11:18 When they heard these things, they fell silent and glorified God, saying, “Then God has granted even the Gentiles repentance leading to life.”

Acts 13:49
The word of the Lord spread through the whole region.
  Acts 19:10 — narrative parallel: Both summaries describe the Lord's word reaching a broad region through missionary proclamation. The later account specifies two years and Asia's Jewish and Greek residents; those duration and geographical details are not assigned to the present whole-region report.
    Acts 19:10 This continued for two years, so that all who lived in Asia heard the word of the Lord, both Jews and Greeks.

Acts 13:50
But the Jews stirred up the devout women of high standing and the leading men of the city, raised persecution against Paul and Barnabas, and drove them out of their territory.
  Acts 17:5–17:9 — narrative parallel: Jewish opponents mobilize local people and civic influence against missionaries in both episodes. Acts 17 names a mob, Jason and city officials, while this account names devout high-standing women, leading men and expulsion. It does not give the same actors or identical departure mechanism.
    Acts 17:5 But the Jews became jealous and took some wicked men from the marketplace, gathered a mob, and threw the city into turmoil. They attacked Jason’s house and sought to bring Paul and Silas out to the populace.
    Acts 17:6 When they did not find them, they dragged Jason and some brothers before the city officials, shouting, “These men who have upset the inhabited world have come here too,
    Acts 17:7 and Jason has welcomed them. They all act against Caesar’s decrees, saying that there is another king, Jesus.”
    Acts 17:8 They disturbed the crowd and the city officials who heard these things.
    Acts 17:9 After taking security from Jason and the others, the officials released them.

Acts 13:51
They shook the dust from their feet against them and went to Iconium.
  Luke 9:5 — allusion: The missionaries' shaking dust from their feet against the rejecting group follows the concrete mission-response pattern Jesus instructed when a city did not receive His messengers. The Lukan instruction initially addresses the Twelve, not a claim that Paul and Barnabas were in that original group.
    Luke 9:5 Wherever people do not receive you, as you leave that city shake the dust from your feet as a testimony against them.”

Acts 13:52
The disciples were filled with joy and the Holy Spirit.
  1 Thessalonians 1:6 — thematic parallel: The disciples' joy and Holy Spirit filling close a persecution episode; the Thessalonians receive the word in great affliction with joy from the Holy Spirit. The passages associate joy with the Spirit amid opposition in different communities; only the epistle explicitly describes the joy as from the Spirit. This does not identify every disciple here with the departing pair.
    1 Thessalonians 1:6 And you became imitators of us and of the Lord, receiving the word in great affliction with joy from the Holy Spirit,

Acts 14:1
In Iconium they entered the Jewish synagogue together and spoke in such a way that a great multitude of both Jews and Greeks believed.
  Acts 18:4 — narrative parallel: The synagogue mission addresses both Jews and Greeks in each account. Iconium has a great multitude believing, whereas the later Corinth account says Paul sought to persuade them every Sabbath. The outcome and missionary company are not made identical.
    Acts 18:4 Every Sabbath he reasoned in the synagogue and sought to persuade both Jews and Greeks.

Acts 14:2
But the Jews who refused to believe stirred up the Gentiles and embittered their minds against the brothers.
  Acts 17:13 — narrative parallel: Unbelieving opponents stir hostility against the mission; later Thessalonian Jews also stir and disturb crowds when Paul's word reaches Berea. The latter is another city's opposition, not an assertion that the Jewish believers just named in Iconium opposed their own faith.
    Acts 17:13 But when the Jews from Thessalonica learned that the word of God had also been proclaimed by Paul in Berea, they came there too, stirring up and disturbing the crowds.

Acts 14:3
So they stayed a considerable time, speaking boldly in reliance on the Lord, who bore witness to the word of His grace by granting signs and wonders to take place through their hands.
  Hebrews 2:3–2:4 — thematic parallel: The Lord attests the word of grace with signs and wonders through the missionaries; Hebrews likewise describes God bearing witness to the saving proclamation through signs, wonders and mighty deeds. Hebrews does not name this Iconium episode or these particular agents.
    Hebrews 2:3 how will we escape if we neglect so great a salvation? It was first spoken through the Lord and was confirmed to us by those who heard,
    Hebrews 2:4 God bearing witness together with signs and wonders and various mighty deeds and distributions of the Holy Spirit according to His own will.

Acts 14:4
The people of the city were divided: some sided with the Jews and others with the apostles.
  Acts 28:24–28:25 — narrative parallel: Iconium's city population divides between the opponents and apostles; Paul's later listeners divide between persuasion and disbelief and disagree with one another. The common divided response has different social scale and groups, not a claim that all Jewish people belong to one unbelieving side.
    Acts 28:24 Some were persuaded by what was said, while others did not believe.
    Acts 28:25 Disagreeing with one another, they began to leave after Paul made one statement: “The Holy Spirit spoke well through Isaiah the prophet to your fathers,

Acts 14:5
When an attempt was made by both Gentiles and Jews, together with their rulers, to mistreat and stone them,
  Acts 14:19 — narrative parallel: An earlier attempt seeks to stone the missionaries; later opponents from Antioch and Iconium persuade a crowd and Paul is actually stoned at Lystra. The later violence does not mean the earlier city's attempt was completed or that Barnabas was also stoned.
    Acts 14:19 Jews came from Antioch and Iconium, persuaded the crowds, stoned Paul, and dragged him outside the city, supposing he was dead.

Acts 14:6
they learned of it and fled to Lystra and Derbe, cities of Lycaonia, and the surrounding region.
  Matthew 10:23 — thematic parallel: Learning of the persecution attempt, the missionaries flee to other cities. Jesus's instruction likewise says to flee from one persecuting city to another. Its accompanying saying about Israel's cities and the Son of Man is not used to date this journey.
    Matthew 10:23 When they persecute you in this city, flee to the other. For truly I tell you, you will certainly not finish going through the cities of Israel until the Son of Man comes.

Acts 14:7
There they continued proclaiming the good news.
  Acts 8:4 — narrative parallel: The missionaries continue good-news proclamation after fleeing persecution; earlier scattered believers likewise go about proclaiming the word as good news. The shared movement-and-continued-preaching pattern concerns different groups and stages of the mission.
    Acts 8:4 Those who were scattered went about proclaiming the word as good news.

Acts 14:8
In Lystra a man was sitting who had no strength in his feet, unable to walk from his mother’s womb; he had never walked.
  Acts 3:2 — narrative parallel: Both healing accounts introduce a man unable to walk from his mother's womb. The Jerusalem man is carried to beg at the temple gate; the Lystra man is said never to have walked. The men and locations remain distinct.
    Acts 3:2 A man unable to walk from his mother’s womb was being carried there. They used to place him each day at the temple gate called Beautiful, to ask for alms from those entering the temple.

Acts 14:9
He listened to Paul speaking. Paul looked intently at him and saw that he had faith to be made well,
  Acts 3:4–3:5 — narrative parallel: Paul's intent look before healing parallels Peter and John's intent look at the other birth-disabled man. Acts 14 explicitly says Paul recognized healing faith; Acts 3 instead describes attention and an expectation of receiving something, not that same explicit pre-healing faith statement.
    Acts 3:4 Peter, together with John, looked intently at him and said, “Look at us.”
    Acts 3:5 He paid attention to them, expecting to receive something from them.

Acts 14:10
and said with a loud voice, “Stand upright on your feet.” He leaped up and began walking.
  Acts 3:6–3:8 — narrative parallel: An apostolic command is followed by a previously disabled man leaping and walking in both episodes. Peter explicitly uses Jesus's name and lifts by the right hand; Paul's quoted command here is stand upright, without those additional narrated actions.
    Acts 3:6 But Peter said, “Silver and gold I do not have, but what I have I give you: in the name of Jesus Christ the Nazarene, get up and walk.”
    Acts 3:7 Taking him by the right hand, he raised him up. At once his feet and ankles were strengthened.
    Acts 3:8 Leaping up, he stood and began walking. He entered the temple with them, walking and leaping and praising God.

Acts 14:11
When the crowds saw what Paul had done, they raised their voice, saying in Lycaonian, “The gods have come down to us in human likeness.”
  Acts 28:6 — narrative parallel: The crowd mistakes a sign involving Paul for evidence of divinity; islanders later likewise call Paul a god after he suffers no harm. These are the onlookers' interpretations, with gods in human likeness here and no Zeus or Hermes naming in the later verse.
    Acts 28:6 They expected him to swell up or suddenly fall down dead. After waiting a long time and seeing nothing unusual happen to him, they changed their minds and said that he was a god.

Acts 14:12
They called Barnabas Zeus and Paul Hermes, because he was the leading speaker.
  Acts 14:14–14:15 — narrative parallel: The crowd's Zeus-and-Hermes labels are directly answered by the named apostles Barnabas and Paul declaring themselves fellow human beings. The report of what the crowd called them is not the narrator's endorsement that they are gods.
    Acts 14:14 When the apostles Barnabas and Paul heard this, they tore their garments and rushed out into the crowd, crying out
    Acts 14:15 and saying, “Men, why are you doing these things? We too are human beings, of the same nature as you. We proclaim good news to you: turn from these worthless things to the living God, who made heaven and earth and the sea and everything in them.

Acts 14:13
The priest of Zeus, whose shrine was before the city, brought bulls and garlands to the gates and wanted to offer sacrifice with the crowds.
  Acts 14:18 — narrative parallel: The priest and crowd want to sacrifice to the missionaries; the later statement says the apostles' words scarcely stop the crowds from sacrificing. This is the same attempted rite and its restrained outcome, not an apostolically approved offering.
    Acts 14:18 Even by saying these things, they scarcely stopped the crowds from sacrificing to them.

Acts 14:14
When the apostles Barnabas and Paul heard this, they tore their garments and rushed out into the crowd, crying out
  2 Kings 5:7–5:8 — thematic parallel: The apostles tear garments before rejecting divine identification in a healing setting; Israel's king tears garments and asks Am I God when asked to cure Naaman. The common gesture and God-human distinction have different motives: refusal of worship here, political alarm about the letter there.
    2 Kings 5:7 When the king of Israel read the letter, he tore his garments and said, ‘Am I God, to put to death and bring to life, that this man sends to me to free a man from his leprosy? Please understand and see: this man is looking for a pretext against me.’
    2 Kings 5:8 When Elisha heard that the king of Israel had torn his garments, he sent word to the king of Israel, saying, ‘Why have you torn your garments? Please let Naaman come to me, and let him know that there is a prophet in Israel.’

Acts 14:15
and saying, “Men, why are you doing these things? We too are human beings, of the same nature as you. We proclaim good news to you: turn from these worthless things to the living God, who made heaven and earth and the sea and everything in them.
  Acts 10:25–10:26 — narrative parallel: Peter raises Cornelius after he bows before him and says he too is human; Barnabas and Paul likewise answer the attempted sacrifice by asserting shared humanity. Bowing at feet and offering sacrifice are different actions, not a declaration that every gesture of respect is identical.
    Acts 10:25 As Peter was about to enter, Cornelius met him, fell at his feet, and bowed before him.
    Acts 10:26 But Peter raised him up, saying, “Rise; I too am a human being.”
  Psalms 145:6 — allusion: The living God's making heaven, earth, sea and everything in them closely matches the psalm's Creator formula. Acts does not name the psalm as its exclusive source, and its call to turn from worthless things is the missionary speech's own setting.
    Psalms 145:6 who made heaven and earth, the sea and all that is in them, who keeps truth for ever,

Acts 14:16
In past generations He allowed all the nations to go their own ways.
  Acts 17:30 — teaching parallel: The Gentile addresses describe earlier divine forbearance before the present call to turn to God: allowing nations their ways here, overlooking times of ignorance and now commanding repentance later. The wording and fuller Athens argument remain distinct.
    Acts 17:30 Therefore, having overlooked the times of ignorance, God now commands all people everywhere to repent,

Acts 14:17
Yet He did not leave Himself without witness in doing good, giving you rains from heaven and fruitful seasons, filling your hearts with food and gladness.”
  Psalms 103:13–103:15 — thematic parallel: Both give God credit for watering the earth and producing food associated with human gladness. The psalm specifies plants, bread, wine and oil; Acts speaks of heavenly rains and fruitful seasons. The common providence does not make their lists identical.
    Psalms 103:13 He waters mountains from His upper chambers; the earth will be filled with the fruit of Thy works.
    Psalms 103:14 He makes grass spring up for livestock and green plants for the service of humans, to bring bread out of the earth;
    Psalms 103:15 and wine gladdens the human heart, to brighten the face with oil, and bread makes the human heart firm.
  Matthew 5:45 — teaching parallel: Acts says God's beneficial rain-witness continued while nations went their own ways; Jesus likewise says the Father sends rain on righteous and unrighteous. The shared providence extends beyond the faithful without identifying every nation or hearer as one moral category.
    Matthew 5:45 that you may become sons of your Father who is in the heavens; for He makes His sun rise upon the evil and the good, and sends rain upon the righteous and the unrighteous.

Acts 14:18
Even by saying these things, they scarcely stopped the crowds from sacrificing to them.
  Acts 14:13 — narrative parallel: The apostles' words scarcely stop the sacrifice that the priest and crowd had wanted to offer earlier. The specific attempted offering supplies the antecedent for this outcome, while the passage never presents it as worship accepted by the missionaries.
    Acts 14:13 The priest of Zeus, whose shrine was before the city, brought bulls and garlands to the gates and wanted to offer sacrifice with the crowds.

Acts 14:19
Jews came from Antioch and Iconium, persuaded the crowds, stoned Paul, and dragged him outside the city, supposing he was dead.
  2 Corinthians 11:25 — narrative parallel: Paul's letter recalls being stoned once, providing a close personal-suffering parallel to the concrete Lystra stoning. The letter does not name Lystra or describe the dragging and presumed death; Acts itself says the attackers supposed him dead.
    2 Corinthians 11:25 Three times I was beaten with rods, once I was stoned, three times I was shipwrecked; I have spent a night and a day in the deep.

Acts 14:20
But when the disciples surrounded him, he rose and entered the city. The next day he went with Barnabas to Derbe.
  2 Corinthians 4:9 — thematic parallel: Paul getting up after the stoning and continuing his mission illustrates the letter's struck down but not destroyed contrast. Acts reports the attackers' supposition of death, not an explicit declaration that disciples raised a dead Paul; the letter does not name this incident.
    2 Corinthians 4:9 persecuted, but not abandoned; struck down, but not destroyed;

Acts 14:21
After proclaiming the good news to that city and making many disciples, they returned to Lystra, Iconium, and Antioch,
  Acts 14:6–14:7 — narrative parallel: The earlier flight brought the missionaries to Lystra and Derbe, where they continued preaching. After evangelizing Derbe, they return to Lystra, Iconium and Antioch. This connects outward and returning stages of the same journey while preserving the differing place lists, without treating the link as an independent map or merging the two Antiochs.
    Acts 14:6 they learned of it and fled to Lystra and Derbe, cities of Lycaonia, and the surrounding region.
    Acts 14:7 There they continued proclaiming the good news.

Acts 14:22
strengthening the souls of the disciples, encouraging them to remain in the faith, and saying, “Through many afflictions we must enter the kingdom of God.”
  2 Timothy 3:11–3:12 — teaching parallel: The letter recalls persecutions at Antioch, Iconium and Lystra and says godly life in Christ entails persecution. The missionary return to these communities likewise teaches perseverance through afflictions toward God's kingdom, without making suffering a payment that earns entry.
    2 Timothy 3:11 persecutions, sufferings, such as happened to me in Antioch, in Iconium, in Lystra, what persecutions I endured; and out of all of them the Lord rescued me.
    2 Timothy 3:12 And all who want to live godly lives in Christ Jesus will be persecuted.

Acts 14:23
They appointed elders for them in each Church and, after praying with fasting, entrusted them to the Lord in whom they had believed.
  Titus 1:5 — teaching parallel: The missionary appointment of elders in each Church parallels the instruction that Titus appoint elders in every Cretan city. The locations and recipients differ; prayer and fasting are narrated in Acts, not repeated in the selected Titus instruction.
    Titus 1:5 For this reason I left you in Crete, so that you might put in order what remained unfinished and appoint elders in every city, as I directed you—

Acts 14:24
They passed through Pisidia and came to Pamphylia.
  Acts 13:13–13:14 — narrative parallel: The earlier outward route goes through Perga in Pamphylia toward Pisidian Antioch; the returning mission now traverses Pisidia toward Pamphylia. The shared regions and reversed travel stage are explicit, without supplying unrecorded roads or dates.
    Acts 13:13 Paul and his companions sailed from Paphos and came to Perga in Pamphylia. John left them and returned to Jerusalem.
    Acts 13:14 They continued from Perga and arrived at Pisidian Antioch. On the Sabbath day they entered the synagogue and sat down.

Acts 14:25
After speaking the word in Perga, they went down to Attalia.
  Acts 13:13 — narrative parallel: Perga, where John had left the outward mission, is now a place where the returning missionaries speak the word. This is a concrete return-location link; the earlier unit does not attest Attalia, this preaching, or John's rejoining the party.
    Acts 13:13 Paul and his companions sailed from Paphos and came to Perga in Pamphylia. John left them and returned to Jerusalem.

Acts 14:26
From there they sailed to Antioch, where they had been entrusted to the grace of God for the work they had now completed.
  Acts 13:1–13:4 — narrative parallel: The return to the Antioch where they were entrusted to grace explicitly closes the work begun with the Spirit's call and the Church's sending. This is the commissioning community's Antioch, distinct from the Pisidian mission stop, with the same work now described as completed.
    Acts 13:1 In the Church at Antioch there were prophets and teachers: Barnabas, Simeon who was called Niger, Lucius of Cyrene, Manaen who had been brought up with Herod the tetrarch, and Saul.
    Acts 13:2 While they were ministering to the Lord and fasting, the Holy Spirit said, “Set apart for Me Barnabas and Saul for the work to which I have called them.”
    Acts 13:3 Then, after fasting and praying and laying their hands upon them, they sent them away.
    Acts 13:4 So, sent out by the Holy Spirit, they went down to Seleucia and from there sailed to Cyprus.

Acts 14:27
When they arrived and gathered the Church, they reported all that God had done with them and how He had opened a door of faith to the Gentiles.
  2 Corinthians 2:12 — thematic parallel: God opening a door of faith to Gentiles parallels Paul's door opened in the Lord when he came to Troas for Christ's good news. Both use an opened-door mission metaphor; the letter describes an opportunity and does not repeat this entire Gentile-conversion report.
    2 Corinthians 2:12 Now when I came to Troas for the good news of Christ, and a door had been opened to me in the Lord,

Acts 14:28
They stayed quite a long time with the disciples.
  Acts 15:35 — narrative parallel: The same pair's later remaining at Antioch with teaching and proclamation parallels the present extended stay with disciples after the mission. These are different intervals around the Jerusalem consultation, and the later verse supplies no exact duration for this earlier stay.
    Acts 15:35 Paul and Barnabas remained in Antioch, teaching and proclaiming the word of the Lord as good news, along with many others.

Acts 15:1
Some men came down from Judea and taught the brothers, “Unless you are circumcised according to the custom of Moses, you cannot be saved.”
  Galatians 5:2–5:4 — thematic contrast: The Judean teachers make Mosaic circumcision necessary for salvation. Galatians states whole-law obligation for one receiving circumcision and warns those seeking justification by law of separation from Christ. Its distinct clauses and conditions remain a separate argument rather than a verbatim report of these teachers or a blanket claim about every circumstance of circumcision.
    Galatians 5:2 Look, I, Paul, tell you that if you receive circumcision, Christ will be of no benefit to you.
    Galatians 5:3 And I testify again to every man who receives circumcision that he is obligated to do the whole law.
    Galatians 5:4 You who seek to be justified by law have been severed from Christ; you have fallen from grace.

Acts 15:2
When Paul and Barnabas had a serious disagreement and debate with them, they appointed Paul and Barnabas and some others from among them to go up to the apostles and elders in Jerusalem about this question.
  Galatians 2:1–2:2 — narrative comparison: Both passages describe Paul going to Jerusalem with Barnabas and presenting concerns about the Gentile gospel to leadership. Galatians adds Titus, a revelation and a private presentation; Acts describes an appointed delegation after public dispute. Their visits and chronology are not forcibly identified.
    Galatians 2:1 Then, after fourteen years, I went up again to Jerusalem with Barnabas, taking Titus along as well.
    Galatians 2:2 I went up in accordance with a revelation, and I set before them the gospel I proclaim among the nations, privately before those held in esteem, lest somehow I was running, or had run, in vain.

Acts 15:3
Sent on their way by the Church, they passed through Phoenicia and Samaria, recounting the conversion of the Gentiles and bringing great joy to all the brothers.
  Acts 14:27–14:28 — internal mission-report parallel: Paul and Barnabas earlier report to the Antioch Church that God opened a door of faith to the Gentiles; here their account of Gentile conversion brings joy in Phoenicia and Samaria. These are different report settings, not one gathering or a second account of every conversion.
    Acts 14:27 When they arrived and gathered the Church, they reported all that God had done with them and how He had opened a door of faith to the Gentiles.
    Acts 14:28 They stayed quite a long time with the disciples.

Acts 15:4
When they arrived in Jerusalem, they were welcomed by the Church, the apostles, and the elders, and reported all that God had done with them.
  Acts 21:17–21:19 — internal narrative parallel: Both Jerusalem arrivals involve welcome and an account of what God did through the mission. Acts15 names Paul and Barnabas reporting to the Church, apostles and elders; Acts21 describes a later arrival and Paul reporting to James with the elders. The visits and participants remain distinct.
    Acts 21:17 When we arrived in Jerusalem, the brothers welcomed us gladly.
    Acts 21:18 The next day Paul went with us to James, and all the elders were present.
    Acts 21:19 After greeting them, he related one by one the things God had done among the Gentiles through his ministry.

Acts 15:5
But some believers from the party of the Pharisees rose and said, “It is necessary to circumcise them and command them to keep the law of Moses.”
  Acts 21:25 — internal proposal-versus-decision contrast: The Pharisaic believers demand circumcision and keeping the law of Moses. The later Jerusalem statement recalls the decision sent to believing Gentiles as four abstentions, without making this proposed circumcision requirement part of its list. The proposal and the reported decision are distinguished.
    Acts 21:25 But concerning the Gentiles who have believed, we have sent word, judging that they should keep themselves from what is sacrificed to idols, blood, what is strangled, and sexual immorality.”

Acts 15:6
The apostles and the elders gathered to consider this matter.
  Acts 21:18–21:25 — internal leadership-and-decision connection: Apostles and elders gather over the Gentile-law question here. At the later Jerusalem visit James and the elders hear Paul, discuss concerns about Moses and Jewish practice, and recall the Gentile decision. This is a later meeting referring to the earlier decision, not the same session.
    Acts 21:18 The next day Paul went with us to James, and all the elders were present.
    Acts 21:19 After greeting them, he related one by one the things God had done among the Gentiles through his ministry.
    Acts 21:20 When they heard, they glorified God and said to him, “You see, brother, how many tens of thousands of Jews have believed, and they are all zealous for the law.
    Acts 21:21 They have been informed about you that you teach all the Jews among the Gentiles to forsake Moses, telling them not to circumcise their children or walk in the customs.
    Acts 21:22 What, then, is to be done? They will certainly hear that you have come.
    Acts 21:23 Therefore do what we tell you. We have four men who have taken a vow upon themselves.
    Acts 21:24 Take these men, purify yourself with them, and pay their expenses so that they may shave their heads. Then everyone will know that there is nothing to what they have been told about you, but that you yourself also walk in step, keeping the law.
    Acts 21:25 But concerning the Gentiles who have believed, we have sent word, judging that they should keep themselves from what is sacrificed to idols, blood, what is strangled, and sexual immorality.”

Acts 15:7
After much debate, Peter rose and said to them, “Men, brothers, you know that in earlier days God made a choice among you, that through my mouth the Gentiles should hear the word of the good news and believe.
  Acts 10:42–10:44 — internal retrospective reference: Peter recalls his earlier role in Gentiles hearing the gospel and believing. In the Cornelius scene Peter speaks about the appointed judge and forgiveness for believers, and the Spirit falls on those hearing him. That narrative supplies the earlier preaching setting without repeating every word of this recollection.
    Acts 10:42 He commanded us to proclaim to the people and bear solemn witness that He is the One appointed by God as judge of the living and the dead.
    Acts 10:43 All the prophets bear witness to Him: everyone who believes in Him receives forgiveness of sins through His name.”
    Acts 10:44 While Peter was still speaking these words, the Holy Spirit fell upon all who were hearing the message.

Acts 15:8
God, who knows the heart, bore witness to them by giving them the Holy Spirit just as He did to us.
  Acts 10:44–10:47 — internal event-and-recollection parallel: Peter interprets the Spirit gift to the Gentiles as God bearing witness to them, with his stated comparison just as to us. Acts10 narrates the Spirit falling on the hearers and Peter explicitly saying they received the Spirit just as his group did. The heart-knowing description belongs to this council speech.
    Acts 10:44 While Peter was still speaking these words, the Holy Spirit fell upon all who were hearing the message.
    Acts 10:45 The believers from the circumcision who had come with Peter were astonished that the gift of the Holy Spirit had been poured out even upon the Gentiles,
    Acts 10:46 for they heard them speaking in tongues and magnifying God. Then Peter said,
    Acts 10:47 “Can anyone withhold the water so that these people, who have received the Holy Spirit just as we have, should not be baptized?”

Acts 15:9
He made no distinction between us and them, cleansing their hearts by faith.
  Acts 11:17–11:18 — internal theological parallel: Here Peter speaks of no distinction and hearts cleansed by faith. His earlier defense speaks of God giving the same gift as to those believing in Jesus, followed by the hearers recognizing Gentile repentance leading to life. The heart-cleansing formulation is not quoted from that earlier account.
    Acts 11:17 If, then, God gave them the same gift as He gave us when we believed in the Lord Jesus Christ, who was I that I could hinder God?”
    Acts 11:18 When they heard these things, they fell silent and glorified God, saying, “Then God has granted even the Gentiles repentance leading to life.”

Acts 15:10
Now, therefore, why are you testing God by placing upon the disciples’ neck a yoke that neither our fathers nor we were able to bear?
  Galatians 5:1–5:4 — thematic parallel: Peter objects to placing an unbearable yoke upon the disciples in this circumcision dispute. Galatians warns against a yoke of slavery and connects receiving circumcision with obligation to the whole law. The speakers and arguments differ; the shared yoke image does not erase those distinctions.
    Galatians 5:1 For freedom Christ set us free. Stand firm, therefore, and do not be held again under a yoke of slavery.
    Galatians 5:2 Look, I, Paul, tell you that if you receive circumcision, Christ will be of no benefit to you.
    Galatians 5:3 And I testify again to every man who receives circumcision that he is obligated to do the whole law.
    Galatians 5:4 You who seek to be justified by law have been severed from Christ; you have fallen from grace.

Acts 15:11
Rather, we believe that we are saved through the grace of the Lord Jesus, in the same way as they are.”
  Ephesians 2:8–2:9 — thematic parallel: Peter states that his group and the Gentiles are saved through the grace of the Lord Jesus in the same way. Ephesians describes salvation by grace through faith as God’s gift rather than from works. The letter does not repeat the council speech or its comparison of the two groups.
    Ephesians 2:8 For by grace you have been saved through faith, and this is not from yourselves; it is the gift of God,
    Ephesians 2:9 not from works, so that no one may boast.

Acts 15:12
The whole multitude became silent and listened to Barnabas and Paul relate all the signs and wonders God had done among the Gentiles through them.
  Acts 14:1–14:3 — internal retrospective reference: Barnabas and Paul report signs and wonders God did among Gentiles through them. The Iconium narrative describes both Jews and Greeks believing and the Lord granting signs and wonders through the missionaries’ hands. This is a concrete earlier example, not an exhaustive identification of all the reported signs.
    Acts 14:1 In Iconium they entered the Jewish synagogue together and spoke in such a way that a great multitude of both Jews and Greeks believed.
    Acts 14:2 But the Jews who refused to believe stirred up the Gentiles and embittered their minds against the brothers.
    Acts 14:3 So they stayed a considerable time, speaking boldly in reliance on the Lord, who bore witness to the word of His grace by granting signs and wonders to take place through their hands.

Acts 15:13
After they fell silent, James spoke: “Men, brothers, listen to me.
  Acts 21:18–21:20 — internal Jerusalem-leadership connection: James speaks after the mission report at this Jerusalem gathering. In the later Jerusalem visit Paul goes to James with all the elders and reports God’s work among the Gentiles. The shared setting and role provide a leadership connection without identifying the two meetings or supplying James’s family relationship.
    Acts 21:18 The next day Paul went with us to James, and all the elders were present.
    Acts 21:19 After greeting them, he related one by one the things God had done among the Gentiles through his ministry.
    Acts 21:20 When they heard, they glorified God and said to him, “You see, brother, how many tens of thousands of Jews have believed, and they are all zealous for the law.

Acts 15:14
Simeon has related how God first visited the Gentiles to take from them a people for His name.
  Acts 10:44–10:48 — internal retrospective reference: James summarizes the Gentile visitation just related by Simeon. Acts10 narrates Gentiles receiving the Spirit during Peter’s preaching and being ordered baptized in Jesus Christ’s name. The narrative is the earlier reception setting, not a verbatim statement that God took a people for His name.
    Acts 10:44 While Peter was still speaking these words, the Holy Spirit fell upon all who were hearing the message.
    Acts 10:45 The believers from the circumcision who had come with Peter were astonished that the gift of the Holy Spirit had been poured out even upon the Gentiles,
    Acts 10:46 for they heard them speaking in tongues and magnifying God. Then Peter said,
    Acts 10:47 “Can anyone withhold the water so that these people, who have received the Holy Spirit just as we have, should not be baptized?”
    Acts 10:48 He ordered them to be baptized in the name of Jesus Christ. Then they asked him to stay for some days.

Acts 15:15
With this the words of the prophets agree, as it is written:
  Amos 9:11–9:12 — quotation introduction: James introduces agreement with the prophets, then cites the rebuilding of David’s tent and the nations seeking. Those linked images occur in the saved Greek-based Amos text. The introduction itself is James’s framing, and the source’s following wording is not made identical to Amos in every clause.
    Amos 9:11 On that day I will raise David’s fallen tent; I will rebuild its fallen parts, raise its demolished parts and rebuild it as in days of old,
    Amos 9:12 so that the remaining human beings and all the nations over whom My name is called may seek,” says the Lord God, who does these things.

Acts 15:16
‘After these things I will return and rebuild David’s fallen tent. I will rebuild its ruins and set it upright,
  Amos 9:11 — quotation: Both passages speak of raising or rebuilding David’s fallen tent and its damaged parts. Acts begins with a return after these things, whereas Amos begins on that day and speaks of rebuilding as in days of old. The quotation’s altered introduction and formulations remain visible.
    Amos 9:11 On that day I will raise David’s fallen tent; I will rebuild its fallen parts, raise its demolished parts and rebuild it as in days of old,

Acts 15:17
so that the rest of humanity may seek the Lord, and all the nations upon whom My name has been called, says the Lord, who makes these things
  Amos 9:12 — quotation: The saved Amos text has remaining human beings and nations over whom God’s name is called seeking; Acts has the rest of humanity seeking the Lord and nations upon whom His name is called. Both end with the Lord’s doing, with their different explicit objects and wording retained.
    Amos 9:12 so that the remaining human beings and all the nations over whom My name is called may seek,” says the Lord God, who does these things.

Acts 15:18
known from long ago.’
  Acts 3:21 — internal prophetic-time parallel: The council quotation concludes with things known from long ago. Peter’s earlier speech describes the restoration of things God spoke through His holy prophets from long ago. This connects their long-ago prophetic framing, without claiming Acts3 is the quoted Amos line or that every restoration detail is restated here.
    Acts 3:21 whom heaven must receive until the times of restoration of all things of which God spoke through the mouth of His holy prophets from long ago.

Acts 15:19
Therefore I judge that we should not trouble those from the Gentiles who are turning to God,
  Acts 21:25 — internal decision-and-recollection parallel: James judges that Gentiles turning to God should not be troubled. The later Jerusalem statement recalls the limited abstentions sent to believing Gentiles. The target records the decision’s requirements, rather than repeating James’s judgment word for word or eliminating its specific obligations.
    Acts 21:25 But concerning the Gentiles who have believed, we have sent word, judging that they should keep themselves from what is sacrificed to idols, blood, what is strangled, and sexual immorality.”

Acts 15:20
but should write to them to abstain from the pollutions of idols, sexual immorality, what is strangled, and blood.
  Acts 21:25 — internal decision-and-recollection parallel: Both lists address Gentile believers and name idol-related pollution or sacrifices, sexual immorality, strangled things and blood. The target recalls the sent decision with a different order and the more specific sacrificed-to-idols formulation; the lists are not silently rewritten to match.
    Acts 21:25 But concerning the Gentiles who have believed, we have sent word, judging that they should keep themselves from what is sacrificed to idols, blood, what is strangled, and sexual immorality.”

Acts 15:21
For since ancient times, people in every city have proclaimed Moses, and he is read in the synagogues every Sabbath.”
  Acts 13:14–13:15 — internal practice-and-example connection: James refers to Moses being read in synagogues every Sabbath. The Pisidian Antioch visit specifically narrates a Sabbath synagogue gathering followed by reading the law and prophets. That scene illustrates the reading practice without independently proving its antiquity or occurrence in every city.
    Acts 13:14 They continued from Perga and arrived at Pisidian Antioch. On the Sabbath day they entered the synagogue and sat down.
    Acts 13:15 After the reading of the law and the prophets, the synagogue rulers sent word to them, saying, “Men, brothers, if you have a word of encouragement for the people, speak.”

Acts 15:22
Then the apostles and elders, with the whole Church, decided to choose men from among them and send them to Antioch with Paul and Barnabas: Judas called Barsabbas, and Silas, leading men among the brothers.
  Acts 15:27 — internal selection-and-letter connection: The narrative names Judas called Barsabbas and Silas as the chosen envoys to Antioch with Paul and Barnabas. The letter subsequently names Judas and Silas as sent to give the same message orally. These are the narrative selection and its written announcement, not independent attestations.
    Acts 15:27 We have therefore sent Judas and Silas, who will themselves report the same things by word of mouth.

Acts 15:23
They wrote by their hand: “The apostles and elders, your brothers, to the brothers from the Gentiles in Antioch, Syria, and Cilicia: greetings.
  Acts 16:4 — internal letter-and-delivery connection: The Jerusalem writers address Gentile brothers in Antioch, Syria and Cilicia. The next journey reports handing on the Jerusalem apostles’ and elders’ decisions for observance in the cities. The target describes wider delivery rather than repeating this greeting or naming exactly the same recipients.
    Acts 16:4 As they traveled through the cities, they handed on to them for observance the decisions made by the apostles and elders in Jerusalem.

Acts 15:24
Since we have heard that some who went out from among us troubled you with words, unsettling your souls, although we gave them no instruction,
  Acts 15:1–15:2 — internal letter-and-dispute connection: The letter says some from their company unsettled the recipients without instruction. The opening narrative records men coming from Judea with the circumcision-for-salvation teaching and the resulting dispute. The letter supplies the lack-of-authorization claim; the opening narrative does not independently state it.
    Acts 15:1 Some men came down from Judea and taught the brothers, “Unless you are circumcised according to the custom of Moses, you cannot be saved.”
    Acts 15:2 When Paul and Barnabas had a serious disagreement and debate with them, they appointed Paul and Barnabas and some others from among them to go up to the apostles and elders in Jerusalem about this question.

Acts 15:25
we have decided with one accord to choose men and send them to you with our beloved Barnabas and Paul,
  Acts 15:22 — internal narrative-and-letter parallel: The letter announces the united decision to choose men to accompany Barnabas and Paul. The narrative describes apostles, elders and the whole Church making that choice and names Judas and Silas. The written announcement and narrated selection belong to the same decision.
    Acts 15:22 Then the apostles and elders, with the whole Church, decided to choose men from among them and send them to Antioch with Paul and Barnabas: Judas called Barsabbas, and Silas, leading men among the brothers.

Acts 15:26
men who have given over their lives for the name of our Lord Jesus Christ.
  Acts 14:19–14:20 — internal commendation-and-example connection: The letter commends Barnabas and Paul as men who gave over their lives for Jesus Christ’s name. Acts14 supplies a concrete danger to Paul, stoned and left for dead before he later departs with Barnabas. It does not say Barnabas was also stoned or exhaust all dangers covered by the commendation.
    Acts 14:19 Jews came from Antioch and Iconium, persuaded the crowds, stoned Paul, and dragged him outside the city, supposing he was dead.
    Acts 14:20 But when the disciples surrounded him, he rose and entered the city. The next day he went with Barnabas to Derbe.

Acts 15:27
We have therefore sent Judas and Silas, who will themselves report the same things by word of mouth.
  Acts 15:32 — internal commission-and-performance connection: The letter promises Judas and Silas will report the message by word of mouth. The narrative later describes those same named prophets encouraging and strengthening the brothers with many words. It does not quote their whole oral report or equate every encouraging word with the letter text.
    Acts 15:32 Judas and Silas, themselves also prophets, encouraged the brothers with many words and strengthened them.

Acts 15:28
For it seemed good to the Holy Spirit and to us to place no further burden upon you except these necessary things:
  Acts 16:4 — internal decision-and-transmission connection: The letter attributes its limited burden to the Holy Spirit and the writers’ judgment. Acts16 reports handing on decisions made by the Jerusalem apostles and elders for observance. The later summary identifies the decision’s human leadership without repeating the letter’s Spirit attribution or its exact list.
    Acts 16:4 As they traveled through the cities, they handed on to them for observance the decisions made by the apostles and elders in Jerusalem.

Acts 15:29
to abstain from things sacrificed to idols, blood, things strangled, and sexual immorality. If you keep yourselves from these, you will do well. Farewell.”
  Acts 21:25 — internal decision-and-recollection parallel: Both accounts list idol sacrifices, blood, strangled things and sexual immorality as the Gentile requirements. The letter also includes the doing-well assurance and farewell, which the later recollection does not repeat. The shared list is a later internal recall, not an independent source for the original letter.
    Acts 21:25 But concerning the Gentiles who have believed, we have sent word, judging that they should keep themselves from what is sacrificed to idols, blood, what is strangled, and sexual immorality.”

Acts 15:30
After being sent off, they went down to Antioch, gathered the multitude, and delivered the letter.
  Acts 15:22–15:23 — internal commission-and-arrival connection: The chosen party reaches Antioch, gathers the multitude and delivers the letter. The earlier decision sends the named men to Antioch with Paul and Barnabas and gives the letter’s greeting. The arrival performs that particular commission; it does not reproduce all their travel details.
    Acts 15:22 Then the apostles and elders, with the whole Church, decided to choose men from among them and send them to Antioch with Paul and Barnabas: Judas called Barsabbas, and Silas, leading men among the brothers.
    Acts 15:23 They wrote by their hand: “The apostles and elders, your brothers, to the brothers from the Gentiles in Antioch, Syria, and Cilicia: greetings.

Acts 15:31
When they read it, they rejoiced at its encouragement.
  Acts 15:3 — internal joy-at-Gentile-reception parallel: The Antioch hearers rejoice at the letter’s encouragement. Earlier along this same council journey the Gentile-conversion report brings joy to brothers in Phoenicia and Samaria. The occasions and audiences differ, but both positive responses concern reception of the Gentile mission.
    Acts 15:3 Sent on their way by the Church, they passed through Phoenicia and Samaria, recounting the conversion of the Gentiles and bringing great joy to all the brothers.

Acts 15:32
Judas and Silas, themselves also prophets, encouraged the brothers with many words and strengthened them.
  Acts 11:27–11:28 — internal Jerusalem-to-Antioch prophetic-ministry parallel: Judas and Silas act as prophets strengthening the brothers at Antioch. The earlier narrative brings prophets from Jerusalem to Antioch and names Agabus’s famine prediction. These are different prophets and messages; the comparison does not make Judas or Silas the famine predictor.
    Acts 11:27 In those days prophets came down from Jerusalem to Antioch.
    Acts 11:28 One of them, named Agabus, stood up and indicated through the Spirit that a great famine would come over the whole inhabited world. This happened in the time of Claudius.

Acts 15:33
After spending some time there, they were sent away in peace by the brothers to those who had sent them.
  Acts 15:40 — internal Silas-itinerary connection: The envoys are sent away to those who commissioned them, while the later departure notice has Paul choose Silas. The passages mark separate stages in Silas’s narrative; they do not explain an intervening return or authorize inserting the absent15:34 notice that he remained.
    Acts 15:40 but Paul chose Silas and departed, entrusted by the brothers to the grace of the Lord.

Acts 15:35
Paul and Barnabas remained in Antioch, teaching and proclaiming the word of the Lord as good news, along with many others.
  Acts 11:25–11:26 — internal Antioch-ministry parallel: Paul and Barnabas remain at Antioch teaching and proclaiming with others. Earlier Barnabas brings Saul to Antioch and the two spend a year gathering with the Church and teaching. The earlier year is not imposed on this later stay, and the two visits are not merged.
    Acts 11:25 He went away to Tarsus to search for Saul,
    Acts 11:26 and when he found him, he brought him to Antioch. For a whole year they gathered with the Church and taught a considerable crowd. It was in Antioch that the disciples were first called Christians.

Acts 15:36
After some days Paul said to Barnabas, “Let us return and visit the brothers in every city where we proclaimed the word of the Lord, to see how they are.”
  Acts 14:21–14:23 — internal mission-follow-up connection: Paul proposes revisiting the brothers where he and Barnabas preached. The earlier journey names their return through Lystra, Iconium and Antioch to strengthen disciples and appoint elders. That supplies the prior mission and follow-up setting, not a statement that the later proposed journey was already completed.
    Acts 14:21 After proclaiming the good news to that city and making many disciples, they returned to Lystra, Iconium, and Antioch,
    Acts 14:22 strengthening the souls of the disciples, encouraging them to remain in the faith, and saying, “Through many afflictions we must enter the kingdom of God.”
    Acts 14:23 They appointed elders for them in each Church and, after praying with fasting, entrusted them to the Lord in whom they had believed.

Acts 15:37
Barnabas wanted to take along John, who was called Mark, as well.
  Acts 12:25 — internal companion identification: Barnabas wishes to take John called Mark. Acts12 had already named John called Mark accompanying Barnabas and Saul after their service. The repeated full designation and companions establish the narrative connection without identifying him as an unnamed Gospel author.
    Acts 12:25 Barnabas and Saul returned to Jerusalem after completing their service, and took along John who was called Mark.

Acts 15:38
But Paul considered it right not to take along the man who had withdrawn from them in Pamphylia and had not gone with them to the work.
  Acts 13:13 — internal retrospective reference: Paul’s objection recalls the man’s withdrawal in Pamphylia. The earlier travel narrative explicitly has John leave Paul’s company at Perga in Pamphylia and return to Jerusalem. It records the departure but does not supply his motive or decide the later dispute between Paul and Barnabas.
    Acts 13:13 Paul and his companions sailed from Paphos and came to Perga in Pamphylia. John left them and returned to Jerusalem.

Acts 15:39
A sharp disagreement arose, so that they separated from one another. Barnabas took Mark and sailed to Cyprus,
  Acts 13:4–13:5 — internal changed-companions-and-destination parallel: Barnabas now sails to Cyprus with Mark after separating from Paul. The earlier Cyprus mission has Barnabas and Saul sent out together and John as their assistant. The destination and companion connections are precise, while the journeys and the later sharp disagreement remain distinct.
    Acts 13:4 So, sent out by the Holy Spirit, they went down to Seleucia and from there sailed to Cyprus.
    Acts 13:5 When they came to Salamis, they proclaimed the word of God in the Jewish synagogues. They also had John as an assistant.

Acts 15:40
but Paul chose Silas and departed, entrusted by the brothers to the grace of the Lord.
  Acts 14:26 — internal mission-entrusting parallel: Paul leaves with Silas entrusted by the brothers to the Lord’s grace. The earlier mission’s return to Antioch recalls where its workers had been entrusted to God’s grace. The formula connects successive missions without assigning the earlier party’s completed work to Silas.
    Acts 14:26 From there they sailed to Antioch, where they had been entrusted to the grace of God for the work they had now completed.

Acts 15:41
He traveled through Syria and Cilicia, strengthening the Churches.
  Acts 15:23 — internal recipient-regions-and-journey connection: Paul travels through Syria and Cilicia strengthening the Churches. Those two regions occur alongside Antioch in the letter’s named Gentile recipients. The shared regional setting links the follow-up journey and decision, without claiming this verse alone documents delivery to every recipient.
    Acts 15:23 They wrote by their hand: “The apostles and elders, your brothers, to the brothers from the Gentiles in Antioch, Syria, and Cilicia: greetings.

Acts 16:1
He also came to Derbe and Lystra. There was a disciple there named Timothy, the son of a believing Jewish woman, but of a Greek father.
  2 Timothy 1:5 — biographical thematic parallel: Acts introduces Timothy as the son of a believing Jewish woman and Greek father. The letter to Timothy recalls sincere faith in his grandmother Lois and mother Eunice and in Timothy. It supplies maternal family names and faith, but does not repeat the father’s ethnicity or all details of this meeting.
    2 Timothy 1:5 having been reminded of the sincere faith in you, which dwelt first in your grandmother Lois and your mother Eunice, and I am convinced that it dwells also in you.

Acts 16:2
He was well spoken of by the brothers in Lystra and Iconium.
  Philippians 2:19–2:22 — biographical thematic parallel: Local brothers in Lystra and Iconium speak well of Timothy. Paul’s letter later commends Timothy’s sincere care and proven service with him for the gospel. These are different witnesses and occasions, not the same recommendation or proof of each local brother’s testimony.
    Philippians 2:19 Now I hope in the Lord Jesus to send Timothy to you soon, so that I too may be encouraged when I know about your circumstances.
    Philippians 2:20 For I have no one of like mind who will sincerely care about your circumstances,
    Philippians 2:21 for all seek their own interests, not those of Jesus Christ.
    Philippians 2:22 But you know his proven character, that as a child with a father he served with me for the gospel.

Acts 16:3
Paul wanted him to go with him, and took him and circumcised him because of the Jews in those places, for they all knew that his father was Greek.
  Galatians 2:3–2:5 — circumcision contrast: Paul circumcises Timothy because of the local Jews who knew his father was Greek. In Galatians, Greek Titus is not compelled to be circumcised amid a dispute about freedom and false brothers. The men and circumstances differ; the explicit local motive is not replaced with an invented universal policy or a harmonizing chronology.
    Galatians 2:3 But not even Titus, who was with me, was compelled to be circumcised, though he was a Greek.
    Galatians 2:4 Yet because of the false brothers secretly brought in, who slipped in to spy out our freedom which we have in Christ Jesus, so that they might enslave us—
    Galatians 2:5 we did not yield to them in submission even for an hour, so that the truth of the gospel might remain with you.

Acts 16:4
As they traveled through the cities, they handed on to them for observance the decisions made by the apostles and elders in Jerusalem.
  Acts 15:28–15:29 — internal decree-and-observance connection: The travelers hand on the decisions of the Jerusalem apostles and elders for observance. The letter gives that decision’s limited necessary abstentions and its Spirit-and-writers attribution. This is transmission of the earlier decision, not a second independently produced list.
    Acts 15:28 For it seemed good to the Holy Spirit and to us to place no further burden upon you except these necessary things:
    Acts 15:29 to abstain from things sacrificed to idols, blood, things strangled, and sexual immorality. If you keep yourselves from these, you will do well. Farewell.”

Acts 16:5
So the Churches were strengthened in the faith and increased in number each day.
  Acts 9:31 — internal Church-growth summary parallel: Both narrative summaries describe the Church or Churches being strengthened or built up and increasing. Acts16 specifies strength in faith and daily numerical growth; Acts9 locates peace, fear of the Lord and Spirit encouragement in Judea, Galilee and Samaria. Those locations and narrative features are not copied into this later summary.
    Acts 9:31 So the Church throughout all Judea, Galilee, and Samaria had peace and was being built up. Walking in the fear of the Lord and in the encouragement of the Holy Spirit, it increased.

Acts 16:6
They passed through Phrygia and the Galatian region, having been prevented by the Holy Spirit from speaking the word in Asia.
  Acts 16:7 — internal successive-guidance parallel: The Holy Spirit prevents speaking in Asia, then the Spirit of Jesus does not permit entry into Bithynia. These are successive restrictions within one journey, with different regions and the actual Spirit designations preserved rather than normalized.
    Acts 16:7 When they came near Mysia, they tried to go into Bithynia, but the Spirit of Jesus did not permit them.

Acts 16:7
When they came near Mysia, they tried to go into Bithynia, but the Spirit of Jesus did not permit them.
  Acts 16:6 — internal successive-guidance parallel: The Bithynia attempt is not permitted by the Spirit of Jesus; the preceding notice says the Holy Spirit had prevented speech in Asia. The related journey restrictions have different objects and regions, and neither verse supplies an unreported motive for them.
    Acts 16:6 They passed through Phrygia and the Galatian region, having been prevented by the Holy Spirit from speaking the word in Asia.

Acts 16:8
Passing by Mysia, they went down to Troas.
  2 Corinthians 2:12–2:13 — itinerary comparison: Acts brings Paul’s party through Mysia to Troas before the Macedonian vision. Paul’s letter describes another Troas-to-Macedonia movement, with an opened gospel door and concern over missing Titus. The shared route is compared without making the vision and Titus concern one departure or the same occasion.
    2 Corinthians 2:12 Now when I came to Troas for the good news of Christ, and a door had been opened to me in the Lord,
    2 Corinthians 2:13 I had no rest in my spirit because I did not find Titus my brother; but taking leave of them, I went out to Macedonia.

Acts 16:9
During the night a vision appeared to Paul: a Macedonian man was standing and urging him, saying, “Cross over to Macedonia and help us.”
  Acts 18:9–18:10 — internal Pauline-night-vision parallel: Paul’s night vision here shows a Macedonian man asking help. The later Corinth vision has the Lord encourage Paul to speak without fear and promises His presence. The vision scenes differ in speaker and purpose; the Macedonian man is not identified as the Lord or an angel.
    Acts 18:9 The Lord said to Paul by a vision in the night, “Do not fear, but speak and do not be silent,
    Acts 18:10 because I am with you, and no one will attack you to harm you, for I have many people in this city.”

Acts 16:10
When he had seen the vision, we immediately sought to go out to Macedonia, concluding that God had called us to proclaim the good news to them.
  Acts 13:2–13:4 — internal missionary-calling parallel: After Paul’s vision the narrating group concludes God called them to evangelize Macedonia. In the earlier mission the Holy Spirit directly names Barnabas and Saul for the work and they are sent out. An inference from a vision is not rewritten as the same quoted command or the same missionary party.
