    Acts 13:2 While they were ministering to the Lord and fasting, the Holy Spirit said, “Set apart for Me Barnabas and Saul for the work to which I have called them.”
    Acts 13:3 Then, after fasting and praying and laying their hands upon them, they sent them away.
    Acts 13:4 So, sent out by the Holy Spirit, they went down to Seleucia and from there sailed to Cyprus.

Acts 16:11
We sailed from Troas and made a straight course to Samothrace, and on the next day to Neapolis,
  Acts 20:6 — internal sea-itinerary comparison: This itinerary sails from Troas by Samothrace to Neapolis before reaching Philippi. The later itinerary sails from Philippi to Troas in five days. The endpoints connect the journeys, while direction, intermediate places and timing remain distinct and no unreported route is supplied.
    Acts 20:6 We sailed from Philippi after the days of Unleavened Bread and came to them at Troas in five days, where we stayed seven days.

Acts 16:12
and from there to Philippi, a city of the first district of Macedonia and a colony. We stayed in that city for some days.
  Philippians 1:1 — mission-location-and-letter connection: The travelers arrive at Philippi, described here as a Macedonian district city and colony. Paul and Timothy’s letter addresses the saints in Philippi with overseers and deacons. The city links narrative mission and letter audience; the greeting does not repeat the colony status or this stay’s duration.
    Philippians 1:1 Paul and Timothy, slaves of Christ Jesus, to all the saints in Christ Jesus who are in Philippi, together with overseers and deacons:

Acts 16:13
On the Sabbath day we went outside the gate beside a river, where we thought there was a place of prayer. We sat down and spoke to the women who had gathered.
  Acts 17:2 — internal Sabbath-mission contrast: At Philippi the group goes by the river where they think there is a place of prayer and addresses gathered women on the Sabbath. At Thessalonica Paul reasons in the synagogue for three Sabbaths. The different venues and audiences are retained, without inferring that Philippi had no synagogue or a particular population requirement.
    Acts 17:2 As was his custom, Paul went in to them, and for three Sabbaths he reasoned with them from the Scriptures,

Acts 16:14
A woman named Lydia, a seller of purple goods from the city of Thyatira, who worshiped God, was listening. The Lord opened her heart to attend to what Paul was saying.
  Luke 24:45 — thematic opening-for-reception parallel: The Lord opens Lydia’s heart to attend to Paul’s words; Jesus opens the disciples’ mind to understand the Scriptures in Luke. The shared divine opening concerns different recipients, objects and settings, and does not make Lydia one of those disciples or supply an unreported conversion mechanism.
    Luke 24:45 Then He opened their mind to understand the Scriptures

Acts 16:15
After she and her household were baptized, she urged us, saying, “If you have judged me faithful to the Lord, come into my house and stay.” And she prevailed upon us.
  Acts 16:31–16:34 — internal Philippian-household parallel: Lydia and her household are baptized and she presses the missionaries to stay at her house. Later the jailer receives a household salvation promise, his household hears the word, his people are baptized and he hosts a meal. The situations and sequences differ, and neither household’s ages are supplied.
    Acts 16:31 They said, “Believe in the Lord Jesus, and you will be saved, you and your household.”
    Acts 16:32 They spoke the word of the Lord to him, together with all who were in his house.
    Acts 16:33 At that very hour of the night he took them and washed their wounds. Immediately he and all his people were baptized.
    Acts 16:34 He brought them up into his house, set food before them, and rejoiced with his whole household, having believed in God.

Acts 16:16
As we were going to the place of prayer, a slave girl with a spirit of divination met us. She brought her masters much profit by fortune-telling.
  Acts 19:24–19:27 — internal religious-business comparison: The divining slave girl earns much for her masters. The Ephesus disturbance later features craftsmen whose prosperity depends on Artemis shrines and who fear loss from Paul’s message. The commercial settings are connected without equating divination with the narrated manufacture of shrines or treating the craftsmen’s divine claims as the narrator’s endorsement.
    Acts 19:24 A silversmith named Demetrius, who made silver shrines of Artemis, provided considerable business for the craftsmen.
    Acts 19:25 He gathered them and the workers in related trades and said, “Men, you know that our prosperity comes from this business.
    Acts 19:26 You see and hear that not only in Ephesus but in almost all Asia this Paul has persuaded and turned away a considerable crowd, saying that things made by hands are not gods.
    Acts 19:27 There is danger not only that this trade of ours will fall into disrepute, but also that the temple of the great goddess Artemis will be reckoned as nothing, and that she whom all Asia and the inhabited world worship will be stripped of her magnificence.”

Acts 16:17
She followed Paul and us, crying out, “These men are servants of God Most High, who proclaim to you the way of salvation.”
  Mark 1:23–1:25 — narrative spirit-associated-recognition parallel: The girl with the divining spirit calls the missionaries servants of God Most High who proclaim salvation. In Mark the man with an unclean spirit identifies Jesus as God’s Holy One before Jesus rebukes the spirit. The speakers, titles and recipients differ; neither comparison endorses divination as an authorized gospel ministry.
    Mark 1:23 Immediately there was in their synagogue a man with an unclean spirit, and he cried out,
    Mark 1:24 “What have we to do with Thee, Jesus of Nazareth? Hast Thou come to destroy us? I know who Thou art—the Holy One of God!”
    Mark 1:25 Jesus rebuked him, saying, “Be muzzled, and come out of him!”

Acts 16:18
She kept doing this for many days. Paul, greatly troubled, turned and said to the spirit, “I command you in the name of Jesus Christ to come out of her.” It came out that very hour.
  Luke 10:17 — narrative authority-in-Jesus-name parallel: Paul commands the spirit to leave in Jesus Christ’s name and it leaves that hour. The seventy-two report to Jesus that demons submit in His name. Their general report and this individual narrated exorcism share the named authority, without making them one event or assigning the girl an unstated diagnosis.
    Luke 10:17 The seventy-two returned with joy, saying, “Lord, even the demons submit to us in Thy name.”

Acts 16:19
When her masters saw that their hope of profit had gone out, they seized Paul and Silas and dragged them into the marketplace before the rulers.
  Acts 19:25–19:27 — internal commercial-opposition parallel: The owners seize Paul and Silas when their hope of profit disappears. Demetrius later gathers the craftsmen and explicitly connects prosperity and the danger to their trade with opposition to Paul. Different cities, trades and reactions remain distinct; the passage does not add a motive beyond the stated profit concerns.
    Acts 19:25 He gathered them and the workers in related trades and said, “Men, you know that our prosperity comes from this business.
    Acts 19:26 You see and hear that not only in Ephesus but in almost all Asia this Paul has persuaded and turned away a considerable crowd, saying that things made by hands are not gods.
    Acts 19:27 There is danger not only that this trade of ours will fall into disrepute, but also that the temple of the great goddess Artemis will be reckoned as nothing, and that she whom all Asia and the inhabited world worship will be stripped of her magnificence.”

Acts 16:20
They brought them before the magistrates and said, “These men are throwing our city into turmoil. They are Jews,
  Acts 17:6–17:7 — internal hostile-accusation parallel: The Philippian accusers portray the missionaries as Jews disrupting the city. The Thessalonian accusers portray Paul’s party as upsetting the world and acting against Caesar by naming Jesus another king. These are different accusations before local authorities, not narrator endorsements or an ethnic judgment.
    Acts 17:6 When they did not find them, they dragged Jason and some brothers before the city officials, shouting, “These men who have upset the inhabited world have come here too,
    Acts 17:7 and Jason has welcomed them. They all act against Caesar’s decrees, saying that there is another king, Jesus.”

Acts 16:21
and they proclaim customs that we, being Romans, are not permitted to accept or practice.”
  Acts 17:7 — internal civic-loyalty-accusation comparison: The owners claim Roman identity and forbidden customs; the later accusation claims the missionaries act against Caesar and speak of another king, Jesus. The civic appeals differ, and neither speech is converted into an independently verified statement of Roman law.
    Acts 17:7 and Jason has welcomed them. They all act against Caesar’s decrees, saying that there is another king, Jesus.”

Acts 16:22
The crowd joined the attack against them. The magistrates tore off their garments and ordered them to be beaten with rods.
  2 Corinthians 11:25 — narrative-and-suffering-recollection comparison: The magistrates order Paul and Silas beaten with rods. Paul’s letter recalls three occasions when he was beaten with rods. The narrative supplies a concrete example of that category of suffering, while the letter neither names Philippi here nor identifies the other two occasions.
    2 Corinthians 11:25 Three times I was beaten with rods, once I was stoned, three times I was shipwrecked; I have spent a night and a day in the deep.

Acts 16:23
After inflicting many blows on them, they threw them into prison and ordered the jailer to keep them securely.
  1 Thessalonians 2:1–2:2 — narrative-and-retrospective-suffering comparison: Acts describes many blows and imprisonment at Philippi. The letter recalls having suffered and been shamefully treated in Philippi before speaking the gospel to the Thessalonians amid struggle. The recollection matches the location and suffering without restating the jailer’s orders or every prison detail.
    1 Thessalonians 2:1 For you yourselves know, brothers, that our coming to you has not been in vain.
    1 Thessalonians 2:2 But after we had already suffered and been shamefully treated in Philippi, as you know, we were bold in our God to speak to you the gospel of God amid much struggle.

Acts 16:24
Having received such an order, he put them in the inner prison and secured their feet in the stocks.
  Acts 12:6 — internal secure-confinement comparison: Paul and Silas are placed in the inner prison with feet in stocks. Peter’s earlier imprisonment has him sleeping between soldiers, bound with two chains and watched at the door. Both describe strong custody before extraordinary openings, but the restraints and prisoners are different.
    Acts 12:6 On the night before Herod was to bring him out, Peter was sleeping between two soldiers, bound with two chains, while guards before the door kept watch over the prison.

Acts 16:25
About midnight Paul and Silas were praying and singing hymns to God, while the prisoners listened to them.
  Acts 12:5 — internal prayer-during-imprisonment comparison: Paul and Silas themselves pray and sing hymns while prisoners listen. During Peter’s confinement the Church makes earnest prayer to God for him. The prayer participants and location differ, and Peter’s account does not independently supply the singing or midnight timing.
    Acts 12:5 So Peter was kept in prison, but earnest prayer for him was being made to God by the Church.

Acts 16:26
Suddenly there was a great earthquake, so that the jail’s foundations were shaken. At once all the doors opened, and everyone’s bonds were loosened.
  Acts 12:7–12:10 — internal extraordinary-prison-opening comparison: The earthquake opens all doors and loosens everyone’s bonds. Peter’s chains fall and an iron gate opens during an angel-led departure. The mechanisms and scope differ: Acts16 does not narrate everyone escaping, while Peter actually follows the angel out.
    Acts 12:7 And look—an angel of the Lord stood beside him, and light shone in the cell. Striking Peter’s side, he woke him and said, “Rise quickly.” The chains fell from his hands.
    Acts 12:8 The angel said to him, “Fasten your belt and put on your sandals.” He did so. Then he said to him, “Wrap your cloak around you and follow me.”
    Acts 12:9 Peter went out and followed. He did not know that what was happening through the angel was real; he thought he was seeing a vision.
    Acts 12:10 They passed the first guard post and the second and came to the iron gate leading into the city. It opened for them of its own accord. They went out and continued along one street, and immediately the angel left him.

Acts 16:27
The jailer awoke and saw the prison doors open. He drew his sword and was about to kill himself, supposing the prisoners had escaped.
  Acts 12:18–12:19 — internal custody-crisis contrast: The Philippian jailer supposes escape and prepares to kill himself; after Peter’s real escape Herod examines the guards and orders their execution. The feared and actual outcomes are distinct, and the comparison does not infer a universal penalty or that this jailer received such an order.
    Acts 12:18 When day came, there was no small disturbance among the soldiers over what had become of Peter.
    Acts 12:19 Herod searched for him and did not find him. After examining the guards, he ordered them to be led away for execution. Then he went down from Judea to Caesarea and stayed there.

Acts 16:28
But Paul cried with a loud voice, “Do yourself no harm, for we are all here.”
  Acts 12:19 — internal prison-keeper-outcome contrast: Paul prevents the jailer’s self-harm by announcing that all are still present. Herod instead has Peter’s guards led away for execution after Peter cannot be found. This contrasts the narrated treatment and outcome of keepers; it does not claim that the Philippian jailer was legally condemned or that Peter remained in custody.
    Acts 12:19 Herod searched for him and did not find him. After examining the guards, he ordered them to be led away for execution. Then he went down from Judea to Caesarea and stayed there.

Acts 16:29
He called for lights, rushed inside, and fell trembling before Paul and Silas.
  Acts 10:25–10:26 — internal falling-before-missionaries comparison: The trembling jailer falls before Paul and Silas. Cornelius falls at Peter’s feet and bows, and Peter raises him while affirming that he too is human. The bodily response is comparable, but Acts16 does not expressly call the jailer’s action worship or repeat Peter’s words.
    Acts 10:25 As Peter was about to enter, Cornelius met him, fell at his feet, and bowed before him.
    Acts 10:26 But Peter raised him up, saying, “Rise; I too am a human being.”

Acts 16:30
He brought them outside and said, “Sirs, what must I do to be saved?”
  Acts 2:37–2:38 — internal salvation-response-question parallel: The jailer asks what he must do to be saved; the Pentecost hearers ask Peter and the apostles what they should do and receive a repentance-and-baptism answer. The questions arise in different situations, and the different stated answers are retained rather than replaced with one quotation.
    Acts 2:37 When they heard this, they were pierced to the heart and said to Peter and the other apostles, “Men, brothers, what should we do?”
    Acts 2:38 Peter said to them, “Repent, and let each one of you be baptized upon the name of Jesus Christ for the forgiveness of your sins, and you will receive the gift of the Holy Spirit.

Acts 16:31
They said, “Believe in the Lord Jesus, and you will be saved, you and your household.”
  John 3:16–3:18 — thematic faith-and-salvation parallel: Paul and Silas direct the jailer to believe in the Lord Jesus and speak of salvation for him and his household. John links belief in God’s Son with life and salvation rather than perishing or judgment. John does not repeat the household promise or establish that one person’s belief substitutes for every other household member’s response.
    John 3:16 For God loved the world in this way: He gave the only-begotten Son, so that everyone who believes in Him might not perish but have eternal life.
    John 3:17 For God did not send the Son into the world to judge the world, but so that the world might be saved through Him.
    John 3:18 The one who believes in Him is not judged; but the one who does not believe has already been judged, because he has not believed in the name of the only-begotten Son of God.

Acts 16:32
They spoke the word of the Lord to him, together with all who were in his house.
  Acts 10:33–10:34 — internal household-hearing comparison: The missionaries speak the Lord’s word to the jailer and everyone in his house. Cornelius says the gathered group is before God to hear what the Lord commanded Peter, who then begins speaking. The households and speeches differ; no ages or identical membership of the audiences are assumed.
    Acts 10:33 So I sent to you at once, and you have done well to come. Now we are all here before God to hear everything the Lord has commanded you.”
    Acts 10:34 Peter opened his mouth and said, “Truly I understand that God shows no partiality,

Acts 16:33
At that very hour of the night he took them and washed their wounds. Immediately he and all his people were baptized.
  Acts 16:14–16:15 — internal Philippian-household-baptism parallel: The jailer tends the missionaries’ wounds and he and all his people are baptized that night. Earlier Lydia attends to Paul and she and her household are baptized. These are two different Philippian households; only the jailer scene contains wound washing and night timing, and neither gives the household members’ ages.
    Acts 16:14 A woman named Lydia, a seller of purple goods from the city of Thyatira, who worshiped God, was listening. The Lord opened her heart to attend to what Paul was saying.
    Acts 16:15 After she and her household were baptized, she urged us, saying, “If you have judged me faithful to the Lord, come into my house and stay.” And she prevailed upon us.

Acts 16:34
He brought them up into his house, set food before them, and rejoiced with his whole household, having believed in God.
  Acts 16:15 — internal Philippian-hospitality parallel: After the baptism the jailer brings the missionaries into his house, serves food and rejoices with his household. Lydia earlier urges the missionaries to stay in her home after her household’s baptism. The hospitality corresponds, while the meal, joy and particular faith wording belong to their respective scenes.
    Acts 16:15 After she and her household were baptized, she urged us, saying, “If you have judged me faithful to the Lord, come into my house and stay.” And she prevailed upon us.

Acts 16:35
When day came, the magistrates sent the officers, saying, “Release those men.”
  Acts 16:39 — internal release-order-and-performance connection: The magistrates initially send officers with a release order. After Paul’s intervening protest, the magistrates come themselves and lead the men out. These are stages of the same release, with the changed personal involvement retained and no unreported reason supplied for the first order.
    Acts 16:39 They came and appealed to them, led them out, and asked them to leave the city.

Acts 16:36
The jailer reported these words to Paul: “The magistrates have sent word for you to be released. Now come out and go in peace.”
  Acts 16:35 — internal order-and-relayed-message parallel: The jailer reports that the magistrates sent word for release, referring back to their order through the officers. His invitation to come out and go in peace is preserved as part of his speech, not treated as a second independently attested order or silently assigned wholly to the magistrates.
    Acts 16:35 When day came, the magistrates sent the officers, saying, “Release those men.”

Acts 16:37
But Paul said to them, “They beat us publicly without a verdict, although we are Roman citizens, and threw us into prison. And now are they sending us away secretly? No indeed! Let them come themselves and lead us out.”
  Acts 22:25–22:29 — internal Roman-status-protest parallel: Paul protests public beating without a verdict despite Roman citizenship. In Jerusalem he questions scourging an uncondemned Roman citizen and the examination stops when his status becomes known. The first protest follows actual beating and speaks of the men plurally; the later scene concerns Paul before the planned scourging.
    Acts 22:25 As they stretched him out with the straps, Paul said to the centurion standing nearby, “Is it lawful for you to scourge a Roman citizen who has not been condemned?”
    Acts 22:26 When the centurion heard this, he went to the tribune and reported it, saying, “What are you about to do? This man is a Roman.”
    Acts 22:27 The tribune came and said to him, “Tell me, are you a Roman?” He said, “Yes.”
    Acts 22:28 The tribune answered, “I acquired this citizenship for a large sum.” Paul said, “But I was born with it.”
    Acts 22:29 Those about to examine him immediately withdrew. The tribune too was afraid when he learned that Paul was a Roman and that he had bound him.

Acts 16:38
The officers reported these words to the magistrates, and they were afraid when they heard that the men were Romans.
  Acts 22:29 — internal officials-fearing-after-citizenship-disclosure parallel: Philippi’s magistrates fear after hearing that the men are Romans. The Jerusalem tribune fears when he learns Paul is Roman and he had bound him. The shared reaction follows different restraints and information channels; the later verse does not independently identify Silas’s citizenship.
    Acts 22:29 Those about to examine him immediately withdrew. The tribune too was afraid when he learned that Paul was a Roman and that he had bound him.

Acts 16:39
They came and appealed to them, led them out, and asked them to leave the city.
  Acts 16:37 — internal request-and-compliance connection: Paul had rejected secret dismissal and demanded that the officials come and lead the men out. They now come, appeal to them, lead them out and ask them to leave the city. The action answers that request; the edition’s appealed wording is not expanded into an explicit apology or legal verdict.
    Acts 16:37 But Paul said to them, “They beat us publicly without a verdict, although we are Roman citizens, and threw us into prison. And now are they sending us away secretly? No indeed! Let them come themselves and lead us out.”

Acts 16:40
After leaving the prison, they went to Lydia’s house. They saw the brothers, encouraged them, and departed.
  Acts 16:14–16:15 — internal return-to-earlier-host connection: After prison the missionaries go to Lydia’s house, see and encourage the brothers, then depart. The earlier scene introduces Lydia’s faith, household baptism and invitation to stay with her. The named host and hospitality connect the visits without asserting an unreported building arrangement or identifying every brother as a household member.
    Acts 16:14 A woman named Lydia, a seller of purple goods from the city of Thyatira, who worshiped God, was listening. The Lord opened her heart to attend to what Paul was saying.
    Acts 16:15 After she and her household were baptized, she urged us, saying, “If you have judged me faithful to the Lord, come into my house and stay.” And she prevailed upon us.

Acts 17:1
After traveling through Amphipolis and Apollonia, they came to Thessalonica, where there was a Jewish synagogue.
  1 Thessalonians 2:1–2:2 — narrative-and-mission-recollection connection: Acts places the arrival at Thessalonica after the Philippian departure, naming Amphipolis and Apollonia along the way. The letter to the Thessalonians recalls coming to them after suffering at Philippi. It connects the mission sequence without supplying the intermediate towns or another travel date.
    1 Thessalonians 2:1 For you yourselves know, brothers, that our coming to you has not been in vain.
    1 Thessalonians 2:2 But after we had already suffered and been shamefully treated in Philippi, as you know, we were bold in our God to speak to you the gospel of God amid much struggle.

Acts 17:2
As was his custom, Paul went in to them, and for three Sabbaths he reasoned with them from the Scriptures,
  Luke 4:16–4:17 — Sabbath-synagogue-practice comparison: Paul customarily enters the synagogue and reasons from Scripture for three Sabbaths. Luke describes Jesus entering Nazareth’s synagogue on the Sabbath according to His custom and standing to read Isaiah. The shared Scripture-centered Sabbath setting does not identify the towns, speakers, readings or lengths of their visits.
    Luke 4:16 He came to Nazareth, where He had been brought up, and according to His custom entered the synagogue on the Sabbath day. He stood up to read.
    Luke 4:17 The scroll of the prophet Isaiah was given to Him. Unrolling it, He found the place where it was written:

Acts 17:3
explaining and setting before them that the Christ had to suffer and rise from the dead, and saying, “This Jesus whom I proclaim to you is the Christ.”
  Luke 24:46 — thematic proclamation parallel: Paul explains that the Christ had to suffer and rise from the dead and identifies the proclaimed Jesus as the Christ. Jesus’ words in Luke likewise state that the Christ should suffer and rise. Luke specifies the third day; that timing is not inserted into this Acts verse or treated as a quoted detail here.
    Luke 24:46 and said to them, “Thus it is written that the Christ should suffer and rise from the dead on the third day,

Acts 17:4
Some of them were persuaded and joined Paul and Silas, as did a great multitude of devout Greeks and not a few of the leading women.
  Acts 13:43 — internal synagogue-response parallel: In Thessalonica some are persuaded and join Paul and Silas, including devout Greeks and leading women. In Pisidian Antioch many Jews and devout converts follow Paul and Barnabas after the synagogue gathering. The responses connect, while the companions, audiences and convert descriptions are not equated.
    Acts 13:43 After the synagogue gathering was dismissed, many Jews and devout converts followed Paul and Barnabas, who spoke with them and urged them to remain in the grace of God.

Acts 17:5
But the Jews became jealous and took some wicked men from the marketplace, gathered a mob, and threw the city into turmoil. They attacked Jason’s house and sought to bring Paul and Silas out to the populace.
  Acts 13:45 — internal jealousy-and-opposition parallel: Both scenes ascribe jealousy to local Jewish opponents of Paul’s preaching. Pisidian Antioch’s opponents contradict and abuse his words; Thessalonica’s gather marketplace men and attack Jason’s house. The different groups and actions remain particular episodes rather than an ethnic generalization.
    Acts 13:45 When the Jews saw the crowds, they were filled with jealousy and contradicted what Paul was saying, speaking abusively.

Acts 17:6
When they did not find them, they dragged Jason and some brothers before the city officials, shouting, “These men who have upset the inhabited world have come here too,
  Acts 16:19–16:20 — internal dragging-before-authorities comparison: At Philippi owners drag Paul and Silas before rulers and accuse them of disturbing the city. Here, when the missionaries are not found, Jason and other brothers are dragged before city officials and accused in connection with the missionaries. The arrested people, cities and actual charges differ.
    Acts 16:19 When her masters saw that their hope of profit had gone out, they seized Paul and Silas and dragged them into the marketplace before the rulers.
    Acts 16:20 They brought them before the magistrates and said, “These men are throwing our city into turmoil. They are Jews,

Acts 17:7
and Jason has welcomed them. They all act against Caesar’s decrees, saying that there is another king, Jesus.”
  John 19:12 — narrative kingship-and-Caesar-accusation parallel: The Thessalonian accusers say the people act against Caesar’s decrees by proclaiming another king, Jesus. In John, Jesus’ opponents tell Pilate that anyone making himself king speaks against Caesar. The shared political framing occurs in different accusations and does not turn either speech into a narrator-endorsed verdict.
    John 19:12 From this Pilate sought to release Him; but the Jews cried out, saying, “If you release this Man, you are not Caesar’s friend. Everyone who makes himself a king speaks against Caesar.”

Acts 17:8
They disturbed the crowd and the city officials who heard these things.
  Acts 16:20–16:22 — internal civic-accusation-response parallel: The Thessalonian charge disturbs the crowd and city officials. At Philippi the forbidden-customs accusation is followed by the crowd joining an attack and magistrates ordering beatings. The responses are related but not identical: this verse does not say Jason or the brothers received the Philippian beating.
    Acts 16:20 They brought them before the magistrates and said, “These men are throwing our city into turmoil. They are Jews,
    Acts 16:21 and they proclaim customs that we, being Romans, are not permitted to accept or practice.”
    Acts 16:22 The crowd joined the attack against them. The magistrates tore off their garments and ordered them to be beaten with rods.

Acts 17:10
The brothers immediately sent Paul and Silas away by night to Berea. When they arrived, they went into the Jewish synagogue.
  Acts 9:25 — internal assisted-night-withdrawal parallel: Brothers send Paul and Silas away by night toward Berea; Saul’s disciples earlier lower him through Damascus’s wall by night in a hamper. Both involve supporters enabling a threatened missionary’s night withdrawal, but the people, mechanism and destinations are distinct and no hamper is inserted into this journey.
    Acts 9:25 His disciples took him by night and let him down through the wall, lowering him in a hamper.

Acts 17:11
These people were more noble-minded than those in Thessalonica: they welcomed the word with all eagerness, examining the Scriptures daily to see whether these things were so.
  John 5:39–5:40 — Scripture-examination-and-response contrast: The Bereans eagerly welcome the word and examine Scripture to test what they hear. In John Jesus says the hearers search the Scriptures that witness concerning Him yet are unwilling to come to Him for life. The passages connect Scripture and response to the message about Jesus without identifying the audiences or treating every examination as unbelief.
    John 5:39 You search the Scriptures because you think that in them you have eternal life; and it is they that bear witness concerning Me.
    John 5:40 Yet you are unwilling to come to Me so that you may have life.

Acts 17:12
Many of them therefore believed, as did not a few Greek women of high standing and men.
  Acts 17:4 — internal contrasting-city-reception parallel: Both the Berean and Thessalonian accounts record belief or persuaded adherence involving Greeks and prominent women. The Berean wording follows their daily Scripture examination and also names men. The comparison retains distinct cities, groups and descriptions, rather than assigning the Bereans the exact numbers or response of Thessalonica.
    Acts 17:4 Some of them were persuaded and joined Paul and Silas, as did a great multitude of devout Greeks and not a few of the leading women.

Acts 17:13
But when the Jews from Thessalonica learned that the word of God had also been proclaimed by Paul in Berea, they came there too, stirring up and disturbing the crowds.
  Acts 14:19 — internal traveling-opposition parallel: Opponents travel from Thessalonica to Berea and disturb crowds against Paul’s preaching. Earlier Jews travel from Antioch and Iconium to Lystra, persuade the crowds and stone Paul. The cross-city pursuit is comparable, but this Berean verse does not report the earlier stoning or identify the opponents as the same people.
    Acts 14:19 Jews came from Antioch and Iconium, persuaded the crowds, stoned Paul, and dragged him outside the city, supposing he was dead.

Acts 17:14
Then the brothers immediately sent Paul away to go as far as the sea, while Silas and Timothy remained there.
  Acts 9:29–9:30 — internal protective-sending-away parallel: Brothers send Paul toward the sea while Silas and Timothy remain. Earlier, after a killing attempt, brothers bring Saul to Caesarea and send him to Tarsus. The supporters’ sending Paul away after opposition connects the accounts, while the routes and destinations differ; Acts17 does not specify a sea voyage or transfer the earlier companions into this scene.
    Acts 9:29 He spoke and disputed with the Hellenists, but they attempted to kill him.
    Acts 9:30 When the brothers learned this, they brought him down to Caesarea and sent him away to Tarsus.

Acts 17:15
Those escorting Paul brought him as far as Athens. After receiving an order for Silas and Timothy to come to him as quickly as possible, they departed.
  Acts 18:5 — internal companion-travel follow-up: Paul’s escorts carry a request for Silas and Timothy to join him quickly at Athens. The later narrative explicitly records those same two arriving from Macedonia while Paul is at Corinth. This follows their travel thread without asserting an unreported Athens visit, its absence, or the exact time they received and fulfilled the request.
    Acts 18:5 When Silas and Timothy came down from Macedonia, Paul devoted himself to the word, bearing solemn witness to the Jews that Jesus is the Christ.

Acts 17:16
While Paul waited for them in Athens, his spirit was provoked within him as he observed that the city was full of idols.
  Acts 17:29 — internal setting-and-argument connection: Paul’s spirit is provoked by Athens’s idols. His later Athens argument rejects thinking of the divine nature as a humanly fashioned gold, silver or stone image. That argument addresses this observed setting; the later words are not inserted as a speech already delivered in this observation verse.
    Acts 17:29 Since we are God’s offspring, we ought not to think that the divine nature is like gold or silver or stone, an image formed by human art and thought.

Acts 17:17
So he reasoned in the synagogue with the Jews and the worshipers, and in the marketplace every day with those who happened to be there.
  Acts 18:4 — internal Pauline-reasoning-practice comparison: In Athens Paul reasons with Jews and worshipers in the synagogue and with passersby in the marketplace daily. In Corinth he reasons every Sabbath in the synagogue, seeking to persuade Jews and Greeks. The repeated activity connects his ministry, while the cities, audiences, venues and schedules remain distinct.
    Acts 18:4 Every Sabbath he reasoned in the synagogue and sought to persuade both Jews and Greeks.

Acts 17:18
Some Epicurean and Stoic philosophers also engaged him. Some said, “What is this babbler trying to say?” Others said, “He seems to be a proclaimer of foreign divinities,” because he proclaimed the good news of Jesus and the resurrection.
  Acts 17:3 — internal message-and-hearers-description contrast: The narrator explains that the foreign-divinities accusation arose because Paul proclaimed Jesus and the resurrection. His earlier Thessalonian preaching explicitly identifies Jesus as the Christ who had to suffer and rise. The connection clarifies the proclaimed subject without endorsing the philosophers’ accusation or inventing a separate goddess called Resurrection.
    Acts 17:3 explaining and setting before them that the Christ had to suffer and rise from the dead, and saying, “This Jesus whom I proclaim to you is the Christ.”

Acts 17:19
They took him and brought him to the Areopagus, saying, “May we know what this new teaching is that you are speaking?
  Acts 17:22–17:23 — internal invitation-and-speech connection: The hearers bring Paul to the Areopagus asking to know his new teaching. The subsequent speech places him in the midst of the Areopagus and begins explaining the worship they do not know. This is the recorded response in that setting, not evidence that the invitation was a formal criminal trial.
    Acts 17:22 Paul stood in the midst of the Areopagus and said, “Men of Athens, I perceive that in every respect you are very religious.
    Acts 17:23 For as I passed through and observed your objects of worship, I also found an altar inscribed, ‘To an unknown god.’ What you worship without knowing, this I proclaim to you.

Acts 17:23
For as I passed through and observed your objects of worship, I also found an altar inscribed, ‘To an unknown god.’ What you worship without knowing, this I proclaim to you.
  John 4:22 — thematic unknown-worship comparison: Paul proclaims what the Athenians worship without knowing, beginning from an altar to an unknown god. Jesus tells the Samaritan woman that her group worships what it does not know and contrasts known worship and salvation from the Jews. The audiences and histories differ, and neither passage makes Athens and Samaria the same cult or endorses their objects of worship.
    John 4:22 You worship what you do not know; we worship what we know, because salvation is from the Jews.

Acts 17:24
The God who made the world and everything in it, being Lord of heaven and earth, does not dwell in sanctuaries made by hands.
  Acts 7:48–7:50 — internal Creator-and-handmade-sanctuary parallel: Paul says the Maker and Lord of heaven and earth does not dwell in handmade sanctuaries. Stephen says the Most High does not dwell in handmade things and quotes the heaven-throne, earth-footstool and divine-hand creation words. The shared claim has different audiences and formulations, rather than making Paul quote every line of Stephen’s speech.
    Acts 7:48 Yet the Most High does not dwell in things made by hands, as the prophet says:
    Acts 7:49 ‘Heaven is My throne, and the earth the footstool of My feet. What kind of house will you build for Me, says the Lord, or what place is there for My rest?
    Acts 7:50 Did not My hand make all these things?’

Acts 17:25
Nor is He served by human hands as though He needed anything, since He Himself gives everyone life and breath and everything.
  Isaiah 42:5 — thematic Creator-as-giver-of-breath parallel: Paul says God gives everyone life, breath and everything rather than needing human service. Isaiah describes the Maker of heaven and earth giving breath to earth’s people and spirit to those walking it. The life-giving claim connects; Isaiah’s verse does not independently supply Paul’s whole argument against treating God as needy.
    Isaiah 42:5 Thus says the Lord God, who made heaven and fixed it in place, established the earth and its contents, and gives breath to the people upon it and spirit to those treading it:

Acts 17:26
From one He made every nation of humanity to live upon the whole face of the earth, determining their appointed times and the boundaries of their dwelling,
  Deuteronomy 32:8 — thematic divine-ordering-of-nations parallel: Both passages describe divine ordering of nations and their boundaries. Deuteronomy names the Most High scattering the sons of Adam and setting bounds according to the angels of God; Acts says from one, with appointed times and dwelling boundaries. Acts’s unnamed one is not expanded into one blood, and Deuteronomy’s actual angels wording is not replaced with another edition.
    Deuteronomy 32:8 When the Most High divided the nations, when He scattered the sons of Adam, He set the bounds of the nations according to the number of the angels of God.

Acts 17:27
so that they might seek God, if perhaps they might feel their way toward Him and find Him, though He is not far from each one of us.
  Deuteronomy 4:29 — thematic seeking-and-finding-God parallel: Acts describes the nations’ purpose of seeking God and perhaps feeling toward and finding Him, who is not far away. Deuteronomy promises finding the Lord when the scattered people seek Him with their whole heart and soul in distress. The addressees, conditions and degree of assurance differ and are not combined into one quotation.
    Deuteronomy 4:29 And there you shall seek the Lord your God, and shall find Him, when you seek Him with all your heart and with all your soul, in your distress.

Acts 17:28
For in Him we live and move and exist, as even some of your own poets have said, ‘For we too are His offspring.’
  Job 12:10 — thematic life-dependent-on-God parallel: Paul speaks of living, moving and existing in God before citing the hearers’ poets about offspring. Job asks whether all living life and every human breath are in God’s hand. The dependence of life on God connects, while the hand and in-Him images differ; Job is not identified as the source of the quoted poets or of the offspring wording.
    Job 12:10 Is not the life of all living things in His hand, and the breath of every human being?

Acts 17:29
Since we are God’s offspring, we ought not to think that the divine nature is like gold or silver or stone, an image formed by human art and thought.
  Isaiah 40:18–40:20 — thematic divine-image critique: Paul rejects identifying the divine nature with gold, silver or stone shaped by human art and thought. Isaiah asks what likeness can represent the Lord and describes an artisan’s gold-gilded or wooden image. The critiques connect without adding Acts’s offspring argument or every named material to Isaiah’s different illustration.
    Isaiah 40:18 To whom did you liken the Lord, and to what likeness did you liken Him?
    Isaiah 40:19 Did a craftsman make an image, or did a goldsmith melt gold and gild it, fashioning it as a likeness?
    Isaiah 40:20 For a craftsman chooses wood that will not rot and skillfully seeks how to set up his image so that it will not shake.

Acts 17:30
Therefore, having overlooked the times of ignorance, God now commands all people everywhere to repent,
  Acts 14:15–14:17 — internal Gentile-speech temporal comparison: Paul contrasts previously overlooked ignorance with God’s present command that all repent. At Lystra the missionaries urge turning to the Creator and say He formerly allowed nations to go their own ways while still giving witness through good gifts. These distinct speeches share past-versus-present framing without defining overlooked ignorance as approval, universal forgiveness or absence of every earlier witness.
    Acts 14:15 and saying, “Men, why are you doing these things? We too are human beings, of the same nature as you. We proclaim good news to you: turn from these worthless things to the living God, who made heaven and earth and the sea and everything in them.
    Acts 14:16 In past generations He allowed all the nations to go their own ways.
    Acts 14:17 Yet He did not leave Himself without witness in doing good, giving you rains from heaven and fruitful seasons, filling your hearts with food and gladness.”

Acts 17:31
because He has fixed a day on which He will judge the inhabited world in righteousness through a Man whom He appointed, giving assurance to everyone by raising Him from the dead.”
  Acts 10:42 — internal appointed-judge proclamation parallel: Paul announces God’s fixed day of righteous judgment through an appointed Man, with resurrection as assurance. Peter’s preaching likewise identifies the One appointed by God as judge of living and dead. The appointment connects, while Acts17’s day, righteousness and assurance clauses are not quoted from Peter’s shorter formulation or used to supply a date.
    Acts 10:42 He commanded us to proclaim to the people and bear solemn witness that He is the One appointed by God as judge of the living and the dead.

Acts 17:32
When they heard of a resurrection of the dead, some mocked, while others said, “We will hear you again about this.”
  1 Corinthians 15:12–15:14 — narrative-and-resurrection-dispute comparison: Athens’s resurrection announcement meets mockery from some and a request for later hearing from others. Paul’s Corinthian argument challenges some saying there is no resurrection and explains the consequence for Christ’s resurrection and proclamation. The objection concerns the same subject, but the groups and responses are not identified or generalized to all Greeks.
    1 Corinthians 15:12 Now if Christ is proclaimed as raised from the dead, how can some among you say that there is no resurrection of the dead?
    1 Corinthians 15:13 But if there is no resurrection of the dead, neither has Christ been raised.
    1 Corinthians 15:14 And if Christ has not been raised, then our proclamation is also empty, and your faith is empty too.

Acts 18:1
After these things he left Athens and came to Corinth.
  1 Corinthians 2:1–2:3 — narrative-and-Corinth-mission-recollection connection: Acts records Paul leaving Athens and coming to Corinth. His letter to Corinth recalls coming to its readers proclaiming God’s mystery and Jesus Christ crucified, in weakness and fear. The recollection connects his Corinth mission without supplying the Athens route or inserting those reported feelings into every step of the narrative arrival.
    1 Corinthians 2:1 And when I came to you, brothers, I did not come with lofty speech or wisdom, proclaiming to you the mystery of God.
    1 Corinthians 2:2 For I decided to know nothing among you except Jesus Christ, and Him crucified.
    1 Corinthians 2:3 And I was with you in weakness and in fear and in much trembling.

Acts 18:2
He found a Jew named Aquila, a native of Pontus who had recently come from Italy with his wife Priscilla, because Claudius had ordered all the Jews to leave Rome. Paul went to them,
  1 Corinthians 16:19 — biographical ministry-couple connection: Acts introduces Aquila and his wife Priscilla, with Aquila’s Pontic origin and their departure from Italy under Claudius. The Corinthian letter sends greetings from Aquila and Prisca and the Church in their house. The paired names and ministry connect, with the Prisca/Priscilla forms retained; the letter does not repeat the expulsion or assign Aquila’s birthplace to his wife.
    1 Corinthians 16:19 The Churches of Asia greet you. Aquila and Prisca, together with the Church in their house, greet you warmly in the Lord.

Acts 18:3
and because he practiced the same trade, he stayed with them and worked, for they were tentmakers by trade.
  Acts 20:34–20:35 — internal manual-work-and-support parallel: Paul works at the tentmaking trade shared with his Corinth hosts. In his later address to the Ephesian elders he says his hands served his own and his companions’ needs and urges work to support the weak. The common labor connects while the locations and descriptions differ; the later speech does not independently name tentmaking.
    Acts 20:34 You yourselves know that these hands served my needs and those of the people with me.
    Acts 20:35 In everything I showed you that by working in this way we must support the weak and remember the words of the Lord Jesus, that He Himself said, ‘It is more blessed to give than to receive.’”

Acts 18:4
Every Sabbath he reasoned in the synagogue and sought to persuade both Jews and Greeks.
  Acts 17:2 — internal synagogue-reasoning parallel: Both visits describe Paul reasoning in a synagogue on Sabbaths. Thessalonica specifies three Sabbaths and Scripture reasoning; Corinth says every Sabbath and persuasion directed to Jews and Greeks. The three-Sabbath count and exact audience are not transferred between the cities.
    Acts 17:2 As was his custom, Paul went in to them, and for three Sabbaths he reasoned with them from the Scriptures,

Acts 18:5
When Silas and Timothy came down from Macedonia, Paul devoted himself to the word, bearing solemn witness to the Jews that Jesus is the Christ.
  Acts 17:15 — internal companion-travel follow-up: Silas and Timothy arrive from Macedonia while Paul is at Corinth, continuing the earlier request that they join him after he reached Athens. The names and travel thread connect; this notice does not establish whether they had also visited Athens or give an unreported itinerary between the two scenes.
    Acts 17:15 Those escorting Paul brought him as far as Athens. After receiving an order for Silas and Timothy to come to him as quickly as possible, they departed.

Acts 18:6
When they opposed him and spoke abusively, he shook out his garments and said to them, “Your blood be upon your own heads. I am clean. From now on I will go to the Gentiles.”
  Acts 13:46–13:51 — internal rejection-and-Gentile-turn parallel: At Corinth Paul responds to opposition by shaking his garments and saying he will go to the Gentiles. At Pisidian Antioch Paul and Barnabas announce a Gentile turn after rejection and later shake dust from their feet. The turns and gestures connect, but garments and feet are not made identical, nor are the opponents or places merged.
    Acts 13:46 Paul and Barnabas spoke boldly and said, “It was necessary for God’s word to be spoken to you first. Since you reject it and judge yourselves unworthy of eternal life, look—we are turning to the Gentiles.
    Acts 13:47 For the Lord has commanded us in this way: ‘I have appointed you as a light for Gentiles, so that you may bring salvation to the end of the earth.’”
    Acts 13:48 When the Gentiles heard this, they rejoiced and glorified the word of the Lord, and as many as had been appointed to eternal life believed.
    Acts 13:49 The word of the Lord spread through the whole region.
    Acts 13:50 But the Jews stirred up the devout women of high standing and the leading men of the city, raised persecution against Paul and Barnabas, and drove them out of their territory.
    Acts 13:51 They shook the dust from their feet against them and went to Iconium.
  Acts 20:26–20:27 — internal clean-from-blood declaration parallel: Paul tells the Corinthian opponents their blood is on their own heads and that he is clean. Later he declares himself clean from everyone’s blood because he did not withhold God’s whole purpose. The shared responsibility language has different audiences and scope; neither declaration is rewritten as Paul threatening physical violence.
    Acts 20:26 Therefore I testify to you this very day that I am clean from the blood of all,
    Acts 20:27 for I did not hold back from declaring to you the whole purpose of God.

Acts 18:8
Crispus, the synagogue ruler, believed in the Lord with his whole household. Many of the Corinthians who heard believed and were baptized.
  1 Corinthians 1:14–1:16 — narrative-and-named-baptism-recollection connection: Acts has Corinth’s synagogue ruler Crispus believe with his household and many Corinthians hear, believe and be baptized. Paul’s letter to Corinth names Crispus among the few he personally baptized. The named Corinthian connection is precise, but the letter does not repeat Crispus’s office or say Paul personally baptized every Corinthian or every member of Crispus’s household.
    1 Corinthians 1:14 I give thanks to God that I baptized none of you except Crispus and Gaius,
    1 Corinthians 1:15 so that no one may say that you were baptized into my own name.
    1 Corinthians 1:16 I did also baptize the household of Stephanas; beyond that, I do not know whether I baptized anyone else.

Acts 18:9
The Lord said to Paul by a vision in the night, “Do not fear, but speak and do not be silent,
  Acts 23:11 — internal nocturnal-Lord-encouragement parallel: The Lord tells Paul in a Corinth night vision to keep speaking without fear. On the later night in Jerusalem the Lord stands beside him, gives courage and speaks of witness in Rome. The encounters share encouragement for witness, but Acts23 is not silently labeled the same vision or given Corinth’s particular promise.
    Acts 23:11 The following night the Lord stood beside him and said, “Take courage, for as you have borne solemn witness about Me in Jerusalem, so you must also testify in Rome.”

Acts 18:10
because I am with you, and no one will attack you to harm you, for I have many people in this city.”
  Isaiah 41:10 — thematic divine-presence assurance: The Corinth vision assures Paul of the Lord’s presence, protection from harm there and many people in that city. Isaiah’s servant-address assurance likewise announces God’s presence and describes His having strengthened, helped and secured the recipient. The addressees and specific protections differ; no general promise that Paul or every believer will never suffer injury is inferred.
    Isaiah 41:10 do not fear, for I am with you; do not wander, for I am your God who strengthened you. I helped you and secured you with My righteous right hand.

Acts 18:11
He stayed a year and six months, teaching the word of God among them.
  Acts 19:10 — internal extended-teaching-duration comparison: Acts specifies a year and six months of Paul teaching in Corinth, and later two years of the Ephesian instruction through which Asia hears the word. These are distinct location-specific time notices, not interchangeable totals or evidence that every period of Paul’s wider ministry lasted the same time.
    Acts 19:10 This continued for two years, so that all who lived in Asia heard the word of the Lord, both Jews and Greeks.

Acts 18:12
While Gallio was proconsul of Achaia, the Jews rose together against Paul and brought him before the tribunal,
  Acts 25:6–25:7 — internal tribunal-and-accusation parallel: Paul is brought before Gallio’s tribunal in Achaia by local Jewish accusers. The later Caesarean scene has the seated governor order Paul brought before him and Jerusalem accusers make charges. The Roman hearing settings connect without identifying the governors, accusers, charges or outcomes.
    Acts 25:6 After staying among them no more than eight or ten days, he went down to Caesarea. The next day he sat on the tribunal and ordered Paul to be brought in.
    Acts 25:7 When he arrived, the Jews who had come down from Jerusalem stood around him, bringing many serious accusations that they could not prove.

Acts 18:13
saying, “This man persuades people to worship God contrary to the law.”
  Acts 21:28 — internal hostile-law-accusation comparison: The Corinthian accusers say Paul persuades unlawful worship. In Jerusalem opponents accuse him of teaching against the people, law and temple, adding an alleged Greek entry. These are different reported accusations, not narrator verdicts; the temple allegation and its stated supposition are not inserted into Gallio’s case.
    Acts 21:28 crying, “Men of Israel, help! This is the man who teaches everyone everywhere against the people, the law, and this place. Moreover, he has even brought Greeks into the temple and defiled this holy place.”

Acts 18:14
As Paul was about to open his mouth, Gallio said to the Jews, “If it were some wrongdoing or wicked crime, O Jews, I would have reason to hear you out.
  Acts 25:18–25:19 — internal Roman-official-dispute-assessment comparison: Gallio distinguishes an ordinary wrong or wicked crime, which he would hear, from the dispute actually presented. Festus later says he expected evil charges but instead found disputes about the accusers’ religion and Jesus’ being alive. The assessments are by different officials; Gallio’s conditional statement is not converted into Festus’s actual report or a universal legal rule.
    Acts 25:18 When his accusers stood up, they brought no charge of the evil things I had expected.
    Acts 25:19 Instead, they had certain disputes with him concerning their own religion and a certain Jesus who had died, whom Paul asserted to be alive.

Acts 18:15
But if these are questions about a word, names, and your own law, see to it yourselves. I do not wish to be a judge of these things.”
  Acts 23:29 — internal law-question-assessment parallel: Gallio calls the case questions of words, names and the accusers’ own law and refuses to judge them. The tribune’s later letter likewise describes questions of their law, but explicitly finds no charge deserving death or imprisonment. The religious-dispute characterization connects while the separate officials’ conclusions retain their actual scope.
    Acts 23:29 I found him accused over questions of their law, but under no charge deserving death or imprisonment.

Acts 18:16
He drove them away from the tribunal.
  Acts 18:15 — internal refusal-and-action connection: Gallio’s driving the accusers from the tribunal follows his stated refusal to judge their questions of words, names and law. The statement and narrated action belong to the same hearing; the action is not expanded into an acquittal on every possible charge or a general religious policy.
    Acts 18:15 But if these are questions about a word, names, and your own law, see to it yourselves. I do not wish to be a judge of these things.”

Acts 18:18
Paul stayed many more days, then took leave of the brothers and sailed for Syria, with Priscilla and Aquila. He had cut his hair at Cenchreae, for he had a vow.
  Acts 21:23–21:24 — internal vow-and-haircut comparison: The departure notice includes a haircut at Cenchreae because of a vow. The later Jerusalem instructions concern four men under a vow, purification, expenses and head shaving. The related vow-and-hair practices are compared without identifying the vows, resolving the source pronoun beyond its wording, or declaring an unstated Nazirite duration or procedure.
    Acts 21:23 Therefore do what we tell you. We have four men who have taken a vow upon themselves.
    Acts 21:24 Take these men, purify yourself with them, and pay their expenses so that they may shave their heads. Then everyone will know that there is nothing to what they have been told about you, but that you yourself also walk in step, keeping the law.

Acts 18:19
They arrived at Ephesus, and he left them there. He himself entered the synagogue and reasoned with the Jews.
  Acts 19:8 — internal Ephesus-synagogue-visit comparison: Paul reasons with Jews in Ephesus on this stop. The later Ephesian stay has him speak boldly in the synagogue for three months about God’s kingdom. The city and practice connect, while the three months and extended later stay are not imposed on this earlier brief visit.
    Acts 19:8 He entered the synagogue and spoke boldly for three months, reasoning and persuading concerning the kingdom of God.

Acts 18:20
When they asked him to stay for a longer time, he did not agree.
  Acts 19:10 — internal shorter-and-longer-Ephesus-stay contrast: Paul declines the request to remain longer on this Ephesian visit. After his later return the instruction continues for two years. The contrasting stays belong to distinct visits and do not supply the unreported reason he declined this first invitation or make his refusal contradict the later stay.
    Acts 19:10 This continued for two years, so that all who lived in Asia heard the word of the Lord, both Jews and Greeks.

Acts 18:21
Instead, he took leave of them, saying, “I will return to you again, God willing,” and sailed from Ephesus.
  Acts 19:1 — internal conditional-plan-and-return connection: Paul leaves Ephesus saying he will return if God wills. The later itinerary explicitly brings him back to Ephesus. That narrative fulfills the stated destination without adding a departure date, the reason for the earlier refusal or an unreported direct command behind the conditional plan.
    Acts 19:1 While Apollos was in Corinth, Paul passed through the upper regions and came down to Ephesus. He found some disciples

Acts 18:22
He landed at Caesarea, went up and greeted the Church, and went down to Antioch.
  Acts 14:26–14:28 — internal Antioch-return comparison: This travel notice reaches Antioch after Caesarea and a greeting of the Church. The earlier mission ends in Antioch with the Church gathered for a report. The returns connect Paul’s mission base, but the unnamed location of this verse’s went-up Church greeting is not silently supplied as Jerusalem or merged with the earlier report.
    Acts 14:26 From there they sailed to Antioch, where they had been entrusted to the grace of God for the work they had now completed.
    Acts 14:27 When they arrived and gathered the Church, they reported all that God had done with them and how He had opened a door of faith to the Gentiles.
    Acts 14:28 They stayed quite a long time with the disciples.

Acts 18:23
After spending some time there, he departed and traveled from place to place through the Galatian region and Phrygia, strengthening all the disciples.
  Acts 16:5–16:6 — internal regional-follow-up comparison: Paul passes through the Galatian region and Phrygia strengthening disciples. The earlier journey reports strengthened Churches and passage through Phrygia and the Galatian region with an Asia-speaking restriction. The regional follow-up connects, while order, time and the earlier Spirit restriction are not transferred to this later trip.
    Acts 16:5 So the Churches were strengthened in the faith and increased in number each day.
    Acts 16:6 They passed through Phrygia and the Galatian region, having been prevented by the Holy Spirit from speaking the word in Asia.

Acts 18:24
A Jew named Apollos, a native of Alexandria, came to Ephesus. He was an eloquent man, powerful in the Scriptures.
  1 Corinthians 3:5–3:6 — biographical ministry connection: Acts introduces Apollos and his scriptural ability before his Achaian ministry. Paul’s Corinthian letter calls Apollos a servant through whom people believed and one who watered after Paul planted. The ministerial connection does not make the letter independently state his Alexandrian origin or the exact form of his eloquence.
    1 Corinthians 3:5 What, then, is Apollos? And what is Paul? Servants through whom you believed, each as the Lord granted.
    1 Corinthians 3:6 I planted, Apollos watered, but God gave the growth.

Acts 18:25
He had been instructed in the way of the Lord, and being fervent in spirit, he spoke and taught accurately the things concerning Jesus, though he knew only John’s baptism.
  Acts 19:3–19:4 — internal John-baptism-instruction comparison: Apollos teaches accurately about Jesus while knowing only John’s baptism. The later Ephesian disciples also name John’s baptism, and Paul explains John’s direction toward Jesus. Their stated knowledge differs; Apollos is not said here to share those disciples’ ignorance of the Spirit, to have founded their group or to undergo their later baptism.
    Acts 19:3 He said, “Into what, then, were you baptized?” They said, “Into John’s baptism.”
    Acts 19:4 Paul said, “John baptized with a baptism of repentance, telling the people to believe in the One coming after him, that is, in Jesus.”

Acts 18:26
He began to speak boldly in the synagogue. When Priscilla and Aquila heard him, they took him aside and explained the way of God to him more accurately.
  Romans 16:3 — biographical coworker-couple connection: Priscilla and Aquila together explain God’s way more accurately to Apollos. Paul’s greeting names Prisca and Aquila as fellow workers in Christ Jesus. The paired ministry connection retains the name forms and different activities, without claiming that the letter retells Apollos’s instruction or assigns the couple an unstated formal office.
    Romans 16:3 Greet Prisca and Aquila, my fellow workers in Christ Jesus,

Acts 18:27
When he wished to cross over to Achaia, the brothers encouraged him and wrote to the disciples to welcome him. On arriving, he greatly helped those who had believed through grace,
  1 Corinthians 3:6 — narrative-and-ministry-description parallel: Apollos helps the believers after being commended to the disciples in Achaia. Paul’s Corinthian letter describes Apollos watering what Paul planted, with God giving growth. The supportive ministry connects, while the letter does not reproduce the commendation or equate all Achaia with one Corinthian audience.
    1 Corinthians 3:6 I planted, Apollos watered, but God gave the growth.

Acts 18:28
for he vigorously refuted the Jews in public, demonstrating through the Scriptures that Jesus is the Christ.
  Acts 17:2–17:3 — internal Scripture-based-Christ-demonstration parallel: Apollos demonstrates through Scripture that Jesus is the Christ; Paul had likewise reasoned from Scripture and identified the proclaimed Jesus as the Christ. The teachers and settings differ, and Paul’s explicit suffering-and-resurrection explanation is not inserted as a quotation of Apollos’s unrecorded argument.
    Acts 17:2 As was his custom, Paul went in to them, and for three Sabbaths he reasoned with them from the Scriptures,
    Acts 17:3 explaining and setting before them that the Christ had to suffer and rise from the dead, and saying, “This Jesus whom I proclaim to you is the Christ.”

Acts 19:1
While Apollos was in Corinth, Paul passed through the upper regions and came down to Ephesus. He found some disciples
  Acts 18:21 — internal return-and-earlier-plan connection: Paul arrives at Ephesus after previously leaving with a conditional promise to return. The named destination and return connect, while this arrival does not supply an exact schedule or make the earlier God-willing wording an unconditional prediction. The simultaneous Apollos-in-Corinth notice remains its own statement.
    Acts 18:21 Instead, he took leave of them, saying, “I will return to you again, God willing,” and sailed from Ephesus.

Acts 19:2
and said to them, “Did you receive the Holy Spirit when you believed?” They said to him, “We have not even heard whether there is a Holy Spirit.”
  Acts 8:15–8:17 — internal prior-response-and-Spirit-reception comparison: Paul asks these disciples about receiving the Spirit, and they report not even hearing whether there is a Holy Spirit. In Samaria baptized hearers have not yet received the Spirit until the apostles pray and lay hands. The Ephesian knowledge report is not supplied for Samaria, and their baptismal contexts differ. Neither sequence becomes a universal rule, and the source’s knowledge statement is not rewritten to mean only that the Spirit had not yet been given.
    Acts 8:15 They went down and prayed for them to receive the Holy Spirit,
    Acts 8:16 for He had not yet fallen upon any of them; they had only been baptized into the name of the Lord Jesus.
    Acts 8:17 Then they laid their hands upon them, and they received the Holy Spirit.

Acts 19:3
He said, “Into what, then, were you baptized?” They said, “Into John’s baptism.”
  Acts 18:25 — internal John-baptism connection: The Ephesian disciples answer that they were baptized into John’s baptism. The preceding Apollos account also mentions knowing only John’s baptism while teaching Jesus accurately. The shared baptismal reference does not identify the disciples as Apollos’s converts or make their stated knowledge and subsequent treatment identical.
    Acts 18:25 He had been instructed in the way of the Lord, and being fervent in spirit, he spoke and taught accurately the things concerning Jesus, though he knew only John’s baptism.

Acts 19:4
Paul said, “John baptized with a baptism of repentance, telling the people to believe in the One coming after him, that is, in Jesus.”
  Matthew 3:11 — narrative-and-Baptist-proclamation connection: Paul describes John’s repentance baptism and direction toward the One coming after him, explicitly identifying Jesus. Matthew records John’s water baptism for repentance and announcement of the stronger coming One who baptizes in the Holy Spirit and fire. Matthew’s verse does not itself name Jesus, and Paul’s summary does not repeat every Spirit-and-fire clause.
    Matthew 3:11 I baptize you in water for repentance, but the One coming after me is stronger than I, whose sandals I am not fit to carry. He will baptize you in the Holy Spirit and fire.

Acts 19:5
When they heard this, they were baptized into the name of the Lord Jesus.
  Acts 8:16 — internal baptismal-name parallel: Both passages explicitly report baptism into the name of the Lord Jesus. The Samarian verse notes the Spirit had not yet fallen, while the Ephesian account proceeds to Paul’s laying on hands. The shared name formulation does not imply the Samarians also had first received John’s baptism or supply a uniform conversion sequence.
    Acts 8:16 for He had not yet fallen upon any of them; they had only been baptized into the name of the Lord Jesus.

Acts 19:6
When Paul laid his hands upon them, the Holy Spirit came upon them, and they spoke in tongues and prophesied.
  Acts 8:17 — internal hand-laying-and-Spirit-reception parallel: Paul lays hands on the Ephesian disciples and the Spirit comes upon them, with tongues and prophecy. In Samaria apostolic hand laying is followed by receiving the Holy Spirit. Acts8 does not specify these same manifestations, and the comparison does not make hand laying a condition stated for every Spirit reception elsewhere.
    Acts 8:17 Then they laid their hands upon them, and they received the Holy Spirit.

Acts 19:8
He entered the synagogue and spoke boldly for three months, reasoning and persuading concerning the kingdom of God.
  Acts 18:19–18:21 — internal Ephesus-return-and-synagogue connection: This return includes three months of bold synagogue reasoning about God’s kingdom. The earlier visit records synagogue reasoning followed by declining a longer stay and a conditional return promise. The same city and ministry thread connect without making the initial visit last three months or giving the same words to both visits.
    Acts 18:19 They arrived at Ephesus, and he left them there. He himself entered the synagogue and reasoned with the Jews.
    Acts 18:20 When they asked him to stay for a longer time, he did not agree.
    Acts 18:21 Instead, he took leave of them, saying, “I will return to you again, God willing,” and sailed from Ephesus.

Acts 19:9
But when some became hardened and refused to believe, speaking evil of the Way before the multitude, he withdrew from them and separated the disciples, reasoning daily in the lecture hall of Tyrannus.
  Acts 18:6–18:7 — internal opposition-and-teaching-venue-change parallel: At Ephesus opposition to the Way leads Paul to withdraw and reason with separated disciples in Tyrannus’s lecture hall. In Corinth he leaves abusive opponents and enters the worshiper Titius Justus’s house beside the synagogue. The withdrawal and venue change connect while the cities, buildings and actors differ; no hall schedule or identity of Tyrannus is supplied.
    Acts 18:6 When they opposed him and spoke abusively, he shook out his garments and said to them, “Your blood be upon your own heads. I am clean. From now on I will go to the Gentiles.”
    Acts 18:7 He left there and entered the house of a man named Titius Justus, a worshiper of God, whose house was next to the synagogue.

Acts 19:10
This continued for two years, so that all who lived in Asia heard the word of the Lord, both Jews and Greeks.
  Acts 20:31 — internal Ephesian-duration-notices comparison: The narrative assigns two years to the instruction just described at Ephesus and reports hearing throughout Asia. Paul’s later speech to the Ephesian elders recalls three years of unceasing admonition with tears. The distinct time notices and scopes are retained without forcing an exact equation, adding unstated intervals or changing two years into the total of every Ephesian activity.
    Acts 20:31 Therefore stay awake, remembering that for three years, night and day, I never ceased admonishing each one with tears.

Acts 19:11
God was doing extraordinary mighty works through Paul’s hands,
  Acts 14:3 — internal God-working-through-missionaries parallel: God does extraordinary mighty works through Paul’s hands here. At Iconium the Lord bears witness to grace by granting signs and wonders through the missionaries’ hands. The shared divine agency and human instrument connect, while the workers, setting and particular works are not merged into a claim of independent human power.
    Acts 14:3 So they stayed a considerable time, speaking boldly in reliance on the Lord, who bore witness to the word of His grace by granting signs and wonders to take place through their hands.

Acts 19:12
so that even cloths or aprons from contact with his skin were carried to the sick, and their diseases left them and the evil spirits went out.
  Acts 5:15–5:16 — internal apostolic-healing-proximity comparison: Acts19 explicitly reports cloths or aprons from Paul’s skin being brought to sick people, with diseases and spirits leaving. Acts5 describes people seeking Peter’s passing shadow and then reports all the brought sick and spirit-troubled people healed. The earlier passage does not expressly assign each healing to the shadow, and neither scene is made an automatic transferable technique.
    Acts 5:15 so that they even carried the sick into the streets and laid them on small beds and mats, so that as Peter came by, at least his shadow might fall upon one of them.
    Acts 5:16 The crowd also came together from the towns around Jerusalem, bringing the sick and those troubled by unclean spirits, and they were all healed.

Acts 19:13
Some itinerant Jewish exorcists also attempted to invoke the name of the Lord Jesus over those who had evil spirits, saying, “I solemnly charge you by the Jesus whom Paul proclaims.”
  Mark 9:38–9:40 — Jesus-name-exorcism contrast: The itinerant exorcists attempt Jesus’ name through the formulation about the Jesus Paul proclaims. In Mark John reports an outsider successfully casting out demons in Jesus’ name and Jesus tells him not to stop the man. The different outcomes and responses prevent equating every outsider with these later failed invokers or making formal affiliation the text’s stated universal test.
    Mark 9:38 John said to Him, “Teacher, we saw someone casting out demons in Thy name, and we tried to stop him, because he was not following us.”
    Mark 9:39 Jesus said, “Do not stop him, for no one who does a mighty work in My name will be able soon afterward to speak evil of Me.
    Mark 9:40 For whoever is not against us is for us.

Acts 19:15
But the evil spirit answered them, “Jesus I know, and Paul I recognize, but who are you?”
  Mark 1:24 — narrative spirit-associated-recognition comparison: The spirit recognizes Jesus and Paul but challenges the invokers. Mark’s unclean-spirit speech recognizes Jesus of Nazareth as God’s Holy One. The Jesus recognition connects, but Paul and the invokers are not inserted into Mark’s speech, and a spirit’s recognition is not equated with discipleship or authority to invoke the name.
    Mark 1:24 “What have we to do with Thee, Jesus of Nazareth? Hast Thou come to destroy us? I know who Thou art—the Holy One of God!”

Acts 19:16
The man in whom the evil spirit was leaped upon them, mastered both of them, and overpowered them, so that they fled from that house naked and wounded.
  Mark 5:3–5:4 — narrative overpowering-strength comparison: The man with the evil spirit overpowers the invokers and they flee wounded. Mark’s man with an unclean spirit breaks chains and fetters and cannot be subdued. The violent strength connects different people and circumstances; Acts’s mastered both wording remains alongside its preceding seven-son notice rather than being silently renumbered.
    Mark 5:3 He made his dwelling among the tombs, and no one could bind him anymore, even with a chain.
    Mark 5:4 He had often been bound with fetters and chains, but he had torn the chains apart and shattered the fetters. No one had the strength to subdue him.

Acts 19:17
This became known to all the Jews and Greeks living in Ephesus. Fear fell upon them all, and the name of the Lord Jesus was magnified.
  Luke 7:16 — narrative fear-and-divine-honor response comparison: Ephesus hears the failed-invocation event, fear falls and Jesus’ name is magnified. After Jesus raises the widow’s son, Luke describes fear and glorifying God with reported claims of prophetic visitation. These are distinct events and reported responses; the comparison does not infer uniform repentance from fear or treat the crowd’s precise claims as identical.
    Luke 7:16 Fear seized everyone, and they glorified God, saying, “A great prophet has arisen among us,” and, “God has visited His people.”

Acts 19:18
Many who had believed came, confessing and reporting their practices.
  Matthew 3:6 — thematic confession-of-conduct parallel: Believers come confessing and reporting their practices; Matthew describes those baptized by John confessing their sins. The shared confession concerns different groups and settings. This Acts19 notice does not thereby become another report of John’s baptism, or identify all confessors as the failed exorcists.
    Matthew 3:6 and they were baptized by him in the Jordan River, confessing their sins.

Acts 19:19
A considerable number who had practiced magic brought their books together and burned them before everyone. They calculated their prices and found the total to be fifty thousand in silver.
  Deuteronomy 7:25–7:26 — thematic costly-religious-object-renunciation comparison: The former magic practitioners publicly burn their own books despite their calculated silver value. Deuteronomy directs Israel to burn carved gods and not covet or take their silver and gold. The rejection despite value connects, while books versus images, voluntary narrative versus covenant instruction, and monetary value versus actual metal remain distinct; no general command to destroy books is inferred.
    Deuteronomy 7:25 You shall burn the graven images of their gods with fire. You shall not covet the silver or gold upon them, nor take it unto yourself, lest you stumble thereby; for it is an abomination unto the Lord your God.
    Deuteronomy 7:26 And you shall not bring an abomination into your house, lest you become a thing under the ban, like unto it. You shall utterly reject it, and utterly abhor it, for it is a thing under the ban.

Acts 19:20
In this way the word of the Lord grew mightily and prevailed.
  Acts 12:24 — internal word-growth-summary parallel: Both section summaries describe God’s or the Lord’s word growing. Acts12 adds multiplication after the Herod narrative; Acts19 adds mighty growth and prevailing after the Ephesian events. The repeated narrative pattern does not make the settings, Lord/God wording or particular outcomes identical.
    Acts 12:24 But the word of God grew and multiplied.

Acts 19:21
After these things were completed, Paul resolved in spirit to pass through Macedonia and Achaia and go to Jerusalem, saying, “After I have been there, I must also see Rome.”
