  Acts 23:11 — internal Jerusalem-to-Rome-purpose connection: Paul resolves to travel through Macedonia and Achaia to Jerusalem and then see Rome. Later the Lord explicitly tells him that after Jerusalem witness he must testify in Rome. The destinations connect, while Paul’s resolved-in-spirit wording is not silently converted into the later direct speech or a fully specified divine travel timetable.
    Acts 23:11 The following night the Lord stood beside him and said, “Take courage, for as you have borne solemn witness about Me in Jerusalem, so you must also testify in Rome.”

Acts 19:22
He sent two of those who assisted him, Timothy and Erastus, to Macedonia, while he himself remained in Asia for a time.
  1 Corinthians 16:8–16:11 — narrative-and-Ephesus-Timothy-plans comparison: Acts sends Timothy and Erastus toward Macedonia while Paul remains in Asia. The Corinthian letter has Paul remaining in Ephesus and gives instructions if Timothy comes, anticipating his return. The stay and Timothy mission connect without identifying every journey stage, making Erastus a named participant in the letter or importing an office from another namesake.
    1 Corinthians 16:8 But I will remain in Ephesus until Pentecost,
    1 Corinthians 16:9 for a great and effective door has opened to me, and there are many opponents.
    1 Corinthians 16:10 If Timothy comes, see that he may be with you without fear, for he works the Lord’s work just as I do.
    1 Corinthians 16:11 Therefore let no one despise him. Send him onward in peace, so that he may come to me, for I await him with the brothers.

Acts 19:23
About that time a serious disturbance arose concerning the Way.
  Acts 24:14 — internal Way-terminology connection: The narrator calls the Ephesian disturbance one concerning the Way. Paul later uses the same designation for his worship of the ancestral God and belief in law and prophets, while noting others call it a sect. The target clarifies his self-description without making the religious designation itself a narrator’s criminal charge.
    Acts 24:14 But I confess this to you: according to the Way, which they call a sect, I worship the God of our fathers, believing everything in accordance with the law and everything written in the prophets,

Acts 19:24
A silversmith named Demetrius, who made silver shrines of Artemis, provided considerable business for the craftsmen.
  Acts 16:16 — internal religious-commerce comparison: Demetrius’s Artemis shrines produce business for craftsmen; the Philippian slave girl’s divination produces profit for her masters. These are different religious practices and economic arrangements affected by Paul’s mission. The comparison neither identifies the trades nor endorses the shrine makers’ divine claims.
    Acts 16:16 As we were going to the place of prayer, a slave girl with a spirit of divination met us. She brought her masters much profit by fortune-telling.

Acts 19:25
He gathered them and the workers in related trades and said, “Men, you know that our prosperity comes from this business.
  Acts 16:19 — internal commercial-motive comparison: Demetrius appeals to his fellow workers’ prosperity when gathering them. The Philippian owners seize Paul and Silas after seeing their profit hope disappear. Both episodes expressly bring commercial interest into opposition, but an appeal about threatened trade is not rewritten as the already realized loss in Philippi.
    Acts 16:19 When her masters saw that their hope of profit had gone out, they seized Paul and Silas and dragged them into the marketplace before the rulers.

Acts 19:26
You see and hear that not only in Ephesus but in almost all Asia this Paul has persuaded and turned away a considerable crowd, saying that things made by hands are not gods.
  Acts 17:29 — internal reported-and-direct-image-critique comparison: Demetrius portrays Paul as saying handmade things are not gods. Paul’s actual Athens speech rejects thinking the divine nature is a humanly fashioned gold, silver or stone image. The related critique is in different voices and settings; Demetrius’s claimed reach of persuasion is not independently verified by the Athens statement.
    Acts 17:29 Since we are God’s offspring, we ought not to think that the divine nature is like gold or silver or stone, an image formed by human art and thought.

Acts 19:27
There is danger not only that this trade of ours will fall into disrepute, but also that the temple of the great goddess Artemis will be reckoned as nothing, and that she whom all Asia and the inhabited world worship will be stripped of her magnificence.”
  Acts 16:19 — internal trade-danger-and-loss comparison: Demetrius warns that the trade may lose standing along with Artemis’s temple and magnificence. In Philippi the owners’ actual loss of profit precipitates seizing the missionaries. The economic concerns connect; only this reported Ephesian speech makes the broad claims about Artemis’s worldwide worship and endangered glory.
    Acts 16:19 When her masters saw that their hope of profit had gone out, they seized Paul and Silas and dragged them into the marketplace before the rulers.

Acts 19:28
When they heard this, they became full of rage and cried out, “Great is Artemis of the Ephesians!”
  Acts 19:34 — internal repeated-cult-cry connection: The workers first respond in rage with the Great-is-Artemis cry. The later theater crowd repeats that same cry for about two hours after recognizing Alexander as Jewish. The repeated words connect stages of this disturbance without equating the initial group with every later participant or transferring the two-hour duration to the first cry.
    Acts 19:34 When they recognized that he was a Jew, one cry arose from them all for about two hours: “Great is Artemis of the Ephesians!”

Acts 19:29
The city was filled with confusion. They rushed together into the theater, seizing Gaius and Aristarchus, Macedonians who traveled with Paul.
  Acts 27:2 — internal named-companion connection: Aristarchus is among Paul’s Macedonian traveling companions seized here. The later voyage explicitly has Aristarchus, a Macedonian from Thessalonica, accompanying the narrating party with Paul. The name, origin and travel association provide the connection; this does not identify Gaius with other people of that name or make Aristarchus a prisoner merely from his presence.
    Acts 27:2 We boarded a ship from Adramyttium that was about to sail to the ports along Asia, and set sail. Aristarchus, a Macedonian from Thessalonica, was with us.

Acts 19:30
Paul wanted to enter among the populace, but the disciples would not let him.
  Acts 21:12–21:14 — internal protective-companions contrast: The disciples prevent Paul entering the dangerous theater gathering. Later Paul’s companions and local people urge him not to go to Jerusalem, but he remains determined and they cease trying to persuade him. The protective concern connects while the outcomes differ; neither decision is assigned an unreported motive or declared disobedience to God.
    Acts 21:12 When we heard these things, both we and the local people urged him not to go up to Jerusalem.
    Acts 21:13 Then Paul answered, “What are you doing, weeping and breaking my heart? For I am ready not only to be bound, but also to die in Jerusalem for the name of the Lord Jesus.”
    Acts 21:14 Since he would not be persuaded, we became silent and said, “Let the Lord’s will be done.”

Acts 19:32
Some shouted one thing and some another, for the assembly was confused, and most did not know why they had come together.
  Acts 21:34 — internal confused-crowd-report parallel: Both scenes say some in a crowd shout one thing and others another. At Ephesus most do not know why they assembled; in Jerusalem the tribune cannot learn anything certain because of the uproar. The shared confusion has different observers and consequences, not a single crowd or an assumption that every person lacks the same knowledge.
    Acts 21:34 Some in the crowd shouted one thing and some another. Since he could learn nothing certain because of the uproar, he ordered Paul to be brought into the barracks.

Acts 19:33
They prompted Alexander from the crowd, the Jews having put him forward. Alexander motioned with his hand and wished to make a defense before the populace.
  Acts 21:40 — internal hand-gesture-before-defense comparison: Alexander motions with his hand wishing to make a defense before the populace. Paul later motions with his hand before addressing the Jerusalem crowd, whose defense follows in the next unit. The gesture and speech setting connect, while Alexander’s intended defense is not supplied, he is not made Paul’s advocate, and his identity is not merged with another Alexander.
    Acts 21:40 When he gave permission, Paul stood on the steps and motioned with his hand to the people. When a great silence fell, he addressed them in the Hebrew language, saying:

Acts 19:34
When they recognized that he was a Jew, one cry arose from them all for about two hours: “Great is Artemis of the Ephesians!”
  Acts 19:28 — internal repeated-cult-cry connection: The theater crowd repeats the earlier Great-is-Artemis cry after recognizing Alexander as Jewish, now with an approximately two-hour duration. The previous cry follows the workers’ anger at Demetrius’s speech. The common slogan connects two moments without transferring this trigger or duration to the earlier one.
    Acts 19:28 When they heard this, they became full of rage and cried out, “Great is Artemis of the Ephesians!”

Acts 19:37
For you have brought these men here, though they are neither temple-robbers nor blasphemers of our goddess.
  Romans 2:22 — distinctive temple-robbing-language comparison: The secretary denies that the men brought before the assembly are temple-robbers or blasphemers of his goddess. Romans rhetorically asks an idol-abhorer whether he robs temples. The distinctive offense language connects a denial with a rhetorical question; Paul is not placed in the theater, nor are these men accused by the letter or its addressee identified with the Ephesian group.
    Romans 2:22 You who say that one must not commit adultery, do you commit adultery? You who abhor idols, do you rob temples?

Acts 19:38
If Demetrius and the craftsmen with him have a charge against anyone, court sessions are held and proconsuls are available; let them bring charges against one another.
  Acts 18:12–18:15 — internal court-channel comparison: The secretary directs Demetrius and the craftsmen to court sessions and proconsuls for charges. The earlier Achaian episode actually brings Paul before Gallio’s tribunal, where the proconsul declines the presented religious questions. Different provinces and outcomes remain distinct; the passage is not expanded into a universal statement of Roman law.
    Acts 18:12 While Gallio was proconsul of Achaia, the Jews rose together against Paul and brought him before the tribunal,
    Acts 18:13 saying, “This man persuades people to worship God contrary to the law.”
    Acts 18:14 As Paul was about to open his mouth, Gallio said to the Jews, “If it were some wrongdoing or wicked crime, O Jews, I would have reason to hear you out.
    Acts 18:15 But if these are questions about a word, names, and your own law, see to it yourselves. I do not wish to be a judge of these things.”

Acts 19:39
But if you seek anything further, it will be settled in the lawful assembly.
  Acts 19:32 — internal lawful-versus-confused-assembly contrast: The secretary directs further business to a lawful assembly, contrasting the present gathering described as confused, with most unaware why they came. The same civic episode supplies the contrast; assembly here is not silently reclassified as a Christian Church or given an unreported formal adjudication.
    Acts 19:32 Some shouted one thing and some another, for the assembly was confused, and most did not know why they had come together.

Acts 19:40a
For we are in danger of being charged with rioting over today’s events, since there is no cause for it; we will not be able to give an account of this gathering.”
  Acts 19:32 — internal gathering-cause-and-accountability connection: The secretary says there is no cause by which they can account for the gathering and warns of a rioting charge. The narrator earlier describes a confused assembly whose majority do not know why they came. The related lack-of-account connects distinct narrative and official statements, without asserting that every participant was actually prosecuted.
    Acts 19:32 Some shouted one thing and some another, for the assembly was confused, and most did not know why they had come together.

Acts 19:40b
After saying these things, he dismissed the assembly.
  Acts 20:1 — internal dismissal-and-aftermath connection: The secretary dismisses the assembly in this original split unit. Acts20 opens after the uproar ceases, when Paul encourages the disciples and departs for Macedonia. The narrative transition connects without renumbering this unit as19:41, joining it to40a, or making the secretary perform Paul’s departure.
    Acts 20:1 After the uproar ceased, Paul summoned the disciples and encouraged them. Taking leave of them, he departed for Macedonia.

Acts 20:1
After the uproar ceased, Paul summoned the disciples and encouraged them. Taking leave of them, he departed for Macedonia.
  Acts 19:40a–19:40b — narrative parallel: The Ephesian city secretary dismisses the assembly after warning about the day's uproar; the next stage begins after that uproar ceases and Paul leaves for Macedonia. This is the narrative transition from the same disturbance, not an independent report of its conclusion.
    Acts 19:40a For we are in danger of being charged with rioting over today’s events, since there is no cause for it; we will not be able to give an account of this gathering.”
    Acts 19:40b After saying these things, he dismissed the assembly.

Acts 20:2
He passed through those regions and encouraged them with many words, then came to Greece
  Acts 19:21 — narrative parallel: Paul's earlier plan names travel through Macedonia and Achaia before Jerusalem; the subsequent narrative reports encouragement through Macedonia and arrival in Greece. This connects the plan with its travel stages without making the place lists identical or supplying unrecorded dates.
    Acts 19:21 After these things were completed, Paul resolved in spirit to pass through Macedonia and Achaia and go to Jerusalem, saying, “After I have been there, I must also see Rome.”

Acts 20:3
and spent three months there. When a plot was made against him by the Jews as he was about to sail for Syria, he decided to return through Macedonia.
  Acts 9:23–9:25 — narrative parallel: In Damascus a plot to kill Saul leads to escape by the wall; here a plot as Paul prepares to sail changes his route to Macedonia. Both describe opposition altering his movements, but the groups, locations and escape methods differ; this verse does not itself specify every plot detail.
    Acts 9:23 After many days had passed, the Jews conspired to kill him,
    Acts 9:24 but their plot became known to Saul. They were also watching the gates day and night to kill him.
    Acts 9:25 His disciples took him by night and let him down through the wall, lowering him in a hamper.

Acts 20:4
He was accompanied by Sopater son of Pyrrhus from Berea; Aristarchus and Secundus from Thessalonica; Gaius from Derbe, and Timothy; and Tychicus and Trophimus from Asia.
  Acts 27:2 — narrative parallel: Aristarchus is named as a Thessalonian companion here and later as a Macedonian from Thessalonica accompanying the voyage. The identification concerns this named companion, not independent confirmation of the entire list or identification of Gaius of Derbe with another Gaius.
    Acts 27:2 We boarded a ship from Adramyttium that was about to sail to the ports along Asia, and set sail. Aristarchus, a Macedonian from Thessalonica, was with us.

Acts 20:5
These men went ahead and waited for us in Troas.
  Acts 20:6 — narrative parallel: The companions go ahead and wait in Troas; the following travel report says that we came to them there. This is an explicit waiting-and-rejoining sequence, without naming the first-person narrator or independently confirming every companion's movements.
    Acts 20:6 We sailed from Philippi after the days of Unleavened Bread and came to them at Troas in five days, where we stayed seven days.

Acts 20:6
We sailed from Philippi after the days of Unleavened Bread and came to them at Troas in five days, where we stayed seven days.
  Acts 20:5 — narrative parallel: The arrival at Troas rejoins the men previously said to be waiting there. This internal callback explains to them; the earlier notice does not independently verify the festival date, five-day crossing or seven-day stay.
    Acts 20:5 These men went ahead and waited for us in Troas.

Acts 20:7
On the first day of the week, when we had gathered to break bread, Paul spoke with them. He intended to leave the next day and prolonged his message until midnight.
  Acts 20:11 — narrative parallel: The gathering's intended bread breaking and prolonged speaking are resumed after the interruption, when Paul breaks bread, eats and talks until dawn. These are stages of the same gathering, not a second independent testimony or a rule inferred for every Church meeting.
    Acts 20:11 He went back up, broke the bread and ate, and talked for a long time until dawn. Then he departed.

Acts 20:9
A young man named Eutychus sat in the window and was sinking into a deep sleep as Paul continued speaking at length. Overcome by sleep, he fell down from the third story and was picked up dead.
  Acts 20:12 — narrative parallel: Eutychus is picked up dead after the fall, whereas the episode ends with the boy brought away alive. This explicit death/life contrast belongs to one narrative; it does not independently verify the height or replace dead with an unreported diagnosis.
    Acts 20:12 They brought the boy away alive and were greatly comforted.

Acts 20:10
Paul went down, fell upon him, embraced him, and said, “Do not be alarmed, for his life is in him.”
  2 Kings 4:32–4:35 — narrative parallel: Paul falls upon and embraces Eutychus before announcing life in him; Elisha prays and makes bodily contact with the dead child before the child's restoration. The bodily-contact restoration motif is comparable, while the actions, words and individuals differ; Elisha's prayer and particular movements are not inserted into Paul's scene.
    2 Kings 4:32 Elisha entered the house, and there was the child, dead, lying on his bed.
    2 Kings 4:33 Elisha entered the house, shut the door behind the two of them, and prayed to the Lord.
    2 Kings 4:34 He went up and lay upon the child, putting his mouth on his mouth, his eyes on his eyes, and his hands on his hands. He bent over him, and the child’s flesh was warmed.
    2 Kings 4:35 He returned and walked in the house this way and that. He went up and bent over the child as many as seven times, and the child opened his eyes.

Acts 20:11
He went back up, broke the bread and ate, and talked for a long time until dawn. Then he departed.
  Acts 20:7 — narrative parallel: Paul's bread breaking and continued conversation fulfil the gathering's stated purpose and extend the address beyond its earlier midnight point to dawn. This recalls the same meeting, not another first-day meal or an independent account of every action.
    Acts 20:7 On the first day of the week, when we had gathered to break bread, Paul spoke with them. He intended to leave the next day and prolonged his message until midnight.

Acts 20:12
They brought the boy away alive and were greatly comforted.
  Acts 20:9 — narrative parallel: The conclusion that the boy is brought away alive answers the earlier report that Eutychus was picked up dead. The link preserves the narrator's two descriptions and the resulting comfort, without explaining away the earlier dead wording.
    Acts 20:9 A young man named Eutychus sat in the window and was sinking into a deep sleep as Paul continued speaking at length. Overcome by sleep, he fell down from the third story and was picked up dead.

Acts 20:13
We went ahead to the ship and sailed for Assos, intending to take Paul aboard there, for he had arranged it this way, intending himself to travel on foot.
  Acts 20:14 — narrative parallel: The party sails ahead intending to collect Paul at Assos while he intends to travel on foot; the next notice records meeting him at Assos and taking him aboard. This is a specific arrangement followed by its narrated execution.
    Acts 20:14 When he met us at Assos, we took him aboard and came to Mitylene.

Acts 20:14
When he met us at Assos, we took him aboard and came to Mitylene.
  Acts 20:13 — narrative parallel: The Assos meeting and boarding follow the previously stated plan that the others would sail ahead and Paul would go on foot. Mitylene is the next reported destination, not a place named in the earlier arrangement.
    Acts 20:13 We went ahead to the ship and sailed for Assos, intending to take Paul aboard there, for he had arranged it this way, intending himself to travel on foot.

Acts 20:15
Sailing from there, the next day we arrived opposite Chios; on the following day we put in at Samos, and on the next we came to Miletus.
  Acts 20:17 — narrative parallel: The sailing sequence ends at Miletus, and the following action begins from Miletus when Paul summons the Ephesian elders. This identifies the speech's narrative staging point; it does not independently verify Chios, Samos or the individual sailing days.
    Acts 20:17 From Miletus he sent to Ephesus and summoned the elders of the Church.

Acts 20:16
For Paul had decided to sail past Ephesus so that he would not spend time in Asia. He was hurrying, if possible, to be in Jerusalem on the day of Pentecost.
  Acts 21:17 — narrative parallel: Paul's stated aim is Jerusalem, if possible by Pentecost; the later narrative records arrival in Jerusalem. The arrival connects with the destination, but it does not itself date arrival to Pentecost or prove that the intended deadline was met.
    Acts 21:17 When we arrived in Jerusalem, the brothers welcomed us gladly.

Acts 20:17
From Miletus he sent to Ephesus and summoned the elders of the Church.
  Acts 20:28 — narrative parallel: The people summoned from Ephesus are the Church's elders; the charge to this audience describes their oversight and shepherding responsibility. This identifies the speech's audience and role language, without supplying a later system of offices or another appointment ceremony.
    Acts 20:28 Pay attention to yourselves and to all the flock, among whom the Holy Spirit has placed you as overseers, to shepherd the Church of God, which He acquired through His own blood.

Acts 20:18
When they came to him, he said, “You know how I was with you the whole time, from the first day I set foot in Asia,
  1 Thessalonians 2:9–2:10 — thematic parallel: Paul appeals to the Ephesian hearers' knowledge of his conduct among them; the letter appeals to its hearers as witnesses of the missionaries' labor and conduct. The public appeal to remembered ministry is comparable, while the communities and the letter's plural speakers differ.
    1 Thessalonians 2:9 For you remember, brothers, our labor and toil: working night and day so as not to burden any of you, we proclaimed to you the gospel of God.
    1 Thessalonians 2:10 You are witnesses, and God also, how devoutly and justly and blamelessly we conducted ourselves toward you who believe,

Acts 20:19
serving the Lord with all humility, tears, and trials that came upon me through the plots of the Jews.
  2 Corinthians 2:4 — thematic parallel: Paul describes service marked by tears and trials; the letter describes writing out of much affliction and anguish with many tears. Both portray tearful apostolic ministry, but the letter's writing occasion does not independently verify the plots or humility described in the Asian ministry.
    2 Corinthians 2:4 For out of much affliction and anguish of heart I wrote to you through many tears, not that you might be made sorrowful, but that you might know the abundant love I have for you.

Acts 20:20
I did not hold back from declaring anything beneficial to you and teaching you publicly and from house to house,
  Acts 20:27 — narrative parallel: The assertion that Paul did not hold back beneficial teaching is repeated as not holding back the whole purpose of God. These are complementary claims within the same farewell speech, not independent proof of every public or household lesson.
    Acts 20:27 for I did not hold back from declaring to you the whole purpose of God.

Acts 20:21
bearing solemn witness to both Jews and Greeks of repentance toward God and faith in our Lord Jesus.
  Acts 26:20 — narrative parallel: The farewell names repentance toward God and faith in Jesus for Jews and Greeks; Paul's later defense describes announcing repentance and turning to God across Jewish regions and among Gentiles. The repentance and broad mission scope correspond, while the geographical list, deeds clause and explicit faith wording differ.
    Acts 26:20 but proclaimed first to those in Damascus and Jerusalem, throughout all the country of Judea, and to the Gentiles that they should repent and turn to God, doing works worthy of repentance.

Acts 20:22
And now, look, bound in spirit, I am going to Jerusalem, not knowing what will meet me there,
  Acts 21:4 — narrative parallel: Paul says he is bound in spirit and going to Jerusalem; at Tyre the disciples tell him through the Spirit not to set foot there. The comparison preserves these different formulations and the apparent tension, rather than changing the prohibition into merely a warning or deciding the lowercase spirit expression beyond the supplied wording.
    Acts 21:4 We sought out the disciples and stayed there seven days. Through the Spirit they told Paul not to set foot in Jerusalem.

Acts 20:23
except that in every city the Holy Spirit bears witness to me, saying that bonds and afflictions await me.
  Acts 21:10–21:11 — narrative parallel: The repeated Holy Spirit testimony about coming bonds and afflictions has a specific later counterpart in Agabus's belt sign and forecast of binding and Gentile custody. The general summary and the named prophecy have different scope; their wording is not made identical to every subsequent arrest action.
    Acts 21:10 While we stayed for several days, a prophet named Agabus came down from Judea.
    Acts 21:11 He came to us, took Paul’s belt, bound his own feet and hands, and said, “Thus says the Holy Spirit: ‘The Jews in Jerusalem will bind the man whose belt this is in this way and hand him over into Gentile hands.’”

Acts 20:24
But I consider my life of no value to myself, so that I may complete my course and the ministry I received from the Lord Jesus: to bear solemn witness to the good news of God’s grace.
  2 Timothy 4:7 — thematic parallel: Paul resolves to complete his course and ministry; the letter uses the retrospective image of having finished the course and kept the faith. The shared course metaphor connects resolve and claimed completion without fixing the two passages to one occasion or equating every metaphorical detail.
    2 Timothy 4:7 I have fought the good fight; I have finished the course; I have kept the faith.

Acts 20:25
And now, look, I know that none of you among whom I went about proclaiming the kingdom will see my face again.
  Acts 20:38 — narrative parallel: The farewell assertion that the hearers will not see Paul's face again is explicitly recalled as the chief cause of their grief. The later verse records their response to his words, not independent verification of the whole future itinerary.
    Acts 20:38 grieving most of all over the word he had spoken, that they would no longer see his face. They accompanied him to the ship.

Acts 20:26
Therefore I testify to you this very day that I am clean from the blood of all,
  Ezekiel 33:6–33:9 — allusion: Paul grounds his claim to be clean from everyone's blood in having declared God's whole purpose in the following verse. Ezekiel's watchman is accountable for blood when he fails to warn, while warning rescues his own life. This is possible watchman background for Paul's responsibility language, not a verbatim quotation or an assertion that every commission detail is identical.
    Ezekiel 33:6 If the watchman sees the sword coming but does not signal with the trumpet, and the people do not take care, and the sword comes and takes a life from them, that life was taken because of its own lawlessness, but I will require the blood from the watchman’s hand.’
    Ezekiel 33:7 And you, son of man, I have appointed you a watchman for Israel’s house, and you will hear a word from My mouth.
    Ezekiel 33:8 When I say to the sinner, ‘You will surely be put to death,’ and you do not speak so that the impious man may guard himself from his way, that lawless man will die in his lawlessness, but I will require his blood from your hand.
    Ezekiel 33:9 But if you forewarn the impious man of his way so that he may turn from it and he does not turn from his way, this man will die in his impiety, and you have rescued your own life.

Acts 20:27
for I did not hold back from declaring to you the whole purpose of God.
  Acts 20:20 — narrative parallel: The statement that Paul did not hold back the whole purpose of God resumes his earlier claim not to withhold beneficial instruction. The repeated wording supports the speech's claim of completeness, while it remains one speaker's account rather than an independent audit of his teaching.
    Acts 20:20 I did not hold back from declaring anything beneficial to you and teaching you publicly and from house to house,

Acts 20:28
Pay attention to yourselves and to all the flock, among whom the Holy Spirit has placed you as overseers, to shepherd the Church of God, which He acquired through His own blood.
  1 Peter 5:1–5:3 — thematic parallel: The elders are charged with attending to the flock and shepherding the Church; Peter's exhortation likewise addresses elders and the shepherding of God's flock. The leadership-and-flock responsibility is shared, while the commands and grounds differ; the letter does not repeat the acquired-through-blood clause or independently attest these overseers' placement.
    1 Peter 5:1 I exhort the elders among you, as a fellow elder and a witness of the sufferings of Christ, and also a participant in the glory about to be revealed:
    1 Peter 5:2 Shepherd the flock of God among you, exercising oversight, not under compulsion but willingly, according to God; not for shameful gain but eagerly;
    1 Peter 5:3 not as lording it over those allotted to you, but becoming examples to the flock.

Acts 20:29
I know that after my departure fierce wolves will enter among you, not sparing the flock.
  Matthew 7:15 — thematic parallel: Paul warns of fierce wolves entering and harming the flock; Jesus warns of false prophets in sheep's clothing who inwardly are ravenous wolves. Both use predatory wolf imagery for danger to the faithful, but the farewell's after-my-departure timing and Matthew's disguise are not transferred between passages.
    Matthew 7:15 Beware of false prophets, who come to you in sheep’s clothing but inwardly are ravenous wolves.

Acts 20:30
Even from among you yourselves men will arise, speaking distorted things to draw the disciples away after themselves.
  2 Peter 2:1–2:3 — thematic parallel: Paul warns that men from within the hearers will speak distorted things and draw disciples after themselves; the letter warns of false teachers among its audience, fabricated words and many following their debaucheries. Both describe dangerous influence from within, while the followed objects and additional charges differ; the groups are not identified as one.
    2 Peter 2:1 But false prophets also arose among the people, just as there will also be false teachers among you. They will secretly bring in destructive factions, even denying the Master who bought them, bringing swift destruction upon themselves.
    2 Peter 2:2 And many will follow their debaucheries, and because of them the way of truth will be slandered.
    2 Peter 2:3 And in greed they will exploit you with fabricated words. Their judgment has not been idle since long ago, and their destruction does not sleep.

Acts 20:31
Therefore stay awake, remembering that for three years, night and day, I never ceased admonishing each one with tears.
  1 Thessalonians 2:11–2:12 — thematic parallel: Paul recalls admonishing each person persistently; the letter recalls treating each hearer as a father does children, encouraging, comforting and solemnly urging them to walk worthily of God. The individual pastoral attention corresponds; the letter does not corroborate the farewell's three years, tears or every night and day.
    1 Thessalonians 2:11 just as you know how we dealt with each one of you, as a father with his own children,
    1 Thessalonians 2:12 encouraging you and comforting you and solemnly urging you to walk worthily of God, who calls you into His own kingdom and glory.

Acts 20:32
And now I entrust you to God and to the word of His grace, which is able to build you up and give you the inheritance among all who have been sanctified.
  Acts 26:18 — narrative parallel: Paul entrusts the elders to God and the word of His grace, able to build up and give an inheritance among the sanctified. His reported commission likewise offers an allotted share among those sanctified by faith in Jesus. Both connect a share with the sanctified community, while the speakers and wording differ.
    Acts 26:18 to open their eyes, so that they may turn from darkness to light and from Satan’s authority to God, so that they may receive forgiveness of sins and an allotted share among those sanctified by faith in Me.’

Acts 20:33
I coveted no one’s silver or gold or clothing.
  1 Thessalonians 2:5 — thematic parallel: Paul denies coveting anyone's valuables; the letter denies a pretext for greed and invokes God as witness. Both reject financial exploitation in ministry, but the letter's formulation does not independently verify the particular silver, gold or clothing list.
    1 Thessalonians 2:5 For we never came with a word of flattery, as you know, nor with a pretext for greed—God is witness—

Acts 20:34
You yourselves know that these hands served my needs and those of the people with me.
  1 Corinthians 4:12 — thematic parallel: Paul says these hands served his own and his companions' needs; the letter says we toil, working with our own hands. The explicit manual-labor motif is shared, while the plural letter does not independently identify all the Ephesian beneficiaries or a particular trade.
    1 Corinthians 4:12 and we toil, working with our own hands. When insulted, we bless; when persecuted, we endure;

Acts 20:35
In everything I showed you that by working in this way we must support the weak and remember the words of the Lord Jesus, that He Himself said, ‘It is more blessed to give than to receive.’”
  Ephesians 4:28 — thematic parallel: Paul's example joins labor to supporting the weak; the letter tells the one who steals to stop, labor with his hands and have something to share with the needy. Both connect work with supplying others, without calling Paul a thief or presenting the letter as another verbatim record of Jesus' quoted giving saying.
    Ephesians 4:28 Let the one who steals steal no longer, but rather let him labor, doing what is good with his own hands, so that he may have something to share with the one in need.

Acts 20:36
After saying these things, he knelt down with them all and prayed.
  Acts 21:5 — narrative parallel: Paul and the elders kneel and pray at their departure; the later Tyre farewell also includes kneeling prayer with the believers on the beach. The posture and farewell setting correspond, but the locations and participating groups differ.
    Acts 21:5 When our days there were completed, we left and went on our way. They all accompanied us, with wives and children, as far as outside the city. We knelt on the beach and prayed,

Acts 20:37
They all wept greatly, threw their arms around Paul’s neck, and kissed him repeatedly,
  Genesis 45:14–45:15 — thematic parallel: The elders weep, embrace Paul's neck and kiss him; Joseph's reunion includes neck embraces, weeping and kisses among his brothers. The affectionate gestures form a bounded narrative comparison, with different actors and a farewell rather than a reunion or the same reconciliation history.
    Genesis 45:14 And he fell upon the neck of Benjamin his brother, and wept over him; and Benjamin wept upon his neck.
    Genesis 45:15 And he kissed all his brothers, and wept over them; and afterward his brothers spoke with him.

Acts 20:38
grieving most of all over the word he had spoken, that they would no longer see his face. They accompanied him to the ship.
  Acts 20:25 — narrative parallel: Their grief explicitly concerns the earlier statement that they would not see Paul's face again. This internal recollection identifies the words causing sorrow; accompanying him to the ship is the present farewell action, not proof about all subsequent meetings.
    Acts 20:25 And now, look, I know that none of you among whom I went about proclaiming the kingdom will see my face again.

Acts 21:1
When we had torn ourselves away from them and set sail, we made a straight course to Cos, the next day to Rhodes, and from there to Patara.
  Acts 20:37–20:38 — narrative parallel: The departure after tearing themselves away follows the elders' weeping embrace and escort to the ship. This identifies the people and farewell behind the transition, without independently verifying the newly listed Cos, Rhodes and Patara stops.
    Acts 20:37 They all wept greatly, threw their arms around Paul’s neck, and kissed him repeatedly,
    Acts 20:38 grieving most of all over the word he had spoken, that they would no longer see his face. They accompanied him to the ship.

Acts 21:2
We found a ship crossing to Phoenicia, went aboard, and set sail.
  Acts 21:3 — narrative parallel: The party boards a ship crossing to Phoenicia; the following voyage notice records reaching Tyre, where that ship was to unload cargo. This is a planned crossing followed by its narrated landing, not a separate confirmation of every maritime detail.
    Acts 21:3 We sighted Cyprus and passed it on our left, sailed toward Syria, and landed at Tyre, for the ship was to unload its cargo there.

Acts 21:3
We sighted Cyprus and passed it on our left, sailed toward Syria, and landed at Tyre, for the ship was to unload its cargo there.
  Acts 21:2 — narrative parallel: The arrival at Tyre continues the crossing on the ship just found for Phoenicia. The internal connection identifies the vessel and voyage stage; the earlier boarding notice does not corroborate the side on which Cyprus was passed or the cargo unloading.
    Acts 21:2 We found a ship crossing to Phoenicia, went aboard, and set sail.

Acts 21:4
We sought out the disciples and stayed there seven days. Through the Spirit they told Paul not to set foot in Jerusalem.
  Acts 20:22 — narrative parallel: The Tyrian disciples tell Paul through the Spirit not to enter Jerusalem, whereas Paul had said he was bound in spirit and going there. These different statements remain intact; the link does not silently turn the disciples' prohibition into only a prediction or force one interpretation of spirit in Paul's earlier words.
    Acts 20:22 And now, look, bound in spirit, I am going to Jerusalem, not knowing what will meet me there,

Acts 21:5
When our days there were completed, we left and went on our way. They all accompanied us, with wives and children, as far as outside the city. We knelt on the beach and prayed,
  Acts 20:36 — narrative parallel: The Tyrian farewell names wives and children among the escort and then reports kneeling prayer on the beach; the Miletus farewell has Paul kneel with the elders and pray. The shared departure prayer connects, without merging the groups, assigning the earlier prayer to a beach or resolving the scope of the later we beyond the narration.
    Acts 20:36 After saying these things, he knelt down with them all and prayed.

Acts 21:6
then said farewell to one another. We boarded the ship, and they returned to their homes.
  Acts 20:38 — narrative parallel: The Tyrian believers part from the voyagers at their boarding, comparable to the Ephesian elders accompanying Paul to the ship in the earlier farewell. The later report alone specifies the local people's return home; the two groups and ports remain distinct.
    Acts 20:38 grieving most of all over the word he had spoken, that they would no longer see his face. They accompanied him to the ship.

Acts 21:8
The next day we departed and came to Caesarea. We entered the house of Philip the evangelist, who was one of the seven, and stayed with him.
  Acts 6:5–6:6 — narrative parallel: Philip is identified here as one of the seven; the earlier selection names Philip among the seven presented to the apostles. This supplies the explicit antecedent for his designation, not an identification with every other Philip or a claim that all seven share the later evangelist title.
    Acts 6:5 The proposal pleased the whole multitude. They chose Stephen, a man full of faith and the Holy Spirit, Philip, Prochorus, Nicanor, Timon, Parmenas, and Nicolaus, a convert from Antioch.
    Acts 6:6 They presented them before the apostles, who prayed and laid their hands upon them.

Acts 21:9
He had four daughters who were virgins and prophesied.
  Acts 2:17–2:18 — thematic parallel: Philip's daughters prophesy; the Pentecost citation explicitly includes daughters and female slaves among those who will prophesy. This is a concrete female-prophecy correspondence, without independently confirming four daughters, their virginity, or claiming this family exhausts the cited promise.
    Acts 2:17 ‘In the last days, God says, I will pour out of My Spirit upon all flesh. Your sons and your daughters will prophesy; your young men will see visions, and your elders will dream dreams.
    Acts 2:18 Even upon My male slaves and My female slaves in those days I will pour out of My Spirit, and they will prophesy.

Acts 21:10
While we stayed for several days, a prophet named Agabus came down from Judea.
  Acts 11:27–11:28 — narrative parallel: Agabus appears earlier among prophets arriving from Jerusalem and foretells famine through the Spirit; here the named prophet comes from Judea and gives another prediction. The recurring named prophet is connected, while the journeys, audiences and predictions remain different.
    Acts 11:27 In those days prophets came down from Jerusalem to Antioch.
    Acts 11:28 One of them, named Agabus, stood up and indicated through the Spirit that a great famine would come over the whole inhabited world. This happened in the time of Claudius.

Acts 21:11
He came to us, took Paul’s belt, bound his own feet and hands, and said, “Thus says the Holy Spirit: ‘The Jews in Jerusalem will bind the man whose belt this is in this way and hand him over into Gentile hands.’”
  Acts 21:30–21:33 — narrative parallel: Agabus forecasts binding by the Jews and handing into Gentile hands; the subsequent narration has the crowd seize and drag Paul and the Roman tribune order him bound with two chains. Bonds and Gentile custody are connected, but the stated agents remain distinct: the narrative does not say the Jewish crowd tied the chains or voluntarily delivered him to the rescuing troops.
    Acts 21:30 The whole city was stirred, and the people ran together. They seized Paul and dragged him outside the temple, and immediately the doors were shut.
    Acts 21:31 While they were seeking to kill him, a report reached the tribune of the cohort that all Jerusalem was in confusion.
    Acts 21:32 He immediately took soldiers and centurions and ran down to them. When they saw the tribune and the soldiers, they stopped beating Paul.
    Acts 21:33 Then the tribune approached, seized him, and ordered him bound with two chains. He asked who he was and what he had done.

Acts 21:12
When we heard these things, both we and the local people urged him not to go up to Jerusalem.
  Acts 21:4 — narrative parallel: After Agabus's warning, the party and local people urge Paul not to go to Jerusalem; the Tyrian disciples had also told him not to enter. These are distinct groups and occasions; the earlier through-the-Spirit wording is preserved rather than attributed automatically to every later plea.
    Acts 21:4 We sought out the disciples and stayed there seven days. Through the Spirit they told Paul not to set foot in Jerusalem.

Acts 21:13
Then Paul answered, “What are you doing, weeping and breaking my heart? For I am ready not only to be bound, but also to die in Jerusalem for the name of the Lord Jesus.”
  Acts 20:24 — narrative parallel: Paul declares readiness for bonds and death for Jesus' name, matching the earlier resolve not to value his own life above completing the ministry from Jesus. This is continuing personal resolve on the journey, not proof that a death in Jerusalem will occur.
    Acts 20:24 But I consider my life of no value to myself, so that I may complete my course and the ministry I received from the Lord Jesus: to bear solemn witness to the good news of God’s grace.

Acts 21:14
Since he would not be persuaded, we became silent and said, “Let the Lord’s will be done.”
  Luke 22:42 — thematic parallel: The companions cease arguing and submit to the Lord's will; Jesus' prayer submits His own will to the Father's. The submission formula is comparable, but the speakers, prayers and decisions differ; the companions are not made the speakers of Jesus' cup petition.
    Luke 22:42 saying, “Father, if Thou art willing, remove this cup from Me. Nevertheless, let not My will but Thine be done.”

Acts 21:15
After these days we made our preparations and went up to Jerusalem.
  Acts 20:22 — narrative parallel: The party now prepares and goes up toward Jerusalem, the destination Paul had previously declared. This is the onward movement of the stated journey, without making the preparations an independent arrival report or resolving the different Spirit-related instructions.
    Acts 20:22 And now, look, bound in spirit, I am going to Jerusalem, not knowing what will meet me there,

Acts 21:17
When we arrived in Jerusalem, the brothers welcomed us gladly.
  Acts 15:4 — narrative parallel: Paul and his party are gladly received in Jerusalem; the earlier visit also records reception by the Jerusalem Church and its leaders. This is a repeated reception setting at different visits, not the same council meeting or an identity imposed on every welcoming brother.
    Acts 15:4 When they arrived in Jerusalem, they were welcomed by the Church, the apostles, and the elders, and reported all that God had done with them.

Acts 21:18
The next day Paul went with us to James, and all the elders were present.
  Acts 15:6–15:13 — narrative parallel: Paul meets James and the elders in Jerusalem; the earlier council scene also has the elders gathered and James speaking after Peter and the missionaries. The link locates James within recurring Jerusalem leadership, without equating the visits, importing an apostle list into this meeting or identifying him with the James already killed.
    Acts 15:6 The apostles and the elders gathered to consider this matter.
    Acts 15:7 After much debate, Peter rose and said to them, “Men, brothers, you know that in earlier days God made a choice among you, that through my mouth the Gentiles should hear the word of the good news and believe.
    Acts 15:8 God, who knows the heart, bore witness to them by giving them the Holy Spirit just as He did to us.
    Acts 15:9 He made no distinction between us and them, cleansing their hearts by faith.
    Acts 15:10 Now, therefore, why are you testing God by placing upon the disciples’ neck a yoke that neither our fathers nor we were able to bear?
    Acts 15:11 Rather, we believe that we are saved through the grace of the Lord Jesus, in the same way as they are.”
    Acts 15:12 The whole multitude became silent and listened to Barnabas and Paul relate all the signs and wonders God had done among the Gentiles through them.
    Acts 15:13 After they fell silent, James spoke: “Men, brothers, listen to me.

Acts 21:19
After greeting them, he related one by one the things God had done among the Gentiles through his ministry.
  Acts 14:27 — narrative parallel: Paul reports what God has done among Gentiles through his ministry; the earlier Antioch report by Paul and Barnabas recounts what God did with them and the opened door of faith for Gentiles. The mission-report pattern and divine agency correspond, with different hearers, visits and reporting personnel.
    Acts 14:27 When they arrived and gathered the Church, they reported all that God had done with them and how He had opened a door of faith to the Gentiles.

Acts 21:20
When they heard, they glorified God and said to him, “You see, brother, how many tens of thousands of Jews have believed, and they are all zealous for the law.
  Acts 15:5 — thematic parallel: The Jerusalem speakers describe many Jewish believers as zealous for the law; the earlier council includes believing Pharisees who demand Gentile circumcision and law observance. Both show law commitment among believers, but this verse does not identify all the Jerusalem believers as Pharisees or say they all make that Gentile demand.
    Acts 15:5 But some believers from the party of the Pharisees rose and said, “It is necessary to circumcise them and command them to keep the law of Moses.”

Acts 21:21
They have been informed about you that you teach all the Jews among the Gentiles to forsake Moses, telling them not to circumcise their children or walk in the customs.
  Acts 16:3 — narrative parallel: The report alleges that Paul tells diaspora Jews not to circumcise their children; the earlier narrative records Paul circumcising Timothy because of local Jews. This is a contrasting concrete action, not a transfer of Timothy's age or mixed parentage to the rumored children's case, nor independent proof concerning every alleged teaching.
    Acts 16:3 Paul wanted him to go with him, and took him and circumcised him because of the Jews in those places, for they all knew that his father was Greek.

Acts 21:23
Therefore do what we tell you. We have four men who have taken a vow upon themselves.
  Numbers 6:2–6:5 — thematic parallel: The four men have a vow, and the following instructions involve purification and shaved heads; Numbers gives the law's setting for vowed consecration and hair restrictions. This is scriptural background, not an assertion that Acts explicitly names their vow type or that every Numbers condition is independently narrated here.
    Numbers 6:2 Speak unto the sons of Israel, and you shall say unto them: If a man or woman solemnly vows a vow of consecration unto the Lord, to be set apart
    Numbers 6:3 from wine and strong drink, he shall be purified from wine. He shall drink neither vinegar of wine, nor vinegar of strong drink, nor anything prepared from grapes; and he shall eat neither fresh grapes nor raisins.
    Numbers 6:4 All the days of his vow he shall consume nothing made from the vine: wine, from pressed grape skins unto the seed.
    Numbers 6:5 All the days of the vow of purification no razor shall come upon his head. Until the days which he vowed unto the Lord are fulfilled, he shall be holy, letting the hair of his head grow long.

Acts 21:24
Take these men, purify yourself with them, and pay their expenses so that they may shave their heads. Then everyone will know that there is nothing to what they have been told about you, but that you yourself also walk in step, keeping the law.
  Numbers 6:13–6:18 — thematic parallel: The proposed expenses and head shaving connect with Numbers' completion rites involving offerings and the consecrated person's shaved hair. Acts has Paul assist the four men and purify with them; it does not say here that Paul shaves his own head or that every tent-based legal detail is reproduced in the temple arrangement.
    Numbers 6:13 And this is the law of him who has vowed: on the day when he fulfils the days of his vow, he shall present his offering at the doors of the Tent of Testimony,
    Numbers 6:14 and he shall bring his gift unto the Lord: one unblemished year-old male lamb for whole burning, and one unblemished year-old ewe lamb for sin, and one unblemished ram for deliverance,
    Numbers 6:15 and a basket of unleavened bread of fine flour, loaves prepared with oil, and unleavened wafers spread with oil, and their sacrifice and their drink offering.
    Numbers 6:16 And the priest shall present them before the Lord, and prepare his offering for sin and his whole burnt offering.
    Numbers 6:17 And he shall prepare the ram as a sacrifice of deliverance unto the Lord, with the basket of unleavened bread; and the priest shall prepare its sacrifice and its drink offering.
    Numbers 6:18 And he who has vowed shall shave the head of his vow at the doors of the Tent of Testimony, and put the hair upon the fire which is under the sacrifice of deliverance.

Acts 21:25
But concerning the Gentiles who have believed, we have sent word, judging that they should keep themselves from what is sacrificed to idols, blood, what is strangled, and sexual immorality.”
  Acts 15:28–15:29 — narrative parallel: The Jerusalem speakers recall the sent decision for Gentile believers and its four requirements, corresponding to the council letter's decree. This is an explicit internal recall; sent word is retained, and no new circumcision requirement or complete Mosaic-law obligation is added to the listed provisions.
    Acts 15:28 For it seemed good to the Holy Spirit and to us to place no further burden upon you except these necessary things:
    Acts 15:29 to abstain from things sacrificed to idols, blood, things strangled, and sexual immorality. If you keep yourselves from these, you will do well. Farewell.”

Acts 21:26
Then Paul took the men, and on the next day, after purifying himself with them, he entered the temple, giving notice of the completion of the days of purification, up to the presentation of the offering for each one of them.
  Acts 24:17–24:18 — narrative parallel: The narrative places Paul purified in the temple with offerings in view; his later defense says that he brought alms and offerings and was found purified there. This is a retrospective report of the same setting, not independent testimony that every planned offering had already been completed before the disturbance.
    Acts 24:17 After several years I came to bring alms to my nation and offerings.
    Acts 24:18 While I was engaged in these, they found me purified in the temple, with neither a crowd nor an uproar.

Acts 21:27
When the seven days were about to be completed, the Jews from Asia saw him in the temple, stirred up the whole crowd, and laid hands on him,
  Acts 24:18–24:19 — narrative parallel: Asian Jews see Paul in the temple and initiate the disturbance; Paul's later defense identifies Jews from Asia in connection with being found purified there. The shared group and setting connect the accounts without treating the defense as an independent witness to the seven-day count or every crowd action.
    Acts 24:18 While I was engaged in these, they found me purified in the temple, with neither a crowd nor an uproar.
    Acts 24:19 But there were some Jews from Asia who ought to be present before you and bring charges, if they had anything against me.

Acts 21:28
crying, “Men of Israel, help! This is the man who teaches everyone everywhere against the people, the law, and this place. Moreover, he has even brought Greeks into the temple and defiled this holy place.”
  Acts 6:13–6:14 — narrative parallel: The charges against Paul name the people, law and holy place; Stephen's accusers likewise allege speech against the holy place and law and altered Mosaic customs. These are comparable accusation patterns, not endorsements of their truth; the Greek-entry allegation belongs to Paul's scene, not Stephen's.
    Acts 6:13 They set up false witnesses who said, “This man never stops speaking words against this holy place and the law.
    Acts 6:14 For we have heard him say that this Jesus the Nazarene will destroy this place and change the customs Moses handed down to us.”

Acts 21:29
For they had previously seen Trophimus the Ephesian with him in the city and supposed that Paul had brought him into the temple.
  Acts 20:4 — narrative parallel: Trophimus appears earlier among Paul's companions from Asia and is here identified more specifically as Ephesian. This connects the named companion; the actual observation is in the city, while his entry into the temple remains the accusers' supposition, not an independently narrated act.
    Acts 20:4 He was accompanied by Sopater son of Pyrrhus from Berea; Aristarchus and Secundus from Thessalonica; Gaius from Derbe, and Timothy; and Tychicus and Trophimus from Asia.

Acts 21:30
The whole city was stirred, and the people ran together. They seized Paul and dragged him outside the temple, and immediately the doors were shut.
  Acts 26:21 — narrative parallel: The earlier narrative records seizure in the temple and the attempt on Paul's life in the following verse; his later defense recalls the Jews seizing him in the temple and trying to kill him. This is Paul's retrospective reference, not a second independent eyewitness account or proof of every motive he gives in the speech.
    Acts 26:21 For these reasons the Jews seized me in the temple and tried to kill me.

Acts 21:31
While they were seeking to kill him, a report reached the tribune of the cohort that all Jerusalem was in confusion.
  Acts 23:27 — narrative parallel: The threat to kill Paul and the tribune's intervention are later summarized in Lysias's letter as seizure, imminent death and rescue. The letter's connection with learning Roman status is kept distinct from the narrative, which reveals that information during the later intended scourging episode; no unreported earlier disclosure is inserted.
    Acts 23:27 This man was seized by the Jews and was about to be killed by them when I came with the troops and rescued him, having learned that he was a Roman.

Acts 21:32
He immediately took soldiers and centurions and ran down to them. When they saw the tribune and the soldiers, they stopped beating Paul.
  Acts 23:10 — narrative parallel: Military intervention stops the crowd’s beating of Paul; the tribune later orders troops to remove him from a dangerously divided council. These are separate interventions by the military, with different groups and stages; fear of his being torn apart is not rewritten as a completed injury.
    Acts 23:10 As the dispute intensified, the tribune feared Paul would be torn apart by them. He ordered the troops to go down, snatch him from their midst, and bring him into the barracks.

Acts 21:33
Then the tribune approached, seized him, and ordered him bound with two chains. He asked who he was and what he had done.
  Acts 22:29 — narrative parallel: The tribune's order binding Paul is recalled when he becomes afraid on learning Paul is Roman and that he had bound him. This is the internal consequence of the same custody, not independent confirmation of two chains or a claim that he already knew the citizenship at the original arrest.
    Acts 22:29 Those about to examine him immediately withdrew. The tribune too was afraid when he learned that Paul was a Roman and that he had bound him.

Acts 21:34
Some in the crowd shouted one thing and some another. Since he could learn nothing certain because of the uproar, he ordered Paul to be brought into the barracks.
  Acts 19:32 — narrative parallel: Both uproars include some shouting one thing and some another, making the crowd's activity confused. Ephesus concerns the Artemis disturbance, Jerusalem the temple accusation; the formula does not identify the crowds or transfer the Ephesian participants' motives to Jerusalem.
    Acts 19:32 Some shouted one thing and some another, for the assembly was confused, and most did not know why they had come together.

Acts 21:35
When he reached the steps, the soldiers had to carry him because of the crowd’s violence,
  Acts 23:10 — narrative parallel: The soldiers have to carry Paul because of the crowd's violence; the later council dispute likewise prompts the tribune to have troops snatch him away for protection. The removal under threat corresponds, but the later scene does not independently attest the steps, carrying or a disabling injury.
    Acts 23:10 As the dispute intensified, the tribune feared Paul would be torn apart by them. He ordered the troops to go down, snatch him from their midst, and bring him into the barracks.

Acts 21:36
for the multitude of the people followed, crying, “Away with him!”
  Luke 23:18 — narrative parallel: The crowd cries for Paul to be taken away, comparable to the cry to remove Jesus before Pilate. The hostile removal demand is shared, while the accused men, officials and occasions differ; the demand to release Barabbas is not inserted into Paul's scene.
    Luke 23:18 But they cried out all together, saying, “Take this man away, and release Barabbas to us!”

Acts 21:37
As Paul was about to be brought into the barracks, he said to the tribune, “May I say something to you?” He said, “Do you know Greek?
  Acts 21:40 — narrative parallel: Paul first asks to speak to the tribune, who then asks him about Greek. In21:39 Paul explicitly asks to address the people, and21:40 grants that later request and records the Hebrew address. This connects the exchange’s stages without making the initial private question the same request as the subsequent public-speech permission.
    Acts 21:40 When he gave permission, Paul stood on the steps and motioned with his hand to the people. When a great silence fell, he addressed them in the Hebrew language, saying:

Acts 21:38
Then are you not the Egyptian who some time ago stirred up a revolt and led the four thousand men of the Sicarii into the wilderness?”
  Acts 21:39 — narrative parallel: The tribune's question suggests an Egyptian insurgent identity; Paul's reply identifies him instead as a Jewish man from Tarsus in Cilicia. The suspicion remains the official's question, not a narrated identification of Paul with that Egyptian or the Sicarii.
    Acts 21:39 Paul said, “I am a Jewish man from Tarsus in Cilicia, a citizen of no insignificant city. I ask you, allow me to speak to the people.”

Acts 21:39
Paul said, “I am a Jewish man from Tarsus in Cilicia, a citizen of no insignificant city. I ask you, allow me to speak to the people.”
  Acts 22:3 — narrative parallel: Paul repeats his Jewish identity and Tarsus/Cilicia origin in the ensuing defense, adding his upbringing and education. The repeated origin does not make Tarsian civic identity proof of Roman citizenship or establish the tribune's earlier suspicion.
    Acts 22:3 “I am a Jewish man, born in Tarsus of Cilicia, but brought up in this city at the feet of Gamaliel, educated in the strict observance of the ancestral law, zealous for God as all of you are today.

Acts 21:40
When he gave permission, Paul stood on the steps and motioned with his hand to the people. When a great silence fell, he addressed them in the Hebrew language, saying:
  Acts 22:2 — narrative parallel: The permission, hand gesture and Hebrew opening lead to the crowd becoming still quieter on hearing that language. This is the continuation of one address; the supplied Hebrew wording is preserved rather than silently replaced by another language name.
    Acts 22:2 When they heard that he addressed them in the Hebrew language, they became still quieter. He said:

Acts 22:1
“Men, brothers and fathers, hear the defense I now make before you.”
  Acts 7:2 — narrative parallel: Paul's men, brothers and fathers opening recalls Stephen's address to his hearers before his defense. The formal appeal to an audience corresponds, but the assemblies, speakers and ensuing arguments are different; no identity of the two hearings is claimed.
    Acts 7:2 He answered, “Men, brothers and fathers, listen. The God of glory appeared to our father Abraham while he was in Mesopotamia, before he lived in Haran,

Acts 22:2
When they heard that he addressed them in the Hebrew language, they became still quieter. He said:
  Acts 21:40 — narrative parallel: The crowd's increased quiet responds to the Hebrew speech just introduced after Paul's hand signal. This is the same speech's continuation, without changing Hebrew to a different language designation or treating the repeated report as an independent account.
    Acts 21:40 When he gave permission, Paul stood on the steps and motioned with his hand to the people. When a great silence fell, he addressed them in the Hebrew language, saying:

Acts 22:3
“I am a Jewish man, born in Tarsus of Cilicia, but brought up in this city at the feet of Gamaliel, educated in the strict observance of the ancestral law, zealous for God as all of you are today.
  Acts 5:34 — narrative parallel: Paul names Gamaliel as his teacher in the ancestral law; the earlier narrative identifies Gamaliel as a Pharisee and respected teacher of the law. That gives the named teacher's narrative antecedent, without independently verifying Paul's classroom history or identifying Gamaliel with every later Pharisaic speaker.
    Acts 5:34 But a Pharisee named Gamaliel, a teacher of the law honored by all the people, stood in the council and ordered the men to be taken outside briefly.

Acts 22:4
I persecuted this Way to the point of death, binding and handing over to prisons both men and women,
  Acts 8:3 — narrative parallel: Paul's confession of imprisoning men and women recalls Saul entering houses and dragging both men and women to prison. This is the same persecutor's retrospective account; the earlier selected verse does not itself detail every death implied by the defense's to-the-point-of-death statement.
    Acts 8:3 But Saul was ravaging the Church, entering house after house, dragging away both men and women, and handing them over to prison.

Acts 22:5
as the high priest and the whole council of elders can testify for me. From them I also received letters to the brothers, and I went toward Damascus to bring those there bound to Jerusalem for punishment.
  Acts 9:1–9:2 — narrative parallel: The defense's letters and Damascus mission to bring prisoners back to Jerusalem recall Saul asking the high priest for letters to Damascus synagogues to bind people of the Way. The reports correspond in authorization and purpose, with different wording and no claim that the council is currently testifying in the narrative.
    Acts 9:1 Saul, still breathing threat and murder against the Lord’s disciples, went to the high priest
    Acts 9:2 and asked him for letters to the synagogues in Damascus, so that if he found any belonging to the Way, both men and women, he might bring them bound to Jerusalem.

Acts 22:6
As I was traveling and drawing near Damascus, about noon, a brilliant light from heaven suddenly flashed around me.
  Acts 26:12–26:13 — narrative parallel: Both defenses place the heavenly-light encounter on the Damascus journey at noon. The Agrippa speech further describes light brighter than the sun around Paul and his companions; those details are not silently added to this verse or taken as separate eyewitness testimony.
    Acts 26:12 In this pursuit I was traveling to Damascus with authority and commission from the chief priests,
    Acts 26:13 when at midday, O king, I saw along the road a light from heaven, brighter than the sun, shining around me and those traveling with me.

Acts 22:7
I fell to the ground and heard a voice saying to me, ‘Saul, Saul, why are you persecuting Me?’
  Acts 9:4 — narrative parallel: Paul's fall and the repeated Saul, Saul persecution question recall the earlier Damascus narration. This is another presentation of the same event, not an independently named witness or a statement that every companion's posture is described identically.
    Acts 9:4 He fell to the ground and heard a voice saying to him, “Saul, Saul, why are you persecuting Me?”

Acts 22:8
I answered, ‘Who art Thou, Lord?’ He said to me, ‘I am Jesus the Nazarene, whom you are persecuting.’
  Acts 9:5 — narrative parallel: The question about the Lord's identity and the answer identifying Jesus recur in the earlier narration. The defense explicitly includes the Nazarene designation, which the selected earlier answer does not; the two quotations are not made word-for-word identical.
    Acts 9:5 He said, “Who art Thou, Lord?” He answered, “I am Jesus, whom you are persecuting.

Acts 22:9
Those with me saw the light, but they did not hear the voice of the One speaking to me.
  Acts 9:7 — narrative parallel: The companion reports differ: the narration says they heard the voice and saw no one; the defense says they saw the light but did not hear the voice of the One speaking to Paul. Both formulations are preserved, without replacing hear with understand or supplying an unreported sequence to harmonize them.
    Acts 9:7 The men traveling with him stood speechless, hearing the voice but seeing no one.

Acts 22:10
I said, ‘What should I do, Lord?’ The Lord said to me, ‘Rise and go into Damascus, and there you will be told about everything appointed for you to do.’
  Acts 9:6 — narrative parallel: Both reports command Saul to rise, enter Damascus/the city and await instructions about what he is to do. The defense includes Paul's question and appointed wording; these are not inserted into the earlier report as though its supplied text contained them.
    Acts 9:6 But rise and enter the city, and you will be told what you must do.”

Acts 22:11
Since I could not see because of the brightness of that light, I was led by the hand by those with me and came into Damascus.
  Acts 9:8 — narrative parallel: Paul's inability to see and being led by hand into Damascus repeat the earlier blindness-and-leading report. The defense supplies the brightness explanation; the earlier verse does not state that cause in the same words or resolve the companion-hearing difference.
    Acts 9:8 Saul rose from the ground, but although his eyes were open, he could see nothing. They led him by the hand and brought him into Damascus.

Acts 22:12
A certain Ananias, a man devout according to the law and well spoken of by all the Jews living there,
  Acts 9:10 — narrative parallel: The named Ananias of Damascus is earlier introduced as a disciple receiving a vision; the defense describes his law devotion and good reputation among local Jews. The complementary descriptions do not make him the later high priest Ananias or independently verify every reputation claim.
    Acts 9:10 There was a disciple in Damascus named Ananias. The Lord said to him in a vision, “Ananias.” He said, “Here I am, Lord.”

Acts 22:13
came to me, stood beside me, and said, ‘Brother Saul, regain your sight.’ At that very hour I regained my sight and looked at him.
  Acts 9:17–9:18 — narrative parallel: Ananias addresses Brother Saul and sight returns promptly in both reports. The earlier narration includes laying on hands and the scale-like falling description; the defense has regain-your-sight words and looking at Ananias at that hour. Their actions and timing expressions remain distinct.
    Acts 9:17 Ananias went and entered the house. Laying his hands upon him, he said, “Brother Saul, the Lord has sent me—Jesus, who appeared to you on the road as you came—so that you may regain your sight and be filled with the Holy Spirit.”
    Acts 9:18 At once something like scales fell from his eyes, and he regained his sight. He rose and was baptized,
