  Acts 9:15–9:16 — narrative parallel: Ananias announces God's appointment for Saul to know, see and hear; the earlier scene has the Lord tell Ananias that Saul is a chosen instrument who will bear His name. The calling corresponds, but the recipient of the earlier divine speech and the wording are different; these are not one identical quotation.
    Acts 9:15 But the Lord said to him, “Go, for this man is a chosen vessel for Me, to carry My name before Gentiles and kings and the sons of Israel.
    Acts 9:16 For I will show him how much he must suffer for My name.”

Acts 22:15
for you will be His witness to all people of what you have seen and heard.
  Acts 26:16 — narrative parallel: The defense through Ananias commissions Paul as witness to what he has seen and heard; the Agrippa speech reports Jesus appointing him servant and witness to what he has seen and future appearances. The witness role corresponds while the speakers, settings and seen/heard versus future wording remain different.
    Acts 26:16 But rise and stand on your feet, for I have appeared to you for this purpose: to appoint you a servant and witness of the things in which you have seen Me and those in which I will appear to you,

Acts 22:16
Now why do you delay? Rise, be baptized, and wash away your sins, calling upon His name.’
  1 Corinthians 6:11 — thematic parallel: Ananias joins baptism, washing away sins and invoking Jesus' name; the letter says you washed yourselves clean, were sanctified and were justified in the Lord Jesus Christ's name and in God's Spirit. The washing and name language correspond, preserving the letter's self-washing wording; it does not narrate Saul's baptism or repeat the command sequence as a formula.
    1 Corinthians 6:11 And some of you were these things. But you washed yourselves clean, but you were sanctified, but you were justified in the name of the Lord Jesus Christ and in the Spirit of our God.

Acts 22:17
After I returned to Jerusalem, while I was praying in the temple, I fell into a trance
  Acts 11:5 — narrative parallel: Paul describes praying in the temple and falling into a trance; Peter's recap describes praying in Joppa and seeing a vision in a trance. Prayer and trance are associated in both, but the men, locations and visions differ and no identical sequence beyond their actual wording is imposed.
    Acts 11:5 “I was in the city of Joppa praying, and in a trance I saw a vision: something like a large sheet descending, lowered by its four corners from heaven, and it came right to me.

Acts 22:18
and saw Him saying to me, ‘Hurry, and go out quickly from Jerusalem, because they will not accept your testimony about Me.’
  Acts 9:28–9:30 — narrative parallel: The defense reports a temple vision ordering departure because the testimony will not be accepted; the earlier account reports opposition and a killing attempt followed by the brothers sending Saul away. These related departure accounts give different reported agents and details; the earlier account does not independently narrate the vision or say every Jerusalem believer rejected him.
    Acts 9:28 He was with them, going in and out in Jerusalem and speaking boldly in the name of the Lord.
    Acts 9:29 He spoke and disputed with the Hellenists, but they attempted to kill him.
    Acts 9:30 When the brothers learned this, they brought him down to Caesarea and sent him away to Tarsus.

Acts 22:19
I said, ‘Lord, they themselves know that I used to imprison and beat in the synagogues those who believe in Thee.
  Acts 26:11 — narrative parallel: Paul recalls imprisoning and beating believers in synagogues; the Agrippa defense likewise recalls repeated punishment in synagogues and efforts to compel blasphemy. The persecution setting is shared, but the further rage, foreign-city and compelled-blasphemy details are not inserted into this verse.
    Acts 26:11 I punished them often in all the synagogues and tried to compel them to blaspheme. In my intense rage against them, I pursued them even to foreign cities.

Acts 22:20
And when the blood of Stephen, Thy witness, was being poured out, I myself stood by, approving and guarding the garments of those who were killing him.’
  Acts 7:58–8:1 — narrative parallel: The defense recalls Stephen's killing, Saul's approval and his guarding the killers' garments; the earlier narrative places garments at Saul's feet and states his consent to the killing. These are linked presentations within Acts, not independent verification of every quoted description.
    Acts 7:58 They drove him outside the city and began stoning him. The witnesses laid their garments at the feet of a young man called Saul.
    Acts 7:59 They stoned Stephen as he called out, saying, “Lord Jesus, receive my spirit.”
    Acts 7:60 Kneeling down, he cried with a loud voice, “Lord, do not hold this sin against them.” After saying this, he fell asleep.
    Acts 8:1 Saul approved of his killing. That day a great persecution arose against the Church in Jerusalem, and all except the apostles were scattered through the regions of Judea and Samaria.

Acts 22:21
He said to me, ‘Go, for I will send you far away to the Gentiles.’”
  Acts 26:17–26:18 — narrative parallel: The temple vision sends Paul far away to Gentiles; the Damascus commission in the Agrippa speech also includes sending him to Gentiles. The mission corresponds, but the visions are not collapsed into one occasion or made identical in their geographical and explanatory wording.
    Acts 26:17 rescuing you from the people and from the Gentiles, to whom I am sending you,
    Acts 26:18 to open their eyes, so that they may turn from darkness to light and from Satan’s authority to God, so that they may receive forgiveness of sins and an allotted share among those sanctified by faith in Me.’

Acts 22:22
They listened to him until this statement, then raised their voice and said, “Remove such a man from the earth, for he ought not to live.”
  Acts 21:36 — narrative parallel: The renewed demand to remove Paul from the earth follows his Gentile-mission statement and recalls the earlier crowd's away-with-him cry. This is escalating hostility in successive stages, not an attribution of that demand to all Jewish believers or every earlier hearer.
    Acts 21:36 for the multitude of the people followed, crying, “Away with him!”

Acts 22:23
As they shouted, flung their garments, and threw dust into the air,
  Acts 7:57–7:58 — narrative parallel: Both hostile crowds shout and move garments in scenes involving Stephen and Paul; Saul had stood on the persecuting side in Stephen's scene. The contrast preserves different actions: Stephen is stoned, while this verse has garments flung and dust thrown, without narrating Paul being stoned here or identifying a particular ritual for the gestures.
    Acts 7:57 They cried out with a loud voice, stopped their ears, and rushed upon him together.
    Acts 7:58 They drove him outside the city and began stoning him. The witnesses laid their garments at the feet of a young man called Saul.

Acts 22:24
the tribune ordered him brought into the barracks and directed that he be examined by scourging, so that he might learn why they were shouting against him in this way.
  Acts 16:22–16:23 — narrative parallel: Roman authorities order bodily punishment amid opposition to Paul in both scenes. Philippi has magistrates order rods and the beating carried out; here the tribune orders examination by scourging, which the following citizenship challenge interrupts. The officials, purposes, instruments and outcomes are not made identical.
    Acts 16:22 The crowd joined the attack against them. The magistrates tore off their garments and ordered them to be beaten with rods.
    Acts 16:23 After inflicting many blows on them, they threw them into prison and ordered the jailer to keep them securely.

Acts 22:25
As they stretched him out with the straps, Paul said to the centurion standing nearby, “Is it lawful for you to scourge a Roman citizen who has not been condemned?”
  Acts 16:37 — narrative parallel: Paul challenges punishment of an uncondemned Roman citizen, as his earlier Philippian protest invokes uncondemned Roman status after a public beating. The legal-status appeal recurs, but this challenge precedes the intended scourging, whereas the Philippian protest follows punishment and speaks in the plural.
    Acts 16:37 But Paul said to them, “They beat us publicly without a verdict, although we are Roman citizens, and threw us into prison. And now are they sending us away secretly? No indeed! Let them come themselves and lead us out.”

Acts 22:26
When the centurion heard this, he went to the tribune and reported it, saying, “What are you about to do? This man is a Roman.”
  Acts 22:25 — narrative parallel: The centurion passes Paul's Roman-citizenship objection to the tribune after hearing it. This is the transmission of the just-stated claim, not an independent account of the source or purchase price of Paul's citizenship.
    Acts 22:25 As they stretched him out with the straps, Paul said to the centurion standing nearby, “Is it lawful for you to scourge a Roman citizen who has not been condemned?”

Acts 22:27
The tribune came and said to him, “Tell me, are you a Roman?” He said, “Yes.”
  Acts 22:25 — narrative parallel: The tribune directly asks Paul to confirm the Roman status already raised in his question to the centurion. The reply confirms Paul's stated claim within the interview; it does not itself give an independent documentary history of citizenship.
    Acts 22:25 As they stretched him out with the straps, Paul said to the centurion standing nearby, “Is it lawful for you to scourge a Roman citizen who has not been condemned?”

Acts 22:29
Those about to examine him immediately withdrew. The tribune too was afraid when he learned that Paul was a Roman and that he had bound him.
  Acts 16:38 — narrative parallel: The tribune becomes afraid after learning Paul's Roman status, as the Philippian magistrates became afraid on hearing that the men were Romans. The response to the status claim corresponds, with different officials, plural versus singular citizens and different timing relative to punishment.
    Acts 16:38 The officers reported these words to the magistrates, and they were afraid when they heard that the men were Romans.

Acts 22:30
The next day, wishing to learn with certainty what accusation the Jews were bringing against him, he unbound him and ordered the chief priests and the whole council to assemble. He brought Paul down and set him before them.
  Acts 23:28 — narrative parallel: The narrative says the tribune convenes the council to learn the accusation; his letter later says he brought Paul to their council wishing to learn its reason. This is the official's later summary of the same action, not a separate hearing or independent acquittal.
    Acts 23:28 Wishing to learn the reason they accused him, I brought him down to their council.

Acts 23:1
Paul looked intently at the council and said, “Men, brothers, I have conducted my life before God with an entirely good conscience to this day.”
  Acts 24:16 — narrative parallel: Paul asserts a good conscience before God at the council and later says he strives for a blameless conscience toward God and people. These are related self-descriptions in separate defenses, not independent proof that every past action was right.
    Acts 24:16 For this reason I myself strive always to have a blameless conscience toward God and human beings.

Acts 23:2
The high priest Ananias ordered those standing beside him to strike his mouth.
  John 18:22–18:23 — narrative parallel: Paul is ordered struck on the mouth by the high priest; Jesus is struck by an officer during a high-priestly exchange and questions the blow. The hearing-and-blow motif corresponds, but the priests, victims, order and replies differ; Ananias is not identified with Jesus' interrogator.
    John 18:22 When He said these things, one of the officers standing nearby gave Jesus a slap, saying, “Is this how Thou answerest the high priest?”
    John 18:23 Jesus answered him, “If I spoke wrongly, bear witness about the wrong; but if rightly, why do you beat Me?”

Acts 23:3
Then Paul said to him, “God is going to strike you, whitewashed wall! You sit judging me according to the law, yet in violation of the law you order me struck?”
  Matthew 23:27–23:28 — thematic parallel: Paul calls the judging high priest a whitewashed wall while exposing conduct contrary to the law; Jesus uses whitewashed tombs for outward appearance hiding uncleanness and lawlessness. The whitewash rebuke is comparable, but walls and tombs, addressees and specific charges differ; this is not a quotation or a narrated fulfilment of Paul's prediction.
    Matthew 23:27 Woe to you, scribes and Pharisees, hypocrites, because you resemble whitewashed tombs, which outwardly appear beautiful, but inwardly are full of dead people’s bones and all uncleanness!
    Matthew 23:28 So you too outwardly appear righteous to people, but inwardly you are full of hypocrisy and lawlessness.

Acts 23:4
Those standing nearby said, “Are you insulting God’s high priest?”
  John 18:22 — narrative parallel: Bystanders accuse Paul of insulting the high priest, while the officer who strikes Jesus challenges how He answers the high priest. The accusation about disrespect corresponds, without endorsing either accusation or treating the groups and blows as the same event.
    John 18:22 When He said these things, one of the officers standing nearby gave Jesus a slap, saying, “Is this how Thou answerest the high priest?”

Acts 23:5
Paul said, “Brothers, I did not know that he was high priest, for it is written, ‘You shall not speak evil of a ruler of your people.’”
  Exodus 22:27 — quotation: Paul cites the ruler prohibition: Acts has a ruler of your people, whereas the supplied Exodus has rulers of your people. The earlier prohibition about gods is not part of his quotation. His statement that he did not know the man was high priest remains his claim, without supplying an unreported cause.
    Exodus 22:27 You shall not revile gods, neither shall you speak evil of the rulers of your people.

Acts 23:6
When Paul perceived that one part consisted of Sadducees and the other of Pharisees, he cried out in the council, “Men, brothers, I am a Pharisee, a son of Pharisees. I am being judged concerning the hope and resurrection of the dead.”
  Acts 26:5–26:8 — narrative parallel: Paul identifies as Pharisaic and makes resurrection hope the issue; the Agrippa defense likewise recalls his Pharisaic life and frames the trial around ancestral promise and God's raising the dead. The arguments recur at different hearings, without turning the earlier council tactic into every prosecutor's agreed description.
    Acts 26:5 They have known me from the first, if they are willing to testify, that I lived as a Pharisee according to the strictest party of our religion.
    Acts 26:6 Now I stand on trial because of hope in the promise made by God to our fathers,
    Acts 26:7 which our twelve tribes, earnestly worshiping night and day, hope to attain. It is concerning this hope that I am accused by Jews, O king.
    Acts 26:8 Why do you consider it unbelievable that God raises the dead?

Acts 23:7
As he said this, dissension arose between the Pharisees and Sadducees, and the multitude was divided.
  Acts 23:9 — narrative parallel: The split between Pharisees and Sadducees develops into some Pharisaic scribes arguing in Paul's defense. This traces the same council dispute's development, without saying every Pharisee accepted every claim or that the council issued a unanimous verdict.
    Acts 23:9 A great outcry arose, and some scribes of the Pharisees’ party stood up and argued fiercely, saying, “We find nothing wrong in this man. But what if a spirit or an angel has spoken to him?”

Acts 23:8
For the Sadducees say there is no resurrection, nor angel nor spirit, but the Pharisees acknowledge both.
  Matthew 22:23 — thematic parallel: Both passages explicitly associate Sadducees with denial of resurrection. Acts additionally names angel and spirit and contrasts the Pharisees; those further distinctions are not attributed to Matthew's selected statement or expanded beyond Acts's supplied both wording.
    Matthew 22:23 On that day Sadducees, who say there is no resurrection, came to Him and questioned Him,

Acts 23:9
A great outcry arose, and some scribes of the Pharisees’ party stood up and argued fiercely, saying, “We find nothing wrong in this man. But what if a spirit or an angel has spoken to him?”
  Acts 5:38–5:39 — thematic parallel: Some Pharisaic scribes allow the possibility that a spirit or angel spoke to Paul; Gamaliel earlier warns that opposing the apostles could mean fighting God if their work is from God. Both interventions invoke possible divine agency, with different speakers and cases; the scribes are not identified as Gamaliel and neither conditional is turned into an unconditional confession.
    Acts 5:38 And now I tell you, keep away from these men and leave them alone. For if this plan or this work is of human origin, it will be overthrown;
    Acts 5:39 but if it is from God, you will not be able to overthrow them. You might even be found fighting against God.” They were persuaded by him.

Acts 23:10
As the dispute intensified, the tribune feared Paul would be torn apart by them. He ordered the troops to go down, snatch him from their midst, and bring him into the barracks.
  Acts 21:32–21:35 — narrative parallel: The tribune again has troops remove Paul from dangerous opponents, here fearing the divided council may tear him apart. The earlier rescue is from the temple crowd and includes carrying him on the steps; those circumstances and any completed injury are not imported into the council scene.
    Acts 21:32 He immediately took soldiers and centurions and ran down to them. When they saw the tribune and the soldiers, they stopped beating Paul.
    Acts 21:33 Then the tribune approached, seized him, and ordered him bound with two chains. He asked who he was and what he had done.
    Acts 21:34 Some in the crowd shouted one thing and some another. Since he could learn nothing certain because of the uproar, he ordered Paul to be brought into the barracks.
    Acts 21:35 When he reached the steps, the soldiers had to carry him because of the crowd’s violence,

Acts 23:11
The following night the Lord stood beside him and said, “Take courage, for as you have borne solemn witness about Me in Jerusalem, so you must also testify in Rome.”
  Acts 27:23–27:25 — narrative parallel: The Lord assures Paul that he must testify in Rome; the later angelic message says he must stand before Caesar and encourages him during the voyage. The continuing assurance of the destination corresponds, but the Lord and the angel are distinguished and the shipboard promise about other passengers is not added to this scene.
    Acts 27:23 For this night an angel of the God to whom I belong and whom I worship stood beside me,
    Acts 27:24 saying, ‘Do not fear, Paul. You must stand before Caesar, and look, God has granted you all who sail with you.’
    Acts 27:25 Therefore take courage, men, for I believe God that it will be just as I have been told.

Acts 23:12
When day came, the Jews formed a conspiracy and bound themselves under a curse, saying they would neither eat nor drink until they had killed Paul.
  Acts 23:21 — narrative parallel: The conspirators' curse-bound refusal to eat or drink until Paul's death is repeated in the nephew's warning to the tribune. This is the same plot relayed within the narrative, not an oath taken by every Jew or an independent report of what eventually happened to the plotters.
    Acts 23:21 But do not be persuaded by them, for more than forty men among them are lying in wait for him. They have bound themselves under a curse neither to eat nor to drink until they kill him, and now they are ready, awaiting your agreement.”

Acts 23:13
More than forty had made this conspiracy.
  Acts 23:21 — narrative parallel: The more-than-forty count recurs in the warning about the same ambushers. It remains more than forty rather than exactly forty; the repetition does not supply their names or report that their fasting later killed them.
    Acts 23:21 But do not be persuaded by them, for more than forty men among them are lying in wait for him. They have bound themselves under a curse neither to eat nor to drink until they kill him, and now they are ready, awaiting your agreement.”

Acts 23:14
They went to the chief priests and elders and said, “We have bound ourselves under a solemn curse to taste nothing until we have killed Paul.
  Acts 23:12–23:13 — narrative parallel: The oath already narrated is now announced by its makers to the chief priests and elders. The formulations neither eat nor drink and taste nothing remain distinct, and the report does not say every elder personally took the oath.
    Acts 23:12 When day came, the Jews formed a conspiracy and bound themselves under a curse, saying they would neither eat nor drink until they had killed Paul.
    Acts 23:13 More than forty had made this conspiracy.

Acts 23:15
Now, therefore, you and the council should inform the tribune to bring him down to you, as though you intended to determine his case more accurately. We are ready to kill him before he comes near.”
  Acts 23:20 — narrative parallel: The proposed pretext of a more accurate council inquiry is repeated in the nephew's report, which also specifies tomorrow. This is the plotting request relayed as a warning, not proof that the tribune had agreed to it or that the purported inquiry was sincere.
    Acts 23:20 He said, “The Jews have agreed to ask you to bring Paul down to the council tomorrow, as though it intended to inquire more accurately about him.

Acts 23:16
Paul’s sister’s son heard about the ambush, came and entered the barracks, and reported it to Paul.
  Acts 23:20–23:21 — narrative parallel: The narrator identifies the informer as Paul's sister's son; his later private report supplies the ambush and council-pretext details. This connects the informer's discovery with its reported content, without giving him an unreported name, exact age or another identity such as Timothy.
    Acts 23:20 He said, “The Jews have agreed to ask you to bring Paul down to the council tomorrow, as though it intended to inquire more accurately about him.
    Acts 23:21 But do not be persuaded by them, for more than forty men among them are lying in wait for him. They have bound themselves under a curse neither to eat nor to drink until they kill him, and now they are ready, awaiting your agreement.”

Acts 23:17
Paul called one of the centurions and said, “Take this young man to the tribune, for he has something to report to him.”
  Acts 23:18 — narrative parallel: Paul asks a centurion to take the young man to the tribune, and the following verse narrates that action with the centurion explaining Paul's request. This is a specific request and its execution, not separate independent evidence for the plot.
    Acts 23:18 So he took him and brought him to the tribune, saying, “The prisoner Paul called me and asked me to bring this young man to you, since he has something to tell you.”

Acts 23:18
So he took him and brought him to the tribune, saying, “The prisoner Paul called me and asked me to bring this young man to you, since he has something to tell you.”
  Acts 23:17 — narrative parallel: The centurion's statement that Paul asked him to bring the young man directly resumes the earlier request. Prisoner is the centurion's description in this report; the repeated message does not identify a different informer.
    Acts 23:17 Paul called one of the centurions and said, “Take this young man to the tribune, for he has something to report to him.”

Acts 23:19
The tribune took him by the hand, withdrew privately, and asked, “What is it that you have to report to me?”
  Acts 23:22 — narrative parallel: The tribune withdraws privately to hear the young man, then orders him not to disclose that he informed him. The private interview and confidentiality instruction belong to the same warning; no injury, secret political motive or name is inferred from taking him by the hand.
    Acts 23:22 The tribune dismissed the young man, ordering him, “Tell no one that you have disclosed these things to me.”

Acts 23:20
He said, “The Jews have agreed to ask you to bring Paul down to the council tomorrow, as though it intended to inquire more accurately about him.
  Acts 23:15 — narrative parallel: The nephew describes the proposed council request using the earlier plot's more-accurate-inquiry pretext. His report includes tomorrow; the comparison does not turn the conspirators' stated intention into an honest inquiry or assume the commander has consented.
    Acts 23:15 Now, therefore, you and the council should inform the tribune to bring him down to you, as though you intended to determine his case more accurately. We are ready to kill him before he comes near.”

Acts 23:21
But do not be persuaded by them, for more than forty men among them are lying in wait for him. They have bound themselves under a curse neither to eat nor to drink until they kill him, and now they are ready, awaiting your agreement.”
  Acts 23:12–23:15 — narrative parallel: The warning repeats the original curse, fasting, more-than-forty group and readiness to kill under a council pretext. This is the same conspiracy relayed to the commander, not another independent conspiracy or knowledge of the plotters' later fate.
    Acts 23:12 When day came, the Jews formed a conspiracy and bound themselves under a curse, saying they would neither eat nor drink until they had killed Paul.
    Acts 23:13 More than forty had made this conspiracy.
    Acts 23:14 They went to the chief priests and elders and said, “We have bound ourselves under a solemn curse to taste nothing until we have killed Paul.
    Acts 23:15 Now, therefore, you and the council should inform the tribune to bring him down to you, as though you intended to determine his case more accurately. We are ready to kill him before he comes near.”

Acts 23:22
The tribune dismissed the young man, ordering him, “Tell no one that you have disclosed these things to me.”
  Acts 23:19 — narrative parallel: The command not to disclose the informing concludes the private interview just introduced. This identifies the confidential exchange being protected, without inventing a subsequent disclosure or the informer's motive.
    Acts 23:19 The tribune took him by the hand, withdrew privately, and asked, “What is it that you have to report to me?”

Acts 23:23
He called two of the centurions and said, “Prepare two hundred soldiers to go as far as Caesarea, with seventy horsemen and two hundred spearmen, leaving at the third hour of the night.”
  Acts 23:31–23:32 — narrative parallel: The night escort order is followed by the soldiers taking Paul by night and the horsemen continuing the next day. The execution of the escort is connected, but the later report does not independently repeat every number or the exact third-hour departure.
    Acts 23:31 So the soldiers, according to their orders, took Paul and brought him by night to Antipatris.
    Acts 23:32 The next day they left the horsemen to go on with him and returned to the barracks.

Acts 23:24
He also ordered mounts to be provided so that they could put Paul on them and bring him safely to Felix the governor.
  Acts 23:33 — narrative parallel: The commander orders safe conveyance to Felix, and the later arrival presents Paul and the letter to the governor. The delivery follows the stated destination; the arrival report does not independently count the mounts or say the tribune personally accompanied him.
    Acts 23:33 When the horsemen entered Caesarea, they delivered the letter to the governor and also presented Paul before him.

Acts 23:25
He wrote a letter in this form:
  Acts 15:23 — thematic parallel: Both passages introduce a written letter whose body follows in the narrative. The formal embedded-letter device is shared, while Lysias's custody dispatch and the Jerusalem council's Gentile instructions have different authors, recipients and authority; their contents are not conflated.
    Acts 15:23 They wrote by their hand: “The apostles and elders, your brothers, to the brothers from the Gentiles in Antioch, Syria, and Cilicia: greetings.

Acts 23:26
“Claudius Lysias to the most excellent governor Felix: greetings.
  Acts 26:25 — thematic parallel: The letter addresses governor Felix as most excellent; Paul later uses the same honorific for Festus. This is a bounded formal-address parallel between different officials, not proof of their moral excellence or identity.
    Acts 26:25 Paul said, “I am not mad, most excellent Festus, but I speak words of truth and sound judgment.

Acts 23:27
This man was seized by the Jews and was about to be killed by them when I came with the troops and rescued him, having learned that he was a Roman.
  Acts 22:25–22:29 — narrative parallel: Lysias's letter links his rescue with having learned Paul was Roman; the narrative discloses the citizenship during the later intended-scourging episode. The differing presentation is kept intact without inserting earlier unreported knowledge or assigning an unreported motive for the letter's wording.
    Acts 22:25 As they stretched him out with the straps, Paul said to the centurion standing nearby, “Is it lawful for you to scourge a Roman citizen who has not been condemned?”
    Acts 22:26 When the centurion heard this, he went to the tribune and reported it, saying, “What are you about to do? This man is a Roman.”
    Acts 22:27 The tribune came and said to him, “Tell me, are you a Roman?” He said, “Yes.”
    Acts 22:28 The tribune answered, “I acquired this citizenship for a large sum.” Paul said, “But I was born with it.”
    Acts 22:29 Those about to examine him immediately withdrew. The tribune too was afraid when he learned that Paul was a Roman and that he had bound him.

Acts 23:28
Wishing to learn the reason they accused him, I brought him down to their council.
  Acts 22:30 — narrative parallel: Lysias's letter says he brought Paul before the council to learn the accusation; the earlier narrative gives that stated purpose, summons and presentation. This is a retrospective summary of the same council appearance, not a second inquiry.
    Acts 22:30 The next day, wishing to learn with certainty what accusation the Jews were bringing against him, he unbound him and ordered the chief priests and the whole council to assemble. He brought Paul down and set him before them.

Acts 23:29
I found him accused over questions of their law, but under no charge deserving death or imprisonment.
  Acts 25:18–25:19 — narrative parallel: Lysias reports law-related questions rather than a charge deserving death or imprisonment; Festus later describes religious disputes and Jesus' being alive rather than the expected crimes. These are different officials' summaries with different addressees and scope, not a narrated release or identical charge lists.
    Acts 25:18 When his accusers stood up, they brought no charge of the evil things I had expected.
    Acts 25:19 Instead, they had certain disputes with him concerning their own religion and a certain Jesus who had died, whom Paul asserted to be alive.

Acts 23:30
When a plot against the man was disclosed to me, I immediately sent him to you, also ordering his accusers to state their case against him before you.”
  Acts 23:20–23:24 — narrative parallel: The letter summarizes disclosure of the plot and the decision to send Paul onward; the prior scene supplies the young man's warning and the resulting escort orders. This is an official recap, not independent verification of every letter claim or proof that all accusers obeyed an order immediately.
    Acts 23:20 He said, “The Jews have agreed to ask you to bring Paul down to the council tomorrow, as though it intended to inquire more accurately about him.
    Acts 23:21 But do not be persuaded by them, for more than forty men among them are lying in wait for him. They have bound themselves under a curse neither to eat nor to drink until they kill him, and now they are ready, awaiting your agreement.”
    Acts 23:22 The tribune dismissed the young man, ordering him, “Tell no one that you have disclosed these things to me.”

    Acts 23:24 He also ordered mounts to be provided so that they could put Paul on them and bring him safely to Felix the governor.

Acts 23:31
So the soldiers, according to their orders, took Paul and brought him by night to Antipatris.
  Acts 23:23 — narrative parallel: The soldiers' night journey follows the command to prepare a nighttime escort toward Caesarea. Antipatris is the narrated intermediate stopping point here, not a replacement name for Caesarea or independent confirmation of the exact third hour.
    Acts 23:23 He called two of the centurions and said, “Prepare two hundred soldiers to go as far as Caesarea, with seventy horsemen and two hundred spearmen, leaving at the third hour of the night.”

Acts 23:32

  Acts 23:23 — narrative parallel: The horsemen continuing with Paul were included in the earlier escort order. This identifies the continuing contingent while others return; the later verse does not repeat all numerical totals or identify each returning unit.
    Acts 23:23 He called two of the centurions and said, “Prepare two hundred soldiers to go as far as Caesarea, with seventy horsemen and two hundred spearmen, leaving at the third hour of the night.”

Acts 23:33
When the horsemen entered Caesarea, they delivered the letter to the governor and also presented Paul before him.
  Acts 23:24–23:26 — narrative parallel: Presentation of Paul and delivery of the letter to the governor carry out the earlier conveyance order and written dispatch to Felix. This is the ordered transfer's outcome, not a new letter or an appearance by Lysias himself.
    Acts 23:24 He also ordered mounts to be provided so that they could put Paul on them and bring him safely to Felix the governor.
    Acts 23:25 He wrote a letter in this form:
    Acts 23:26 “Claudius Lysias to the most excellent governor Felix: greetings.

Acts 23:34
He read it, asked what province Paul was from, and learned that he was from Cilicia.
  Acts 21:39 — narrative parallel: The governor learns that Paul is from Cilicia, the region Paul earlier named with his Tarsus origin. The regional identity corresponds, without turning this provincial inquiry into a new citizenship grant or inferring an unstated jurisdiction ruling.
    Acts 21:39 Paul said, “I am a Jewish man from Tarsus in Cilicia, a citizen of no insignificant city. I ask you, allow me to speak to the people.”

Acts 23:35
He said, “I will hear you fully when your accusers also arrive,” and ordered him kept under guard in Herod’s praetorium.
  Acts 24:1 — narrative parallel: The governor says he will hear Paul when the accusers arrive; the following episode records their arrival and presentation of the case. This connects the stated condition with the next hearing stage, not a final judgment or a recorded visit from Lysias.
    Acts 24:1 After five days the high priest Ananias came down with some elders and an advocate named Tertullus. They presented their case against Paul to the governor.

Acts 24:1
After five days the high priest Ananias came down with some elders and an advocate named Tertullus. They presented their case against Paul to the governor.
  Acts 23:35 — narrative parallel: The arrival of Ananias, elders and Tertullus supplies the accusers whose presence the governor had awaited. The earlier order does not independently confirm the five-day interval or identify every accompanying elder; this high priest Ananias is not the Damascus disciple.
    Acts 23:35 He said, “I will hear you fully when your accusers also arrive,” and ordered him kept under guard in Herod’s praetorium.

Acts 24:2
When Paul was called, Tertullus began to accuse him, saying, “Through you we enjoy much peace, and reforms are being made for this nation through your foresight.
  Acts 24:27 — thematic parallel: Tertullus offers a favorable portrait of Felix's peace and reforms; the narrator later explicitly gives favor with the Jews as Felix's reason for leaving Paul bound. The two portrayals of the same official may be compared without treating the advocate's praise as narrator-certified fact or claiming that every alleged reform is disproved.
    Acts 24:27 When two years were completed, Felix was succeeded by Porcius Festus. Wishing to gain favor with the Jews, Felix left Paul bound.

Acts 24:3
In every way and everywhere we acknowledge this, most excellent Felix, with all gratitude.
  Acts 23:26 — thematic parallel: Tertullus's most excellent Felix address repeats the honorific and recipient in Lysias's letter. The formal courtesy corresponds, but neither expression independently certifies the governor's righteousness or the truth of the surrounding praise.
    Acts 23:26 “Claudius Lysias to the most excellent governor Felix: greetings.

Acts 24:4
But so that I may not detain you further, I ask you in your kindness to hear us briefly.
  Acts 24:10 — thematic parallel: Tertullus asks the governor to hear briefly after his courteous opening; Paul's answering opening gives a different reason for confidence in addressing the same judge. These are opposed sides' introductions at one hearing, not the same speaker or a shared endorsement of every compliment.
    Acts 24:10 When the governor motioned for him to speak, Paul answered, “Knowing that you have been a judge of this nation for many years, I cheerfully make my defense concerning myself.

Acts 24:5
For we have found this man to be a plague, stirring up disturbances among all the Jews throughout the inhabited world, and a ringleader of the party of the Nazarenes.
  Acts 17:6–17:7 — narrative parallel: The advocate alleges worldwide disturbance by Paul; the earlier Thessalonian accusation likewise portrays the missionaries as unsettling the world and opposing Caesar. This is a recurring hostile accusation pattern, not narrator endorsement; the Nazarene-party label and the specific earlier king charge remain distinct.
    Acts 17:6 When they did not find them, they dragged Jason and some brothers before the city officials, shouting, “These men who have upset the inhabited world have come here too,
    Acts 17:7 and Jason has welcomed them. They all act against Caesar’s decrees, saying that there is another king, Jesus.”

Acts 24:6
He even tried to profane the temple, and we seized him.
  Acts 21:28–21:30 — narrative parallel: The accusation that Paul tried to profane the temple and was seized recalls the earlier defilement accusation and seizure. The narration also says the Greek-entry charge rested on a supposition about Trophimus; tried-to-profane and has-defiled remain the respective accusers' formulations, not independently established guilt.
    Acts 21:28 crying, “Men of Israel, help! This is the man who teaches everyone everywhere against the people, the law, and this place. Moreover, he has even brought Greeks into the temple and defiled this holy place.”
    Acts 21:29 For they had previously seen Trophimus the Ephesian with him in the city and supposed that Paul had brought him into the temple.
    Acts 21:30 The whole city was stirred, and the people ran together. They seized Paul and dragged him outside the temple, and immediately the doors were shut.

Acts 24:8
By examining him yourself, you will be able to learn about all these things of which we accuse him.”
  Acts 24:10–24:13 — narrative parallel: The advocate says examining the accused will establish the charges; Paul's response denies provoking a crowd and challenges the accusers' ability to prove them. This is the exchange of accusation and defense, not a governor's finding. The missing verse is not restored to make Lysias the antecedent of examining him.
    Acts 24:10 When the governor motioned for him to speak, Paul answered, “Knowing that you have been a judge of this nation for many years, I cheerfully make my defense concerning myself.
    Acts 24:11 You can confirm that it is no more than twelve days since I went up to Jerusalem to worship.
    Acts 24:12 They did not find me disputing with anyone or gathering a crowd, whether in the temple, in the synagogues, or in the city.
    Acts 24:13 Nor can they present proof to you of the things they now accuse me of.

Acts 24:9
The Jews also joined in the attack, asserting that these things were so.
  Acts 24:13 — narrative parallel: The accusers affirm that the allegations are true, while Paul denies their ability to present proof. These are opposing assertions within the hearing, neither converted into an independent narrator verdict or a completed decision by Felix.
    Acts 24:13 Nor can they present proof to you of the things they now accuse me of.

Acts 24:10
When the governor motioned for him to speak, Paul answered, “Knowing that you have been a judge of this nation for many years, I cheerfully make my defense concerning myself.
  Acts 24:2–24:4 — thematic parallel: Paul grounds his cheerful defense in Felix's years as judge; Tertullus's opening instead praises peace and reforms and asks a brief hearing. The differing introductions address the same official, without making their reasons, speakers or truth status identical.
    Acts 24:2 When Paul was called, Tertullus began to accuse him, saying, “Through you we enjoy much peace, and reforms are being made for this nation through your foresight.
    Acts 24:3 In every way and everywhere we acknowledge this, most excellent Felix, with all gratitude.
    Acts 24:4 But so that I may not detain you further, I ask you in your kindness to hear us briefly.

Acts 24:11
You can confirm that it is no more than twelve days since I went up to Jerusalem to worship.
  Acts 21:17 — narrative parallel: Paul dates his Jerusalem arrival to no more than twelve days before and states worship as its purpose; the narrative records that arrival. The callback does not independently calculate twelve days, establish Pentecost timing or repeat the defense's purpose clause.
    Acts 21:17 When we arrived in Jerusalem, the brothers welcomed us gladly.

Acts 24:12
They did not find me disputing with anyone or gathering a crowd, whether in the temple, in the synagogues, or in the city.
  Acts 21:27–21:30 — narrative parallel: Paul denies being found stirring a crowd; the earlier narration assigns the temple crowd's incitement to the Asian accusers who seize him. This is a specific correspondence about that disturbance, not an exhaustive alibi for every unreported conversation or all the city and synagogue locations named in his defense.
    Acts 21:27 When the seven days were about to be completed, the Jews from Asia saw him in the temple, stirred up the whole crowd, and laid hands on him,
    Acts 21:28 crying, “Men of Israel, help! This is the man who teaches everyone everywhere against the people, the law, and this place. Moreover, he has even brought Greeks into the temple and defiled this holy place.”
    Acts 21:29 For they had previously seen Trophimus the Ephesian with him in the city and supposed that Paul had brought him into the temple.
    Acts 21:30 The whole city was stirred, and the people ran together. They seized Paul and dragged him outside the temple, and immediately the doors were shut.

Acts 24:13
Nor can they present proof to you of the things they now accuse me of.
  Acts 24:5–24:6 — narrative parallel: Paul's proof challenge responds to the advocate's disturbance and temple-profanation allegations. The linkage identifies what he contests, without replacing the competing speeches with a narrator's legal verdict or assuming the governor has acquitted him.
    Acts 24:5 For we have found this man to be a plague, stirring up disturbances among all the Jews throughout the inhabited world, and a ringleader of the party of the Nazarenes.
    Acts 24:6 He even tried to profane the temple, and we seized him.

Acts 24:14
But I confess this to you: according to the Way, which they call a sect, I worship the God of our fathers, believing everything in accordance with the law and everything written in the prophets,
  Acts 28:23 — narrative parallel: Paul professes continuity with the law and prophets; the later Rome scene depicts him explaining Jesus from Moses' law and the prophets. This is a concrete scriptural-teaching correspondence, not independent verification of every professed belief or narrator adoption of the opponents' sect label.
    Acts 28:23 They appointed a day with him and came to his lodging in greater numbers. From morning until evening he explained to them, bearing solemn witness to the kingdom of God and seeking to persuade them concerning Jesus from both the law of Moses and the prophets.

Acts 24:15
having hope in God—which these people themselves also await—that there will be a resurrection of both the righteous and the unrighteous.
  John 5:28–5:29 — thematic parallel: Paul expects resurrection of righteous and unrighteous; Jesus describes those in tombs coming out, with good-doers to life and evil-doers to judgment. Both include opposed classes in resurrection, while the class descriptions, speakers and surrounding claims differ; this does not mean everyone receives the same outcome.
    John 5:28 Do not marvel at this, because an hour is coming in which all who are in the tombs will hear His voice
    John 5:29 and come out: those who did good things to a resurrection of life, but those who practiced base things to a resurrection of judgment.

Acts 24:16
For this reason I myself strive always to have a blameless conscience toward God and human beings.
  Acts 23:1 — narrative parallel: Paul's striving for a blameless conscience toward God and people recalls his good-conscience claim at the council. The conscience language belongs to related defenses and remains his self-description, not independent proof of moral perfection or approval of every earlier persecution.
    Acts 23:1 Paul looked intently at the council and said, “Men, brothers, I have conducted my life before God with an entirely good conscience to this day.”

Acts 24:17
After several years I came to bring alms to my nation and offerings.
  Romans 15:25–15:27 — thematic parallel: Paul's defense says he came with alms for his nation and offerings; the letter says he is now traveling to Jerusalem serving the saints and describes the contribution for poor saints there. These are specific relief-journey correspondences, but the letter's present travel perspective and narrower recipients do not independently establish receipt of every offering, the several-year interval or an exact date.
    Romans 15:25 but now I am traveling to Jerusalem, serving the saints.
    Romans 15:26 For Macedonia and Achaia were pleased to make a contribution for the poor among the saints in Jerusalem.
    Romans 15:27 For they were pleased, and they are their debtors; for if the Gentiles shared in their spiritual things, they also owe them service in material things.

Acts 24:18
While I was engaged in these, they found me purified in the temple, with neither a crowd nor an uproar.
  Acts 21:26–21:27 — narrative parallel: The defense says Paul was found purified in the temple without a crowd or uproar; the earlier narrative puts purification before the Asian Jews see him and stir the crowd. This preserves the disturbance's stated sequence rather than attributing its instigation to Paul or claiming all offerings were completed before arrest.
    Acts 21:26 Then Paul took the men, and on the next day, after purifying himself with them, he entered the temple, giving notice of the completion of the days of purification, up to the presentation of the offering for each one of them.
    Acts 21:27 When the seven days were about to be completed, the Jews from Asia saw him in the temple, stirred up the whole crowd, and laid hands on him,

Acts 24:19
But there were some Jews from Asia who ought to be present before you and bring charges, if they had anything against me.
  Acts 21:27 — narrative parallel: Paul points to Jews from Asia as the people who should appear with any charge; the original disturbance likewise begins with Asian Jews seeing him in the temple. The group and incident correspond, while the earlier verse does not supply a complete attendance list for the later court.
    Acts 21:27 When the seven days were about to be completed, the Jews from Asia saw him in the temple, stirred up the whole crowd, and laid hands on him,

Acts 24:20
Or let these men themselves say what wrongdoing they found when I stood before the council,
  Acts 23:9 — narrative parallel: Paul challenges the present accusers to name wrongdoing from the council scene, where some Pharisaic scribes said they found nothing wrong with him. This is a specific earlier assessment to compare, not a unanimous council acquittal or proof that every present prosecutor had said those words.
    Acts 23:9 A great outcry arose, and some scribes of the Pharisees’ party stood up and argued fiercely, saying, “We find nothing wrong in this man. But what if a spirit or an angel has spoken to him?”

Acts 24:21
except for this one declaration I cried out while standing among them: ‘It is concerning the resurrection of the dead that I am being judged before you today.’”
  Acts 23:6 — narrative parallel: Paul singles out the resurrection declaration as the disputed issue, recalling his council appeal to hope and resurrection. This is an explicit retrospective reference, not an assertion that no other sentence was spoken at the council or an independently verified account of every accuser's motive.
    Acts 23:6 When Paul perceived that one part consisted of Sadducees and the other of Pharisees, he cried out in the council, “Men, brothers, I am a Pharisee, a son of Pharisees. I am being judged concerning the hope and resurrection of the dead.”

Acts 24:22
Felix, who had more accurate knowledge of the Way, adjourned their case, saying, “When Lysias the tribune comes down, I will decide your case.”
  Acts 23:33–23:35 — narrative parallel: Felix had already received the letter and Paul, promising a hearing when accusers arrived; he now adjourns the case until Lysias comes. The separate hearing conditions and stages are preserved, without treating the letter as Lysias's personal appearance or inventing an eventual visit or final decision.
    Acts 23:33 When the horsemen entered Caesarea, they delivered the letter to the governor and also presented Paul before him.
    Acts 23:34 He read it, asked what province Paul was from, and learned that he was from Cilicia.
    Acts 23:35 He said, “I will hear you fully when your accusers also arrive,” and ordered him kept under guard in Herod’s praetorium.

Acts 24:23
He ordered the centurion to keep Paul in custody but grant him some freedom, and not prevent any of his own people from attending to him.
  Acts 28:16 — narrative parallel: Paul remains in custody with some permitted liberty; in Rome he is likewise allowed separate lodging while guarded by a soldier. These are distinct forms of guarded custody, not an unrestricted release, identical accommodation or the same officials and location.
    Acts 28:16 When we entered Rome, Paul was permitted to stay by himself with the soldier guarding him.

Acts 24:24
After some days Felix came with his own wife Drusilla, who was Jewish. He summoned Paul and heard him concerning faith in Christ Jesus.
  Acts 26:27–26:29 — thematic parallel: Felix and Drusilla hear Paul concerning faith in Christ; a later royal hearing has Paul engage Agrippa about belief and becoming Christian. The personal hearing of Christian claims by rulers is comparable, while the audiences and exchanges differ; no conversion of Felix, Drusilla or Agrippa is thereby asserted.
    Acts 26:27 King Agrippa, do you believe the prophets? I know that you believe.”
    Acts 26:28 Agrippa said to Paul, “With so little, you are trying to persuade me to become a Christian.”
    Acts 26:29 Paul said, “Whether with little or with much, I would pray to God that not only you but also all who hear me today might become as I am, except for these bonds.”

Acts 24:25
As Paul reasoned about righteousness, self-control, and the coming judgment, Felix became afraid and answered, “Go away for now. When I find an opportunity, I will summon you.”
  Acts 17:30–17:31 — thematic parallel: Paul's reasoning includes righteousness and coming judgment; the Athens address likewise announces God's appointed judgment of the world in righteousness. The recurring judgment proclamation corresponds, but the selected address does not repeat self-control or independently attest Felix's fear, and resurrection is explicit in Athens rather than added to this verse.
    Acts 17:30 Therefore, having overlooked the times of ignorance, God now commands all people everywhere to repent,
    Acts 17:31 because He has fixed a day on which He will judge the inhabited world in righteousness through a Man whom He appointed, giving assurance to everyone by raising Him from the dead.”

Acts 24:26
At the same time he hoped that Paul would give him money. Therefore he summoned him more frequently and conversed with him.
  Exodus 23:8 — thematic parallel: Felix hopes for money while repeatedly hearing the prisoner; the law forbids receiving gifts because they blind the eyes of those who see and corrupt righteous words. This is qualified ethical background for a governor holding a case, not a claim that Acts records an actual payment, a completed bribe or the terms of an unreported release deal.
    Exodus 23:8 And you shall receive no gifts; for gifts blind the eyes of those who see, and corrupt righteous words.

Acts 24:27
When two years were completed, Felix was succeeded by Porcius Festus. Wishing to gain favor with the Jews, Felix left Paul bound.
  Acts 25:1–25:3 — narrative parallel: The succession from Felix to Festus continues with Festus taking up the province and hearing renewed efforts against Paul. This traces the change of governor and continuing case, while Felix's favor-seeking motive and two-year interval remain the preceding narrator's statements rather than independently repeated facts or the later two years in Rome.
    Acts 25:1 Three days after arriving in the province, Festus went up from Caesarea to Jerusalem.
    Acts 25:2 The chief priests and the leading men of the Jews presented their case against Paul to him and urged him,
    Acts 25:3 asking as a favor against Paul that he summon him to Jerusalem, while planning an ambush to kill him along the road.

Acts 25:1
Three days after arriving in the province, Festus went up from Caesarea to Jerusalem.
  Acts 24:27 — narrative parallel: The preceding account names Porcius Festus as Felix's successor; Festus now arrives in the province and travels to Jerusalem. This establishes the change of governor behind the visit without independently repeating its three-day interval.
    Acts 24:27 When two years were completed, Felix was succeeded by Porcius Festus. Wishing to gain favor with the Jews, Felix left Paul bound.

Acts 25:2
The chief priests and the leading men of the Jews presented their case against Paul to him and urged him,
  Acts 24:1 — narrative parallel: Chief-priestly and Jewish leaders again bring Paul's case before the governor. The earlier delegation names Ananias, elders and Tertullus before Felix; the new delegation is described differently before Festus, without identifying every participant as the same person.
    Acts 24:1 After five days the high priest Ananias came down with some elders and an advocate named Tertullus. They presented their case against Paul to the governor.

Acts 25:3
asking as a favor against Paul that he summon him to Jerusalem, while planning an ambush to kill him along the road.
  Acts 23:12–23:15 — narrative parallel: Both plots use a requested transfer of Paul as an opportunity to kill him en route. The earlier conspirators approach priests and elders through a proposed council examination; the later request is made to Festus. The plots and stages are not collapsed.
    Acts 23:12 When day came, the Jews formed a conspiracy and bound themselves under a curse, saying they would neither eat nor drink until they had killed Paul.
    Acts 23:13 More than forty had made this conspiracy.
    Acts 23:14 They went to the chief priests and elders and said, “We have bound ourselves under a solemn curse to taste nothing until we have killed Paul.
    Acts 23:15 Now, therefore, you and the council should inform the tribune to bring him down to you, as though you intended to determine his case more accurately. We are ready to kill him before he comes near.”

Acts 25:4
Festus answered that Paul was being kept at Caesarea, and that he himself would soon be going there.
  Acts 25:6 — narrative parallel: Festus's announced return to Caesarea is followed by his actual descent there and the tribunal hearing. The later narrative states a stay of no more than eight or ten days and a hearing the day after his descent, rather than independently measuring soon.
    Acts 25:6 After staying among them no more than eight or ten days, he went down to Caesarea. The next day he sat on the tribunal and ordered Paul to be brought in.

Acts 25:5
He said, “Let the influential men among you go down with me, and if there is anything wrong in the man, let them accuse him.”
  Acts 25:7 — narrative parallel: Festus invites influential accusers to go down with him and accuse Paul if there is wrongdoing; Jerusalem accusers subsequently appear and bring serious accusations. The narrator adds that they cannot prove them, so the invitation does not itself establish guilt.
    Acts 25:7 When he arrived, the Jews who had come down from Jerusalem stood around him, bringing many serious accusations that they could not prove.

Acts 25:6
After staying among them no more than eight or ten days, he went down to Caesarea. The next day he sat on the tribunal and ordered Paul to be brought in.
  Acts 25:17 — narrative parallel: Festus later recounts the same next-day tribunal appearance after the parties gather at Caesarea. This is his retrospective summary of the hearing, not a second hearing or an erasure of the preceding Jerusalem stay.
    Acts 25:17 So when they gathered here, I made no delay, but on the next day sat on the tribunal and ordered the man brought in.

Acts 25:7
When he arrived, the Jews who had come down from Jerusalem stood around him, bringing many serious accusations that they could not prove.
  Acts 24:13 — narrative parallel: The narrator says the new accusations cannot be proved; at the earlier hearing Paul himself had said his accusers could not present proof. The shared lack-of-proof issue has different immediate speakers and hearings.
    Acts 24:13 Nor can they present proof to you of the things they now accuse me of.

Acts 25:8
Paul made his defense: “I have committed no offense against the law of the Jews, against the temple, or against Caesar.”
  Acts 24:12–24:13 — narrative parallel: Paul earlier denies causing disputes or a crowd in the temple, synagogues or city and challenges the evidence; he now denies offenses against Jewish law, temple and Caesar. The earlier defense supports the related temple-and-conduct dispute without reproducing all three new categories.
    Acts 24:12 They did not find me disputing with anyone or gathering a crowd, whether in the temple, in the synagogues, or in the city.
    Acts 24:13 Nor can they present proof to you of the things they now accuse me of.

Acts 25:9
But Festus, wishing to gain favor with the Jews, answered Paul, “Are you willing to go up to Jerusalem and be judged there before me concerning these things?”
  Acts 25:20 — narrative parallel: Festus later repeats his question about a Jerusalem hearing. The narrator here states his wish to gain favor, whereas his own retelling presents uncertainty about investigating the matters. The different accounts of motive are preserved.
    Acts 25:20 Uncertain how to investigate these matters, I asked whether he wished to go to Jerusalem and be judged there concerning them.

Acts 25:10
Paul said, “I am standing before Caesar’s tribunal, where I ought to be judged. I have done no wrong to the Jews, as you yourself know very well.
  Acts 25:11–25:12 — narrative parallel: Paul's assertion that he stands before Caesar's tribunal leads into his explicit appeal and Festus's acceptance of it. This connects the stated forum with the narrated appeal, without deriving a complete Roman jurisdictional rule from the exchange.
    Acts 25:11 If, then, I am doing wrong and have done anything deserving death, I do not refuse to die. But if there is nothing to their accusations against me, no one can hand me over to them as a favor. I appeal to Caesar.”
    Acts 25:12 Then Festus conferred with his council and answered, “You have appealed to Caesar; to Caesar you will go.”

Acts 25:11
If, then, I am doing wrong and have done anything deserving death, I do not refuse to die. But if there is nothing to their accusations against me, no one can hand me over to them as a favor. I appeal to Caesar.”
  Acts 25:21 — narrative parallel: Festus's later summary directly recalls Paul's appeal and the resulting custody pending an imperial decision. Paul's willingness to die is conditional on an actual death-worthy offense, not an admission that he committed one.
    Acts 25:21 But when Paul appealed to be kept for Augustus’s decision, I ordered him held until I could send him to Caesar.”

Acts 25:12
Then Festus conferred with his council and answered, “You have appealed to Caesar; to Caesar you will go.”
  Acts 26:32 — narrative parallel: Agrippa's later statement that Paul could have been released without his appeal looks back to the accepted Caesar appeal here. It is a counterfactual comment after the hearing, not a narrated release or a decision by Caesar.
    Acts 26:32 Agrippa said to Festus, “This man could have been released if he had not appealed to Caesar.”

Acts 25:13
After some days had passed, King Agrippa and Bernice arrived in Caesarea to greet Festus.
  Acts 25:23 — narrative parallel: Agrippa and Bernice arrive to greet Festus and later participate in the hearing. The intervening account says they stayed several days, and the next-day hearing follows the promise in25:22, not an explicit day-after-arrival interval. Their repeated names establish the visitor-to-audience connection without adding a family or marital relationship.
    Acts 25:23 The next day Agrippa and Bernice came with great pomp and entered the audience hall with the tribunes and the city’s prominent men. At Festus’s order, Paul was brought in.

Acts 25:14
While they stayed there for several days, Festus laid Paul’s case before the king, saying, “There is a man left as a prisoner by Felix.
  Acts 24:27 — narrative parallel: Festus describes Paul as a prisoner left by Felix; the preceding narrative explicitly says Felix left Paul bound when Festus succeeded him. This is a direct custody-and-succession recap.
    Acts 24:27 When two years were completed, Felix was succeeded by Porcius Festus. Wishing to gain favor with the Jews, Felix left Paul bound.

Acts 25:15
When I was in Jerusalem, the chief priests and elders of the Jews presented their case against him, asking for his condemnation.
  Acts 25:2–25:3 — narrative parallel: Festus summarizes the Jerusalem leaders' case and request for condemnation. The earlier narration gives their chief-priestly and leading-men delegation, requested Jerusalem transfer and concealed ambush plan. His retelling does not repeat every narrated purpose or use an identical group description.
    Acts 25:2 The chief priests and the leading men of the Jews presented their case against Paul to him and urged him,
    Acts 25:3 asking as a favor against Paul that he summon him to Jerusalem, while planning an ambush to kill him along the road.

Acts 25:16
I answered them that it is not Roman custom to hand anyone over as a favor before the accused has met his accusers face to face and received an opportunity to defend himself concerning the charge.
  Acts 24:9–24:10 — thematic parallel: Festus describes a custom of facing accusers and allowing a defense; the earlier hearing shows accusers asserting their case before the governor signals Paul to answer. This concrete procedural parallel does not independently establish the universal scope of Festus's Roman-custom statement.
    Acts 24:9 The Jews also joined in the attack, asserting that these things were so.
    Acts 24:10 When the governor motioned for him to speak, Paul answered, “Knowing that you have been a judge of this nation for many years, I cheerfully make my defense concerning myself.

Acts 25:17
So when they gathered here, I made no delay, but on the next day sat on the tribunal and ordered the man brought in.
  Acts 25:6 — narrative parallel: Festus's first-person account of sitting on the tribunal and bringing Paul in the next day corresponds to the earlier narrated Caesarean hearing. Its no-delay wording concerns that hearing after the parties gather, not a claim that the Jerusalem visit lasted no days.
    Acts 25:6 After staying among them no more than eight or ten days, he went down to Caesarea. The next day he sat on the tribunal and ordered Paul to be brought in.

Acts 25:18
When his accusers stood up, they brought no charge of the evil things I had expected.
  Acts 23:29 — thematic parallel: Festus says the accusers did not bring the kinds of evil charges he expected; Lysias's earlier letter likewise characterizes the case as law questions rather than a death-or-imprisonment charge. These are distinct officials' assessments, not an unqualified statement that no accusation of any kind existed.
    Acts 23:29 I found him accused over questions of their law, but under no charge deserving death or imprisonment.

Acts 25:19
Instead, they had certain disputes with him concerning their own religion and a certain Jesus who had died, whom Paul asserted to be alive.
  Acts 26:22–26:23 — narrative parallel: Festus attributes to Paul the claim that the dead Jesus is alive; Paul's ensuing defense directly proclaims the Christ's suffering and resurrection. The governor is reporting Paul's position rather than himself confessing that proclamation.
    Acts 26:22 Having therefore received help from God, I stand to this day, testifying to small and great, saying nothing beyond what the prophets and Moses said would happen:
    Acts 26:23 that the Christ would suffer and, as the first to rise from the dead, would proclaim light both to the people and to the Gentiles.”

Acts 25:20
Uncertain how to investigate these matters, I asked whether he wished to go to Jerusalem and be judged there concerning them.
  Acts 25:9 — narrative parallel: The proposed Jerusalem hearing repeats Festus's earlier question. His retelling stresses investigative uncertainty; the narrator earlier specifies his wish for favor. The comparison does not replace one stated motive with the other.
    Acts 25:9 But Festus, wishing to gain favor with the Jews, answered Paul, “Are you willing to go up to Jerusalem and be judged there before me concerning these things?”

Acts 25:21
But when Paul appealed to be kept for Augustus’s decision, I ordered him held until I could send him to Caesar.”
  Acts 25:11–25:12 — narrative parallel: The governor's report of Paul's appeal and intended transfer to Caesar recaps the appeal and accepted destination already narrated. Augustus is kept as the supplied imperial designation; no unnamed emperor is identified by adding outside chronology.
    Acts 25:11 If, then, I am doing wrong and have done anything deserving death, I do not refuse to die. But if there is nothing to their accusations against me, no one can hand me over to them as a favor. I appeal to Caesar.”
    Acts 25:12 Then Festus conferred with his council and answered, “You have appealed to Caesar; to Caesar you will go.”

Acts 25:22
Agrippa said to Festus, “I would like to hear the man myself.” He said, “Tomorrow you will hear him.”
  Acts 25:23 — narrative parallel: Agrippa's wish to hear Paul and Festus's promise of tomorrow are followed by the next-day assembly and Paul's being brought in. The narrative connection concerns the appointed hearing, not an assumption that Agrippa already accepted Paul's message.
    Acts 25:23 The next day Agrippa and Bernice came with great pomp and entered the audience hall with the tribunes and the city’s prominent men. At Festus’s order, Paul was brought in.

Acts 25:23
The next day Agrippa and Bernice came with great pomp and entered the audience hall with the tribunes and the city’s prominent men. At Festus’s order, Paul was brought in.
  Acts 26:1 — narrative parallel: Paul's being brought before the assembled hearing is followed by Agrippa granting him permission to defend himself. This connects the formal presentation with the actual speaking opportunity; the later verse does not independently repeat the pomp or all civic attendees.
    Acts 26:1 Agrippa said to Paul, “You are permitted to speak for yourself.” Then Paul stretched out his hand and made his defense:

Acts 25:24
Festus said, “King Agrippa and all you men present with us, you see this man concerning whom the whole multitude of the Jews petitioned me, both in Jerusalem and here, shouting that he ought not to live any longer.
  Acts 22:22 — narrative parallel: Festus reports petitions and cries that Paul ought not to live; the Jerusalem crowd earlier cried to remove him from the earth because he ought not to live. The concrete death-demand wording corresponds, without proving the identities or total extent of every group Festus describes.
    Acts 22:22 They listened to him until this statement, then raised their voice and said, “Remove such a man from the earth, for he ought not to live.”

Acts 25:25
I found that he had done nothing deserving death, but since he himself appealed to Augustus, I decided to send him.
  Acts 26:31–26:32 — narrative parallel: Festus's no-death-worthy-offense assessment is followed by the hearing party's comment that Paul deserves neither death nor imprisonment. The latter adds imprisonment and discusses the appeal; neither passage narrates the emperor's eventual judgment.
    Acts 26:31 After withdrawing, they spoke to one another, saying, “This man is doing nothing deserving death or imprisonment.”
    Acts 26:32 Agrippa said to Festus, “This man could have been released if he had not appealed to Caesar.”

Acts 25:26
I have nothing certain to write to the sovereign about him. Therefore I have brought him before you all, and especially before you, King Agrippa, so that after the examination I may have something to write.
  Acts 23:25–23:30 — thematic parallel: Festus needs something definite to write when sending Paul onward; the earlier transfer already included Lysias's official letter stating his assessment, law-related accusations and reasons for sending Paul to Felix. The writers, recipients and stages differ, and the letter remains its author's account.
    Acts 23:25 He wrote a letter in this form:
    Acts 23:26 “Claudius Lysias to the most excellent governor Felix: greetings.
    Acts 23:27 This man was seized by the Jews and was about to be killed by them when I came with the troops and rescued him, having learned that he was a Roman.
    Acts 23:28 Wishing to learn the reason they accused him, I brought him down to their council.
    Acts 23:29 I found him accused over questions of their law, but under no charge deserving death or imprisonment.
    Acts 23:30 When a plot against the man was disclosed to me, I immediately sent him to you, also ordering his accusers to state their case against him before you.”

Acts 25:27
For it seems unreasonable to me to send a prisoner without also indicating the charges against him.”
  Acts 23:25–23:30 — thematic parallel: The earlier official transfer letter actually accompanies the prisoner with an account of the case and charges; Festus now explains why sending a prisoner without indicating charges seems unreasonable. This is a concrete documentary-practice parallel, not independent proof of every Roman legal requirement.
    Acts 23:25 He wrote a letter in this form:
    Acts 23:26 “Claudius Lysias to the most excellent governor Felix: greetings.
    Acts 23:27 This man was seized by the Jews and was about to be killed by them when I came with the troops and rescued him, having learned that he was a Roman.
    Acts 23:28 Wishing to learn the reason they accused him, I brought him down to their council.
    Acts 23:29 I found him accused over questions of their law, but under no charge deserving death or imprisonment.
    Acts 23:30 When a plot against the man was disclosed to me, I immediately sent him to you, also ordering his accusers to state their case against him before you.”

Acts 26:1
Agrippa said to Paul, “You are permitted to speak for yourself.” Then Paul stretched out his hand and made his defense:
  Acts 21:40 — narrative parallel: Paul receives permission and uses a hand gesture before a defense in both scenes. Acts 21 describes motioning from the steps before a Hebrew address; Acts 26 says he stretched out his hand before Agrippa. The gestures, venues and stated language details are not made identical.
    Acts 21:40 When he gave permission, Paul stood on the steps and motioned with his hand to the people. When a great silence fell, he addressed them in the Hebrew language, saying:

Acts 26:2
“Concerning everything of which I am accused by the Jews, King Agrippa, I consider myself fortunate to make my defense before you today,
  Acts 24:10 — narrative parallel: Paul positively introduces his defense before Agrippa, as he earlier cheerfully defended himself before Felix. The adjoining explanations appeal to each hearer's relevant experience; they are courteous speech openings in distinct hearings, addressing Agrippa the king and Felix the governor.
    Acts 24:10 When the governor motioned for him to speak, Paul answered, “Knowing that you have been a judge of this nation for many years, I cheerfully make my defense concerning myself.

Acts 26:3
especially since you are familiar with all the Jewish customs and disputes. Therefore I ask you to hear me patiently.
  Acts 24:10 — narrative parallel: Paul bases his request for Agrippa's patient hearing on familiarity with Jewish customs and disputes; the earlier defense appeals to Felix's many years judging the nation. These are Paul's stated assessments of different officials' experience, not identical credentials.
    Acts 24:10 When the governor motioned for him to speak, Paul answered, “Knowing that you have been a judge of this nation for many years, I cheerfully make my defense concerning myself.

Acts 26:4
All the Jews know my manner of life from my youth, lived from the beginning among my nation and in Jerusalem.
  Acts 22:3 — narrative parallel: Paul's earlier account explicitly distinguishes birth in Tarsus from upbringing in Jerusalem under Gamaliel. That clarifies his present Jerusalem-youth background without converting it into a Jerusalem birthplace or independently proving that every Jew knew his history.
    Acts 22:3 “I am a Jewish man, born in Tarsus of Cilicia, but brought up in this city at the feet of Gamaliel, educated in the strict observance of the ancestral law, zealous for God as all of you are today.

Acts 26:5
They have known me from the first, if they are willing to testify, that I lived as a Pharisee according to the strictest party of our religion.
  Philippians 3:5–3:6 — thematic parallel: Paul likewise identifies himself as a Pharisee with respect to law in the epistle and recalls persecuting the Church. The letter adds lineage and other self-descriptions; it does not independently demonstrate the present claim about all earlier witnesses or rank every Jewish party's strictness.
    Philippians 3:5 circumcised on the eighth day, of the people of Israel, of the tribe of Benjamin, a Hebrew from Hebrews; with respect to law, a Pharisee;
    Philippians 3:6 with respect to zeal, persecuting the Church; with respect to righteousness in law, having become blameless.

Acts 26:6
Now I stand on trial because of hope in the promise made by God to our fathers,
  Acts 23:6 — teaching parallel: Paul earlier declares before the council that he is judged concerning the hope and resurrection of the dead. His present trial explanation again centers on hope in God's ancestral promise, developed into the resurrection question immediately afterward.
    Acts 23:6 When Paul perceived that one part consisted of Sadducees and the other of Pharisees, he cried out in the council, “Men, brothers, I am a Pharisee, a son of Pharisees. I am being judged concerning the hope and resurrection of the dead.”

Acts 26:7
which our twelve tribes, earnestly worshiping night and day, hope to attain. It is concerning this hope that I am accused by Jews, O king.
  Acts 24:14–24:15 — teaching parallel: Before Felix Paul likewise connects worship of the fathers' God, law and prophets with hope in resurrection. This supports the shared ancestral-worship-and-hope argument, without independently establishing the exact scope of the twelve-tribes or night-and-day claim.
    Acts 24:14 But I confess this to you: according to the Way, which they call a sect, I worship the God of our fathers, believing everything in accordance with the law and everything written in the prophets,
    Acts 24:15 having hope in God—which these people themselves also await—that there will be a resurrection of both the righteous and the unrighteous.

Acts 26:8
Why do you consider it unbelievable that God raises the dead?
  1 Corinthians 15:12–15:14 — teaching parallel: Both arguments challenge disbelief in resurrection in connection with the risen Christ. Corinthians explicitly reasons from denying resurrection to denying Christ's raising; Acts poses the question to this hearing's audience. The audiences and full argument forms differ.
    1 Corinthians 15:12 Now if Christ is proclaimed as raised from the dead, how can some among you say that there is no resurrection of the dead?
    1 Corinthians 15:13 But if there is no resurrection of the dead, neither has Christ been raised.
    1 Corinthians 15:14 And if Christ has not been raised, then our proclamation is also empty, and your faith is empty too.

Acts 26:9
I myself thought that I must do many things against the name of Jesus the Nazarene.
  1 Timothy 1:13 — thematic parallel: The epistle likewise recalls Paul as formerly a persecutor and violent abuser acting in ignorance and unbelief.
