This corresponds to his former belief that he should oppose Jesus's name, not a declaration that such opposition was right because he once thought it necessary.
    1 Timothy 1:13 though formerly I was a blasphemer and persecutor and violent abuser. But I was shown mercy because I acted in ignorance in unbelief,

Acts 26:10
This I did in Jerusalem: I shut up many of the saints in prisons, having received authority from the chief priests, and when they were being killed, I cast my vote against them.
  Acts 8:1–8:3 — narrative parallel: The earlier narrative records Saul approving Stephen's killing and imprisoning men and women. Paul now recalls imprisonments and his vote against those being killed. The comparison does not turn approval into proof of a formal council seat or identify every unnamed victim.
    Acts 8:1 Saul approved of his killing. That day a great persecution arose against the Church in Jerusalem, and all except the apostles were scattered through the regions of Judea and Samaria.
    Acts 8:2 Devout men buried Stephen and made great lamentation over him.
    Acts 8:3 But Saul was ravaging the Church, entering house after house, dragging away both men and women, and handing them over to prison.

Acts 26:11
I punished them often in all the synagogues and tried to compel them to blaspheme. In my intense rage against them, I pursued them even to foreign cities.
  Acts 22:19–22:20 — narrative parallel: Paul earlier tells the Lord of imprisoning and beating believers in synagogues and approving Stephen's killing. These fit his present persecution recollection; tried to compel blasphemy and pursuit of foreign cities are additional details, not reported successful apostasy in every victim.
    Acts 22:19 I said, ‘Lord, they themselves know that I used to imprison and beat in the synagogues those who believe in Thee.
    Acts 22:20 And when the blood of Stephen, Thy witness, was being poured out, I myself stood by, approving and guarding the garments of those who were killing him.’

Acts 26:12
In this pursuit I was traveling to Damascus with authority and commission from the chief priests,
  Acts 9:1–9:2 — narrative parallel: The earlier narrative describes Saul requesting the high priest's letters for Damascus in order to bind followers of the Way. Paul now recounts the same journey under chief-priestly authority, with the authorization phrased differently.
    Acts 9:1 Saul, still breathing threat and murder against the Lord’s disciples, went to the high priest
    Acts 9:2 and asked him for letters to the synagogues in Damascus, so that if he found any belonging to the Way, both men and women, he might bring them bound to Jerusalem.

Acts 26:13
when at midday, O king, I saw along the road a light from heaven, brighter than the sun, shining around me and those traveling with me.
  Acts 22:6 — narrative parallel: The other defense likewise places the heavenly light on the Damascus journey about noon. Acts 26 additionally says brighter than the sun and surrounding companions; Acts 22's selected wording says around me. The descriptions are not silently expanded into identical accounts.
    Acts 22:6 As I was traveling and drawing near Damascus, about noon, a brilliant light from heaven suddenly flashed around me.

Acts 26:14
When we had all fallen to the ground, I heard a voice speaking to me in the Hebrew language: ‘Saul, Saul, why are you persecuting Me? It is hard for you to kick against goads.’
  Acts 9:4–9:7 — narrative parallel: The repeated Saul, Saul, why persecute Me voice identifies the same encounter. Acts 9 describes Saul falling and his companions standing speechless; Acts 26 says all fell and specifies Hebrew and the goads saying. No unreported falling-and-rising sequence or missing goads clause is supplied to harmonize them.
    Acts 9:4 He fell to the ground and heard a voice saying to him, “Saul, Saul, why are you persecuting Me?”
    Acts 9:5 He said, “Who art Thou, Lord?” He answered, “I am Jesus, whom you are persecuting.
    Acts 9:6 But rise and enter the city, and you will be told what you must do.”
    Acts 9:7 The men traveling with him stood speechless, hearing the voice but seeing no one.

Acts 26:15
I said, ‘Who art Thou, Lord?’ The Lord said, ‘I am Jesus, whom you are persecuting.
  Acts 9:5 — narrative parallel: Both accounts give Saul's Who art Thou, Lord question and Jesus's identification as the One he persecutes. This is a direct retelling of the encounter, not a separate naming ceremony or a change from Saul to Paul at that moment.
    Acts 9:5 He said, “Who art Thou, Lord?” He answered, “I am Jesus, whom you are persecuting.

Acts 26:16
But rise and stand on your feet, for I have appeared to you for this purpose: to appoint you a servant and witness of the things in which you have seen Me and those in which I will appear to you,
  Acts 22:14–22:15 — narrative parallel: Both accounts describe appointment to witness what Paul has seen and heard. Acts 22 places that explanation in Ananias's speech in Damascus; Acts 26 presents the Lord's direct commission with future appearances. The speaker arrangement and additional clauses are retained rather than equated word for word.
    Acts 22:14 He said, ‘The God of our fathers appointed you to know His will, to see the Righteous One, and to hear a voice from His mouth,
    Acts 22:15 for you will be His witness to all people of what you have seen and heard.

Acts 26:17
rescuing you from the people and from the Gentiles, to whom I am sending you,
  Acts 22:21 — narrative parallel: Paul's other defense also quotes the Lord sending him to the Gentiles. Acts 22 locates that saying in the Jerusalem-temple trance after his return, whereas this commission is presented in the Damascus-road account. The shared sending does not force both sayings into one unreported chronology.
    Acts 22:21 He said to me, ‘Go, for I will send you far away to the Gentiles.’”

Acts 26:18
to open their eyes, so that they may turn from darkness to light and from Satan’s authority to God, so that they may receive forgiveness of sins and an allotted share among those sanctified by faith in Me.’
  Isaiah 42:6–42:7 — allusion: The commission to open eyes and turn people from darkness closely recalls the divinely called servant's light-for-nations task of opening blind eyes and releasing those in darkness. Isaiah's physical captivity imagery and Acts's forgiveness-and-faith explanation remain distinct, and Isaiah does not name Paul.
    Isaiah 42:6 “I, the Lord God, called you in righteousness; I will hold your hand and strengthen you. I gave you as a covenant for a people, a light for nations,
    Isaiah 42:7 to open blind people’s eyes, bring those bound out of bonds, and those sitting in darkness out of the prison house.
  Colossians 1:12–1:14 — teaching parallel: Both passages join light, deliverance from hostile authority, forgiveness and a share among holy people. Acts names Satan's authority and sanctification by faith in Jesus; Colossians thanks the Father for rescue from darkness and transfer into His Son's kingdom. The specific agents and formulations are preserved.
    Colossians 1:12 giving thanks to the Father who made you fit for a share of the inheritance of the saints in the light,
    Colossians 1:13 who rescued us from the authority of darkness and transferred us into the kingdom of the Son of His love,
    Colossians 1:14 in whom we have redemption, the forgiveness of sins.

Acts 26:19
Therefore, King Agrippa, I was not disobedient to the heavenly vision,
  Acts 9:20–9:22 — narrative parallel: The earlier narrative shows Saul immediately proclaiming Jesus and demonstrating Him to be the Christ after the encounter. This supplies a concrete initial missionary response corresponding to Paul's claim of obedience, without purporting to list every subsequent action.
    Acts 9:20 Immediately he proclaimed Jesus in the synagogues, saying, “He is the Son of God.”
    Acts 9:21 All who heard him were astonished and said, “Is this not the one who devastated those in Jerusalem who call upon this name? And had he not come here for this purpose, to bring them bound before the chief priests?”
    Acts 9:22 But Saul grew stronger and confounded the Jews living in Damascus by demonstrating that Jesus is the Christ.

Acts 26:20
but proclaimed first to those in Damascus and Jerusalem, throughout all the country of Judea, and to the Gentiles that they should repent and turn to God, doing works worthy of repentance.
  Acts 9:27–9:29 — narrative parallel: Barnabas's report mentions Paul's bold Damascus proclamation, and the following narrative places his bold witness in Jerusalem. These match the first named centers here; the selected earlier passage does not cover the entire Judean and Gentile itinerary.
    Acts 9:27 Barnabas took him and brought him to the apostles. He related to them how Saul had seen the Lord on the road, how He had spoken to him, and how Saul had spoken boldly in Damascus in the name of Jesus.
    Acts 9:28 He was with them, going in and out in Jerusalem and speaking boldly in the name of the Lord.
    Acts 9:29 He spoke and disputed with the Hellenists, but they attempted to kill him.
  Luke 3:8 — teaching parallel: Paul's call to repent, turn to God and do works worthy of repentance parallels John's instruction to produce fruits worthy of repentance. The concrete repentance-and-fitting-conduct connection does not make works and fruits identical English wording or turn this into the same sermon.
    Luke 3:8 Produce fruits worthy of repentance, and do not begin saying among yourselves, ‘We have Abraham as father.’ For I tell you that God can raise up children for Abraham from these stones.

Acts 26:21
For these reasons the Jews seized me in the temple and tried to kill me.
  Acts 21:27–21:31 — narrative parallel: The temple seizure and attempted killing are directly narrated earlier. That account reports accusations about people, law and temple and explicitly calls Trophimus's temple entry a supposition; Paul's present summary relates the attack to his ministry. No alleged Gentile entry is promoted to fact.
    Acts 21:27 When the seven days were about to be completed, the Jews from Asia saw him in the temple, stirred up the whole crowd, and laid hands on him,
    Acts 21:28 crying, “Men of Israel, help! This is the man who teaches everyone everywhere against the people, the law, and this place. Moreover, he has even brought Greeks into the temple and defiled this holy place.”
    Acts 21:29 For they had previously seen Trophimus the Ephesian with him in the city and supposed that Paul had brought him into the temple.
    Acts 21:30 The whole city was stirred, and the people ran together. They seized Paul and dragged him outside the temple, and immediately the doors were shut.
    Acts 21:31 While they were seeking to kill him, a report reached the tribune of the cohort that all Jerusalem was in confusion.

Acts 26:22
Having therefore received help from God, I stand to this day, testifying to small and great, saying nothing beyond what the prophets and Moses said would happen:
  Luke 24:44–24:47 — teaching parallel: Jesus's teaching likewise places His suffering, resurrection and proclamation to the nations within the fulfillment of Moses and the prophets. Paul appeals to that scriptural framework for his testimony; the passages do not independently identify every prophetic text or repeat an identical message.
    Luke 24:44 And He said to them, “These are My words that I spoke to you while I was still with you: everything written about Me in the Law of Moses and the Prophets and Psalms must be fulfilled.”
    Luke 24:45 Then He opened their mind to understand the Scriptures
    Luke 24:46 and said to them, “Thus it is written that the Christ should suffer and rise from the dead on the third day,
    Luke 24:47 and that repentance for the forgiveness of sins should be proclaimed in His name to all the nations—beginning from Jerusalem.

Acts 26:23
that the Christ would suffer and, as the first to rise from the dead, would proclaim light both to the people and to the Gentiles.”
  1 Corinthians 15:20–15:23 — teaching parallel: Christ's priority in resurrection corresponds to the epistle's raised-Christ firstfruits language and its ordered resurrection argument. Acts also speaks of proclaiming light; Corinthians develops Adam, Christ and those belonging to Him rather than narrating the present hearing.
    1 Corinthians 15:20 But as it is, Christ has been raised from the dead, the firstfruits of those who have fallen asleep.
    1 Corinthians 15:21 For since death came through a human being, resurrection of the dead also comes through a human being.
    1 Corinthians 15:22 For just as in Adam all die, so also in Christ all will be made alive.
    1 Corinthians 15:23 But each in his own order: Christ the firstfruits, then those who belong to Christ at His coming,
  Isaiah 49:6 — allusion: The proclamation of light to the people and Gentiles corresponds to the servant's task concerning Jacob's tribes, Israel's dispersion and light for nations. Acts identifies the Christ's mission; Isaiah's fuller promise does not name Paul or provide this hearing's resurrection wording.
    Isaiah 49:6 and He said to me, “It is a great thing for you to be called My servant, to establish Jacob’s tribes and bring back Israel’s dispersion. Behold, I have appointed you as a covenant for a race, a light for nations, so that you may bring salvation to the earth’s extremity.”

Acts 26:24
As he was making this defense, Festus said with a loud voice, “You are mad, Paul! Your great learning is turning you to madness.”
  Acts 26:25 — narrative parallel: Festus's accusation of madness is directly answered by Paul's denial and claim to truth and sound judgment. This is an explicit report-and-rebuttal pair, not the narrator's medical diagnosis of Paul.
    Acts 26:25 Paul said, “I am not mad, most excellent Festus, but I speak words of truth and sound judgment.

Acts 26:25
Paul said, “I am not mad, most excellent Festus, but I speak words of truth and sound judgment.
  Acts 26:24 — narrative parallel: Paul's not-mad response specifically negates Festus's preceding characterization. The contrast records the two speakers' claims about his defense and does not independently settle a clinical question.
    Acts 26:24 As he was making this defense, Festus said with a loud voice, “You are mad, Paul! Your great learning is turning you to madness.”

Acts 26:26
For the king knows about these things, and I speak freely to him. I am persuaded that none of these things has escaped his notice, for this has not been done in a corner.
  Luke 24:18–24:20 — thematic parallel: Cleopas likewise treats recent events concerning Jesus as widely knowable when questioning the unrecognized visitor. Paul appeals to Agrippa's knowledge and the events' public character. The different encounters do not prove that every individual knew them or identify Cleopas with another similarly named person.
    Luke 24:18 One named Cleopas answered Him, “Art Thou the only visitor in Jerusalem who does not know the things that have happened there in these days?”
    Luke 24:19 And He said to them, “What things?” They said to Him, “The things concerning Jesus the Nazarene, who was a prophet mighty in deed and word before God and all the people,
    Luke 24:20 and how our chief priests and rulers handed Him over to a judgment of death and crucified Him.

Acts 26:27
King Agrippa, do you believe the prophets? I know that you believe.”
  Acts 26:22–26:23 — thematic parallel: The question about Agrippa believing the prophets follows Paul's explicit claim that Moses and the prophets foretold the Christ's suffering and resurrection message. This connects the appeal to its stated scriptural premise; Paul's I know claim is not a recorded Christian profession by the king.
    Acts 26:22 Having therefore received help from God, I stand to this day, testifying to small and great, saying nothing beyond what the prophets and Moses said would happen:
    Acts 26:23 that the Christ would suffer and, as the first to rise from the dead, would proclaim light both to the people and to the Gentiles.”

Acts 26:28
Agrippa said to Paul, “With so little, you are trying to persuade me to become a Christian.”
  Acts 11:26 — thematic parallel: The Christian designation in Agrippa's response corresponds to the narrative's earlier account of disciples first being called Christians at Antioch. His supplied with-so-little wording is not rewritten as a statement that he almost converted or actually became Christian.
    Acts 11:26 and when he found him, he brought him to Antioch. For a whole year they gathered with the Church and taught a considerable crowd. It was in Antioch that the disciples were first called Christians.

Acts 26:29
Paul said, “Whether with little or with much, I would pray to God that not only you but also all who hear me today might become as I am, except for these bonds.”
  Acts 26:28 — narrative parallel: Paul's whether with little or much directly takes up Agrippa's with so little response, then extends the Christian wish from the king to all hearers while excepting his bonds. This is a specific rhetorical reply and prayer, not a report that the hearers converted.
    Acts 26:28 Agrippa said to Paul, “With so little, you are trying to persuade me to become a Christian.”

Acts 26:30
The king rose, along with the governor, Bernice, and those seated with them.
  Acts 25:23 — narrative parallel: The king, governor, Bernice and fellow hearers rise at the close of the hearing whose assembled scene earlier names Agrippa, Bernice and the civic audience. The repeated party frames this same event without independently repeating every attendee's rank or implying an acquittal at their rising.
    Acts 25:23 The next day Agrippa and Bernice came with great pomp and entered the audience hall with the tribunes and the city’s prominent men. At Festus’s order, Paul was brought in.

Acts 26:31
After withdrawing, they spoke to one another, saying, “This man is doing nothing deserving death or imprisonment.”
  Acts 25:25 — narrative parallel: After withdrawing, the hearers find nothing deserving death or imprisonment; Festus had earlier found nothing deserving death. The later private conversation adds imprisonment to the assessment and is not itself a narrated release order.
    Acts 25:25 I found that he had done nothing deserving death, but since he himself appealed to Augustus, I decided to send him.

Acts 26:32
Agrippa said to Festus, “This man could have been released if he had not appealed to Caesar.”
  Acts 25:11–25:12 — narrative parallel: Agrippa's conditional statement refers directly to Paul's accepted Caesar appeal. The earlier exchange explains that reference, but neither text records withdrawal of the appeal, actual release or the emperor's final decision.
    Acts 25:11 If, then, I am doing wrong and have done anything deserving death, I do not refuse to die. But if there is nothing to their accusations against me, no one can hand me over to them as a favor. I appeal to Caesar.”
    Acts 25:12 Then Festus conferred with his council and answered, “You have appealed to Caesar; to Caesar you will go.”

Acts 27:1
When it was decided that we should sail to Italy, they handed Paul and some other prisoners over to a centurion named Julius, of the Augustan cohort.
  Acts 25:21 — narrative parallel: Paul's handover for the Italy-bound voyage follows the governor's stated intention to keep him until sending him to Caesar. This explains Paul's continuing transfer in custody, without assigning the same appeal history to the other prisoners or identifying the Augustan cohort with another named cohort.
    Acts 25:21 But when Paul appealed to be kept for Augustus’s decision, I ordered him held until I could send him to Caesar.”

Acts 27:2
We boarded a ship from Adramyttium that was about to sail to the ports along Asia, and set sail. Aristarchus, a Macedonian from Thessalonica, was with us.
  Acts 20:4 — narrative parallel: Aristarchus of Thessalonica appears earlier among Paul's traveling companions and now accompanies this voyage. The shared name, city and companion role establish the narrative link; the earlier list does not identify this particular Adramyttian vessel or call Aristarchus one of Julius's prisoners.
    Acts 20:4 He was accompanied by Sopater son of Pyrrhus from Berea; Aristarchus and Secundus from Thessalonica; Gaius from Derbe, and Timothy; and Tychicus and Trophimus from Asia.

Acts 27:3
The next day we put in at Sidon. Julius treated Paul kindly and allowed him to go to his friends and receive care.
  Acts 24:23 — narrative parallel: Julius allows Paul's friends to provide care; Felix's earlier custody order likewise permits Paul's own people to attend him. Both combine custody with access to help, in distinct settings and without identifying the earlier unnamed centurion as Julius.
    Acts 24:23 He ordered the centurion to keep Paul in custody but grant him some freedom, and not prevent any of his own people from attending to him.

Acts 27:4
From there we set sail and sailed under the shelter of Cyprus because the winds were contrary.
  Acts 27:7 — narrative parallel: Contrary winds lead to sailing under Cyprus's shelter; later adverse wind leads to shelter under Crete. This is a repeated wind-and-island navigation response on different legs and vessels, not an identical course or an identification of Cyprus with Crete.
    Acts 27:7 We sailed slowly for many days and with difficulty came off Cnidus. Since the wind would not permit us to proceed, we sailed under the shelter of Crete off Salmone.

Acts 27:6
There the centurion found an Alexandrian ship sailing to Italy and put us aboard it.
  Acts 28:11 — narrative parallel: An Alexandrian ship carries the Rome-bound party after the Myra change; after the wreck they sail again in an Alexandrian ship that had wintered at the island. The origin recurs, while the vessels and embarkations are distinct; the wrecked ship is not said to have been repaired.
    Acts 28:11 After three months we sailed in an Alexandrian ship that had wintered at the island, with the Dioscuri as its emblem.

Acts 27:7
We sailed slowly for many days and with difficulty came off Cnidus. Since the wind would not permit us to proceed, we sailed under the shelter of Crete off Salmone.
  Acts 27:4 — narrative parallel: The wind's preventing progress and the use of Crete's shelter repeat the earlier response to contrary winds under Cyprus. The later leg has its own places and different ship after the Myra transfer.
    Acts 27:4 From there we set sail and sailed under the shelter of Cyprus because the winds were contrary.

Acts 27:8
Coasting along it with difficulty, we came to a place called Fair Havens, near which was the city of Lasea.
  Acts 27:12 — narrative parallel: The later judgment that the harbor is unsuitable for wintering refers to the Fair Havens arrival described here. This identifies the decision's starting harbor and distinguishes it from hoped-for Phoenix, without claiming the party actually reached Phoenix.
    Acts 27:12 Since the harbor was unsuitable for wintering, the majority decided to sail from there, hoping somehow to reach Phoenix, a harbor of Crete facing southwest and northwest, and winter there.

Acts 27:9
Much time had passed, and sailing was already dangerous because even the Fast had now gone by. Paul advised them,
  Acts 27:12 — narrative parallel: The stated lateness and dangerous sailing season bear directly on the subsequent choice of where to winter. The link preserves the supplied Fast marker without assigning an unprinted modern date, treating the voyage's food deprivation as the same observance, or identifying the Fast from this comparison alone.
    Acts 27:12 Since the harbor was unsuitable for wintering, the majority decided to sail from there, hoping somehow to reach Phoenix, a harbor of Crete facing southwest and northwest, and winter there.

Acts 27:10
saying, “Men, I perceive that the voyage will bring damage and great loss, not only of the cargo and the ship but also of our lives.”
  Acts 27:21–27:24 — narrative parallel: Paul later recalls his disregarded advice and then gives the new assurance based on an angel's message. The initial I perceive includes danger to lives; the later message promises survival with ship loss. The source and changing content of the two statements are retained rather than made identical divine predictions.
    Acts 27:21 After a long time without food, Paul stood among them and said, “Men, you ought to have listened to me and not sailed from Crete, and so avoided this damage and loss.
    Acts 27:22 Now I urge you to take courage, for there will be no loss of life among you, but only of the ship.
    Acts 27:23 For this night an angel of the God to whom I belong and whom I worship stood beside me,
    Acts 27:24 saying, ‘Do not fear, Paul. You must stand before Caesar, and look, God has granted you all who sail with you.’

Acts 27:11
But the centurion was more persuaded by the pilot and the shipowner than by what Paul said.
  Acts 27:21 — narrative parallel: The centurion's preference for pilot and owner over Paul gives concrete background to Paul's later you ought to have listened to me. The later address concerns the wider group and the resulting damage, not a statement that every pilot's advice is always wrong.
    Acts 27:21 After a long time without food, Paul stood among them and said, “Men, you ought to have listened to me and not sailed from Crete, and so avoided this damage and loss.

Acts 27:12
Since the harbor was unsuitable for wintering, the majority decided to sail from there, hoping somehow to reach Phoenix, a harbor of Crete facing southwest and northwest, and winter there.
  Acts 27:13 — narrative parallel: The majority's hope of sailing to Phoenix for winter explains the purpose they think the gentle south wind has secured. Weighing anchor follows that expectation; the narrative does not report arrival at Phoenix before the hurricane intervenes.
    Acts 27:13 When a south wind blew gently, they supposed they had gained their purpose, weighed anchor, and sailed close along Crete.

Acts 27:13
When a south wind blew gently, they supposed they had gained their purpose, weighed anchor, and sailed close along Crete.
  Acts 27:14–27:15 — narrative parallel: The favorable-wind assumption is immediately contrasted with the hurricane and the ship's inability to face it. The point is the difference between the travelers' expectation and ensuing conditions, not a claim that their purpose had objectively been completed.
    Acts 27:14 But not long afterward a hurricane wind called Euraquilo rushed down from the island.
    Acts 27:15 The ship was seized and could not face the wind, so we gave way and were driven along.

Acts 27:14
But not long afterward a hurricane wind called Euraquilo rushed down from the island.
  Jonah 1:4 — wind-and-ship-danger comparison: Acts names the hurricane wind Euraquilo rushing from the island; Jonah describes a great sea surge after the Lord stirs up wind, threatening the ship. The maritime danger corresponds, while the named wind and stated divine agency differ. Paul is not assigned Jonah's flight or made the narrated cause of this storm.
    Jonah 1:4 The Lord stirred up a wind upon the sea, and there was a great surge on the sea; the ship was in danger of being shattered.

Acts 27:15
The ship was seized and could not face the wind, so we gave way and were driven along.
  Jonah 1:13 — failed navigation in a storm comparison: Acts says the ship cannot face the wind and gives way to being driven; Jonah says the men strain to return to land but cannot as the sea grows fiercer. Both depict failed resistance to storm conditions, with different attempted maneuvers and no identical course or crew inferred.
    Jonah 1:13 But the men strained to return to land and could not, for the sea kept rising more fiercely against them.

Acts 27:16
Running under the shelter of a small island called Cauda, we were barely able to secure the boat.
  Acts 27:30–27:32 — narrative parallel: The auxiliary boat barely secured here is later lowered in the sailors' attempted escape and then cut loose by the soldiers. This traces the specific boat's role across the episode, keeping it distinct from the main ship and from the sailors' claimed bow-anchor operation.
    Acts 27:30 The sailors sought to flee from the ship and lowered the boat into the sea, pretending that they were about to lay out anchors from the bow.
    Acts 27:31 Paul said to the centurion and the soldiers, “Unless these men remain in the ship, you cannot be saved.”
    Acts 27:32 Then the soldiers cut the boat’s ropes and let it fall away.

Acts 27:17
After hoisting it aboard, they used supports to hold the ship together from beneath. Fearing they would run aground on the Syrtis, they lowered the gear and were driven along in this way.
  Acts 27:30 — narrative parallel: The boat hoisted aboard here is later explicitly lowered toward the sea in the escape attempt. That concrete reversed handling concerns the auxiliary boat; it does not identify the lowered gear as a particular modern device or independently reproduce the undergirding and Syrtis details.
    Acts 27:30 The sailors sought to flee from the ship and lowered the boat into the sea, pretending that they were about to lay out anchors from the bow.

Acts 27:18
As we were violently battered by the storm, the next day they began throwing cargo overboard.
  Jonah 1:5 — storm-time unloading comparison: Acts reports cargo thrown overboard after violent storm battering; Jonah reports equipment thrown into the sea to lighten the load. The shared unloading response has different stated objects. Jonah's explicit lightening purpose and Acts' next-day timing remain in their respective accounts, rather than identifying the cargo as a particular commodity.
    Jonah 1:5 The sailors were afraid, and each was crying out to their god. They threw the equipment in the ship into the sea, to lighten their load. But Jonah had gone down into the hollow of the ship, and was sleeping and snoring.

Acts 27:19
On the third day they threw the ship’s tackle overboard with their own hands.
  Jonah 1:5 — ship-equipment jettison comparison: Acts specifically names the ship's tackle being thrown overboard by their own hands on the third day; Jonah names equipment thrown into the sea to lighten the load. The equipment disposal corresponds without making every item identical or adding Acts' day count and hand-action wording to Jonah.
    Jonah 1:5 The sailors were afraid, and each was crying out to their god. They threw the equipment in the ship into the sea, to lighten their load. But Jonah had gone down into the hollow of the ship, and was sleeping and snoring.

Acts 27:20
When neither sun nor stars appeared for many days, and a fierce storm kept battering us, all remaining hope of our being saved was taken away.
  Psalms 106:26–106:28 — mariners' distress comparison: Acts describes many days without sun or stars and the loss of remaining hope of rescue. The psalm describes storm-tossed mariners' melting courage, swallowed-up wisdom and cry to the Lord. These depict maritime desperation; the psalm's prayer and narrated rescue are not inserted into this particular Acts verse or its weather details.
    Psalms 106:26 They rise as far as the heavens and descend as far as the deeps; their soul melted in evils.
    Psalms 106:27 They were troubled and shaken like a drunken man, and all their wisdom was swallowed up.
    Psalms 106:28 They cried to the Lord when they were afflicted, and He brought them out of their distresses,

Acts 27:21
After a long time without food, Paul stood among them and said, “Men, you ought to have listened to me and not sailed from Crete, and so avoided this damage and loss.
  Acts 27:9–27:11 — narrative parallel: Paul's retrospective you ought to have listened directly resumes his earlier voyage warning and the decision to trust others. The damage-and-loss reminder does not repeat every earlier prediction, while the next assurance has a newly reported angelic basis.
    Acts 27:9 Much time had passed, and sailing was already dangerous because even the Fast had now gone by. Paul advised them,
    Acts 27:10 saying, “Men, I perceive that the voyage will bring damage and great loss, not only of the cargo and the ship but also of our lives.”
    Acts 27:11 But the centurion was more persuaded by the pilot and the shipowner than by what Paul said.

Acts 27:22
Now I urge you to take courage, for there will be no loss of life among you, but only of the ship.
  Acts 27:41–27:44 — narrative parallel: The promised ship loss with no loss of life is matched by the ship breaking and everyone reaching land. The intervening threats and the centurion's actions remain part of the narrated preservation; the outcome is bodily survival, not a statement that every passenger converted.
    Acts 27:41 But they struck a place between two seas and ran the ship aground. The bow stuck fast and remained immovable, while the stern was breaking apart under the force of the waves.
    Acts 27:42 The soldiers planned to kill the prisoners so that none might swim away and escape.
    Acts 27:43 But the centurion, wishing to save Paul, prevented them from carrying out their plan. He ordered those able to swim to jump overboard first and make for land,
    Acts 27:44 and the rest to follow, some on planks and some on pieces from the ship. In this way everyone reached land safely.

Acts 27:23
For this night an angel of the God to whom I belong and whom I worship stood beside me,
  Acts 23:11 — narrative parallel: Both accounts give Paul a night-time assurance with a figure standing beside him and a future Roman witness in view. Acts 23 names the Lord; here Paul reports an angel of the God he serves. The messenger identities are not merged or supplied with the same unreported physical details.
    Acts 23:11 The following night the Lord stood beside him and said, “Take courage, for as you have borne solemn witness about Me in Jerusalem, so you must also testify in Rome.”

Acts 27:24
saying, ‘Do not fear, Paul. You must stand before Caesar, and look, God has granted you all who sail with you.’
  Acts 23:11 — narrative parallel: The earlier Lord's promise that Paul must testify in Rome corresponds to the angel's assurance that he must stand before Caesar. The later message also grants all his fellow voyagers; the earlier saying does not mention that whole passenger group.
    Acts 23:11 The following night the Lord stood beside him and said, “Take courage, for as you have borne solemn witness about Me in Jerusalem, so you must also testify in Rome.”

Acts 27:25
Therefore take courage, men, for I believe God that it will be just as I have been told.
  Acts 27:23–27:24 — narrative parallel: Paul's confidence that events will match what he has been told points directly to the preceding reported angelic message. He expresses belief in God as its source, not an identification of the angel as God or a recorded profession of faith by every hearer.
    Acts 27:23 For this night an angel of the God to whom I belong and whom I worship stood beside me,
    Acts 27:24 saying, ‘Do not fear, Paul. You must stand before Caesar, and look, God has granted you all who sail with you.’

Acts 27:26
But we must run aground on some island.”
  Acts 28:1 — narrative parallel: The unnamed island on which they must run aground is subsequently identified as Melite after reaching safety. This is the narrative's prediction-to-identification link, without replacing its printed island name with an external modern identification.
    Acts 28:1 After reaching safety, we learned that the island was called Melite.

Acts 27:27
When the fourteenth night came, as we were being driven across the Adria, about midnight the sailors suspected that some land was approaching them.
  Acts 27:33 — narrative parallel: The fourteenth-night drift marker corresponds to Paul's fourteenth-day food appeal before daybreak. This is an internal elapsed-time recurrence within the same voyage, not an independently verified calendar or a calculation of a modern date and exact night boundaries.
    Acts 27:33 As day was about to come, Paul urged everyone to take food, saying, “Today is the fourteenth day you have continued waiting without food, taking nothing.

Acts 27:29
Fearing we might run aground somewhere on rocky places, they dropped four anchors from the stern and prayed for day to come.
  Acts 27:40 — narrative parallel: The four stern anchors dropped during the night are the anchors later abandoned when they make for the beach. The repeated concrete object links anchoring and departure actions, without transferring the later steering-oar and foresail operations to the night scene.
    Acts 27:40 They cast off the anchors and left them in the sea, while also loosening the lashings of the steering oars. They raised the foresail to the wind and headed for the beach.

Acts 27:30
The sailors sought to flee from the ship and lowered the boat into the sea, pretending that they were about to lay out anchors from the bow.
  Acts 27:31–27:32 — narrative parallel: The sailors' attempted escape under a bow-anchor pretext is answered by Paul's stay-on-board warning and the soldiers cutting the boat loose. This connects an explicitly stated intention to the response that prevents it; laying actual bow anchors is not asserted.
    Acts 27:31 Paul said to the centurion and the soldiers, “Unless these men remain in the ship, you cannot be saved.”
    Acts 27:32 Then the soldiers cut the boat’s ropes and let it fall away.

Acts 27:31
Paul said to the centurion and the soldiers, “Unless these men remain in the ship, you cannot be saved.”
  Acts 27:30 — narrative parallel: The preceding sailors seeking to flee identify these men in Paul's condition that they must remain. The warning is addressed to the centurion and soldiers and concerns this voyage's rescue; no unprinted universal seafaring rule or reconstructed divine speech is supplied.
    Acts 27:30 The sailors sought to flee from the ship and lowered the boat into the sea, pretending that they were about to lay out anchors from the bow.

Acts 27:32
Then the soldiers cut the boat’s ropes and let it fall away.
  Acts 27:30 — narrative parallel: Cutting the boat's ropes responds to the sailors' lowering it to escape. The boat and purpose are concrete antecedents, while letting it fall away is not rewritten as an explicit sinking or destruction of the main ship.
    Acts 27:30 The sailors sought to flee from the ship and lowered the boat into the sea, pretending that they were about to lay out anchors from the bow.

Acts 27:33
As day was about to come, Paul urged everyone to take food, saying, “Today is the fourteenth day you have continued waiting without food, taking nothing.
  Acts 27:21 — narrative parallel: Paul's fourteenth-day statement about taking no food elaborates the narrator's earlier long time without food. The earlier wording does not independently quantify the duration or every person's intake, and this voyage deprivation is not automatically the named Fast of 27:9.
    Acts 27:21 After a long time without food, Paul stood among them and said, “Men, you ought to have listened to me and not sailed from Crete, and so avoided this damage and loss.

Acts 27:34
Therefore I urge you to take food, for this is for your safety. Not a hair from the head of any of you will be lost.”
  Luke 21:18 — thematic parallel: The no-hair-lost assurance closely matches Jesus's not-a-hair-perish wording. Acts gives a specific shipwreck-survival assurance; Luke's persecution discourse also mentions some being put to death. The shared preservation idiom is not turned into identical physical outcomes in both contexts.
    Luke 21:18 Yet certainly not a hair from your head will perish.

Acts 27:35
After saying these things, he took bread, gave thanks to God before everyone, broke it, and began to eat.
  Luke 24:30 — thematic parallel: Both accounts describe taking and breaking bread with blessing or thanksgiving in company. At Emmaus Jesus gives it to others; here Paul thanks God and begins to eat before the voyagers. The shared meal actions do not by themselves identify all passengers as disciples or the meal as a stated Eucharistic rite.
    Luke 24:30 And it happened that as He reclined at table with them, He took the bread, blessed it, broke it, and gave it to them.

Acts 27:36
They all took courage and themselves took food.
  Acts 27:33–27:35 — narrative parallel: Everyone's taking courage and food is the explicit response to Paul's food appeal, reassurance and example. The narrator describes eating and renewed courage, not a statement that all 276 made Paul's own I believe God profession.
    Acts 27:33 As day was about to come, Paul urged everyone to take food, saying, “Today is the fourteenth day you have continued waiting without food, taking nothing.
    Acts 27:34 Therefore I urge you to take food, for this is for your safety. Not a hair from the head of any of you will be lost.”
    Acts 27:35 After saying these things, he took bread, gave thanks to God before everyone, broke it, and began to eat.

Acts 27:37
There were two hundred and seventy-six persons in all aboard the ship.
  Acts 27:44 — narrative parallel: The numbered whole group aboard supplies the scope of everyone later reaching land safely. This is an internal headcount-to-outcome connection, not a second independent count of 276 or an invented list of names.
    Acts 27:44 and the rest to follow, some on planks and some on pieces from the ship. In this way everyone reached land safely.

Acts 27:38
When they had eaten enough, they lightened the ship by throwing the grain into the sea.
  Jonah 1:5 — lightening a ship comparison: Acts explicitly says the ship was lightened by throwing grain into the sea after the people had eaten. Jonah describes equipment thrown into the sea to lighten the load. The common purpose coexists with different objects; only Acts here places disposal after eating. Jonah's equipment is not called grain, and the disposals remain separate voyages.
    Jonah 1:5 The sailors were afraid, and each was crying out to their god. They threw the equipment in the ship into the sea, to lighten their load. But Jonah had gone down into the hollow of the ship, and was sleeping and snoring.

Acts 27:39
When day came, they did not recognize the land, but noticed a bay with a beach and planned, if they could, to drive the ship onto it.
  Acts 28:1 — narrative parallel: The voyagers do not yet recognize the land when they notice the bay and beach; after reaching safety they learn its name, Melite. The later identification resolves the knowledge gap without independently naming the bay or specifying an exact modern landing site.
    Acts 28:1 After reaching safety, we learned that the island was called Melite.

Acts 27:40
They cast off the anchors and left them in the sea, while also loosening the lashings of the steering oars. They raised the foresail to the wind and headed for the beach.
  Acts 27:29 — narrative parallel: The anchors abandoned in preparation for the beach run correspond to the stern anchors previously dropped in fear of rocks. The comparison preserves the separate boat-rope cutting and does not equate the anchors with the steering-oar lashings.
    Acts 27:29 Fearing we might run aground somewhere on rocky places, they dropped four anchors from the stern and prayed for day to come.

Acts 27:41
But they struck a place between two seas and ran the ship aground. The bow stuck fast and remained immovable, while the stern was breaking apart under the force of the waves.
  Acts 27:22–27:26 — narrative parallel: The grounding and breaking ship correspond to the earlier assurance of ship loss and the prediction of grounding on an island. The bow, stern and place-between-two-seas details belong to the narrated event, not an unprinted geographical identification or a claim that lives were lost with the ship.
    Acts 27:22 Now I urge you to take courage, for there will be no loss of life among you, but only of the ship.
    Acts 27:23 For this night an angel of the God to whom I belong and whom I worship stood beside me,
    Acts 27:24 saying, ‘Do not fear, Paul. You must stand before Caesar, and look, God has granted you all who sail with you.’
    Acts 27:25 Therefore take courage, men, for I believe God that it will be just as I have been told.
    Acts 27:26 But we must run aground on some island.”

Acts 27:42
The soldiers planned to kill the prisoners so that none might swim away and escape.
  Acts 16:27–16:28 — narrative parallel: Fear of prisoner escape produces a lethal intention in both episodes, and it is prevented. Here soldiers plan to kill prisoners; earlier the jailer prepares to kill himself until Paul says everyone remains. The intended victims and actual escape circumstances differ, and no automatic Roman execution law is inferred.
    Acts 16:27 The jailer awoke and saw the prison doors open. He drew his sword and was about to kill himself, supposing the prisoners had escaped.
    Acts 16:28 But Paul cried with a loud voice, “Do yourself no harm, for we are all here.”

Acts 27:43
But the centurion, wishing to save Paul, prevented them from carrying out their plan. He ordered those able to swim to jump overboard first and make for land,
  Acts 27:3 — narrative parallel: The centurion's wish to save Paul continues the earlier account of Julius treating him kindly and allowing care. His stated motive here is Paul's preservation, with the prisoners benefiting from the prevented plan; neither action establishes his conversion or a general release from custody.
    Acts 27:3 The next day we put in at Sidon. Julius treated Paul kindly and allowed him to go to his friends and receive care.

Acts 27:44
and the rest to follow, some on planks and some on pieces from the ship. In this way everyone reached land safely.
  Acts 27:22–27:24 — narrative parallel: Everyone arriving safely realizes the assurance of no loss of life and God's granting Paul all fellow voyagers. The account retains different means of reaching shore and distinguishes rescue from the wreck from a report of everyone's spiritual conversion.
    Acts 27:22 Now I urge you to take courage, for there will be no loss of life among you, but only of the ship.
    Acts 27:23 For this night an angel of the God to whom I belong and whom I worship stood beside me,
    Acts 27:24 saying, ‘Do not fear, Paul. You must stand before Caesar, and look, God has granted you all who sail with you.’

Acts 28:1
After reaching safety, we learned that the island was called Melite.
  Acts 27:26 — narrative parallel: Learning that the island is Melite identifies the previously unnamed island of Paul's grounding prediction. The printed name and the timing of learning it after safety are preserved rather than supplied in the earlier prediction.
    Acts 27:26 But we must run aground on some island.”

Acts 28:2
The people who did not speak Greek showed us extraordinary kindness. They kindled a fire and welcomed us all because of the rain that had begun and because of the cold.
  James 2:15–2:16 — teaching parallel: The islanders actually provide fire and welcome for cold, rain-affected strangers. This gives a concrete contrast to merely saying be warmed while supplying no bodily help in James. The groups and stated needs differ; Acts does not identify the hosts as the epistle's Christian addressees.
    James 2:15 If a brother or sister is without clothing and lacks daily food,
    James 2:16 and one of you says to them, “Go in peace; be warmed and filled,” but you do not give them what the body needs, what good is it?

Acts 28:3
Paul gathered a bundle of brushwood and put it on the fire. A viper came out because of the heat and fastened onto his hand.
  Acts 28:5 — narrative parallel: The creature subsequently shaken into the fire is the viper that fastened onto Paul's hand while he handled brushwood. The response and unharmed outcome concern this same incident, not deliberate snake-handling, a named modern species or a separately identified medical mechanism.
    Acts 28:5 But he shook the creature into the fire and suffered no harm.

Acts 28:4
When the people who did not speak Greek saw the creature hanging from his hand, they said to one another, “This man is certainly a murderer. Although he escaped from the sea, Justice has not allowed him to live.”
  Luke 13:1–13:5 — thematic parallel: The islanders infer a murderer and fatal Justice from Paul's apparent misfortune; Jesus's comparison of the Galilean deaths and Siloam victims rejects inferring greater sinners or debtors from their suffering. These are different incidents and speaker judgments, not the narrator endorsing the islanders' inference or a claim about every earlier act in Paul's life.
    Luke 13:1 At that very time some people were present who told Him about the Galileans whose blood Pilate had mixed with their sacrifices.
    Luke 13:2 And He answered them, “Do you think these Galileans were greater sinners than all the other Galileans because they suffered these things?
    Luke 13:3 No, I tell you; but unless you repent, you will all likewise perish.
    Luke 13:4 Or those eighteen on whom the tower in Siloam fell and killed them—do you think they were greater debtors than all the people living in Jerusalem?
    Luke 13:5 No, I tell you; but unless you repent, you will all perish in the same way.”

Acts 28:5
But he shook the creature into the fire and suffered no harm.
  Luke 10:19 — thematic parallel: Paul's suffering no harm from the creature parallels Jesus's assurance involving snakes and nothing harming His messengers. Luke speaks of treading upon snakes and enemy power; Acts describes an unexpected attachment while gathering brushwood, not the identical action or a claim that Paul was in the original addressed group.
    Luke 10:19 Behold, I have given you authority to tread upon snakes and scorpions, and over all the enemy’s power, and nothing will ever harm you.

Acts 28:6
They expected him to swell up or suddenly fall down dead. After waiting a long time and seeing nothing unusual happen to him, they changed their minds and said that he was a god.
  Acts 14:11–14:15 — narrative parallel: Onlookers interpret a sign involving Paul as divinity in both episodes. At Lystra they name Barnabas and Paul as gods and the apostles explicitly protest their humanity; here the islanders change their assessment and call Paul a god, without a narrated repetition of that earlier protest or sacrifice.
    Acts 14:11 When the crowds saw what Paul had done, they raised their voice, saying in Lycaonian, “The gods have come down to us in human likeness.”
    Acts 14:12 They called Barnabas Zeus and Paul Hermes, because he was the leading speaker.
    Acts 14:13 The priest of Zeus, whose shrine was before the city, brought bulls and garlands to the gates and wanted to offer sacrifice with the crowds.
    Acts 14:14 When the apostles Barnabas and Paul heard this, they tore their garments and rushed out into the crowd, crying out
    Acts 14:15 and saying, “Men, why are you doing these things? We too are human beings, of the same nature as you. We proclaim good news to you: turn from these worthless things to the living God, who made heaven and earth and the sea and everything in them.

Acts 28:7
Near that place were lands belonging to the island’s chief man, named Publius. He welcomed us and hospitably gave us lodging for three days.
  Hebrews 13:2 — teaching parallel: Publius's welcome and lodging exemplify the practical hospitality commended to strangers in Hebrews. The comparison does not identify any guest as an angel, determine how many of the276 lodged there or supply an unreported conversion of the host.
    Hebrews 13:2 Do not forget hospitality, for through it some unknowingly entertained angels.

Acts 28:8
Publius’s father lay sick with fevers and dysentery. Paul went in to him, prayed, laid his hands upon him, and healed him.
  Luke 4:38–4:40 — narrative parallel: Both narratives move from a household member's fever illness toward wider healing. Publius's father has fevers and dysentery and Paul prays and lays hands; Simon's mother-in-law has a high fever that Jesus rebukes, with hand-laying described for later patients. The patients, methods and additional illness remain distinct.
    Luke 4:38 Leaving the synagogue, He entered Simon’s house. Simon’s mother-in-law was gripped by a high fever, and they asked Him about her.
    Luke 4:39 Standing over her, He rebuked the fever, and it left her. Immediately she rose and served them.
    Luke 4:40 As the sun was setting, everyone who had people sick with various diseases brought them to Him. He laid His hands on each one and healed them.

Acts 28:9
After this happened, the other sick people on the island also came and were healed.
  Luke 4:40 — narrative parallel: Following an initial household healing, other sick people come or are brought and are healed. Luke explicitly describes Jesus laying hands on each; Acts's wider island-healing summary does not repeat a technique for every patient or name an additional healer.
    Luke 4:40 As the sun was setting, everyone who had people sick with various diseases brought them to Him. He laid His hands on each one and healed them.

Acts 28:10
They honored us with many honors, and when we sailed, they supplied what we needed.
  Titus 3:13 — teaching parallel: The islanders supply what the voyagers need at departure; the epistle likewise urges provision for Zenas and Apollos's journey so that they lack nothing. This is practical travel support in different communities, not a claim of identical recipients, payment for healing or the same itinerary.
    Titus 3:13 Make every effort to send Zenas the legal expert and Apollos on their way, so that they lack nothing.

Acts 28:11
After three months we sailed in an Alexandrian ship that had wintered at the island, with the Dioscuri as its emblem.
  Acts 27:6 — narrative parallel: The Alexandrian origin of the new, wintered vessel recalls the Alexandrian ship boarded earlier at Myra. The first ship was wrecked; the new ship has its own wintering and Dioscuri emblem, which are not retroactively assigned to the earlier vessel.
    Acts 27:6 There the centurion found an Alexandrian ship sailing to Italy and put us aboard it.

Acts 28:14
There we found brothers and were invited to stay with them seven days. And so we came to Rome.
  Acts 21:4–21:5 — narrative parallel: Both voyages include finding disciples or brothers, a seven-day stay and then onward travel. Tyre is an earlier Jerusalem-bound stay with its own warning and farewell; Puteoli is on the Rome-bound journey, without those additional actions or the same hosts asserted.
    Acts 21:4 We sought out the disciples and stayed there seven days. Through the Spirit they told Paul not to set foot in Jerusalem.
    Acts 21:5 When our days there were completed, we left and went on our way. They all accompanied us, with wives and children, as far as outside the city. We knelt on the beach and prayed,

Acts 28:15
From there the brothers, having heard about us, came to meet us as far as the Forum of Appius and Three Taverns. When Paul saw them, he thanked God and took courage.
  Romans 1:11–1:12 — thematic parallel: Paul's gratitude and encouragement on meeting Roman brothers correspond to the epistle's longing to see that community and share mutual encouragement through faith. The actual welcome provides a concrete narrative counterpart without proving every proposed travel plan or dating detail of the letter.
    Romans 1:11 For I long to see you, to share with you some spiritual gift so that you may be strengthened,
    Romans 1:12 that is, to be encouraged together among you through one another’s faith, both yours and mine.

Acts 28:16
When we entered Rome, Paul was permitted to stay by himself with the soldier guarding him.
  Acts 24:23 — narrative parallel: Paul again remains guarded while receiving some freedom: the earlier Caesarean order allows attendance by his own people, whereas Rome permits private residence with a guarding soldier. The custody arrangements and officials differ; neither is a narrated final acquittal.
    Acts 24:23 He ordered the centurion to keep Paul in custody but grant him some freedom, and not prevent any of his own people from attending to him.

Acts 28:17
After three days he called together the leading Jews. When they gathered, he said to them, “Men, brothers, although I had done nothing against the people or the ancestral customs, I was handed over as a prisoner from Jerusalem into Roman hands.
  Acts 25:8 — narrative parallel: Paul's earlier defense likewise denies offenses against Jewish law and temple, now restated as nothing against the people or ancestral customs. These are his defenses in different audiences, not an assertion that the Roman Jews are the same accusers or that every earlier clause is repeated.
    Acts 25:8 Paul made his defense: “I have committed no offense against the law of the Jews, against the temple, or against Caesar.”

Acts 28:18
After examining me, they wished to release me, because there was no cause for death in my case.
  Acts 26:31–26:32 — narrative parallel: The earlier hearing party says Paul deserves neither death nor imprisonment and Agrippa comments on possible release. These relate to Paul's present report that examiners wished to release him for lack of a death cause; the earlier comments are not rewritten as a completed acquittal or release order.
    Acts 26:31 After withdrawing, they spoke to one another, saying, “This man is doing nothing deserving death or imprisonment.”
    Acts 26:32 Agrippa said to Festus, “This man could have been released if he had not appealed to Caesar.”

Acts 28:19
But when the Jews objected, I was compelled to appeal to Caesar, though I had no accusation to bring against my nation.
  Acts 25:9–25:12 — narrative parallel: The narrated proposed Jerusalem hearing and Paul's appeal supply the earlier episode behind his present account of being compelled to appeal. The retelling mentions Jewish objection and no accusation against his nation; the earlier exchange does not reproduce every objection or an unreported release hearing.
    Acts 25:9 But Festus, wishing to gain favor with the Jews, answered Paul, “Are you willing to go up to Jerusalem and be judged there before me concerning these things?”
    Acts 25:10 Paul said, “I am standing before Caesar’s tribunal, where I ought to be judged. I have done no wrong to the Jews, as you yourself know very well.
    Acts 25:11 If, then, I am doing wrong and have done anything deserving death, I do not refuse to die. But if there is nothing to their accusations against me, no one can hand me over to them as a favor. I appeal to Caesar.”
    Acts 25:12 Then Festus conferred with his council and answered, “You have appealed to Caesar; to Caesar you will go.”

Acts 28:20
For this reason I asked to see you and speak with you, for it is because of Israel’s hope that I wear this chain.”
  Acts 23:6 — teaching parallel: Paul earlier identifies hope and resurrection of the dead as the issue for which he is judged. He now identifies Israel's hope as the reason for his chain. This is his repeated explanation of the dispute, not a claim that all Jewish parties hold one position about every resurrection question.
    Acts 23:6 When Paul perceived that one part consisted of Sadducees and the other of Pharisees, he cried out in the council, “Men, brothers, I am a Pharisee, a son of Pharisees. I am being judged concerning the hope and resurrection of the dead.”

Acts 28:21
They said to him, “We have received no letters from Judea about you, nor has any of the brothers who arrived reported or spoken anything evil about you.
  Acts 28:22 — narrative parallel: The speakers distinguish receiving no adverse letters or reports about Paul from knowing that his group is widely opposed and wanting to hear his views. This is an explicit contrast of information about the individual and the group, not a declaration that they know nothing else about Paul or that no letter reached any Roman recipient.
    Acts 28:22 But we consider it right to hear from you what you think, for concerning this sect we know that it is opposed everywhere.”

Acts 28:22
But we consider it right to hear from you what you think, for concerning this sect we know that it is opposed everywhere.”
  Acts 24:14 — thematic parallel: Paul earlier acknowledges the Way that others call a sect and explains his worship and scriptural belief. The Roman speakers now use the sect label while requesting his views. The repeated classification concerns the contested group, without endorsing every hostile charge or turning everywhere into proof of every individual's opposition.
    Acts 24:14 But I confess this to you: according to the Way, which they call a sect, I worship the God of our fathers, believing everything in accordance with the law and everything written in the prophets,

Acts 28:23
They appointed a day with him and came to his lodging in greater numbers. From morning until evening he explained to them, bearing solemn witness to the kingdom of God and seeking to persuade them concerning Jesus from both the law of Moses and the prophets.
  Luke 24:44–24:47 — teaching parallel: Paul's explanation concerning Jesus from Moses's law and the prophets parallels Jesus's interpretation of those Scriptures in relation to Himself, His suffering, resurrection and proclamation. The Roman hearing's day-long length and mixed response are not supplied to the earlier Gospel scene.
    Luke 24:44 And He said to them, “These are My words that I spoke to you while I was still with you: everything written about Me in the Law of Moses and the Prophets and Psalms must be fulfilled.”
    Luke 24:45 Then He opened their mind to understand the Scriptures
    Luke 24:46 and said to them, “Thus it is written that the Christ should suffer and rise from the dead on the third day,
    Luke 24:47 and that repentance for the forgiveness of sins should be proclaimed in His name to all the nations—beginning from Jerusalem.

Acts 28:24
Some were persuaded by what was said, while others did not believe.
  Acts 14:4 — narrative parallel: The mixed response of persuaded and unbelieving listeners recalls a city divided over the apostolic mission. The Rome lodging audience and Iconium's civic factions are different scales and groups; neither permits making every Jewish hearer unbelieving.
    Acts 14:4 The people of the city were divided: some sided with the Jews and others with the apostles.

Acts 28:25
Disagreeing with one another, they began to leave after Paul made one statement: “The Holy Spirit spoke well through Isaiah the prophet to your fathers,
  Isaiah 6:9–6:10 — allusion: Paul explicitly attributes the following words to the Holy Spirit through Isaiah. The actual hearing, seeing and heart-and-eyes warning identifies the cited passage; the introduction itself is not treated as containing every quoted clause or as naming every contemporary opponent.
    Isaiah 6:9 And He said, Go, and say to this people: Hearing, you will hear and certainly not understand; and seeing, you will see and certainly not perceive.
    Isaiah 6:10 For the heart of this people has grown dull, and with their ears they have heard heavily, and they have closed their eyes; lest they see with their eyes, and hear with their ears, and understand with their heart, and turn, and I will heal them.

Acts 28:26
saying, ‘Go to this people and say: “You will hear and hear, yet never understand; you will look and look, yet never see.
  Isaiah 6:9 — quotation: The command to go to this people and the hear-without-understanding, see-without-perceiving warning correspond to Isaiah's commission. Acts applies the quoted warning within Paul's speech; it is not a different edition's wording or an independently narrated new Isaiah vision.
    Isaiah 6:9 And He said, Go, and say to this people: Hearing, you will hear and certainly not understand; and seeing, you will see and certainly not perceive.

Acts 28:27
For this people’s heart has grown dull, their ears have become hard of hearing, and they have closed their eyes, lest they see with their eyes and hear with their ears and understand with their heart and turn back, and I would heal them.”’
  Isaiah 6:10 — quotation: The supplied Isaiah text matches the dull heart, heavy hearing, self-closed eyes and turning-and-healing sequence. The human closing of eyes is preserved, without replacing it with another Gospel's differently attributed blinding or supplying an explanation that erases the agency difference.
    Isaiah 6:10 For the heart of this people has grown dull, and with their ears they have heard heavily, and they have closed their eyes; lest they see with their eyes, and hear with their ears, and understand with their heart, and turn, and I will heal them.

Acts 28:28
Therefore let it be known to you that this salvation of God has been sent to the Gentiles; they will listen.”
  Acts 13:46–13:48 — teaching parallel: Paul earlier announces a turn to Gentiles after local opposition, and Gentiles respond with rejoicing and belief. This parallels the present declaration that God's salvation is sent to Gentiles who will listen, without making every Gentile believe or erasing the persuaded Jewish hearers just mentioned.
    Acts 13:46 Paul and Barnabas spoke boldly and said, “It was necessary for God’s word to be spoken to you first. Since you reject it and judge yourselves unworthy of eternal life, look—we are turning to the Gentiles.
    Acts 13:47 For the Lord has commanded us in this way: ‘I have appointed you as a light for Gentiles, so that you may bring salvation to the end of the earth.’”
    Acts 13:48 When the Gentiles heard this, they rejoiced and glorified the word of the Lord, and as many as had been appointed to eternal life believed.

Acts 28:30
He remained two whole years in his own rented lodging and welcomed all who came to him,
  Acts 28:16 — narrative parallel: The extended rented-lodging welcome follows the earlier permission to reside privately under a soldier's guard. This keeps the ministry's accommodation and custody context together, while the two-year statement does not report a final verdict or prove one unchanged street address.
    Acts 28:16 When we entered Rome, Paul was permitted to stay by himself with the soldier guarding him.

Acts 28:31
proclaiming the kingdom of God and teaching the things concerning the Lord Jesus Christ with all boldness, unhindered.
  2 Timothy 2:9 — thematic parallel: The unhindered proclamation while Paul remains a guarded prisoner corresponds to the epistle's contrast between his chains and God's unchained word. The letter does not identify this exact two-year lodging period or supply an unreported acquittal after the book's ending.
    2 Timothy 2:9 for which I suffer hardship even to chains as a criminal. But the word of God has not been chained.

Reviewed units still unfinished
Acts 2:9: The named peoples and regions remain the actual Pentecost list. Shared later geographical names do not identify these visitors or establish who founded a distant Church.
Acts 2:10: The continued regions and Roman visitors are preserved without identifying individuals or deriving an unreported Roman-Church founding from their presence.
Acts 6:9: The Freedmen's synagogue and listed regional groups are particular to this dispute. I do not infer Saul's membership or identify the congregation with another gathering.
Acts 12:21: Herod's appointed-day royal dress, tribunal and address are not identified with a different ruler's judicial hearing or generic ceremonial clothing. The particular scene remains deferred.
Acts 17:9: The officials’ taking security from Jason and the others before release is a particular disposition. No precise matching account is retained; a generic release would not establish this security arrangement, its amount or its legal rationale.
Acts 17:20: The hearers’ particular statement that the teaching sounds strange and their wish to know its meaning is not independently matched here. A generic new-teaching reaction elsewhere would add little or establish neither their understanding nor a formal accusation.
Acts 17:21: The narrator’s specific observation about Athenian and visiting residents spending leisure on the latest news has no precise retained counterpart. An unrelated warning about curiosity or novelty would import a moral judgment not stated in this notice.
Acts 17:22: Paul’s opening appraisal of these Athenians as very religious belongs to this speech’s particular audience and rhetorical approach. An unrelated group’s zeal would not provide a sufficiently precise connection or justify changing the edition’s wording to another appraisal.
Acts 17:33: The brief departure from their midst has no further precise retained counterpart here. Unrelated departures would add little, and the statement must not be expanded into expulsion, conversion of the whole audience or another unreported action.
Acts 17:34: No sufficiently precise additional account of Dionysius the Areopagite, Damaris and the other Athenian adherents is retained. Generic believing crowds elsewhere would not clarify these particular people, and their names, social positions or later histories are not inferred.
Acts 18:7: Titius Justus’s worshiper description and house beside the synagogue have no precise additional retained account here. He is not identified with Titus or another Justus merely from a similar name, and an unrelated host’s hospitality would not establish this house’s location.
Acts 18:17: The source names Sosthenes as the beaten synagogue ruler, whereas the Corinthian letter’s Sosthenes is called the brother without an explicit identification with this man.
